Fractal geometry of the space-time difference profile in the directed landscape via construction of geodesic local times
Shirshendu Ganguly, Lingfu Zhang

Abstract
The Directed Landscape, ℒ \mathcal{L} , a random four parameter energy field, was constructed in the breakthrough work of Dauvergne, Ortmann, and Virág [ DOV18 ] , and has since been shown in [ DV21 ] to be the scaling limit of various integrable models of Last Passage percolation, a central member of the Kardar-Parisi-Zhang universality class, where one considers the maximum passage time between points attained by weight maximizing paths termed as geodesics. Here ℒ ⁡ ( x , s , y , t ) \mathcal{L}(x,s;y,t) denotes the scaled passage time between the points ( x , s ) (x,s) and ( y , t ) . (y,t). ℒ \mathcal{L} exhibits several scale invariance properties making it a natural source of rich fractal behavior, a topic that has been of constant investigation since its construction. This was initiated in [ BGH21 ] , where a novel object called the difference profile: 𝒟 ⁡ ( y , t ) = ℒ ⁡ ( − 1 , 0 , y , t ) − ℒ ⁡ ( − 1 , 0 , y , t ) \mathcal{D}(y,t)=\mathcal{L}(-1,0;y,t)-\mathcal{L}(-1,0;y,t) , the difference of passage times from ( − 1 , 0 ) (-1,0) and ( 1 , 0 ) (1,0) to ( y , t ) (y,t) was introduced. Owing to geodesic geometry, it turns out that this process is almost surely locally constant and the set of non-constancy inherits remarkable fractal properties. In particular, for any fixed t , t, the “spatial process” 𝒟 ⁡ ( ⋅ , t ) \mathcal{D}(\cdot,t) is a monotone function, which can be interpreted as the distribution function of a random fractal measure. Across the articles [ BGH21 , BGH19 , GH21 , Dau21 ] , it has been established that this random measure is supported on a fractal set of Hausdorff dimension 1 / 2 1/2 , connected to disjointness of geodesics and bears a rather strong resemblance to Brownian local time. However, the arguments in the above works crucially rely on the aforementioned monotonicity property which is absent when the temporal structure of 𝒟 ⁡ ( ⋅ , ⋅ ) \mathcal{D}(\cdot,\cdot) is probed, necessitating the development of new methods. In this paper, we put forth several new ideas en-route to computing new fractal exponents governing the same. More precisely, we show that the set of non-constancy of the two dimensional process 𝒟 ⁡ ( ⋅ , ⋅ ) \mathcal{D}(\cdot,\cdot) and the one dimensional temporal process 𝒟 ⁡ ( 0 , ⋅ ) \mathcal{D}(0,\cdot) (a mean zero process owing to symmetry) have Hausdorff dimensions 5 / 3 5/3 and 2 / 3 2/3 respectively. Beyond the construction of infinite geodesics, Busemann functions, competition interfaces for ℒ \mathcal{L} (also constructed independently and simultaneously in [ RV21 ] and [ BSS22 ] ), all objects of much broader interest, a particularly crucial ingredient in our analysis is the novel construction of a local time process for the geodesic akin to Brownian local time, supported on the “zero set” of the geodesic. Further, we show that the latter has Hausdorff dimension 1 / 3 1/3 in contrast to the zero set of Brownian motion which has dimension 1 / 2 . 1/2. Figure 1 : Simulation of the difference profile for the discrete model of Exponential LPP on the positive quadrant ℤ + 2 \mathbb{Z}^{2}_{+} , rooted at the points ( 0 , 50 ) (0,50) and ( 50 , 0 ) (50,0) and hence defined on the cone emanating from (50,50) (rotated by 45 ∘ 45^{\circ} ). Colors denote the level sets. (Courtesy of Milind Hegde.)



Contents

1
Introduction

The Kardar-Parisi-Zhang (KPZ) universality class encompasses a broad family of models of one-dimensional random growth
which are believed to exhibit certain common features, such as local growth driven by white noise and a
smoothing effect from some notion of surface tension and a slope dependent non-linear growth.
It is expected that the fluctuation theories of such models are dictated by universal scaling exponents and limiting distributions,
exhibited by solutions to a stochastic PDE known as the KPZ equation
[
Cor12
,
Cor16
,
HQ18
]
.
The models known or believed to belong to this collection, include asymmetric exclusion processes, first and last passage percolation, and directed polymers in random media.
For these
(1+1)
-dimensional models, the resulting evolution of the growth interface
h(t,x)
is described by the characteristic triple
(1,\frac{1}{3},\frac{2}{3})
of exponents: at time
t
, the value of
h(t,x)
is of order
t^{1}
, with order
t^{1/3}
deviations from its mean, and further, non-trivial correlations are observed when
x
is varied on the scale of
t^{2/3}
.
Furthermore,
h(t,x)
on proper centering and scaling
according to these exponents, is expected to converge to a universal limit
t\to\infty

[
QR14
,
MQR16
]
.

Nonetheless, in spite of the breadth of models thought to lie in the class, only a handful have been rigorously shown to do so.An important subclass of models
believed to be members of the KPZ universality class is
known as last passage percolation (LPP). Ignoring microscopic specifications, in general terms they all consist of an environment of random noise
through which
directed
paths travel, accruing the integral of the noise along it—a quantity known as energy or weight. Given two endpoints, a maximization is done
over the weights of all paths with these endpoints with the
optimizing path called a
geodesic
, although, traditionally, the latter is used to denote
shortest
paths in a metric space.

While most of the above picture is conjectural, recent
developments have
confirmed the convergence of several exactly solvable models to a well-defined scaling limit.
This object was introduced in
[
DOV18
]
and named the
directed landscape
which possesses interesting fractal properties which has been the subject of intense recent study since its construction (see e.g.,
[
Gan21
]
for an account of such and related advances).

This paper continues this program and makes several novel contributions. Before elaborating on this further, let us introduce the key object of study formally.

1.1
Directed landscape

The directed landscape
\mathcal{L}
is a random continuous function from the parameter space

\mathbb{R}^{4}_{\uparrow}=\{u=(p;q)=(x,s;y,t)\in\mathbb{R}^{4}:s<t\}

to
\mathbb{R}
constructed in the breakthrough work
[
DOV18
]
. It satisfies the following composition law

\mathcal{L}(x,r;z,t)=\max_{y\in\mathbb{R}}\mathcal{L}(x,r;y,s)+\mathcal{L}(y,s;z,t)

(1.1)

for any
x,z\in\mathbb{R}
and
r<s<t
.
For a continuous path
\pi:[s,t]\to\mathbb{R}
, its weight under the metric
\mathcal{L}
is given by

\|\pi\|_{\mathcal{L}}=\inf_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\inf_{s=t_{0}<t_{1}<\cdots<t_{k}=t}\sum_{i=1}^{k}\mathcal{L}(\pi(t_{i-1}),t_{i-1};\pi(t_{i}),t_{i}).

Such a path
\pi
is said to be a geodesic from
(\pi(s),s)
to
(\pi(t),t)
if
\|\pi\|_{\mathcal{L}}=\mathcal{L}(\pi(s),s;\pi(t),t)
.
It is shown in
[
DOV18
]
that almost surely, there exists at least one geodesic from
p
to
q
for any
u=(p;q)=(x,s;y,t)\in\mathbb{R}^{4}_{\uparrow}
.
We let
\pi_{u}=\pi_{(p;q)}=\pi_{(x,s;y,t)}
denote any such geodesic from
p
to
q
.
Further, for any fixed
u\in\mathbb{R}^{4}_{\uparrow}
, almost surely the geodesic
\pi_{u}
is unique.

Semi-infinite geodesics:
Going beyond finite geodesics,
for each
p=(x,s)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
and
r\in\mathbb{R}
, we denote by
\pi_{p}^{r}:[s,\infty)\to\mathbb{R}
a semi-infinite geodesic started from
p
in the
r
direction; i.e. we have
t^{-1}\pi_{p}^{r}(t)\to r
as
t\to\infty
, and for any
s\leq s_{1}<s_{2}<\infty
, the restriction of
\pi_{p}^{r}
on
[s_{1},s_{2}]
is a geodesic (from
(\pi_{p}^{r}(s_{1}),s_{1})
to
(\pi_{p}^{r}(s_{2}),s_{2})
).
When
r=0
, we will drop the
r
dependence and simply use
\pi_{p}=\pi_{p}^{0}
. The issues regarding the existence and uniqueness of such semi-infinite geodesics are not straightforward and will be addressed in Section
4
.

The landscape admits invariance under appropriate scalings guided by the exponent triple
(1,1/3,2/3)
, much like Brownian motion which is invariant under diffusive scaling. Hence, as in the case of the latter,
\mathcal{L}
is expected to exhibit various fractal or self-similar properties.

The present paper continues a recently initiated program devoted to studying such properties and makes several novel contributions.

While the main motivation of the paper lies in studying an object called the “
difference profile
”, to be defined shortly, en-route we introduce the
geodesic local time
(GLT), analogous to the classical Brownian local time (BLT), an object we expect to be independently interesting on its own, much akin to its Brownian counterpart which has witnessed intense investigation since its construction by Lévy (see the monograph
[
BC91
]
for a beautiful account of the remarkable properties of BLT, and
[
MP10
, Section 6]
for a comprehensive treatment).

Thus, one of the main contributions of this paper is to formally construct, for the semi-infinite geodesic
\pi_{(0,0)}
, its local time, a non-decreasing function
L:\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}\to\mathbb{R}
recording how much time
\pi_{(0,0)}
has spent at
0
. Further, while much remains to be understood about
L
, we do succeed in establishing some of its properties, tailored for our applications, such as Hölder regularity and the fractal dimension of its support.

We now move towards defining the aforementioned difference profile, an object introduced in
[
BGH21
]
and subsequently the subject of several recent works
[
BGH19
,
GH21
,
Dau21
]
.

To motivate its origin, we begin by pointing out that another central object in the KPZ universality class which had been rigorously established much before
\mathcal{L}
, is known as the parabolic Airy
2
process,
\mathcal{P}_{1}:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}

[
PS02
]
obtained as the projection
\mathcal{L}(0,0,\cdot,1)
, i.e., when the second spatial coordinate is allowed to vary while the remaining space coordinate is fixed to be
0
and the two temporal coordinates are frozen to be
0
and
1
respectively. In words, the parabolic Airy
2
process traces out the last passage time in
\mathcal{L}
from the origin to the points
(\cdot,1).

However, by natural translation invariant properties of
\mathcal{L},
we have the following equality in distribution (among many other symmetries, see e.g., Lemma
3.7
) for any
y\in\mathbb{R},

\mathcal{L}(0,0;\cdot,1)\overset{d}{=}\mathcal{L}(y,0;y+\cdot,1).

Thus the landscape
\mathcal{L}
provides a natural coupling of infinitely many parabolic Airy
2
processes “rooted” at different spatial points, making the coupling structure an intriguing object of study. Such a study was initiated in
[
BGH21
]
, who introduced the following process, which, as we will soon see, encodes valuable information about the coupling.

Difference profile:
Fix
y_{a},y_{b}\in\mathbb{R}
with
y_{a}<y_{b}
. Consider the random function
\mathcal{D}:\mathbb{H}\to\mathbb{R}
(where
\mathbb{H}=\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}_{+}
denotes the upper half plane) given by

\mathcal{D}(x,t)=\mathcal{L}(y_{a},0,x,t)-\mathcal{L}(y_{b},0,x,t).

(1.2)

We will call
\mathcal{D}
the
weight difference profile
; it is the difference in the weights of two geodesics with differing but fixed starting points and a common ending point as the latter varies.

For concreteness, we will fix
y_{a}=-1
and
y_{b}=1
respectively.

Much of this paper is devoted towards computing new fractal exponents associated to
\mathcal{D}
. Since its introduction in
[
BGH21
]
, several of its properties have already been recorded in the literature. We list some of them below.

Owing to certain key properties of geodesics, it turns out that
\mathcal{D}
is almost everywhere locally constant, with probability one (while this is a consequence of known arguments, this has not been completely spelled out in the literature. One of our main results, Theorem
2.1
, will in fact imply this). Further, the following is known.

Lemma 1.1
.

Almost surely, for any fixed
t,

\mathcal{D}(\cdot,t)
is a continuous non-increasing function.

This has been proved several times in the literature, for example
[
DOV18
, Lemma 9.1]
or
[
BGH21
, Theorem 1.1(2)]
.
Thus, in particular, Lemma
1.1
implies that, for any fixed
t,
at any given point
x
,
\mathcal{D}(x,t)
is either constant in a neighborhood of
x
or decreasing at
x
on at least one side.

Moreover, it is an easy argument that the non-constant points of a continuous non-increasing function must form a perfect set, i.e., be closed and have no isolated points. Perfect sets must necessarily be uncountable. In particular,
-\mathcal{D}(\cdot,t)
can be interpreted as the distribution function of a random measure supported on the uncountable set
\overline{\mathsf{NC}}_{t}
(the notation
\overline{\mathsf{NC}}_{t}
is chosen to be in correspondence with the 2D and temporal counterparts
\mathsf{NC}
and
\widehat{\mathsf{NC}}_{x}
, to be defined shortly). Canonical examples of a similar nature include the Cantor function or the non-constant points of BLT. Associated to any such set is its fractal dimension, which quantifies how “sparse” the set is. With the dimension of the mentioned two examples being classically known, one is led to ask: what is the fractal dimension of
\overline{\mathsf{NC}}_{t}
?

This question was originally raised and answered in
[
BGH21
]
, where it was shown that the Hausdorff dimension of
\overline{\mathsf{NC}}_{t}
is
\frac{1}{2}
almost surely. (The definition of the Hausdorff dimension of a set is recalled ahead in Definition
2.5
for the reader’s convenience.)
Further, in
[
BGH19
]
, it was shown that
\overline{\mathsf{NC}}_{t}
is precisely the set of points
x,
which admit geodesics to the points
(-1,0)
and
(1,0)
respectively, which are disjoint except at their initial point
(x,t).

Finally, more recently, in
[
GH21
]
(and subsequently
[
Dau21
]
), comparisons were made between
\mathcal{D}(\cdot,t)
and BLT, showing, among other things, that the former is absolutely continuous with respect to the latter in a certain precise sense.

However, in all the above works, the monotonicity of
\mathcal{D}(\cdot,t)
was crucially used with the arguments breaking down if the temporal coordinate was varied instead, in which case the process
\mathcal{D}(x,\cdot)
is no longer monotone, necessitating the development of new ideas. (See Figure
2
for simulations of
\mathcal{D}
.)

This is the main purpose of this article. Thus, to summarize, we will investigate the spatio-temporal behavior of the process
\mathcal{D}
and compute new fractal dimensions associated to it. As a key ingredient, we introduce and study the notion of GLT, an object we anticipate to be of broader interest and applicability. The connection between the difference profile and GLT is established through a novel level set decomposition which is presented shortly, in Section
2.3
.

Figure 2
:
Simulations of the difference profile
\mathcal{D}
: the left panel is
\mathcal{D}(\cdot,1)
, and the right panel is
\mathcal{D}(0,\cdot)
. (Courtesy of Milind Hegde.)

We now proceed to stating our main results.

2
Main results

As indicated already, the key object of our investigation is the difference profile
\mathcal{D}(x,t)=\mathcal{L}(-1,0;x,t)-\mathcal{L}(1,0;x,t).

2.1
Fractal properties of the difference profile

Our main results pertain to the fractal dimension of the support set of
\mathcal{D}
which is almost surely locally constant.
To this end, we define
\mathsf{NC}\subset\mathbb{H}
to be the set of all
(x,t)
where the difference profile
\mathcal{D}
is not constant in a neighborhood. A one dimensional variant is
\widehat{\mathsf{NC}}_{x}\subset\mathbb{R}_{+}
consisting of all
t
such that the function
t\mapsto\mathcal{D}(x,t)
is not constant in a neighborhood of
t
.
We note that
\widehat{\mathsf{NC}}_{x}\subset\{t\in\mathbb{R}_{+}:(x,t)\in\mathsf{NC}\}
. The set
\widehat{\mathsf{NC}}_{x}
is exactly the temporal analogue of the spatial set
\overline{\mathsf{NC}}_{t}
of all
x
such that
\mathcal{D}(x,t)
is not locally constant, considered in the papers
[
BGH21
,
BGH19
,
GH21
,
Dau21
]
.
As we will mostly work with the temporal non-constant set at
x=0
, we let
\widehat{\mathsf{NC}}=\widehat{\mathsf{NC}}_{0}
.

With the above preparation, we can now state our two main results.

Theorem 2.1
.

Almost surely, the set
\mathsf{NC}
has Hausdorff dimension
5/3
.

Theorem 2.2
.

Almost surely, the set
\widehat{\mathsf{NC}}
has Hausdorff dimension
2/3
.

As the reader will notice from the arguments presented in the paper, unlike the process
\mathcal{D}(\cdot,t)
which is monotone and decreases from
-\infty
to
\infty
as
x
varies on
\mathbb{R},
the temporal process
\mathcal{D}(0,\cdot)
eventually becomes a constant owing to geodesic coalescence. Further, the process, almost surely, changes sign infinitely often as
t
approaches
0
owing to symmetry, de-correlation across different values of
t
as the latter converges to
0
suitably fast, and ergodicity of the directed landscape.

As already indicated in the introduction, the proofs of our main theorems rely on a key ingredient, namely, a novel construction of the geodesic local time (GLT), and analysis of its fractal properties which we include as our final main result.
Recall that we use
\pi_{p}^{r}
to denote the semi-infinite geodesic started from
p
in the
r
direction (the formal treatement will appear in Section
4
), and write
\pi_{p}=\pi_{p}^{0}
.

2.2
The geodesic local time

Throughout this paper we let
\mathbf{0}
denote the origin
(0,0)
. Consider the geodesic
\pi_{\mathbf{0}}
, and its intersection with the line
\{(0,t):t\geq 0\}
.
Denote by

Z:=\{t\geq 0:\pi_{\mathbf{0}}(t)=0\},

(2.1)

the geodesic zero set.

Theorem 2.3
.

Almost surely, the set
Z
has Hausdorff dimension
1/3
.

The proofs of all these three theorems rely on the construction of GLT, a non-decreasing function
L:\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}\to\mathbb{R}
, which records the time
\pi_{\mathbf{0}}
has spent at
0
.
This induces a (random) measure
\mu
on
\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
, supported on the set
Z
similar to how BLT is supported on the zero set of Brownian motion. An important step in the construction of
L
is the construction of the geodesic occupation measure.
We define the occupation measure
\nu_{h}
for the geodesic
\pi_{\mathbf{0}}
at height
h
, by setting for any measurable
A\subset\mathbb{R}
,

\nu_{h}(A)=\int_{0}^{h}\mathds{1}[\pi_{\mathbf{0}}(t)\in A]dt.

(2.2)

Given the above, the construction of
L
is completed by proving the existence of the density of
\nu_{h}
at
0
.

Proposition 2.4
.

For any height
h>0,
almost surely the limit
\lim_{w\searrow 0}(2w)^{-1}\nu_{h}([-w,w])
exists and is finite.

Before proceeding further, it is worth pointing out some other recent advances around the theme of fractal properties of last passage percolation. While the works
[
JRA20
,
JRAS19
,
SS21a
]
focussed on pre-limiting models,
[
CHHM21
,
Dau22
]
worked with the evolution induced by the directed landscape started from rather general initial data (an object termed as the KPZ-fixed point, see
[
MQR16
]
). In another direction, fractal properties often manifests in remarkable behavior of the system pertaining to “chaos and noise-sensitivity”. Refer e.g. to the success story of the study of dynamical critical percolation summarized beautifully in
[
GS14
]
. In the KPZ universality paradigm, such a study was initiated recently in
[
GH20b
]
.

2.3
Key ideas in the proofs

The arguments in this paper involve several new ideas, which we describe in some detail in this section.

We first setup some notations that will be used throughout this paper.
For any set
A,
we use
|A|
to denote its cardinality. We use
\mathscr{L}
to denote the Lebesgue measure on
\mathbb{R}
. The notation
\|\cdot\|
denotes the
l^{2}
norm unless otherwise stated. Recall that we let
\mathbf{0}=(0,0)
. For any
a\leq b
, we let
\llbracket a,b\rrbracket=[a,b]\cap\mathbb{Z}
.
We would need to consider the space of all non-empty compact subsets of
\mathbb{R}^{2}
which equipped with the Hausdorff metric, becomes a complete metric space, with the induced topology referred to as the Hausdorff topology.
We will also freely use the notation
\argmax
even when there isn’t a unique maximum (but there must exist at least one) using it to denote an arbitrary one.

We next recall the definitions of the Hausdorff dimension and Hausdorff measure of a set.

Definition 2.5
.

For any
d\geq 0
and metric space
X
, the
d
-dimensional Hausdorff measure of
X
is defined as

\lim_{\delta\searrow 0}\inf\left\{\sum_{i}\diam(U_{i})^{d}:\{U_{i}\}\text{ is a countable cover of }X\text{ with }0<\diam(U_{i})<\delta\right\}.

The Hausdorff dimension of
X
is
\inf\{d>0:\text{ the $d$-dimensional Hausdorff measure of $X$ is zero }\}
.

We start by stating two general strategies to obtain upper and lower bounds for the Hausdorff dimension for
X
being a subset of
\mathbb{R}^{n}
(for some
n\in\mathbb{N}
).

•

Upper bound: Show that for any
U\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
,
\mathbb{P}[X\cap U\neq\emptyset]<\diam(U)^{n-d}
(potentially with some extra logarithmic factors).
Then for any compact
S\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
, on dividing
S
into roughly
\delta^{-n}
subsets, each with diameter
\leq\delta
,
X
is covered by the union of roughly
\delta^{-d}
of them. Thus for any
\epsilon>0
, the
d+\epsilon
-dimensional Hausdorff measure of
X
is zero. So the Hausdorff dimension of
X
is at most
d
.

•

Lower bound: This is usually the harder direction and involves constructing a measure
m
whose support is contained in
X
, such that
m(X)>0
, and for any measurable
U\subset X
the Hölder estimate
m(U)<\diam(U)^{d}
(potentially with extra sub-polynomial factors) holds. Then for any countable cover
\{U_{i}\}
of
X
, one must have
\sum_{i}\diam(U_{i})^{d}>m(X)
, implying that the Hausdorff dimension of
X
is at least
d
.

Before diving into our arguments, for contrast, let us first briefly sketch the nature of the arguments appearing in the analysis of
\mathcal{D}(\cdot,t)
in
[
BGH21
]
(from here on we will fix
t=1
) and why they fail in the case of
\mathcal{D}(x,\cdot)
(we will fix
x=0
).
A key starting observation in
[
BGH21
]
is the following (also see Figure
3(a)
): for two points
x_{1}<x_{2},

\mathcal{D}(x,1)
is constant on
[x_{1},x_{2}]
if the geodesics
\pi_{(-1,0;x_{1},1)}

\pi_{(1,0;x_{2},1)}
coalesce.

(-1,0)
(1,0)
(x_{1},1)
(x_{2},1)

(a)

If the geodesics
\pi_{(-1,0;x_{1},1)}
and
\pi_{(1,0;x_{2},1)}
coalesce, any geodesic from
(-1,0)
or
(1,0)
to any
(x,1)
with
x\in[x_{1},x_{2}]
must stay with
\pi_{(-1,0;x_{1},1)}
or
\pi_{(1,0;x_{2},1)}
up to the end of their intersection. This implies that
\mathcal{D}(x,1)
is constant on
[x_{1},x_{2}]
.

(-1,0)
(1,0)
(0,t_{1})
(z,t_{1})
(0,t_{2})

(b)

If all geodesics from
(-1,0)
and
(1,0)
to
(x,t_{1})
coalescence, for
x
in an order
(t_{2}-t_{1})^{2/3}
neighborhood of
0
, the process
\mathcal{D}(0,\cdot)
is (likely to be) constant on
[t_{1},t_{2}]
.

Figure 3
:
Illustrations for the arguments in
[
BGH21
]
(for upper bounding the Hausdorff dimension), and the same strategy in the temporal direction.

Thus to prove an upper bound on the Hausdorff dimension of
\overline{\mathsf{NC}}_{1},

[
BGH21
]
uses as input the following estimate from
[
Ham20
]
:
For any
x_{1}<x_{2}
,
y_{1}<y_{2}
, and
s<t
, if we let
\Dist_{(x_{1},x_{2})\to(y_{1},y_{2})}^{[s,t]}
be the event that
\pi_{(x_{1},s;y_{1},t)}
and
\pi_{(x_{2},s;y_{2},t)}
are unique and disjoint, then

Theorem 2.6
.

There is a constant
C
, such that for any
\epsilon>0
, we have

\mathbb{P}[\Dist_{(-1,1)\to(-\epsilon,\epsilon)}^{[0,1]}]<C\epsilon^{1/2}e^{C|\log(\epsilon)|^{5/6}}.

Thus, for any small
\epsilon,
ignoring sub-polynomial factors, the probability that
\overline{\mathsf{NC}}_{1}
intersects any interval of size
\epsilon
is at most
\epsilon^{1/2}
, which then leads to an upper bound of
1/2
on the Hausdorff dimension, according to the above general strategy.

For the lower bound, the argument relies on Hölder regularity of
\mathcal{L}.
Namely that the Parabolic Airy
2
process is locally
1/2-
Hölder-regular (in fact it is globally parabolic and locally Brownian, see e.g.,
[
CH14
,
Ham16
,
CHH19
]
) and so is
\mathcal{D}(\cdot,1)
being the difference of two Airy
2
processes rooted at
\pm 1
.
By interpreting
-\mathcal{D}(\cdot,1)
as the distribution function of a measure and the above general strategy, we get a Hausdorff dimension lower bound of
1/2
.

It is instructive to apply now the same strategy to analyze the process
\mathcal{D}(0,\cdot)
instead (also see Figure
3(b)
). For two points
t_{1}<t_{2}
, consider the geodesics
\pi_{(-1,0;0,t_{1})}
and
\pi_{(1,0;0,t_{2})}.
Let the latter intersect the line
t=t_{1}
at location
z,
then by a similar argument as before, one can essentially conclude (the precise conclusion is somewhat different which we will not spell out here) that
\mathcal{D}(0,\cdot)
is constant on
[t_{1},t_{2}]
unless the geodesics
\pi_{(-1,0;0,t_{1})}
and
\pi_{(1,0;z,t_{1})}
are disjoint. Now, it is known, and goes back to the work of Johansson
[
Joh00
]
(see also
[
BSS14
,
HS20
,
DSV20
]
), that the geodesics in LPP and hence
\mathcal{L}
are
2/3-
Hölder-regular, which then implies that
z\approx|t_{2}-t_{1}|^{2/3}.

This in conjunction with the above estimate on existence of disjoint geodesics imply that the probability that
\widehat{\mathsf{NC}}
intersects
[t_{1},t_{2}]
is at most, up to sub-polynomial factors,
|t_{2}-t_{1}|^{1/3}
which translates into a Hausdorff dimension upper bound of
2/3
for
\widehat{\mathsf{NC}}.

On the other hand, applying the same reasoning as before, we now seek to use the fact that
\mathcal{L}
is
1/3-
Hölder-regular in the temporal direction, implying at least as much regularity for
\mathcal{D}(0,\cdot)
. This then yields, again by the above general strategy, a lower bound of
1/3
for the Hausdorff dimension which unlike the spatial case, falls short of matching the upper bound.

The reason for the disparity is that the process
\mathcal{D}(0,\cdot)
is a mean zero process and hence not monotone.
In the spatial case, when applying the above general strategy, the process
\mathcal{D}(\cdot,1)
was itself inducing a measure owing to monotonicity, whereas the process
\mathcal{D}(0,\cdot)
induces a signed measure instead. Thus the argument which essentially works with the “absolute value” of the measure, or the “un-signed” measure fails to capture certain cancellations exhibited by
\mathcal{D}(0,\cdot),
which we expect leads to a square-root fluctuation effect boosting the lower bound from
1/3
to
2/3.

Thus to obtain a sharp lower bound we will seek to construct new measures capturing the appropriate cancellation.
This involves many new ideas and is quite technical. We will review the key steps in the sequel but first let us digress a bit allowing us to introduce a few new objects which will play central roles in our arguments.

GLT and the zero set:
As is the case in Brownian motion, the zero set of the semi-infinite geodesic
\pi_{\mathbf{0}}
(see the beginning of Section
2.2
) denoted by
Z
, will turn out to be a particularly fundamental object. As indicated in Section
2.2
, we will construct GLT
L
and show that it is
1/3
Hölder-regular, and using that we will show that
Z
has Hausdorff dimension
1/3
(as stated in Theorem
2.3
). This will follow from the following estimate, whose proof will be briefly reviewed at the end of this section:

Lemma 2.7
.

There are universal constants
C,c>0
such that the following is true.
For any closed interval
I\subset\mathbb{R}
and
J\subset\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
, we have

\mathbb{P}[\mathscr{L}(\{t\in J:\pi_{\mathbf{0}}(t)\in I\})>M\mathscr{L}(I)\mathscr{L}(J)^{1/3}]<Ce^{-cM}

for any
M>0
.

Competition interfaces:
The next object that we need to consider pertains to level sets. As is perhaps not surprising from the fact that we are investigating the behavior of the difference profile
\mathcal{D}
, it would be particularly convenient to consider level sets of the form

\vartheta_{\ell}(t)=\inf\{x\in\mathbb{R}:\mathcal{D}(x,t)\leq\ell\}.

(2.3)

It turns out however that a remarkable identity holds if we were to take
\ell=0
and consider the difference profile not rooted at
\pm 1
, but with Brownian initial data instead.
Namely, let
\mathcal{B}
be a standard two sided Brownian motion.
For any
x\in\mathbb{R},t>0
, we let

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}^{L}(x,t)

\displaystyle=\sup_{y\leq 0}\mathcal{L}(y,0;x,t)+\mathcal{B}(y),

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}^{R}(x,t)

\displaystyle=\sup_{y\geq 0}\mathcal{L}(y,0;x,t)+\mathcal{B}(y),

be the passage times induced by the Brownian initial data from the negative and positive parts of the
x-
axis respectively.
One can show that the function
x\mapsto\mathcal{L}^{L}(x,t)-\mathcal{L}^{R}(x,t)
is non-increasing in
x
(see the beginning of Section
5
).
We then let

\vartheta^{\mathcal{B}}(t)=\inf\{x\in\mathbb{R}:\mathcal{L}^{L}(x,t)-\mathcal{L}^{R}(x,t)\leq 0\}.

(2.4)

One then has the remarkable duality result:

Proposition 2.8
.

The processes
\vartheta^{\mathcal{B}}
and
\pi_{\mathbf{0}}
are equal in distribution.

In the discrete setting (of exponential LPP), this goes back to
[
FP05
,
BCS06
,
FMP09
]
, and we get the above directed landscape version using a limit transition from
[
DV21
]
. During the preparation of this paper, two articles
[
RV21
,
SS21b
]
were posted on the arXiv where this was independently developed. While
[
RV21
]
worked with the directed landscape as in the present paper,
[
SS21b
]
considered the prelimiting model of Brownian LPP instead.

Now, how does this help us? Suppose for the moment that the same were true for the level sets
\vartheta_{\ell}(\cdot)
for all
\ell
, in place of
\vartheta^{\mathcal{B}}(\cdot).

Recalling that we only seek to prove a lower bound of
2/3
for the Hausdorff dimension of
\widehat{\mathsf{NC}},
we will now construct a measure supported on
\widehat{\mathsf{NC}}
which will be
2/3
Hölder-regular. At a very high level, this is achieved by taking the average of the local time for each of the level sets. The construction proceeds as in the case of GLT. Now using the above assumed comparison between level sets and the geodesic, it follows that each of the individual level set local times are
1/3
Hölder-regular using Lemma
2.7
. Given this, we obtain the desired
2/3
Hölder regularity, using Hölder regularity of
\mathcal{L}
.

To see this, consider an interval
[t_{1},t_{2}].
Now each
\vartheta_{\ell}
induces a
1/3
Hölder-regular measure
\kappa_{\ell}
. To define this we start by defining the corresponding occupation measures analogous to (
2.2
).
For each
h>0
, we denote by
\lambda_{\ell,h}
the measure on
\mathbb{R}
, such that

\lambda_{\ell,h}(A)=\int_{0}^{h}\mathds{1}[\vartheta_{\ell}(t)\in A]dt,

(2.5)

for any measurable
A\subset\mathbb{R}
. Given this,
\kappa_{\ell}
on an interval
(g,h)
is then defined to be the density of
\lambda_{\ell,h}-\lambda_{\ell,g}
at
0,
that is,

\kappa_{\ell}((g,h))=\lim_{w\searrow 0}(2w)^{-1}(\lambda_{\ell,h}([-w,w])-\lambda_{\ell,g}([-w,w])).

(2.6)

(The actual definition proceeds a bit differently owing to certain measure theoretic considerations which we will ignore in this discussion).
Define now the measure
\kappa
by setting

\kappa(A)=\int\kappa_{\ell}(A)d\ell.

(2.7)

(0,0)
(0,t_{1})
(0,t_{2})
\vartheta_{\ell_{1}}
\vartheta_{\ell_{2}}

(a)

If both
\vartheta_{\ell_{1}}
and
\vartheta_{\ell_{2}}
intersect the interval
\{0\}\times[t_{1},t_{2}]
, it is likely that
|\ell_{2}-\ell_{1}|
is in the order of at most
|t_{2}-t_{1}|^{1/3}
.

\mathbf{0}
\mathcal{B}

(b)

An illustration of the event where the Brownian motion
\mathcal{B}
has a very spiky behavior with spikes near
\pm 1
, and geodesic coalescence.

Figure 4
:

This is the measure that we use to lower bound the Hausdorff dimension of
\widehat{\mathsf{NC}}
by showing that
\kappa
is supported in
\widehat{\mathsf{NC}}
and further for any interval
[t_{1},t_{2}],

\kappa([t_{1},t_{2}])\approx|t_{2}-t_{1}|^{2/3}.
Very briefly, the latter holds because by definition
\kappa([t_{1},t_{2}])=\int\kappa_{\ell}([t_{1},t_{2}])d\ell.
Now by the claimed
1/3
Hölder regularity of GLT and the assumed comparison between
\kappa_{\ell}
and the same, the integrand is at most
|t_{2}-t_{1}|^{1/3}.
Now by the
1/3
Hölder regularity of the landscape in the temporal direction (a consequence of
1/3
being the universal exponent governing weight fluctuations in the KPZ universality class), for
\vartheta_{\ell_{1}}
and
\vartheta_{\ell_{2}}
to both intersect the interval
\{0\}\times[t_{1},t_{2}]
, we must have, again ignoring sub-polynomial factors,
|\ell_{2}-\ell_{1}|\leq|t_{2}-t_{1}|^{1/3}
(see Figure
4(a)
), which yields the desired estimate
\kappa([t_{1},t_{2}])=\int\kappa_{\ell}([t_{1},t_{2}])=O(|t_{2}-t_{1}|^{2/3})
since the integrand is non-zero only on a interval of size at most
|t_{2}-t_{1}|^{1/3}.

Note that the above reasoning is contingent on the assumption that the measures
\kappa_{\ell}
are
1/3
Hölder-regular which was a consequence of the claim that the level curves
\vartheta_{\ell}
are “similar” to the geodesic
\pi_{\mathbf{0}}.
We rely on Proposition
2.8
to prove a result of this kind which suffices for our purpose. At a very high level, the arguments in this part is based on the observation that if the Brownian motion
\mathcal{B}
has a very spiky behavior with spikes near
\pm 1
respectively as in Figure
4(b)
then this resembles the original situation of considering the difference profile rooted at
\pm 1
respectively. In particular, in presence of such spikes, owing to geodesic coalescence,
\vartheta^{\mathcal{B}}
is the same as
\vartheta_{\ell}
for some random
\ell
, where the law of the latter dominates a multiple of Lebesgue measure owing to the fluctuation of the difference in the height of the spikes at
\pm 1
, which can be obtained by resampling suitable parts of
\mathcal{B}
.

Theorem
2.1
is proved in the same way as Theorem
2.2
and is in fact somewhat easier technically since instead of working with the “density” measures in (
2.6
), we work with the occupation measures in (
2.5
) instead and the “occupation” counterpart of
\kappa
by taking their integral over different values of
\ell
. The same set of arguments then concludes the proof. In this case the measure we work with is simply
\zeta
, such that for any
a<b
and
0\leq g<h
, we let

\zeta([a,b]\times(g,h))=\int_{g}^{h}[\mathcal{D}(a,t)-\mathcal{D}(b,t)]dt=\int_{\ell}[\lambda_{\ell,h}([a,b])-\lambda_{\ell,g}([a,b])]d\ell.

(2.8)

Now when
b-a=h-g\approx\epsilon,
using the same argument as before one can conclude that
\zeta([a,b]\times(g,h))\approx\epsilon^{5/3}
leading to the desired lower bound on the dimension.

We end the above discussion by pointing out that while the difference profile
\mathcal{D}
we consider is rooted at
(\pm 1,0),
one may generalize our argument to a rather general class of “rooting functions” where for functions
f_{1},f_{2},
one considers

(x,t)\mapsto\sup_{y}[\mathcal{L}(y,0;x,t)+f_{1}(y)]-\sup_{y}[\mathcal{L}(y,0;x,t)+f_{2}(y)].

In particular, for the special case
f_{1},f_{2}:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}\cup\{-\infty\}
, such that (1) they are bounded from above on any compact interval, (2) there exists some
x_{*}>0
such that
f_{1}(x)=-\infty
for any
x>-x_{*}
and
f_{2}(x)=-\infty
for any
x<x_{*}
, (3)
\limsup_{|x|\to\infty}f_{1}(x)/x^{2},f_{2}(x)/x^{2}\leq 0
, essentially our arguments can be carried out without any significant changes to prove the counterparts of Theorems
2.1
and
2.2
in this setting. However we do not pursue spelling out all the details which will be done in future work where more general
f_{1}
and
f_{2}
will be treated as well.

The remainder of the discussion is devoted to providing a brief overview of the ideas going into the other novel aspects of the paper. This includes the construction of the semi-infinite geodesic
\pi_{\mathbf{0}}
as well as the proof of the duality relation with the competition interface (
2.4
), and finally the construction of GLT and the local times for the level curves (
2.3
). As already mentioned, these objects and similar ideas have recently appeared on the arXiv in the articles
[
RV21
,
SS21b
,
BSS22
]
during the preparation of this paper.

Construction of
\pi_{\mathbf{0}}
: The construction relies on geodesic coalescence. To construct a semi-infinite geodesic we show that the finite geodesics
\pi_{(p;z_{n},h_{n})},
for any sequences
\{z_{n}\}_{n=1}^{\infty}
and
\{h_{n}\}_{n=1}^{\infty}
satisfying that
z_{n}\to\infty
as
n\to\infty
and
|z_{n}-h_{n}r|<h_{n}^{2/3}
for each
n\in\mathbb{N}
, owing to geodesic coalescence, all share the same initial segment. Further, the length of the initial segment itself goes off to infinity as
n\to\infty.

\pi_{\mathbf{0}}
is then defined to be the path made up of such initial segments.

While the construction is intuitive, it does involve some amount of work to show that it works. Namely it has the desired direction. We further show that there is a unique such semi-infinite geodesic.

We will not comment much on how the above is carried out except that we rely on a construction of the Busemann function in the vertical direction (again relying on geodesic coalescence).
Busemann functions were originally used to study the large-scale geometry of geodesics in Riemannian manifolds, and was introduced to study general first passage percolation (FPP) models by Hoffman
[
Hof05
,
Hof08
]
. It has also been intensively studied and used in the exponential LPP setting; see e.g.
[
Sep17
]
.
We then show that in our setting the Busemann function is a Brownian motion and that
\pi_{\mathbf{0}}(t)
, is the argmax of the sum of the Busemann function and an independent copy of an appropriately scale Parabolic Airy
2
process. By the known Brownianity of both the functions and their independence, it follows that
\pi_{\mathbf{0}}(t)
is unique, at least for rational
t
and hence for all
t
by continuity. Further, it follows by estimates of Brownian motion as well as the Parabolic Airy
2
process, that
\pi_{\mathbf{0}}(t)\approx O(t^{2/3})
and thereby implying that
\pi_{\mathbf{0}}
indeed has the desired direction.

Proof of duality:
The duality is known to hold in the discrete model of Exponential LPP. To prove the directed landscape version, we rely on the recent convergence results proved in
[
DV21
]
, which in particular implies that both the semi-infinite geodesic and the competition interfaces in the discrete setting converge to their counterparts
\pi_{\mathbf{0}}
and
\vartheta^{\mathcal{B}}
which is enough to conclude the proof. To be completely precise, however, the results in
[
DV21
]
only can be used to deduce convergence of certain “compactified” versions of the above objects. To make use of this, we work with certain truncations of
\pi_{\mathbf{0}}
and
\vartheta^{\mathcal{B}}
, and their discrete versions, show that with high probability the truncated versions agree with the untruncated ones, the discrete ones converge to the continuous counterparts and hence the discrete duality transfers to the desired continuous duality statement.

Construction of GLT:
Akin to the construction of BLT, this proceeds by first constructing the occupation measure for
\pi_{\mathbf{0}}
as defined in (
2.2
) at height
h
, by setting for any measurable
A\subset\mathbb{R}
,

\nu_{h}(A)=\int_{0}^{h}\mathds{1}[\pi_{\mathbf{0}}(t)\in A]dt.

(2.9)

All that remains is to show that
\nu_{h}
has a density at
0,
(see the Proposition
2.4
), i.e.,
almost surely, the limit
\lim_{w\searrow 0}(2w)^{-1}\nu_{h}([-w,w])
exists and is finite. While the finiteness is a consequence of the estimate in Lemma
2.7
, we briefly explain the idea behind the proof of the existence of a density. The starting point is the observation that, by Lebesgue’s theorem for the differentiability of monotone functions, for any measure on
\mathbb{R}
that is locally finite, almost every point is a point of density. However, a priori, this set of density points (which is a random set) could avoid
0
. We now want to show that almost surely that is not the case. Now this would be immediate if the occupation measure
\nu_{h}
was translation invariant in law, i.e., for any real number
x,

\nu_{h}(\cdot)\overset{d}{=}\nu_{h}(\cdot+x),
since any translation invariant set must contain a given point, in this case
0
, with probability one. The proof now proceeds by constructing
\nu^{\prime}_{h},
a translation invariant “proxy” for
\nu_{h},
namely considering the occupation measure of a collection of semi-infinite geodesics started from a Poisson process of intensity one. The latter measure does admit a density at
0
almost surely given a sample of the Poisson process and the landscape
\mathcal{L}.
Now owing to geodesic coalescence and that there is a positive probability that the Poisson process consists of a point quite close to the origin and no other points in the vicinity, it follows that the occupation measure of the two measures agree, (at least away from
0
), i.e.,

\nu^{\prime}_{h}-\nu^{\prime}_{g}=\nu_{h}-\nu_{g}

for any
0<g<h.
This is enough to finish the proof once we show that
\nu_{g}
is negligible if
g
is small enough.

We finish with a brief discussion on the proof of Lemma
2.7
.
A slightly more general statement in the discrete setting of Exponential LPP had already appeared previously in
[
SSZ21
]
. For completeness we include the arguments adapted to the setting of the Directed Landscape and describe some of the ideas here.

To begin, a simple argument shows that
\mathscr{L}(I)\mathscr{L}(J)^{1/3}
is the correct order of the random variable of interest, namely
\mathscr{L}(\{t\in J:\pi_{\mathbf{0}}(t)\in I\})
. This is because, by KPZ transversal fluctuation considerations, for any height
t,

\pi_{\mathbf{0}}(t)
is distributed roughly uniformly on an interval
t^{2/3}
and hence, if
J=[a,b],

\mathbb{E}[\mathscr{L}(\{t\in J:\pi_{\mathbf{0}}(t)\in I\})]\approx\int_{a}^{b}\frac{\mathscr{L}(I)}{t^{2/3}}\lesssim\mathscr{L}(I)\mathscr{L}(J)^{1/3}

The proof of the lemma now relies on the above expectation bound for “all”
I
and
J
and dominating
\mathscr{L}(\{t\in J:\pi_{\mathbf{0}}(t)\in I\}
by a sum of independent random variables and appealing to standard concentration of measure results. However the geodesic does not admit a decomposition into independent segments. To get around this, one uses the observation that a similar expectation bound as above holds when one considers the maximum occupation time, say among all geodesics with endpoints outside
I\times J.
Note that such a class of geodesics in particular includes segments of the geodesic
\pi_{\mathbf{0}}
crossing such a box but crucially such a quantity across different boxes is independent.

Thus the occupation time in a box can be dominated by an independent sum of occupation times in smaller boxes with the same width, which by scale invariance, corresponds to the original box with a larger width. At this point we use induction, assuming the desired exponential tail for larger widths (which is straightforward to establish for a large enough width) and standard concentration bounds to conclude the same for a smaller width, going down in dyadic scales.

Organization of the remaining text.

The rest of this paper is organized in the following manner.
In Section
3
we set up formally the directed landscape and the related pre-limit model we will be analyzing, and recall some basic results from the literature.
In Section
4
we construct the Busemann function and semi-infinite geodesics in the directed landscape.
The duality between semi-infinite geodesics and competition interface is given in Section
5
.
The last three sections contain proofs of the main results concerning Hausdorff dimensions: Section
6
is for the 2D non-constant set (Theorem
2.1
), Section
7
focusses on GLT (Theorem
2.3
) while Section
8
is for the non-constant set in the temporal direction (Theorem
2.2
).
The appendices contain proofs of several useful intermediate results which could be of independent interest. They are either technical or resemble arguments appearing earlier in the literature.

, and Evan Sorensen for helpful comments.

3
Preliminaries

As indicated in the previous section, our arguments will rely on various existing definitions and results in the last passage percolation literature which we collect in this section. This includes various results about the pre-limiting model of exponential LPP on the lattice (Section
3.1
, a significant part of it will be devoted to reviewing the duality between competition interfaces and infinite geodesics as well as the recent results proving its convergence to the directed landscape), basic properties of the directed landscape (Section
3.2
) and the Airy line ensemble (Section
3.3
). The reader can skip this section on first read and come back to it later as needed.

3.1
Exponential LPP

We consider directed last passage percolation (LPP) on
\mathbb{Z}^{2}
with i.i.d. exponential weights on the vertices, i.e., we have a random field
\{\omega_{p}:p\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}\}
where
\omega_{p}
are i.i.d.
\Exp(1)
random variables. For any up/right path
\gamma
from
p
to
q
where
p\preceq q
(i.e.,
p
is co-ordinate wise smaller or equal than
q
) the weight of
\gamma
, denoted
T(\gamma)
is defined by

T(\gamma):=\sum_{w\in\gamma}\omega_{w}.

For any two points
p
and
q
with
p\preceq q
, we shall denote by
T_{p,q}
the last passage time from
p
to
q
; i.e., the maximum weight among weights of all directed paths from
p
to
q
.
The (almost surely unique) directed path with the maximum weight is the geodesic from
p
to
q
, denoted as
\Gamma_{p,q}
.
When
p\not\preceq q
, we let
T_{p,q}=-\infty
.

In the setting of exponential LPP, the existences and uniqueness of semi-infinite geodesics have been established (see
[
Cou11
,
FP05
]
), and we summarize the known facts here.
For any
p\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}
, there is an almost surely unique upper-right path from it, denoted as

\Gamma_{p}=\{p=(x_{0},y_{0}),(x_{1},y_{1}),\cdots\},

such that for any
0\leq i\leq j
, the path
(x_{i},y_{i}),\cdots,(x_{j},y_{j})
is
\Gamma_{(x_{i},y_{i}),(x_{j},y_{j})}
, and
\lim_{i\to\infty}\frac{x_{i}}{y_{i}}=1
.
The path
\Gamma_{p}
is also referred to as the
(1,1)
-directional semi-infinite geodesic from
p
.
Almost surely, for any sequence
q_{n}\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}
that goes to infinity in the
(1,1)
direction,
\Gamma_{p,q_{n}}
converges to
\Gamma_{p}
, in the sense that in any compact set
\Gamma_{p,q_{n}}
is the same as
\Gamma_{p}
when
n
is large enough.
The collection of all the semi-infinite geodesics (in the
(1,1)
-direction) exhibits a tree structure. In particular, for any
p,q\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}
, the paths
\Gamma_{p}
and
\Gamma_{q}
are the same except for finitely many initial points.

Below we shall always assume (the probability
1
event) that for any
p\preceq q\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}
, there is a unique geodesic
\Gamma_{p,q}
, and a unique
(1,1)
-direction semi-infinite geodesic
\Gamma_{p}
.

For the rest of this paper, we let
d,ad:\mathbb{Z}^{2}\to\mathbb{Z}
be the function where
d(x,y)=x+y
and
ad(x,y)=x-y
. For each
n\in\mathbb{Z}
the anti-diagonal line
\mathbb{L}_{n}=\{p\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}:d(p)=2n\}
will be important.

3.1.1
Convergence to the directed landscape

In
[
DV21
]
, it is proved that the exponential LPP converges to the directed landscape, in a sense that we record precisely next.
Define a metric
\mathcal{K}_{n}
on
\mathbb{R}^{2}
as follows.
For any
(x,s;y,t)\in\mathbb{R}^{4}
, we let

\mathcal{K}_{n}(x,s;y,t)=2^{-4/3}n^{-1/3}F(ns+2^{5/3}n^{2/3}x,ns;nt+2^{5/3}n^{2/3}y,nt),

where
F(p;q)=T_{p,q}-2d(q-p)-\omega_{p}
for
p,q\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}
,
and
F
is extended to be a function on
\mathbb{R}^{4}
using the following rounding from
[
DV21
]
.
Let
\mathsf{r}:\mathbb{R}^{2}\to\mathbb{Z}^{2}
be the function where

\mathsf{r}(x,y)=\begin{cases}(x,y)&x,y\in\mathbb{Z};\\
(\lfloor x\rfloor,y)&x\not\in\mathbb{Z},y\in\mathbb{Z};\\
(x,\lfloor y\rfloor)&x\in\mathbb{Z},y\not\in\mathbb{Z};\\
(\lceil x\rceil,\lfloor y\rfloor)&x\not\in\mathbb{Z},y\not\in\mathbb{Z}.\end{cases}

We then let
F(x,y;z,w)=T_{(\lceil x\rceil,\lceil y\rceil),\mathsf{r}(z,w)}-2d(\mathsf{r}(z,w)-\mathsf{r}(x,y))-\omega_{(x,y)}\mathds{1}[x,y\in\mathbb{Z}]
.

Given this, we define the geodesic sets in
\mathcal{K}_{n}
as follows.
For any
p,q\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
, a set
A\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
is called a
geodesic set
in
\mathcal{K}_{n}
from
p
to
q
, if there is a total ordering
\preceq_{A}
on
A
, such that
p\preceq_{A}p^{\prime}\preceq_{A}q
for any
p^{\prime}\in A
;
and for any
p_{1}\preceq_{A}p_{2}\preceq_{A}p_{3}\in A
, there is
\mathcal{K}_{n}(p_{1};p_{2})+\mathcal{K}_{n}(p_{2};p_{3})=\mathcal{K}_{n}(p_{1};p_{3})
.
Such a set
A
is called a
maximal geodesic set
in
\mathcal{K}_{n}
if it is not contained in any other geodesic set from
p
to
q
.

Definition 3.1
.

For any
p=(x,y)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
, let
\mathsf{R}_{n}(p)=(2^{-5/3}n^{-2/3}(x-y),n^{-1}y)
and
for any set
A\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
and
n\in\mathbb{N}
, let
\mathsf{R}_{n}(A)=\{(\mathsf{R}_{n}(p):p\in A\}
. Finally, for any continuous path
\pi:I\to\mathbb{R}
, where
I\subset\mathbb{R}
is any subset, define its
graph

\Graph(\pi)
as the set
\{(\pi(t),t):t\in I\}
.

Given the above, it is straightforward to check that for any
p,q\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}
, the set
\mathsf{R}_{n}(\Gamma_{p,q})
is a maximal geodesic set from
\mathsf{R}_{n}(p)
to
\mathsf{R}_{n}(q)
.
We end with the following recent convergence result.

Theorem 3.2

(
[
DV21
, Theorem 13.8(2)]
)
.

There is a coupling of
\mathcal{L}
with
\mathcal{K}_{n}
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
, such that the following holds almost surely.
First, for any compact
K\subset\mathbb{R}^{4}_{\uparrow}
, we have
\mathcal{K}_{n}\to\mathcal{L}
uniformly in
K
.
Second, for any
(p_{n};q_{n})\to(p;q)\in\mathbb{R}^{4}_{\uparrow}
,
and any
\mathcal{K}_{n}
maximal geodesic set from
p_{n}
to
q_{n}
, denoted as
\pi_{n}
, they are pre-compact in Hausdorff topology, and any subsequential limit is
\Graph(\pi)
, for some
\mathcal{L}
geodesic
\pi
from
p
to
q
.

3.1.2
Transversal fluctuation

Estimates on transversal fluctuation of geodesics have appeared and been used in several works recently (see e.g.
[
BGZ21
,
MSZ21
,
BSS14
,
BSS19
,
GH20a
,
BGHH20
]
. In this paper we would use the following estimate for semi-infinite geodesics.

Lemma 3.3

(
[
MSZ21
, Corollary 2.11]
)
.

There exist constants
C,c>0
such that the following is true.
Take any
n\in\mathbb{N}
large enough and any
x>0
, and denote
m_{n,x}=xn^{2/3}
.
Consider the rectangle whose four vertices are

(-m_{n,x},m_{n,x})
,
(m_{n,x},-m_{n,x})
, and
(n-m_{n,x},n+m_{n,x})
,
(n+m_{n,x},n-m_{n,x})
.
Then with probability
1-Ce^{-cx^{3}}
, the part of the geodesic
\Gamma_{\mathbf{0}}
below
\mathbb{L}_{n}
is contained in that rectangle.

We also use the following verison in this paper, where the rectangle is replaced by a parallelogram.

Lemma 3.4
.

There exist constants
C,c>0
such that the following is true.
Take any
n\in\mathbb{N}
large enough and any
x>0
, and denote
m_{n,x}=xn^{2/3}
.
Consider the parallelogram whose four vertices are

(-m_{n,x},0)
,
(m_{n,x},0)
, and
(n-m_{n,x},n)
,
(n+m_{n,x},n)
.
Then with probability
1-Ce^{-c(x^{3}\wedge n^{2/3}x)}
, we have
\Gamma_{\mathbf{0}}\cap(\mathbb{Z}\times\llbracket 0,n\rrbracket)
is contained in this parallelogram.

Proof.

We consider the rectangle whose four vertices are

(-m_{n,x}/2,m_{n,x}/2)
and
(m_{n,x}/2,-m_{n,x}/2)
, and
(n+m_{n,x},n)
and
(n+m_{n,x},n)
.
If the part of the geodesic
\Gamma_{\mathbf{0}}
below
\mathbb{L}_{n}
is contained in this rectangle, then
\Gamma_{\mathbf{0}}\cap(\mathbb{Z}\times\llbracket 0,n\rrbracket)
is contained in the parallelogram.
Thus by Lemma
3.3
, the probability is at least
1-C^{\prime}e^{-c^{\prime}m_{n,x}^{3}(n+m_{n,x})^{-2}}
for some constants
C,c>0
; and this implies the conclusion.
∎

3.1.3
Duality

We next state the already alluded to duality between semi-infinite geodesics and competition interfaces in exponential LPP.
Towards this we begin by setting up the appropriate Busemann function (recall that we had indicated how this will serve as a valuable device in our constructions in Section
2.3
) in exponential LPP.

Definition 3.5
.

By the already stated tree structure of semi-infinite geodesics, for each
p\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}
we let
G(p)=T_{p,\mathbf{c}}-T_{\mathbf{0},\mathbf{c}}
, where
\mathbf{c}\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}
is the coalescing point of
\Gamma_{p}
and
\Gamma_{\mathbf{0}}
;
i.e.
\mathbf{c}
is the vertex in
\Gamma_{p}\cap\Gamma_{\mathbf{0}}
with the smallest
d(\mathbf{c})
.

Then
G
satisfies the following properties.

1.

For any
p=(x,y)\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}
and
n>y
, if we take
m_{*}=\argmax_{m\geq x}T_{p,(m,n)}+G(m,n+1)
, then
\Gamma_{p}=\Gamma_{p,(m_{*},n)}\cup\Gamma_{(m_{*},n+1)}
, and
G(p)=T_{p,(m_{*},n)}+G((m_{*},n+1))
.

2.

For any down-right path
\mathcal{U}=\{u_{k}\}_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}
, the random variables
G(u_{k})-G(u_{k-1})
are independent. The law of
G(u_{k})-G(u_{k-1})
is
\Exp(1/2)
if
u_{k}=u_{k-1}-(0,1)
, and is
-\Exp(1/2)
if
u_{k}=u_{k-1}+(1,0)
. They are also independent of
\omega(p)
for all
p\in\mathcal{U}_{-}
, where
\mathcal{U}_{-}
is the lower part of
\mathbb{Z}^{2}\setminus\mathcal{U}
,

The first property is a consequence of the definition, and a proof of the second property can be found in
[
Sep20
]
.

Now, for each
p\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}
, let
\Gamma_{p}^{\vee}
be the semi-infinite geodesic in the
(-1,-1)
direction. We shall always assume the almost sure event where
\Gamma_{p}^{\vee}
exists and is unique for every
p\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}
.

We let
G^{\vee}
be the
(-1,-1)
direction Busemann function; i.e. for any
p\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}
we let
G^{\vee}(p)=T_{\mathbf{c},p}-T_{\mathbf{c},\mathbf{0}}
, where
\mathbf{c}\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}
is the coalescing point of
\Gamma_{p}^{\vee}
and
\Gamma_{\mathbf{0}}^{\vee}
.
By symmetry,
G^{\vee}
has the same distribution and hence similar properties as
G
.

We next split
\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}^{2}\setminus\{\mathbf{0}\}
into two parts: let
P_{1}
consist of
p\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}^{2}\setminus\{\mathbf{0}\}
such that
\Gamma_{p}^{\vee}\cap\{(x,0):x\in\mathbb{N}\}\neq\emptyset
, and
P_{2}
consist of
p\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}^{2}\setminus\{\mathbf{0}\}
such that
\Gamma_{p}^{\vee}\cap\{(0,x):x\in\mathbb{N}\}\neq\emptyset
.
Equivalently, let
\mathbb{N}\times\{0\}\subset P_{1}
, and for any
p\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}\times\mathbb{N}
, if we let
m_{*}=\argmax_{m\in\mathbb{Z}}G^{\vee}(m,0)+T_{(m,1),p}
, then
p\in P_{1}
if and only if
m_{*}>0
.

One can think of the boundary of
P_{1}
and
P_{2}
as a path in the dual lattice
\mathbb{Z}^{2}+(1/2,1/2)
,
Let
\Delta\subset\mathbb{Z}^{2}
be the boundary shifted by
(-1/2,-1/2)
; or equivalently, let
\Delta
consist of all
p\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}^{2}
, such that
p+(1,0)\in P_{1}
, and
p+(0,1)\in P_{2}
.
See the left panel in Figure
9
below for an illustration.

As developed in
[
FP05
,
FMP09
]
and also
[
BCS06
]
, the following remarkable duality between competition interfaces and semi-infinite geodesics holds.

Lemma 3.6

(
[
FMP09
, Lemma 4.3]
)
.

\Delta
and
\Gamma_{\mathbf{0}}
have the same distribution.

While we have already introduced and defined the Directed Landscape, in this section we record many of its properties that we will be using throughout the article.

3.2
Directed landscape

3.2.1
Basic properties

There are several symmetries of the directed landscape.

Lemma 3.7

(
[
DOV18
, Lemma 10.2]
,
[
DV21
, Proposition 1.23]
)
.

As continuous functions on
\mathbb{R}^{4}_{\uparrow}
,
\mathcal{L}
is equal in distribution as

(x,s;y,t)\mapsto\mathcal{L}(-y,-t;-x,-s),\quad(x,s;y,t)\mapsto\mathcal{L}(-x,s,-y,t);

and

(x,s;y,t)\mapsto\mathcal{L}(x+cs+z,s+r;y+ct+z,t+r)+(t-s)^{-1}((x-y-c(t-s))^{2}-(x-y)^{2})

for any
c,z,r\in\mathbb{R}
; and

(x,s;y,t)\mapsto w\mathcal{L}(w^{-2}x,w^{-3}s;w^{-2}y,w^{-3}t);

for any
w>0
.
We shall refer to these invariances as flip invariance, skew-shift invariance (or translation invariance when
c=0
), and scaling invariance, respectively.

A basic property of the directed landscape is the following quadrangle inequality, which is equivalent to Lemma
1.1
. This and versions of it in different settings has been proved and widely used in many previous studies
[
DOV18
,
DZ21
,
DV21
,
BGH21
]
.

Lemma 3.8

(
[
DOV18
, Lemma 9.1]
)
.

For any
s<t
,
x_{1}<x_{2}
,
y_{1}<y_{2}
, we have

\mathcal{L}(x_{1},s;y_{1},t)+\mathcal{L}(x_{2},s;y_{2},t)\geq\mathcal{L}(x_{1},s;y_{2},t)+\mathcal{L}(x_{2},s;y_{1},t).

We next state the following basic regularity estimates on
\mathcal{L}
. Recall (from Section
2.3
) that
\|\cdot\|
denotes the usual
l^{2}
norm.

Lemma 3.9

(
[
DOV18
, Corollary 10.7]
)
.

There is a random number
R
such that the following is true.
First, for any
M>0
we have
\mathbb{P}[R>M]<Ce^{-cM^{3/2}}
for some universal constants
c,C>0
.
Second, for any
u=(x,s;y,t)\in\mathbb{R}_{\uparrow}^{4}
, we have

\left|\mathcal{L}(x,s;y,t)+\frac{(x-y)^{2}}{t-s}\right|\leq R(t-s)^{1/3}\log^{4/3}(2(\|u\|+2)^{3/2}/(t-s))\log^{2/3}(\|u\|+2).

We remark that the logarithmic factors come from the exponential tails of
\mathcal{L}(u)
for any fixed
u\in\mathbb{R}_{\uparrow}^{4}
, continuity estimates of
\mathcal{L}
, and a union bound and one cannot hope to obtain a uniform estimate without such factors due to the translation invariance and space-time decorrelation of
\mathcal{L}
.
We will also use the following continuity estimate of
\mathcal{L}
.

Lemma 3.10

(
[
DOV18
, Proposition 1.6]
)
.

Let
K
be a compact subset of
\mathbb{R}_{\uparrow}^{4}
.
There is a random number
R
depending only on
K
, such that the following is true.
First, for any
M>0
we have
\mathbb{P}[R>M]<Ce^{-cM^{3/2}}
for some universal constants
c,C>0
.
Second, for any
(x,s;y,t),(x^{\prime},s^{\prime};y^{\prime},t^{\prime})\in K
, we have

\left|\left(\mathcal{L}(x,s;y,t)+\frac{(x-y)^{2}}{t-s}\right)-\left(\mathcal{L}(x^{\prime},s^{\prime};y^{\prime},t^{\prime})+\frac{(x^{\prime}-y^{\prime})^{2}}{t^{\prime}-s^{\prime}}\right)\right|\\
\leq R\left(\tau^{1/3}\log^{2/3}(1+\tau^{-1})+\xi^{1/2}\log^{1/2}(1+\xi^{-1})\right),

where
\tau=|s-s^{\prime}|\vee|t-t^{\prime}|
and
\tau=|x-x^{\prime}|\vee|y-y^{\prime}|
.

Like in the exponential LPP setting, (using these regularity estimates above) in the directed landscape we get the following estimates on transversal fluctuation of geodesics, which is uniform in the endpoints.

Lemma 3.11
.

There is a random number
R
such that the following is true.
First, for any
M>0
we have
\mathbb{P}[R>M]<Ce^{-cM^{9/4}\log(M)^{-4}}
for some constants
c,C>0
.
Second, for any
u=(x,s;y,t)\in\mathbb{R}_{\uparrow}^{4}
, any geodesic
\pi_{u}
, and
(s+t)/2\leq r<t
, we have

\left|\pi_{u}(r)-\frac{x(t-r)+y(r-s)}{t-s}\right|<R(t-r)^{2/3}\log^{3}(1+\|u\|/(t-r)).

(3.1)

Similar bound holds when
s<r<(s+t)/2
by symmetry.

Estimates similar to this have appeared in the literature, see e.g.
[
DOV18
, Proposition 12.3]
, and
[
GH20a
, Theorem 1.4]
for a uniform in endpoints estimate (in the Brownian LPP setting).
The proof of Lemma
3.11
does not contain particularly new ideas, and relies on computations using Lemma
3.9
and Lemma
3.10
.
For completeness, we give the details in Appendix
A
.

The next subsection records several estimates concerning the geometry of geodesics in
\mathcal{L}.

3.2.2
Intersections of geodesics

We begin with an estimate on the number of intersections that geodesics can have with a line.

Lemma 3.12
.

For any
\alpha\in(0,1),
there are constants
c,C>0
such that the following is true.
For any
M>\ell^{3}
, we have
\mathbb{P}[|\{\pi_{(x,0;y,1)}(\alpha):|x|,|y|\leq\ell,\pi_{(x,0;y,1)}\text{is unique}\}|>M]<Ce^{-cM^{1/384}}
.

This can be deduced from a similar result in the exponential LPP setting, proved in
[
BHS18
]
.
The condition “
\pi_{(x,0;y,1)}
is unique” is assumed due to technical reasons, and is used in passing the geodesics in exponential LPP to the limit.
In fact, while adapting the arguments of
[
BHS18
]
to the directed landscape setting, we can prove a stronger version of Lemma
3.12
by removing this extra condition, we choose to not pursue this for the simplicity of the proof. The current formulation would suffice for applications in this paper.

Proposition 3.13

(
[
BHS18
, Proposition 3.10]
)
.

Let
A_{n}
and
B_{n}
be line segment along the lines
\mathbb{L}_{0}
and
\mathbb{L}_{n}
, with midpoints
(xn^{2/3},-xn^{2/3})
and
(n,n)
, respectively, and each has length
2n^{2/3}
.
Let
F_{n,m}=\left(\bigcup_{u\in A_{n},v\in B_{n}}\Gamma_{u,v}\right)\cap\mathbb{L}_{m}
, for
0<m<n
.
For any
\psi\in(0,1)
,
\alpha\in(0,1/2)
, there is a constant
c>0
, such that when
|x|<\psi n^{1/3}
and
\alpha n<m<(1-\alpha)n
, we have

\mathbb{P}[|F_{n,m}|>M]<e^{-cM^{1/128}}

for any sufficiently large
M<n^{0.01}
and sufficiently large
n
.

We remark that while the statement of
[
BHS18
, Proposition 3.10]
is for
\alpha=1/3
, the proof therein goes through essentially verbatim for a general
\alpha\in(0,1)
.

Using this and the limit transition from Theorem
3.2
, we can prove Lemma
3.12
.

Proof of Lemma
3.12
.

Below we let
C,c>0
be constants depending on
\alpha
, whose values may change from line to line.

Let
A_{n,\ell}
and
B_{n,\ell}
be the line segments along the lines
\mathbb{L}_{0}
and
\mathbb{L}_{n}
, with midpoints
\mathbf{0}
and
(n,n)
, respectively, and length
2^{5/3}\ell n^{2/3}
.
Let
F_{n,m,\ell}=\left(\bigcup_{u\in A_{n,\ell},v\in B_{n,\ell}}\Gamma_{u,v}\right)\cap\mathbb{L}_{m}
, for
0<m<n
.
Then by splitting
A_{n,\ell}
and
B_{n,\ell}
into segments of length
2n^{2/3}
, and applying Proposition
3.13
to each pair, we get the following result. For any
M>0
, and any
n
large enough (depending on
\ell
and
M
), we have

\mathbb{P}[|F_{n,\lfloor\alpha n\rfloor,\ell}|>\ell^{2}M]<C\ell^{2}e^{-cM^{1/128}}.

Thus, when
M>\ell^{3}
, we have

\mathbb{P}[|F_{n,\lfloor\alpha n\rfloor,\ell}|>M]<Ce^{-cM^{1/384}}.

Finally, consider the coupling from Theorem
3.2
.
Suppose that in the directed landscape, we have
|\{\pi_{(x,0;y,1)}(\alpha):|x|,|y|\leq\ell,\pi_{(x,0;y,1)}\text{is unique}\}|>M
for some
M>\ell^{3}
.
Then for any
n
large enough, one would have
|F_{n,\lfloor\alpha n\rfloor,\ell}|>M
, since any unique geodesic
\pi_{(x,0;y,1)},
is the Hausdorff limit of a sequence of pre-limiting geodesics. Thus the conclusion follows.
∎

3.2.3
Ordering of geodesics

We recall the notion of leftmost geodesics from
[
DOV18
]
: for any
(x,s;y,t)\in\mathbb{R}^{4}_{\uparrow}
, a geodesic
\pi
from
(x,s)
to
(y,t)
is called the leftmost geodesic, if for any other geodesic
\tau
from
(x,s)
to
(y,t)
, there is
\tau(t^{\prime})\geq\pi(t^{\prime})
for any
t^{\prime}\in[s,t]
.
By
[
DOV18
, Lemma 13.2]
, almost surely, for any
(x,s;y,t)\in\mathbb{R}^{4}_{\uparrow}
there is a leftmost geodesic from
(x,s)
to
(y,t)
.
We also have the following ordering property.

(x_{2},s_{2})
(x_{1},s_{1})
(x_{2},s_{2})
(x_{1},s_{1})
(y_{1},t_{1})
(y_{2},t_{2})
(y_{1},t_{1})
(y_{2},t_{2})
s_{*}
t_{*}

Figure 5
:
An illustration of the proof of Lemma
3.14
: the left figure depicts
\pi_{(x_{1},s_{1};y_{1},t_{1})}
and
\pi_{(x_{2},s_{2};y_{2},t_{2})}
, and the right figure depicts
\pi_{1}^{\prime}
(the red path) and
\pi_{2}^{\prime}
(the blue path), which are alternative candidates for the geodesics from
(x_{1},s_{1})
to
(y_{1},t_{1})
and from
(x_{2},s_{2})
to
(y_{2},t_{2})
, respectively.

Lemma 3.14
.

Almost surely the following is true.
For any
(x_{1},s_{1};y_{1},t_{1}),(x_{2},s_{2};y_{2},t_{2})\in\mathbb{R}^{4}_{\uparrow}
with
s_{1}\vee s_{2}\leq t_{1}\wedge t_{2}
, the set
O=\{t^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}:\pi_{(x_{1},s_{1};y_{1},t_{1})}(t^{\prime})=\pi_{(x_{2},s_{2};y_{2},t_{2})}(t^{\prime})\}
is either empty or a closed interval, if one of the following two conditions hold:

1.

both
\pi_{(x_{1},s_{1};y_{1},t_{1})}
and
\pi_{(x_{2},s_{2};y_{2},t_{2})}
are the leftmost geodesics;

2.

at least one of
\pi_{(x_{1},s_{1};y_{1},t_{1})}
and
\pi_{(x_{2},s_{2};y_{2},t_{2})}
is the unique geodesic (between its endpoints).

Proof.

We assume that
O\neq\emptyset
.
The denote
s_{*}=\inf O
and
t_{*}=\sup O
.
As the geodesics are continuous, we have that
s_{*},t_{*}\in O
.
Now we define
\pi_{1}^{\prime}:[s_{1},t_{1}]\to\mathbb{R}
and
\pi_{2}^{\prime}:[s_{2},t_{2}]\to\mathbb{R}
as follows:
for any
t^{\prime}\in[s_{1},t_{1}]\setminus[s_{*},t_{*}]
, we let
\pi_{1}^{\prime}(t^{\prime})=\pi_{(x_{1},s_{1};y_{1},t_{1})}(t^{\prime})
, and for any
t^{\prime}\in[s_{2},t_{2}]\setminus[s_{*},t_{*}]
we let
\pi_{2}^{\prime}(t^{\prime})=\pi_{(x_{2},s_{2};y_{2},t_{2})}(t^{\prime})
.
For any
t^{\prime}\in(s_{*},t_{*})
we let
\pi_{1}^{\prime}(t^{\prime})=\pi_{(x_{2},s_{2};y_{2},t_{2})}(t^{\prime})
and
\pi_{2}^{\prime}(t^{\prime})=\pi_{(x_{1},s_{1};y_{1},t_{1})}(t^{\prime})
.
See Figure
5
for an illustration.
Then it is straightforward to check that

\|\pi_{1}^{\prime}\|_{\mathcal{L}}+\|\pi_{2}^{\prime}\|_{\mathcal{L}}=\|\pi_{(x_{1},s_{1};y_{1},t_{1})}\|_{\mathcal{L}}+\|\pi_{(x_{2},s_{2};y_{2},t_{2})}\|_{\mathcal{L}}=\mathcal{L}(x_{1},s_{1};y_{1},t_{1})+\mathcal{L}(x_{2},s_{2};y_{2},t_{2}).

This implies that
\pi_{1}^{\prime}
and
\pi_{2}^{\prime}
are also geodesics.

In the case where both
\pi_{(x_{1},s_{1};y_{1},t_{1})}
and
\pi_{(x_{2},s_{2};y_{2},t_{2})}
are the leftmost geodesics, for any
t^{\prime}\in(s_{*},t_{*})
we must have that
\pi_{1}^{\prime}(t^{\prime})\geq\pi_{(x_{1},s_{1};y_{1},t_{1})}(t^{\prime})
, which implies that
\pi_{(x_{2},s_{2};y_{2},t_{2})}(t^{\prime})\geq\pi_{(x_{1},s_{1};y_{1},t_{1})}(t^{\prime})
. By a symmetric argument we have that
\pi_{(x_{2},s_{2};y_{2},t_{2})}(t^{\prime})\leq\pi_{(x_{1},s_{1};y_{1},t_{1})}(t^{\prime})
, so we must have that
t^{\prime}\in O
, and
O=[s_{*},t_{*}]
.

We next consider the case where
\pi_{(x_{1},s_{1};y_{1},t_{1})}
is the unique geodesic.
Then by uniqueness we must have
\pi_{1}^{\prime}=\pi_{(x_{1},s_{1};y_{1},t_{1})}
, so we conclude that
\pi_{(x_{2},s_{2};y_{2},t_{2})}(t^{\prime})=\pi_{(x_{1},s_{1};y_{1},t_{1})}(t^{\prime})
for any
t^{\prime}\in(s_{*},t_{*})
, and
O=[s_{*},t_{*}]
.
By symmetry the case where
\pi_{(x_{2},s_{2};y_{2},t_{2})}
is the unique geodesic also follows.
∎

3.2.4
Disjointness of geodesics

Recall that for any
x_{1}<x_{2}
,
y_{1}<y_{2}
, and
s<t
,
\Dist_{(x_{1},x_{2})\to(y_{1},y_{2})}^{[s,t]}
is the event where
\pi_{(x_{1},s;y_{1},t)}
and
\pi_{(x_{2},s;y_{2},t)}
are unique and disjoint.
This disjointness would be equivalent to strict inequality of the quadrangle inequality (Lemma
3.8
).

Lemma 3.15
.

For any
x_{1}<x_{2}
,
y_{1}<y_{2}
, and
s<t
, the event
\Dist_{(x_{1},x_{2})\to(y_{1},y_{2})}^{[s,t]}
is equivalent to that the geodesics
\pi_{(x_{1},s;y_{1},t)}
and
\pi_{(x_{2},s;y_{2},t)}
are both unique, and

\mathcal{L}(x_{1},s;y_{1},t)+\mathcal{L}(x_{2},s;y_{2},t)>\mathcal{L}(x_{1},s;y_{2},t)+\mathcal{L}(x_{2},s;y_{1},t).

(3.2)

Statements similar to this have also appeared in the proof of
[
BGH19
, Proposition 4.1]
.

Proof.

Whenever
\pi_{(x_{1},s;y_{1},t)}
and
\pi_{(x_{2},s;y_{2},t)}
are not disjoint, we can take
x_{*},t_{*}
with

\pi_{(x_{1},s;y_{1},t)}(t_{*})=\pi_{(x_{2},s;y_{2},t)}(t_{*})=x_{*},

and construct
\pi_{1}^{\prime}
from
(x_{1},s)
to
(y_{2},t)
and
\pi_{2}^{\prime}
from
(x_{2},s)
to
(y_{1},t)
, by switching the paths after
t_{*}
.
Then
\|\pi_{1}^{\prime}\|_{\mathcal{L}}+\|\pi_{2}^{\prime}\|_{\mathcal{L}}=\mathcal{L}(x_{1},s;y_{1},t)+\mathcal{L}(x_{2},s;y_{2},t)
, and (
3.2
) cannot hold.

Now we assume that (
3.2
) does not hold; then by the quadrangle inequality (Lemma
3.8
), we must have that the left hand side equals the right hand side.
We then consider any geodesic
\pi_{(x_{1},s;y_{2},t)}
and
\pi_{(x_{2},s;y_{1},t)}
.
Take
x_{*},t_{*}
with

\pi_{(x_{1},s;y_{2},t)}(t_{*})=\pi_{(x_{1},s;y_{2},t)}(t_{*})=x_{*},

then we construct
\pi_{1}^{\prime}
from
(x_{1},s)
to
(y_{1},t)
and
\pi_{2}^{\prime}
from
(x_{2},s)
to
(y_{2},t)
, by switching the paths after
t_{*}
.
Now we have

\|\pi_{1}^{\prime}\|_{\mathcal{L}}+\|\pi_{2}^{\prime}\|_{\mathcal{L}}=\mathcal{L}(x_{1},s;y_{2},t)+\mathcal{L}(x_{1},s;y_{2},t)=\mathcal{L}(x_{1},s;y_{1},t)+\mathcal{L}(x_{2},s;y_{2},t),

so
\pi_{1}^{\prime}
and
\pi_{2}^{\prime}
must be geodesics.
By uniqueness we have
\pi_{1}^{\prime}=\pi_{(x_{1},s;y_{1},t)}
and
\pi_{2}^{\prime}=\pi_{(x_{2},s;y_{2},t)}
, and they are not disjoint.
∎

We recall the following estimate on the disjoint probability. This is a limiting version of
[
Ham20
, Theorem 1.1]
, and is slightly more general than Theorem
2.6
.

Theorem 3.16
.

There is a constant
C
depending on
g,h
, such that for any
g\leq t\leq h
, and
\epsilon>0
,
l>1
we have

\mathbb{P}[\Dist_{(-l,l)\to(-\epsilon,\epsilon)}^{[0,t]}]<Cl\epsilon^{1/2}e^{C|\log(\epsilon)|^{5/6}}.

Proof.

By Lemma
3.15
,
\Dist_{(-l,l)\to(-\epsilon,\epsilon)}^{[0,t]}
implies that

\mathcal{L}(-1,0;-\epsilon,t)-\mathcal{L}(-1,0;\epsilon,t)>\mathcal{L}(1,0;-\epsilon,t)-\mathcal{L}(1,0;\epsilon,t);

thus for some
k\in\llbracket 0,\lceil l\rceil
, we have

\mathcal{L}(-\lceil l\rceil+2k,0;-\epsilon,t)-\mathcal{L}(-\lceil l\rceil+2k,0;\epsilon,t)>\mathcal{L}(-\lceil l\rceil+2k+2,0;-\epsilon,t)-\mathcal{L}(-\lceil l\rceil+2k+2,0;\epsilon,t).

Assuming (the probability
1
event) that these geodesics are unique, this implies

\bigcup_{k=0}^{\lceil l\rceil}\Dist_{(-\lceil l\rceil+2k,-\lceil l\rceil+2k+2)\to(-\epsilon,\epsilon)}^{[0,t]},

by Lemma
3.15
.
Then by the skew-shift invariance of the directed landscape, we have

\mathbb{P}[\Dist_{(-l,l)\to(-\epsilon,\epsilon)}^{[0,t]}]\leq\lceil l\rceil\mathbb{P}[\Dist_{(-1,1)\to(-\epsilon,\epsilon)}^{[0,t]}].

We now claim that

\mathbb{P}[\Dist_{(-1,1)\to(-\epsilon,\epsilon)}^{[0,t]}]<C\epsilon^{1/2}e^{C|\log(\epsilon)|^{5/6}}.

for some constant
C
depending on
g,h
.
Such a disjointness probability estimate is proved in the setting of Brownian LPP, as

[
Ham20
, Theorem 1.1]
; and we pass it to the directed landscape limit using
[
DOV18
, Theorem 1.8]
, which states that (under an appropriate coupling) any unique geodesic in the directed landscape is the limit (under uniform convergence) of the corresponding ones in Brownian LPP.
The above two inequalities imply the conclusion.
∎

3.3
Airy line ensemble and properties

The parabolic Airy line ensemble
\mathcal{P}=\{\mathcal{P}_{n}\}_{n=1}^{\infty}
is an ordered family of random processes on
\mathbb{R}
, constructed in
[
CH14
]
.
We summarize some crucial properties of it that we will make use of.
Define
\overline{\mathcal{P}}_{n}(x):=\mathcal{P}_{n}(x)+x^{2}
for any
x\in\mathbb{R}
,
n\in\mathbb{N}
.

1.

Stationarity:
\overline{\mathcal{P}}=\{\overline{\mathcal{P}}_{n}\}_{n=1}^{\infty}
is stationary with respect to horizontal shift.

2.

Ergodicity:
\overline{\mathcal{P}}=\{\overline{\mathcal{P}}_{n}\}_{n=1}^{\infty}
is ergodic with respect to horizontal shift.

3.

Brownian Gibbs property: for any
m\leq n\in\mathbb{N}
and
x\leq y
, let
S=\llbracket m,n\rrbracket\times[x,y]
. We can think of
\mathcal{P}
as a (random) function on
\mathbb{N}\times\mathbb{R}
, then the process
\mathcal{P}|_{S}
given
\mathcal{P}|_{S^{c}}
is just
n-m+1
Brownian bridges connecting up the points
\mathcal{P}_{i}(x)
and
\mathcal{P}_{i}(y)
, for
m\leq i\leq n
, conditioned on that
\mathcal{P}_{i}(z)>\mathcal{P}_{i+1}(z)
for any
1\vee(m-1)\leq i\leq n
and
z\in[x,y]
.

The first property is from its construction, and the third property is proved in
[
CH14
]
. The second property is proved in
[
CS14
]
.
We note that here and throughout this paper, all Brownian motions and Brownian bridges have diffusive coefficients equal to
2
.

The first line
\mathcal{P}_{1}
(of
\mathcal{P}
) is the parabolic Airy
2
process on
\mathbb{R}
, and it has the same distribution as the function
x\mapsto\mathcal{L}(\mathbf{0};x,1)
.
This process also has the following Brownian property.
For
K\in\mathbb{R},d>0
, let
\mathcal{B}^{[K,K+d]}
denote a Brownian motion on
[K,K+d]
, taking value
0
at
K
. Let
\mathcal{P}_{1}^{[K,K+d]}
denote the random function on
[K,K+d]
defined by

\mathcal{P}_{1}^{[K,K+d]}(x):=\mathcal{P}_{1}(x)-\mathcal{P}_{1}(K),\penalty\ \forall x\in[K,K+d].

Theorem 3.17

(
[
CHH19
, Theorem 1.1]
)
.

There exists an universal constant
G>0
such that the following holds.
For any fixed
M>0,
there exists
a_{0}=a(M)
such that for all intervals
[K,K+d]\subset[-M,M]
and for all measurable
A\subset C^{0}([K,K+d])
with
0<\mathbb{P}[\mathcal{B}^{[K,K+d]}\in A]=a\leq a_{0},

\mathbb{P}\left(\mathcal{P}_{1}^{[K,K+d]}\in A\right)\leq ae^{G{M}(\log a^{-1})^{5/6}}.

Here
C^{0}([K,K+d])
is the space of all continuous functions on
[K,K+d]
.

We will also need the Brownian absolute continuity for the evolution induced by the directed landscape, with some general initial conditions.
Namely, take any
t>0
and any function
f_{0}:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}\cup\{-\infty\}
, such that
f(x)\neq-\infty
for some
x
, and
\frac{f_{0}(x)-x^{2}/t}{|x|}\to-\infty
as
|x|\to\infty
. Define

f_{t}(y):=\sup_{x\in\mathbb{R}}f_{0}(x)+\mathcal{L}(x,0;y,t).

Such evolution of functions is also called the KPZ fixed point (see
[
MQR16
]
).

Theorem 3.18

(
[
SV21
, Theorem 1.2]
)
.

For any fixed
t>0
and
f_{0}
satisfying the above conditions, and any
y_{1}<y_{2}
, the law of
y\mapsto f_{t}(y+y_{1})-f(y_{1})
for
y\in[0,y_{2}-y_{1}]
is absolutely continuous with respect to the law of a Brownian motion on
[0,y_{2}-y_{1}]
.

4
Busemann function and semi-infinite geodesics

In this section we construct semi-infinite geodesics in the directed landscape. As stated in Section
2.3
, we shall first construct the Busemann function, and use it to define the semi-infinite geodesics. We will further deduce various useful properties of these constructed objects.

4.1
Construction of Busemann function

For each
l>0,
let

U_{l}=[-2l^{2/3},2l^{2/3}]\times[l,2l].

(4.1)

For any compact set
B\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
, we denote as
\mathcal{C}_{B,l}
the following coalescence event: there exists
(x_{*},t_{*})
such that for any
p\in B
and
q\in U_{l}
, any geodesic
\pi_{(p;q)}
contains
t_{*}
in its range, and
\pi_{(p;q)}(t_{*})=x_{*}
. The following shows that this is rather likely.

Lemma 4.1
.

For any compact
B\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
, there is a constant
C>0
, such that
\mathbb{P}[\mathcal{C}_{B,l}]>1-Cl^{-1/3}e^{C|\log(l)|^{5/6}}
for any
l>0
.

(-\log(l)^{5}l^{-2/3},l^{-1})
(\log(l)^{5}l^{-2/3},l^{-1})
[-l^{-2/3},l^{-2/3}]\times[-l^{-1},l^{-1}]
U_{1}=[-2,2]\times[1,2]
(-\log(l)^{5},1)
(\log(l)^{5},1)
(x,t)
q

Figure 6
:
An illustration of the proof of Lemma
4.1
.

The above is a general version of statements that have already appeared in the literature before (see e.g.,
[
BSS19
,
BGZ21
,
Zha20
]
which works in the exponential LPP setting). We follow a similar proof relying on the bound given by Theorem
3.16
(which accounts for the extra factor of
e^{C|\log(l)|^{5/6}}
), combined with transversal fluctuation estimates and the ordering of geodesics.

Proof.

By scale invariance, we can assume that
B=[-1,1]\times[-1,1]
without loss of generality. We also assume that
l
is large enough (since otherwise the conclusion holds obviously by taking
C
large enough). Let the event
\mathcal{C}_{l}^{\prime}
be the scaled version of
\mathcal{C}_{B,l},
i.e., there exists
(x_{*},t_{*})
such that
l^{-1}\leq t_{*}\leq 1
, and
\pi_{(x,t;q)}(x_{*})=t_{*}
for any
|x|\leq l^{-2/3}
,
|t|\leq l^{-1}
, and
q\in U_{1}
, and any geodesic
\pi_{(x,t;q)}
. Hence
\mathbb{P}[\mathcal{C}_{B,l}]=\mathbb{P}[\mathcal{C}_{l}^{\prime}]
,

Below we let
C,c>0
denote large and small constants, whose values may change from line to line.
Let
R
be the random varialbe given by Lemma
3.11
.
We now consider the following events.

1.

Let
\mathcal{E}_{1}
be the event that
R>\log(l)
.

2.

Let
\mathcal{E}_{2}
be the event
\Dist_{(-\log(l)^{5}l^{-2/3},\log(l)^{5}l^{-2/3})\to(-\log(l)^{5},\log(l)^{5})}^{[l^{-1},1]}
; recall that this is the event where the geodesics
\pi_{(-\log(l)^{5}l^{-2/3},l^{-1};-\log(l)^{5},1)}
and
\pi_{(\log(l)^{5}l^{-2/3},l^{-1};\log(l)^{5},1)}
are unique and disjoint (while the coordinates are somewhat technical looking, we hope Figure
6
would convey the basic setup).

We next show that (up to a probability zero event)
\mathcal{E}_{1}^{c}\cap\mathcal{E}_{2}^{c}\subset\mathcal{C}_{l}^{\prime}
(see Figure
6
for an illustration).
Using Lemma
3.11
, for any
|x|\leq l^{-2/3}
,
|t|\leq l^{-1}
, and
q\in U_{1}
, we have
|\pi_{(x,t;q)}(1)|\leq\log(l)^{5}
and
|\pi_{(x,t;q)}(l^{-1})|\leq\log(l)^{5}l^{-2/3}
under
\mathcal{E}_{1}^{c}
.
Now on
\mathcal{E}_{2}^{c}
and hence on
\mathcal{E}_{1}^{c}\cap\mathcal{E}_{2}^{c}
, we can find
(x_{*},t_{*})
such that

\pi_{(-\log(l)^{5}l^{-2/3},l^{-1};-\log(l)^{5},1)}(t_{*})=\pi_{(\log(l)^{5}l^{-2/3},l^{-1};\log(l)^{5},1)}(t_{*})=x_{*}.

We assume (the probability
1
event) that
\pi_{(-\log(l)^{5}l^{-2/3},l^{-1};-\log(l)^{5},1)}
and
\pi_{(\log(l)^{5}l^{-2/3},l^{-1};\log(l)^{5},1)}
are unique. By the ordering of finite geodesics (Lemma
3.14
), for any
|x|\leq l^{-2/3}
,
|t|\leq l^{-1}
, and
q\in U_{1}
, one also has
\pi_{(x,t;q)}(t_{*})=x_{*}
.

By Lemma
3.11
, we have
\mathbb{P}[\mathcal{E}_{1}]<Ce^{-c(\log(l))^{9/4}\log(\log(l))^{-4}}
,
by Theorem
3.16
we have

\mathbb{P}[\mathcal{E}_{2}]<C\log(l)^{15/2}l^{-1/3}e^{C|\log(l)|^{5/6}}.

Adding these bounds together yields the conclusion.
∎

We then immediately get the following convergence of difference of passage times.

Corollary 4.2
.

For any compact
B\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
and any
r\in\mathbb{R}
, almost surely there is a (random) number
h_{0}
such that the following is true.
For any
p,q\in B
,
h\geq h_{0}
, and
z\in\mathbb{R}
with
|z-hr|<h^{2/3}
, we have that

\mathcal{L}(p;z,h)-\mathcal{L}(q;z,h)=\mathcal{L}(p;h_{0}r,h_{0})-\mathcal{L}(q;h_{0}r,h_{0})
.

Proof.

By skew-shift invariance of the directed landscape (Lemma
3.7
), we can assume that
r=0
without loss of generality.
By Lemma
4.1
and the Borel-Cantelli lemma, there is a random
k_{0}\in\mathbb{N}
, such that
\mathcal{C}_{B,2^{k}}
holds for any
k\geq k_{0}
.
Then for any
p,q\in B
,
k\geq k_{0}
and
p^{\prime}\in U_{2^{k}}
, we have
\mathcal{L}(p;0,2^{k})-\mathcal{L}(q;0,2^{k})=\mathcal{L}(p;p^{\prime})-\mathcal{L}(q;p^{\prime})
; in particular
\mathcal{L}(p;0,2^{k})-\mathcal{L}(q;0,2^{k})=\mathcal{L}(p;0,2^{k+1})-\mathcal{L}(q;0,2^{k+1})
.
This implies that for any
h\geq 2^{k_{0}}
and
|z|<h^{2/3}
, we have

\mathcal{L}(p;0,2^{k_{0}})-\mathcal{L}(q;0,2^{k_{0}})=\mathcal{L}(p;z,h)-\mathcal{L}(q;z,h)
, since
(z,h)\in\cup_{k\geq k_{0}}U_{2^{k}}
.
Thus the conclusion follows by taking
h_{0}=2^{k_{0}}
.
∎

As in the Exponential LPP setting, the above coalescence results allow us to define the Busemann function in the directed landscape.

Definition 4.3
.

We define the Busemann function as

\mathcal{M}^{r}(x,s;y,t):=\lim_{n\to\infty}\mathcal{L}(x,s;z_{n},h_{n})-\mathcal{L}(y,t;z_{n},h_{n})

for any
(x,s;y,t)\in\mathbb{R}^{4}
and
r\in\mathbb{R}
, where
\{z_{n}\}_{n=1}^{\infty}
and
\{h_{n}\}_{n=1}^{\infty}
are any sequences such that
z_{n}\to\infty
as
n\to\infty
, and
|z_{n}-h_{n}r|<h_{n}^{2/3}
for each
n\in\mathbb{N}
.
By Corollary
4.2
we see that for any fixed
r\in\mathbb{R}
, almost surely this limit exists for any
(x,s;y,t)\in\mathbb{R}^{4}
, and is independent of the choice of the sequences. Also,
\mathcal{M}^{r}
is almost surely continuous as a function on
\mathbb{R}^{4}
.
For simplicity of notations, we would often write
\mathcal{M}
for
\mathcal{M}^{0}
.

The next lemma asserts Brownianity of the Busemann functions.

Lemma 4.4
.

For any
r,t\in\mathbb{R}
, the process of

x\mapsto\mathcal{M}^{r}(x,t;0,t)-2xr
is a Brownian motion.

Proof.

By skew-shift invariance (Lemma
3.7
) it suffices to assume
r=t=0
.
For any
h>0
, by scaling invariance, the process

x\mapsto\mathcal{L}(x,0;0,h)-\mathcal{L}(\mathbf{0};0,h)
has the same law as
x\mapsto h^{-1/3}\mathcal{L}(xh^{-2/3},0;0,1)-\mathcal{L}(\mathbf{0};0,1)
, which is the same as
x\mapsto h^{-1/3}(\mathcal{P}_{1}(h^{-2/3}x)-\mathcal{P}_{1}(0))
, where
\mathcal{P}_{1}
is the parabolic Airy
2
process.
By
[
MQR16
, Theorem 4.14]
, as
h\to\infty
it converges to Brownian motion, in finite dimensional distributions.
Also by Corollary
4.2
,

x\mapsto\mathcal{L}(x,0;0,h)-\mathcal{L}(\mathbf{0};0,h)
converges to
x\mapsto\mathcal{M}(x,t;0,t)
uniformly in compact sets,
so
x\mapsto\mathcal{M}(x,0;\mathbf{0})
