has the same finite dimensional distributions as a Brownian motion.
Since
\mathcal{M}
is continuous, the conclusion follows.
∎

Lemma 4.5
.

For any
r_{1}<r_{2}
, the following is true almost surely.
For any
x_{1}<x_{2}
and
t\in\mathbb{R}
, there is

\mathcal{M}^{r_{1}}(x_{1},t;x_{2},t)\geq\mathcal{M}^{r_{2}}(x_{1},t;x_{2},t).

(4.2)

Proof.

It suffices to prove this for fixed (thus all rational)
x_{1}<x_{2}
and
t\in\mathbb{R}
since then by continuity of
\mathcal{M}^{r_{1}}
and
\mathcal{M}^{r_{2}}
we can get (
4.2
) to hold for all
x_{1}<x_{2}
and
t\in\mathbb{R}
simultaneously.

From the construction, we can find some rational
h_{0}>t
, such that

\mathcal{M}^{r_{1}}(x_{1},t;x_{2},t)=\mathcal{L}(x_{1},t;h_{0}r_{1},h_{0})-\mathcal{L}(x_{2},t;h_{0}r_{1},h_{0})
, and

\mathcal{M}^{r_{2}}(x_{1},t;x_{2},t)=\mathcal{L}(x_{1},t;h_{0}r_{2},h_{0})-\mathcal{L}(x_{2},t;h_{0}r_{2},h_{0})
.
Then by the quadrangle inequality (Lemma
3.8
), the conclusion follows.
∎

We now move on to the formal construction of semi-infinite geodesics.

4.2
Construction of semi-infinite geodesics

Lemma 4.6
.

For any
r\in\mathbb{R}
, almost surely the following is true.
For any
p=(x,s)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
, there exists a continuous path
\pi_{p}^{r}:[s,\infty)\to\mathbb{R}

satisfying the following condition.

Consider any compact
B\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
, and any sequences
\{z_{n}\}_{n=1}^{\infty}
and
\{h_{n}\}_{n=1}^{\infty}
satisfying
z_{n}\to\infty
as
n\to\infty
and
|z_{n}-h_{n}r|<h_{n}^{2/3}
for each
n\in\mathbb{N}
, and
t\in\mathbb{R}
.
Then there is some
n_{0}\in\mathbb{N}
such that
\pi_{(p;z_{n},h_{n})}(t^{\prime})=\pi_{p}^{r}(t^{\prime})
for any
n\geq n_{0}
, any
p=(x,s)\in B
with
s\leq t
, and any
s\leq t^{\prime}\leq t
. Here
\pi_{(p;z_{n},h_{n})}
is taken as the leftmost geodesic.

Proof.

The finite geodesics in this proof are taken to be the leftmost one.
We also just prove for the case where
r=0
, since the argument for general
r
follows from the skew-shift invariance.

By Lemma
4.1
and using the Borel-Cantelli lemma, almost surely, for each
m\in\mathbb{N}
there is a random
k_{m}\in\mathbb{N}
, such that the coalescence event
\mathcal{C}_{[-m,m]\times[-m,m],2^{k}}
(recall the definition from the beginning of Section
4.1
) holds for any
k\geq k_{m}
.
Then for any
p=(x,s)\in[-m,m]\times[-m,m]
,
k\geq k_{m}
,
s\leq t\leq m
, and
p^{\prime}\in U_{2^{k}}
(defined in (
4.1
)), we have
\pi_{(p;0,2^{k})}(t)=\pi_{(p;p^{\prime})}(t)
by Lemma
3.14
;
in particular
\pi_{(p;0,2^{k})}(m)=\pi_{(p;0,2^{k+1})}(m)
.
This implies that for any
h\geq 2^{k_{m}}
and
|z|<h^{2/3}
, and
s\leq t\leq m
, we have

\pi_{(p;0,2^{k_{m}})}(t)=\pi_{(p;z,h)}(t)
, since
(z,h)\in\cup_{k\geq k_{m}}U_{2^{k}}
.

For any
p=(x,s)\in[-m,m]\times[-m,m],
we just define
\pi_{p}^{0}(t)=\pi_{(p;0,2^{k_{m}})}(t)
for any
s\leq t\leq m
.
Since
\pi_{(p;0,2^{k_{m}})}(t)=\pi_{(p;0,2^{k_{m+1}})}(t)
(for any
s\leq t\leq m
) we would have that this definition is consistent for different
m
.
Now for a general compact set
B\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
and
t\in\mathbb{R}
, the lemma follows by taking
m
large enough so that
B\subset[-m,m]\times[-m,m]
and
m>t
.
∎

As already indicated in the introduction, given
r\in\mathbb{R}
, unless otherwise noted, for any
p\in\mathbb{R}^{2},
we will use
\pi_{p}^{r}
to denote the path constructed in Lemma
4.6
and
\pi_{p}=\pi_{p}^{0}
for simplicity of notations.

Just like the composition law (
1.1
) satisfied by finite geodesics, the number
\pi_{p}^{r}(t)
would be the maximizer of the sum of two profiles: the passage time from
p
, and the Busemann function in the
r
-direction.

Lemma 4.7
.

For any fixed
r,t\in\mathbb{R}
, almost surely the following is true.
For any
p=(x,s)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
with
s<t
, we have

\mathcal{L}(p;\pi_{p}^{r}(t),t)+\mathcal{M}^{r}(\pi_{p}^{r}(t),t;0,t)=\max_{x}\mathcal{L}(p;x,t)+\mathcal{M}^{r}(x,t;0,t).

Proof.

By Corollary
4.2
and Lemma
4.6
,
for any
n\in\mathbb{N}
, we can find
h_{n}
large enough such that

\mathcal{M}^{r}(x,t;0,t)=\mathcal{L}(x,t;h_{n}r,h_{n})-\mathcal{L}(0,t;h_{n}r,h_{n})
for any
|x|\leq n
, and
\pi_{p}^{r}(t)=\pi_{(p;h_{n}r,h_{n})}(t)
for any
p\in[-n,n]\times[-n,t)
.

Then for any
p=(x,s)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
with
s<t
, and any
n\in\mathbb{N}
with
n>2|\pi_{p}^{r}(t)|
and
p\in[-n,n]\times[-n,n]
, by the composition law (
1.1
) we have that

\mathcal{L}(p;\pi_{p}^{r}(t),t)+\mathcal{M}^{r}(\pi_{p}^{r}(t),t;0,t)=\max_{|x|\leq n}\mathcal{L}(p;x,t)+\mathcal{M}^{r}(x,t;0,t).

By sending
n\to\infty
the conclusion follows.
∎

We next verify that the constructed paths
\pi_{p}^{r}
are semi-infinite geodesics.

It is straight forward to see that the paths are locally geodesics.
Fix any
r\in\mathbb{R}
.
By Lemma
4.6
, for each
p=(x,s)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
, and any
s\leq t_{1}<t_{2}
, the restriction of
\pi_{p}^{r}
to
[t_{1},t_{2}]
is a subpath of
\pi_{(p;hr,h)}
for some large enough
h
; so it is also a geodesic.

It remains to show that
\pi_{p}^{r}
have the desired asymptotic directions.
For that, we need the following transversal fluctuation estimate on semi-infinite geodesics. It will also be used in several other places in the rest of this paper, and can be viewed as a limiting version of Lemmas
3.3
and
3.4
. While we do not prove this, up to the constants, this is also the best upper bound one can hope for, as a matching lower bound can be obtained by analyzing the location of the
\argmax
of the parabolic Airy
2
process plus a Brownian motion, and using the Brownian Gibbs property of the Airy line ensemble (the latter is an invariance property which is crucially used in Appendix
C
).

Lemma 4.8
.

There exist constants
C,c>0
such that the following is true.
For any
r,t\in\mathbb{R}
and
p=(x,s)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
satisfying
s<t
, we have
\mathbb{P}[|\pi_{p}^{r}(t)-(x+r(t-s))|>w(t-s)^{2/3}]<Ce^{-cw^{3}}
.

Proof.

In this proof, we let
C,c>0
be universal constants, whose value can change from line to line.
By translation and skew-shift invariance of the directed landscape (thus of semi-infinite geodesics), we just need to prove this for
p=\mathbf{0}
,
r=0
, and
t=1
.
By Lemma
4.7
, the event
|\pi_{\mathbf{0}}(1)|>w
implies that

\max_{|y|>w}\mathcal{L}(\mathbf{0};y,1)+\mathcal{M}(y,1;0,1)\geq\mathcal{L}(\mathbf{0};0,1).

Denote this event as
\mathcal{E}
. For any
n\in\mathbb{Z}
we let
\mathcal{E}_{n}
be the event where

\max_{y\in[n,n+1]}\mathcal{L}(\mathbf{0};y,1)+\mathcal{M}^{r}(y,1;0,1)\geq\mathcal{L}(\mathbf{0};0,1).

Then we have
\mathbb{P}[\mathcal{E}]\leq\sum_{n\geq w}\mathbb{P}[\mathcal{E}_{n}]+\sum_{n\leq w-1}\mathbb{P}[\mathcal{E}_{n}]
.

Next, note that
\mathcal{E}_{n}
implies one of the following three events:

1.

\max_{y\in[n,n+1]}\mathcal{L}(\mathbf{0};y,1)>-2n^{2}/3
.

2.

\mathcal{L}(\mathbf{0};0,1)<-n^{2}/3
.

3.

\max_{y\in[n,n+1]}\mathcal{M}^{r}(y,1;0,1)>n^{2}/3
.

For the first event, by skew-shift invariance of the directed landscape, it has the same probability as the follow event:

\max_{y\in[0,1]}\mathcal{L}(\mathbf{0};y,1)-(y+n)^{2}+y^{2}>-2n^{2}/3.

By Lemma
3.9
, its probability is bounded by
Ce^{-cn^{3}}
. Also, the probabilities of the second and third event above are also bounded by
Ce^{-cn^{3}}
, using Lemma
3.9
and estimates on Brownian motions, respectively.
Thus we have
\mathbb{P}[\mathcal{E}_{n}]<Ce^{-cn^{3}}
.
By summing over
n\geq w
and
n\leq w-1
, the conclusion follows.
∎

We also need the following result which is on the overlaps between the constructed paths, and essentially says that the paths are “ordered”. It is a consequence of the construction and Lemma
3.14
.
It will be improved later (Lemma
4.14
) for the case of paths in the same direction.

Lemma 4.9
.

For any
r_{1},r_{2}\in\mathbb{R}
, almost surely the following is true.
For any
p_{2}=(x_{1},s_{1}),p_{2}=(x_{2},s_{2})\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
, let
O=\{t\geq s_{1}\vee s_{2}:\pi_{p_{1}}^{r_{1}}(t)=\pi_{p_{2}}^{r_{2}}(t)\}
be the set of “overlap times”.
If
r_{1}\neq r_{2}
, then
O
is either empty or a closed interval.
If
r_{1}=r_{2}
, then
O
is either empty, or
O=[t_{*},\infty)
for some
t_{*}\geq s_{1}\vee s_{2}
.

Proof.

We first consider the case where
r_{1}\neq r_{2}
.
In this case we must have that
O
is contained in a compact set, since the asymptotic directions of
\pi_{p_{1}}^{r_{1}}
and
\pi_{p_{2}}^{r_{2}}
are different.
For any
t>s_{1}\vee s_{2}
, by Lemma
4.6
there is some (random)
h
large enough (depending on
p_{1}
,
p_{2}
,
r_{1}
,
r_{2}
and
t
) such that

\pi_{p_{1}}^{r_{1}}(t^{\prime})=\pi_{(p_{1};hr_{1},h)}(t^{\prime})
for any
s_{1}\leq t^{\prime}\leq t
, and
\pi_{p_{2}}^{r_{2}}(t^{\prime})=\pi_{(p_{2};hr_{2},h)}(t^{\prime})
for any
s_{2}\leq t^{\prime}\leq t
.
Thus by Lemma
3.14
, the set
O\cap(-\infty,t]
is either empty, or a closed interval. By sending
t\to\infty
we get the conclusion.

We next consider the case where
r_{1}=r_{2}
.
Again we take any
t>s_{1}\vee s_{2}
, and by Lemma
4.6
there is some (random)
h
large enough (depending on
p_{1}
,
p_{2}
,
r_{1}=r_{2}
and
t
) such that

\pi_{p_{1}}^{r_{1}}(t^{\prime})=\pi_{(p_{1};hr_{1},h)}(t^{\prime})
for any
s_{1}\leq t^{\prime}\leq t
, and
\pi_{p_{2}}^{r_{2}}(t^{\prime})=\pi_{(p_{2};hr_{1},h)}(t^{\prime})
for any
s_{2}\leq t^{\prime}\leq t
.
By Lemma
3.14
, the
set
\{s_{1}\vee s_{2}\leq t^{\prime}\leq h:\pi_{(p_{1};hr_{1},h)}(t^{\prime})=\pi_{(p_{2};hr_{1},h)}(t^{\prime})\}
is a closed interval with right endpoint being
h
;
so the set
O\cap(-\infty,t]
is either empty, or a closed interval with right endpoint being
t
. Again by sending
t\to\infty
we get the conclusion.
∎

Now we finish proving that the constructed paths are semi-infinite geodesics.

Lemma 4.10
.

For any fixed
r\in\mathbb{R}
, almost surely the constructed path
\pi_{p}^{r}
is a semi-infinite geodesic, for each
p\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
.

Proof.

Using skew-shift invariance, it suffice to prove for the case where
r=0
.

We start with a fixed
p\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
.
Above we have stated that any segment of
\pi_{p}
is a geodesic, and it remains to show that it has the desired asymptotic direction.
By Lemma
4.8
, and the Borel-Cantelli lemma, almost surely there exists some random
k_{0}>0
, such that for any
k>k_{0}
,
k\in\mathbb{N}
, we have
|\pi_{p}(2^{k})|<2^{2k/3+0.1k}
.
For any
k>k_{0}
, since
\pi_{p}
between
(\pi_{p}(2^{k}),2^{k})
and
(\pi_{p}(2^{k+1}),2^{k+1})
is a geodesic, using Lemma
3.11
we can bound
\sup_{t\in[2^{k},2^{k+1}]}|\pi_{p}(t)|
. Thus we conclude that
\lim_{t\to\infty}t^{-1}\pi_{p}(t)=0
.

Now we have that almost surely, for any
p\in\mathbb{Q}^{2}
, we have
\lim_{t\to\infty}t^{-1}\pi_{p}(t)=0
. Assuming this event, and consider some
p=(x,s)\not\in\mathbb{Q}^{2}
.
Then we can find some
t,x_{-},x_{+}\in\mathbb{Q}
, such that
t>s
and
x_{-}<\pi_{p}(s)<x_{+}
. By Lemma
4.9
, we must have that
\pi_{(x_{-},t)}(t^{\prime})\leq\pi_{p}(t^{\prime})\leq\pi_{(x_{+},t)}(t^{\prime})
for any
t^{\prime}\geq t
.
Since
\lim_{t\to\infty}t^{-1}\pi_{(x_{-},t)}(t)=\lim_{t\to\infty}t^{-1}\pi_{(x_{+},t)}(t)=0
, we must have
\lim_{t\to\infty}t^{-1}\pi_{p}(t)=0
.
∎

4.3
Properties of semi-infinite geodesics

The first property is about the overlap of semi-infinite geodesics for starting points around a fixed point.
For any continuous path
\pi_{n}:[s_{n},t_{n}]\to\mathbb{R}
,
n\in\mathbb{N}
, and
\pi:[s,t]\to\mathbb{R}
, we say that
\pi_{n}\to\pi

in the overlap topology
, if for all large enough
n
,
O_{n}=\{t^{\prime}\in[s,t]\cap[s_{n},t_{n}]:\pi_{n}(t^{\prime})=\pi(t^{\prime})\}
is an interval, and the endpoints of
O_{n}
converge to
s
and
t
.
We quote the following result on convergence in the overlap topology for finite geodesics.

Lemma 4.11

(
[
DSV20
, Lemma 3.1, Lemma 3.3]
)
.

Almost surely the following is true.
Let
(p_{n};q_{n})\to(p;q)\in\mathbb{R}^{4}_{\uparrow}
, and let
\pi_{n}
be any sequence of geodesics from
p_{n}
to
q_{n}
.
Suppose that there is a unique geodesic
\pi
from
p
to
q
.
Then
\pi_{n}\to\pi
in the overlap topology.

From this, we can get overlap of semi-infinite geodesics.

Lemma 4.12
.

For any
r\in\mathbb{R}
,
p=(x,s)\in\mathbb{R}
and
t>s
, almost surely there is an open neighborhood
\hat{B}
of
p
, such that
\pi_{p}^{r}(t^{\prime})=\pi_{p^{\prime}}^{r}(t^{\prime})
for any
p^{\prime}\in\hat{B}
and
t^{\prime}\geq t
.

Proof.

Take a compact set
B
containing an open neighborhood of
p
.
We assume (the probability
1
event) that for any
h\in\mathbb{N}
,
h>s
, there is a unique geodesic
\pi_{(p;hr,h)}
from
p
to
(hr,h)
.

By Lemma
4.6
, we can find some large enough (random)
h\in\mathbb{N}
with
h>t
, such that
\pi_{(p^{\prime};hr,h)}(t)=\pi_{p^{\prime}}^{r}(t)
for any
p^{\prime}=(x^{\prime},s^{\prime})\in B
with
s^{\prime}\leq t
.
By Lemma
4.11
, there is an open neighborhood
\hat{B}
of
p
, such that
\hat{B}\subset B
, and for any
p^{\prime}\in\hat{B}
there is
\pi_{(p^{\prime};hr,h)}(t)=\pi_{(p;hr,h)}(t)
.
We then have that
\pi_{p}^{r}(t)=\pi_{p^{\prime}}^{r}(t)
for any
p^{\prime}\in\hat{B}
.
By Lemma
4.9
we have
\pi_{p}^{r}(t^{\prime})=\pi_{p^{\prime}}^{r}(t^{\prime})
for any
p^{\prime}\in\hat{B}
and
t^{\prime}\geq t
.
∎

\mathbf{0}
\pi_{\mathbf{0}}^{-1/n}
\pi_{\mathbf{0}}
\pi_{\mathbf{0}}^{1/n}
t

(a)

Lemma
4.13
: both
\pi_{\mathbf{0}}^{-1/n}(t)
and
\pi_{\mathbf{0}}^{1/n}(t)
converge to
\pi_{\mathbf{0}}(t)
.

t
p_{1}
p_{2}
-t^{2/3+0.01}
t^{2/3+0.01}

(b)

Lemma
4.14
: under
\mathcal{E}_{1}^{c}
where
|\pi_{p_{1}}(t)|\vee|\pi_{p_{2}}(t)|\leq t^{2/3+0.01}
and
\mathcal{E}_{2}^{c}
where
x\mapsto\mathcal{L}(p_{1};x,t)-\mathcal{L}(p_{2};x,t)
is constant in
[-t^{2/3+0.01},t^{2/3+0.01}]
,
\pi_{p_{1}}
and
\pi_{p_{2}}
would coalesce before
t
.

Figure 7
:
Illustrations for the proofs of Lemma
4.13
and
4.14
.

If we fix both the starting point and the direction, then almost surely the semi-infinite geodesic is unique.

Lemma 4.13
.

For fixed
r\in\mathbb{R}
and
p\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
, almost surely there is a unique semi-infinite geodesic from
p
in the
r
direction.

The proof uses the ordering of semi-infinite geodesics to sandwich any semi-infinite geodesic between two other semi-infinite geodesics with the same starting point but slightly different directions. We will also use the maximizer formulation of semi-infinite geodesics (Lemma
4.7
), and monotonicity of Busemann functions (Lemma
4.5
).

Proof.

It suffices to prove uniqueness.
By skew-shift invariance of the directed landscape (Lemma
3.7
), without loss of generality we can assume that
r=0
and
p=\mathbf{0}
.
We will work on the almost sure event that for any
m,n\in\mathbb{N}
, there is a unique geodesic from
\mathbf{0}
to
(m/n,m)
, and a unique geodesic from
\mathbf{0}
to
(-m/n,m)
.

Let
\pi:\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}\to\mathbb{R}
be a semi-infinite geodesic in the
0
direction.
Take any fixed
t>0
, and
n,n^{\prime}\in\mathbb{N}
with
n<n^{\prime}
. By the asymptotic directions and Lemma
4.6
, we can find some
m\in\mathbb{N}
such that
\pi_{\mathbf{0}}^{r}(t)=\pi_{(\mathbf{0};mr,m)}(t)
, for each
r\in\{-1/n,1/n,-1/n^{\prime},1/n^{\prime}\}
,
and
|\pi(m)|<m/n^{\prime}
.
Thus by Lemma
3.14
we have

\pi_{\mathbf{0}}^{-1/n}(t)\leq\pi_{\mathbf{0}}^{-1/n^{\prime}}(t)\leq\pi(t)\leq\pi_{\mathbf{0}}^{1/n^{\prime}}(t)\leq\pi_{\mathbf{0}}^{1/n}(t).

Then the limits
x_{-}:=\lim_{n\to\infty}\pi_{\mathbf{0}}^{-1/n}(t)
and
x_{+}:=\lim_{n\to\infty}\pi_{\mathbf{0}}^{1/n}(t)
exist, and
x_{-}\leq\pi(t)\leq x_{+}
. We would next show that
x_{-}=x_{+}
, and then
\pi(t)
must equal
x_{-}=x_{+}
. See Figure
7(a)
for an illustration.

Before proceeding further, we explain how to prove that
x_{-}=x_{+}
. Recall (from Lemma
4.7
) that the function
\mathcal{L}(\mathbf{0};\cdot,t)+\mathcal{M}^{-1/n}(\cdot,t;0,t)
takes its maximum value at
\pi_{\mathbf{0}}^{-1/n}(t)
. Then we prove uniform convergence of
\mathcal{M}^{-1/n}(\cdot,t;0,t)
to
\mathcal{M}(\cdot,t;0,t)
, in a compact interval.
By passing to the
n\to\infty
limit, we get that
x_{-}
is the
\argmax
of the function
\mathcal{L}(\mathbf{0};\cdot,t)+\mathcal{M}(\cdot,t;0,t)
; and similarly this is also true for
x_{+}
. By uniqueness of the
\argmax
we conclude that
x_{-}=x_{+}
.

By Lemma
4.7
, for any
y\in\mathbb{R}
we have

\mathcal{L}(\mathbf{0};\pi_{\mathbf{0}}^{-1/n}(t),t)+\mathcal{M}^{-1/n}(\pi_{\mathbf{0}}^{-1/n}(t),t;x_{-},t)\geq\mathcal{L}(\mathbf{0};y,t)+\mathcal{M}^{-1/n}(y,t;x_{-},t).

(4.3)

We would next send
n\to\infty
for this equation.
By continuity of
\mathcal{L}
and
\lim_{n\to\infty}\pi_{\mathbf{0}}^{-1/n}(t)=x_{-}
, we have that

\lim_{n\to\infty}\mathcal{L}(\mathbf{0};\pi_{\mathbf{0}}^{-1/n}(t),t)=\mathcal{L}(\mathbf{0};x_{-},t).

By Lemma
4.5
, we have that

\mathcal{M}(\pi_{\mathbf{0}}^{-1/n}(t),t;x_{-},t)\leq\mathcal{M}^{-1/n}(\pi_{\mathbf{0}}^{-1/n}(t),t;x_{-},t)\leq\mathcal{M}^{-1}(\pi_{\mathbf{0}}^{-1/n}(t),t;x_{-},t).

By continuity of
\mathcal{M}
and
\mathcal{M}^{-1}
, we have
\mathcal{M}(\pi_{\mathbf{0}}^{-1/n}(t),t;x_{-},t)\to 0
and
\mathcal{M}^{-1}(\pi_{\mathbf{0}}^{-1/n}(t),t;x_{-},t)\to 0
as
n\to\infty
; so

\lim_{n\to\infty}\mathcal{M}^{-1/n}(\pi_{\mathbf{0}}^{-1/n}(t),t;x_{-},t)=0.

For
\mathcal{M}^{-1/n}(y,t;x_{-},t)
, by Lemma
4.5
it is non-decreasing as
n\to\infty
, and
\mathcal{M}^{-1/n}(y,t;x_{-},t)\leq\mathcal{M}(y,t;x_{-},t)
.
We claim that almost surely, for any
y\in\mathbb{R}
,

\lim_{n\to\infty}\mathcal{M}^{-1/n}(y,t;x_{-},t)=\mathcal{M}(y,t;x_{-},t).

(4.4)

It suffices to show that for any
N\in\mathbb{N}
, almost surely this convergence holds for all
y\in[-N,N]
, conditional on that
|x_{-}|<N
.
We note that when
|x_{-}|,|y|<N
, by Lemma
4.5
there is

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}^{-1/n}(-N,t;N,t)-\mathcal{M}^{-1/n}(y,t;x_{-},t)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}^{-1/n}(-N,t;y,t)+\mathcal{M}^{-1/n}(x_{-},t;N,t)

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}(-N,t;y,t)+\mathcal{M}(x_{-},t;N,t)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}(-N,t;N,t)-\mathcal{M}(y,t;x_{-},t),

i.e.,

\mathcal{M}^{-1/n}(y,t;x_{-},t)-\mathcal{M}(y,t;x_{-},t)\leq\mathcal{M}^{-1/n}(-N,t;N,t)-\mathcal{M}(-N,t;N,t).

The right hand side is almost surely positive, while the expectation equals
4N/n
; it is also non-increasing as
n\to\infty
. Thus by sending
n\to\infty
we have that
\mathcal{M}^{-1/n}(-N,t;N,t)-\mathcal{M}(-N,t;N,t)\to 0
almost surely.
Hence, conditional on
|x_{-}|<N
, we must have that

\limsup_{n\to\infty}\mathcal{M}^{-1/n}(y,t;x_{-},t)\leq\mathcal{M}(y,t;x_{-},t).

Similarly we have
\liminf_{n\to\infty}\mathcal{M}^{-1/n}(y,t;x_{-},t)\geq\mathcal{M}(y,t;x_{-},t)
. So conditional on
|x_{-}|<N
, (
4.4
) holds for all
y\in[-N,N]
. Then (
4.4
) almost surely holds for all
y\in\mathbb{R}
.

Then by sending
n\to\infty
for (
4.3
), we conclude that almost surely,
\mathcal{L}(\mathbf{0};x_{-},t)\geq\mathcal{L}(\mathbf{0};y,t)+\mathcal{M}(y,t;x_{-},t)
for any
y\in\mathbb{R}
; by symmetry we also have

\mathcal{L}(\mathbf{0};x_{+},t)\geq\mathcal{L}(\mathbf{0};y,t)+\mathcal{M}(y,t;x_{+},t)
for any
y\in\mathbb{R}
.
Thus we have that the function
x\mapsto\mathcal{L}(\mathbf{0};x,t)+\mathcal{M}(x,t;0,t)
achieves its maximum at both
x_{-}
and
x_{+}
.
However,
x\mapsto\mathcal{L}(\mathbf{0};x,t)
is a scaled Airy
2
process, so it is absolutely continuous with respect to Brownian motion (by
[
CH14
]
or Theorem
3.17
) in any compact interval; also
x\mapsto\mathcal{M}(x,t;0,t)
is a Brownian motion by Lemma
4.4
.
These imply that
x\mapsto\mathcal{L}(\mathbf{0};x,t)+\mathcal{M}(x,t;0,t)
is also absolutely continuous with respect to Brownian motion in any compact interval.
As Brownian motion almost surely has a unique maximum in any compact interval, we conclude that
x_{-}=x_{+}
almost surely.

Thus the above implies that for a given
t>0
, almost surely
\pi(t)
is uniquely determined, which then by considering all rational
t>0
along with continuity of
\pi
, implies the uniqueness of
\pi
.
∎

Finally, we state an improvement of Lemma
4.9
for semi-infinite geodesics in a fixed direction; namely, for fixed two points, the semi-infinite geodesics to this direction coalesce almost surely.

Lemma 4.14
.

For any fixed
r\in\mathbb{R}
and
p_{1}=(x_{1},s_{1}),p_{2}=(x_{2},s_{2})\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
, almost surely the following is true.
Let
O=\{t\geq s_{1}\vee s_{2}:\pi_{p_{1}}^{r}(t)=\pi_{p_{2}}^{r}(t)\}
, then
O=[t_{*},\infty)
for some
t_{*}\geq s_{1}\vee s_{2}
.

Proof.

By Lemma
3.7
, without loss of generality we will only consider the case
r=0
. It suffices to show that
\mathbb{P}[\pi_{p_{1}}(t)=\pi_{p_{2}}(t)]\to 1
as
t\to\infty
; then we have that almost surely
O\neq\emptyset
, and by Lemma
4.9
the conclusion follows.

For fixed
t>s_{1}\vee s_{2}
, we consider the following events.

1.

Let
\mathcal{E}_{1}
be the event where
|\pi_{p_{1}}(t)|\vee|\pi_{p_{2}}(t)|>t^{2/3+0.01}
.

2.

Let
\mathcal{E}_{2}
be the event where the function
x\mapsto\mathcal{L}(p_{1};x,t)-\mathcal{L}(p_{2};x,t)
is not constant for
x\in[-t^{2/3+0.01},t^{2/3+0.01}]
.

By Lemma
4.7
, the functions
x\mapsto\mathcal{L}(p_{1};x,t)+\mathcal{M}(x,t;0,t)
and
x\mapsto\mathcal{L}(p_{2};x,t)+\mathcal{M}(x,t;0,t)
take their maximum values at
\pi_{p_{1}}(t)
and
\pi_{p_{2}}(t)
, respectively.
Under
\mathcal{E}_{2}^{c}
, these two functions differ by a constant in the interval
[-t^{2/3+0.01},t^{2/3+0.01}]
.
As argued in the proof of Lemma
4.13
, these two functions are absolutely continuous with respect to a Brownian motion in any compact interval, so almost surely they have a unique
\argmax
in the interval
[-t^{2/3+0.01},t^{2/3+0.01}]
.
Thus (up to a probability zero event)

\mathcal{E}_{1}^{c}\cap\mathcal{E}_{2}^{c}
implies that
\pi_{p_{1}}(t)=\pi_{p_{2}}(t)
. See Figure
7(b)
for an illustration.

As
t\to\infty
, by Lemma
4.8
we have that
\mathbb{P}[\mathcal{E}_{1}]\to 0
. We net show that
\mathbb{P}[\mathcal{E}_{2}]\to 0
.
For each
i\in\mathbb{Z}
,
|i|<\lceil t^{0.01}\rceil
, let
\mathcal{E}_{2,i}
be the event where
x\mapsto\mathcal{L}(p_{1};x,t)-\mathcal{L}(p_{2};x,t)
is not constant for
x\in[it^{2/3},(i+1)t^{2/3}]
.
Then
\mathcal{E}_{2}\subset\cup_{|i|<\lceil t^{0.01}\rceil}\mathcal{E}_{2,i}
, and we need to bound each
\mathbb{P}[\mathcal{E}_{2,i}]
.
Using scaling and skew-shift invariance of the directed landscape, we get that
\mathbb{P}[\mathcal{E}_{2,i}]=\mathbb{P}[\mathcal{E}_{2,i}^{\prime}]
, with
\mathcal{E}_{2,i}^{\prime}
being the event where

x\mapsto\mathcal{L}(t^{-1/6}x_{1}-it^{-1/2}s_{1},t^{-1/4}s_{1};x,t^{3/4})-\mathcal{L}(t^{-1/6}x_{2}-it^{-1/2}s_{2},t^{-1/4}s_{2};x,t^{3/4})

is not constant for
x\in[0,t^{1/2}]
.
Let
B=[-1,1]\times[-1,1]
.
Then for
t
large enough, we would have
(t^{-1/6}x_{1}-it^{-1/2}s_{1},t^{-1/4}s_{1})
,
(t^{-1/6}x_{2}-it^{-1/2}s_{2},t^{-1/4}s_{2})\in B
for any
|i|<\lceil t^{0.01}\rceil
.
So by Lemma
4.1
, we conclude that there is a constant
C>0
such that

\mathbb{P}[\mathcal{E}_{2,i}^{\prime}]<Ct^{-1/4}e^{C|\log(t)|^{5/6}},

for each
|i|<\lceil t^{0.01}\rceil
.
Thus we have
\mathbb{P}[\mathcal{E}_{2}]\leq\sum_{|i|<\lceil t^{0.01}\rceil}\mathbb{P}[\mathcal{E}_{2,i}^{\prime}]<Ct^{-1/4}e^{C|\log(t)|^{5/6}}(2\lceil t^{0.01}\rceil+1)
when
t
is large enough.
We then have that
\mathbb{P}[\mathcal{E}_{2}]\to 0
as
t\to\infty
, and hence
\mathbb{P}[\pi_{p_{1}}(t)=\pi_{p_{2}}(t)]\to 1
as
t\to\infty
, and the conclusion follows.
∎

Remark 4.15
.

We choose to state Lemma
4.14
in the current form as it will be convenient for a later application. In fact, via the ordering of geodesics and bounds on transversal fluctuations, one can prove that almost surely, the statement holds simultaneously for any starting points in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
.

5
Duality in the limit

In this section we prepare the necessary framework of competition interfaces in the directed landscape. We will start from Lemma
3.6
, i.e., the equality of law of the competition interface starting from the stationary initial condition and the semi-infinite geodesic in the prelimiting model of exponential LPP, and use Theorem
3.2
from
[
DV21
]
to pass to the limit and conclude the same for the directed landscape.

Let
\mathcal{B}
be a standard two sided Brownian motion.
Recall from Section
2.3
that

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}^{L}(x,t)

\displaystyle=\sup_{y\leq 0}\mathcal{L}(y,0;x,t)+\mathcal{B}(y),

(5.1)

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}^{R}(x,t)

\displaystyle=\sup_{y\geq 0}\mathcal{L}(y,0;x,t)+\mathcal{B}(y),

(5.2)

for any
x\in\mathbb{R},t>0
.
Using the quadrangle inequality (Lemma
3.8
), we can show that the function
x\mapsto\mathcal{L}^{L}(x,t)-\mathcal{L}^{R}(x,t)
is non-increasing in
x
.
Indeed, for any
x_{1}<x_{2}
and
y_{1}\leq 0\leq y_{2}
, by Lemma
3.8
we have

(\mathcal{L}(y_{1},0;x_{1},t)+\mathcal{B}(y_{1}))+(\mathcal{L}(y_{2},0;x_{2},t)+\mathcal{B}(y_{2}))\geq(\mathcal{L}(y_{1},0;x_{2},t)+\mathcal{B}(y_{1}))+(\mathcal{L}(y_{2},0;x_{1},t)+\mathcal{B}(y_{2})).

By taking the supremum over
y_{1}\leq 0\leq y_{2}
in the right hand side, we have

\mathcal{L}^{L}(x_{1},t)+\mathcal{L}^{R}(x_{2},t)\geq\mathcal{L}^{L}(x_{2},t)+\mathcal{L}^{R}(x_{1},t).

We then let

\vartheta^{\mathcal{B}}(t)=\inf\{x\in\mathbb{R}:\mathcal{L}^{L}(x,t)-\mathcal{L}^{R}(x,t)\leq 0\},

as given in (
2.4
).

The goal of this section is to establish Propositon
2.8
, i.e.,
\vartheta^{\mathcal{B}}
and
\pi_{\mathbf{0}}
are equal in distribution.
Relying on the same statement for exponential LPP (Lemma
3.6
), the Proposition
2.8
follows immediately from the following convergence in distribution. Recall the maps
\mathsf{R}_{n}
and
\Graph
from Definition
3.1
.

Proposition 5.1
.

For any
0<h
,
\mathsf{R}_{n}(\Gamma_{\mathbf{0}})\cap(\mathbb{R}\times[0,h])\to\Graph(\pi_{\mathbf{0}})\cap(\mathbb{R}\times[0,h])
in distribution in the Hausdorff topology, as
n\to\infty
.

Proposition 5.2
.

For any
0<g<h
,
\mathsf{R}_{n}(\Delta)\cap(\mathbb{R}\times[g,h])\to\Graph(\vartheta^{\mathcal{B}})\cap(\mathbb{R}\times[g,h])
in distribution in the Hausdorff topology, as
n\to\infty
.

In Proposition
5.2
we need
g>0
due to using Theorem
3.2
, where a compact set needs to be taken for uniform convergence.

It remains to prove Proposition
5.1
and
5.2
, in the following two subsections respectively.

5.1
Weak convergence of semi-infinite geodesics

Let us reiterate from Section
2.3
, the general strategy to prove Proposition
5.1
.
For simplicity and by scaling invariance, it suffices to prove the result for
h=1
.

Our proof would rely on the convergence of the exponential LPP to the directed landscape.
However, a technical issue arises since the convergence stated in Theorem
3.2
is only for compact sets. Thus to use the latter, we will define a “truncated” version of the geodesic
\Gamma_{\mathbf{0}}
, parameterized by some
H>0
. Theorem
3.2
then allows us to deduce a scaling convergence to a “truncated” version of
\pi_{\mathbf{0}}
.
The final step would be to show that the truncated versions agree with the original versions of the geodesics with probabilities increasing to
1
as
H\to\infty
. For the pre-limiting semi-infinite geodesics, this needs to be done uniformly in
n
. This last step can also be understood as proving tightness for the pre-limiting semi-infinite geodesics, i.e., to give a uniform bound on the transversal fluctuations.

We first truncate the semi-infinite geodesic
\pi_{\mathbf{0}}
in
\mathcal{L}
, and the idea is to force
\pi_{\mathbf{0}}(1)
to stay in a compact interval
[-H,H]
.
Recall the Busemann function
\mathcal{M}
from Definition
4.3
.
For each
H>0
, we define the
H-
geodesic in
\mathcal{L}
as follows.
We take
x_{*}=\argmax_{x\in[-H,H]}\mathcal{L}(\mathbf{0};x,1)+\mathcal{M}(x,1;0,1)
, and let
\pi_{\mathbf{0},H}=\pi_{(\mathbf{0};x_{*},1)}
.

Lemma 5.3
.

Almost surely the
H
-geodesic
\pi_{\mathbf{0},H}
is unique.

This is by the uniqueness of the
\argmax
of the sum of two weight profiles, at each rational time. Similar arguments have been used to prove uniqueness of finite geodesics in
[
DOV18
]
.

Proof.

By Theorem
3.18
and Lemma
4.4
, for any
0<t<1
the function

y\mapsto\sup_{x\in[-H,H]}\mathcal{L}(y,t;x,1)+\mathcal{M}(x,1;0,1)

is absolutely continuous with respect to a Brownian motion, on any compact set.
Moreover, on account of being a scaled parabolic Airy
2
process, the function
y\mapsto\mathcal{L}(\mathbf{0};y,t)
is also absolutely continuous with respect to a Brownian motion on any compact set.
Thus, almost surely, the function

y\mapsto\mathcal{L}(\mathbf{0};y,t)+\sup_{x\in[-H,H]}\mathcal{L}(y,t;x,1)+\mathcal{M}(x,1;0,1)

has a unique maximum in any compact interval.
Since this function also has parabolic decay as
|y|\to\infty
(by Lemma
3.9
), it has a unique global maximum, which we denote as
y_{t,*}
. Then we must have
\pi_{\mathbf{0},H}(t)=y_{t,*}
and hence almost surely the uniqueness of
\pi_{\mathbf{0},H}(t)
holds for all rational
0<t<1
. By continuity of
\pi_{\mathbf{0},H}
the conclusion follows.
∎

We next carry out the counterpart truncation for the pre-limiting semi-infinite geodesics.
Similarly as above, we define the
exponential LPP
(n,H)
-geodesic

\Gamma_{\mathbf{0},H,n}
as follows.
Recalling the Busemann function
G(\cdot,\cdot)
from Definition
3.5
, let

m_{*}[n]=\argmax_{m\in\mathbb{Z},|m-n|\leq{2^{5/3}Hn^{2/3}}}T_{\mathbf{0},(m,n)}+G((m,n+1)),

which is almost surely unique (due to continuity of the distribution functions).
Let
\Gamma_{\mathbf{0},H,n}=\Gamma_{\mathbf{0},(m_{*}[n],n)}
.
Noting that the constant
2^{5/3}
is a result of the scaling function in Section
3.1.1
, we have the following convergence result.

Lemma 5.4
.

For any
H>0
, we have
\mathsf{R}_{n}(\Gamma_{\mathbf{0},H,n})\to\Graph(\pi_{\mathbf{0},H})
in distribution in the Hausdorff topology.

Proof.

We take the coupling from Theorem
3.2
of all
\mathcal{K}_{n}
and
\mathcal{L}
, on the set
\{(x,s;y,t)\in\mathbb{R}^{4}:s<t\leq 1\}\subset\mathbb{R}^{4}_{\uparrow}
.
We also consider the function
\mathcal{M}_{n}:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
, where

\mathcal{M}_{n}(x)=2^{-4/3}n^{-1/3}(G((\lfloor n+2^{5/3}n^{2/3}x\rfloor,n+1))-G((n,n+1))+2(\lfloor n+2^{5/3}n^{2/3}x\rfloor-n)).

This is in the same scale as and converges to the function
x\mapsto\mathcal{M}(x,1;0,1)
.
As stated in Section
3.1.3
, along any down-right path,
G
is a random walk with i.i.d. steps. Thus
\mathcal{M}_{n}
is a (rescaled) random walk with i.i.d. steps, and (using Skorokhod’s representation theorem) we couple
\mathcal{M}_{n}
with
x\mapsto\mathcal{M}(x,1;0,1)
such that
\mathcal{M}_{n}
converges to the latter almost surely uniformly in each compact set.
We show that
\mathsf{R}_{n}(\Gamma_{\mathbf{0},H,n})\to\Graph(\pi_{\mathbf{0},H})
almost surely under this coupling.

We work under the probability one event where
\pi_{\mathbf{0},H}
is unique.
In particular, this implies that
\pi_{\mathbf{0},H}(1)
is the unique maximum of the function
x\mapsto\mathcal{L}(\mathbf{0};x,1)+\mathcal{M}(x,1;0,1)
in
[-H,H]
, and
\pi_{\mathbf{0},H}
is the unique geodesic from
\mathbf{0}
to
(\pi_{\mathbf{0},H}(1),1)
.

Using previous notations,
\mathsf{R}_{n}((m_{*}[n],n))
is a maximum of the function
x\mapsto\mathcal{K}_{n}(\mathbf{0};x,1)+\mathcal{M}_{n}(x)

\mathsf{R}_{n}((m_{*}[n],n))
in
[-H,H]
.
Since
x\mapsto\mathcal{K}_{n}(\mathbf{0};x,1)+\mathcal{M}_{n}(x)
uniformly converges to
x\mapsto\mathcal{L}(\mathbf{0};x,1)+\mathcal{M}(x,1;0,1)
in
[-H,H]
, we have that
\mathsf{R}_{n}((m_{*}[n],n))\to\pi_{\mathbf{0},H}(1)
.
Since
\Gamma_{\mathbf{0},H,n}
is the geodesic from
\mathbf{0}
to
(m_{*}[n],n)
,
\mathsf{R}_{n}(\Gamma_{\mathbf{0},H,n})
is a maximal geodesic set of
\mathcal{K}_{n}
from
\mathbf{0}
to
\mathsf{R}_{n}((m_{*}[n],n))
.
Thus we conclude that any subsequential limit of
\mathsf{R}_{n}(\Gamma_{\mathbf{0},H,n})
would be
\Graph(\pi)
for some
\mathcal{L}
geodesic
\pi
from
\mathbf{0}
to
(\pi_{\mathbf{0},H}(1),1)
(by Theorem
3.2
), thus we must have
\pi=\pi_{\mathbf{0},H}
, and the conclusion follows.
∎

We next prove tightness, which says that in the pre-limiting setting, the truncated semi-infinite geodesics are the same as the original semi-infinite geodesics with high probability.
This is achieved via estimates on the transversal fluctuations of semi-infinite geodesics, given by Lemma
3.4
.

Lemma 5.5
.

There are constants
c,C>0
, such that for any
H>0
and any
n
large enough (depending on
H
), we have
\mathbb{P}[\mathsf{R}_{n}(\Gamma_{\mathbf{0},H,n})\neq\mathsf{R}_{n}(\Gamma_{\mathbf{0}})\cap(\mathbb{R}\times[0,1])]<Ce^{-cH}
.

Proof.

Let
m_{*}\in\mathbb{Z}
such that
(m_{*},n),(m_{*},n+1)\in\Gamma_{\mathbf{0}}
.
Then for any
m\in\mathbb{Z}
,
m\neq m_{*}
, we must have

T_{\mathbf{0},(m_{*},n)}+G((m_{*},n+1))>T_{\mathbf{0},(m,n)}+G((m,n+1))

since otherwise we would have
T_{\mathbf{0},(m_{*},n)}+T_{(m_{*},n+1),\mathbf{c}}\leq T_{\mathbf{0},(m,n)}+T_{(m,n+1),\mathbf{c}}
where
\mathbf{c}
is any point in
\Gamma_{(m_{*},n+1)}\cap\Gamma_{(m,n+1)}
.
This implies that the path obtained by concatenating
\Gamma_{\mathbf{0},(m,n)}
and
\Gamma_{(m,n+1),\mathbf{c}}
has a total weight no less than the total weight of the part of
\Gamma_{\mathbf{0}}
up to
\mathbf{c}
.
However, from the definition of
\Gamma_{\mathbf{0}}
we have that the part of
\Gamma_{\mathbf{0}}
up to
\mathbf{c}
is the geodesic
\Gamma_{\mathbf{0},\mathbf{c}}
.
Thus we get a contradiction with the assumption that there is a unique geodesic between
\mathbf{0}
and
\mathbf{c}
.

Then the event where
\mathsf{R}_{n}(\Gamma_{\mathbf{0},H,n})\neq\mathsf{R}_{n}(\Gamma_{\mathbf{0}})\cap(\mathbb{R}\times[0,1])
is equivalent to the event
|m_{*}-n|>2^{5/3}Hn^{2/3}
.
By Lemma
3.4
its probability is upper bounded by
Ce^{-cH}
for some constants
c,C>0
, so the conclusion follows.
∎

The relation between
\pi_{\mathbf{0}}
and
\pi_{\mathbf{0},H}
is more obvious, since we have that
\pi_{\mathbf{0},H}
is the same as
\pi_{\mathbf{0}}
on
[0,1]
, when
H>|\pi_{\mathbf{0}}(1)|
.
Now we can finish the proof of Proposition
5.1
.

Proof of Proposition
5.1
.

We just prove for the case where
h=1
, and the case of general
h>0
follows similar arguments.
For any continuous function
f
on the space of all compact subsets of
\mathbb{R}^{2}
with
0\leq f\leq 1
, by Lemma
5.4
we have

\mathbb{E}[f(\mathsf{R}_{n}(\Gamma_{\mathbf{0},H,n}))]\to\mathbb{E}[f(\Graph(\pi_{\mathbf{0},H}))]
as
n\to\infty
for any
H>0
.
Then we have

\limsup_{n\to\infty}\big|\mathbb{E}[f(\mathsf{R}_{n}(\Gamma_{\mathbf{0}})\cap(\mathbb{R}\times[0,1]))]-\mathbb{E}[f(\Graph(\pi_{\mathbf{0}})\cap(\mathbb{R}\times[0,1]))]\big|\\
\leq\limsup_{n\to\infty}\mathbb{P}[\mathsf{R}_{n}(\Gamma_{\mathbf{0},H,n})\neq\mathsf{R}_{n}(\Gamma_{\mathbf{0}})\cap(\mathbb{R}\times[0,1])]+\mathbb{P}[\Graph(\pi_{\mathbf{0},H})\neq\Graph(\pi_{\mathbf{0}})\cap(\mathbb{R}\times[0,1])]\\
\leq Ce^{-cH}+\mathbb{P}[\Graph(\pi_{\mathbf{0},H})\neq\Graph(\pi_{\mathbf{0}})\cap(\mathbb{R}\times[0,1])],

where the last inequality is by Lemma
5.5
.
As
H\to\infty
, we have
Ce^{-cH}\to 0
and
\mathbb{P}[\Graph(\pi_{\mathbf{0},H})\neq\Graph(\pi_{\mathbf{0}})\cap(\mathbb{R}\times[0,1])]\leq\mathbb{P}[|\pi_{\mathbf{0}}(1)|<H]\to 0
, by Lemma
4.8
.
So as
H\to\infty
the last line decays to zero. Thus we have
\mathbb{E}[f(\mathsf{R}_{n}(\Gamma_{\mathbf{0}})\cap(\mathbb{R}\times[0,1]))]\to\mathbb{E}[f(\Graph(\pi_{\mathbf{0}})\cap(\mathbb{R}\times[0,1]))]
, and the conclusion follows.
∎

We next carry out the counterpart convergence arguments for the competition interface.

5.2
Weak convergence of the competition interface

For Proposition
5.2
, we again just prove it for
h=1
, without loss of generality.
The proof follows a similar general framework as the proof of Proposition
5.1
, since one encounters the same technical difficulty that the convergence stated in Theorem
3.2
only applies to compact sets.
We similarly define a “truncated” version of
\Delta
, and use Theorem
3.2
to deduce that its scaling limit is a “truncated” version of
\vartheta^{\mathcal{B}}
.
As before, the last step entails showing that the truncated versions of
\Delta
are the same as the original version with high probability.
However, compared to the geodesics setting in the last subsection, now it is not immediate the truncated versions of
\vartheta^{\mathcal{B}}
are close (in total variation distance) to
\vartheta^{\mathcal{B}}
, and this requires some further argument.

We first truncate
\vartheta^{\mathcal{B}}
. The idea is to only use the boundary condition
\mathcal{B}
only in a compact interval.
For each
H>0
, we define the
H
-competition interface in
\mathcal{L}
as follows.
For any
x\in\mathbb{R},t>0
, we let

\mathcal{L}^{L}_{H}(x,t)=\sup_{-H\leq y\leq 0}\mathcal{L}(y,0;x,t)+\mathcal{B}(y),

and

\mathcal{L}^{R}_{H}(x,t)=\sup_{0\leq y\leq H}\mathcal{L}(y,0;x,t)+\mathcal{B}(y).

By arguments similar to the proof of the quadrangle inequality (Lemma
3.8
), or the arguments before Propositoin
5.1
, we have that the function
x\mapsto\mathcal{L}^{L}_{H}(x,t)-\mathcal{L}^{R}_{H}(x,t)
is non-increasing in
x
. So we can let

\vartheta^{\mathcal{B}}_{H}(t)=\inf\{x\in\mathbb{R}:\mathcal{L}^{L}_{H}(x,t)-\mathcal{L}^{R}_{H}(x,t)\leq 0\}.

We can show that actually, almost surely, for each
t>0
there is exactly one
x\in\mathbb{R}
with
\mathcal{L}^{L}_{H}(x,t)=\mathcal{L}^{R}_{H}(x,t)
.

Lemma 5.6
.

For any
H>0
, almost surely the following is true.
For any
t>0
,
|\{x\in\mathbb{R}:\mathcal{L}^{L}_{H}(x,t)-\mathcal{L}^{R}_{H}(x,t)=0\}|=1
.
Thus via continuity of
\mathcal{L}
and
\mathcal{B}
,
\vartheta^{\mathcal{B}}_{H}
must be continuous.

The proof proceeds as follows: first we show that
\mathcal{L}^{L}_{H}(x,t)\neq\mathcal{L}^{R}_{H}(x,t)
, for any
x
and
t>0
, when they are both rational.
Then if there is some
t>0
and
x_{1}<x_{2}
such that
\mathcal{L}^{L}_{H}(x_{1},t)-\mathcal{L}^{R}_{H}(x_{1},t)=\mathcal{L}^{L}_{H}(x_{2},t)-\mathcal{L}^{R}_{H}(x_{2},t)=0
, the difference
\mathcal{L}^{L}_{H}-\mathcal{L}^{R}_{H}
would be zero in the segment connecting
(x_{1},t)
and
(x_{2},t)
. We then show that one can find a rational point
(x_{*},t_{*})
where
\mathcal{L}^{L}_{H}-\mathcal{L}^{R}_{H}
equals zero, which is already ruled out by the previous line.
Finally, using continuity of
\mathcal{L}^{L}_{H}-\mathcal{L}^{R}_{H}
, and that (for any
t>0
)
\mathcal{L}^{L}_{H}(x,t)-\mathcal{L}^{R}_{H}(x,t)
tends to
\infty
(resp.
-\infty
) as
x\to-\infty
(resp.
x\to\infty
), we conclude that there must exist some
x
, and hence exactly one such, with
\mathcal{L}^{L}_{H}(x,t)=\mathcal{L}^{R}_{H}(x,t)
.

Proof.

We first show that for any fixed
x\in\mathbb{R},t>0
, almost surely we have
\mathcal{L}^{L}_{H}(x,t)\neq\mathcal{L}^{R}_{H}(x,t)
.
Indeed, this would imply that the function
y\mapsto\mathcal{L}(y,0;x,t)+\mathcal{B}(y)
has the same maximum in
[-H,0]
and
[0,H]
, and almost surely this cannot happen since
y\mapsto\mathcal{L}(y,0;x_{*},t)+\mathcal{B}(y)
in
[-H,H]
is absolutely continuous with respect to a Brownian motion, by
[
CH14
]
or Theorem
3.17
.
Thus, almost surely
\mathcal{L}^{L}_{H}(x,t)\neq\mathcal{L}^{R}_{H}(x,t)
, for any rational
x
and
t>0
.

We next show that
|\{x\in\mathbb{R}:\mathcal{L}^{L}_{H}(x,t)-\mathcal{L}^{R}_{H}(x,t)=0\}|\leq 1
for any (not necessarily rational)
t>0
.
For this, we use continuity of
\mathcal{L}
and geodesics, and basic inequalities of the directed landscape.

(y_{2},0)
(y_{1},0)
\mathbf{0}
(x_{1},t)
(x_{2},t)
(x_{*},t_{*})
(x_{1,*},t_{*})
(x_{2,*},t_{*})

Figure 8
:
An illustration of the arguments for
|\{x\in\mathbb{R}:\mathcal{L}^{L}_{H}(x,t)-\mathcal{L}^{R}_{H}(x,t)=0\}|\leq 1
in the proof of Lemma
5.6
: suppose that
\mathcal{L}^{L}_{H}(x_{1},t)-\mathcal{L}^{R}_{H}(x_{1},t)=\mathcal{L}^{L}_{H}(x_{2},t)-\mathcal{L}^{R}_{H}(x_{2},t)=0
, the difference
\mathcal{L}^{L}_{H}-\mathcal{L}^{R}_{H}
would be zero in the whole blue region, which must include a rational point
(x_{*},t_{*})
.

We argue by contradiction (see Figure
8
for an illustration).
Suppose that there is some
t>0
and
x_{1}<x_{2}
such that
\mathcal{L}^{L}_{H}(x_{1},t)-\mathcal{L}^{R}_{H}(x_{1},t)=\mathcal{L}^{L}_{H}(x_{2},t)-\mathcal{L}^{R}_{H}(x_{2},t)=0
.
Take any
y_{1}=\argmax_{y\in[0,H]}\mathcal{L}(y,0;x_{1},t)+\mathcal{B}(y)
.
Take any geodesic
\pi_{(y_{1},0;x_{1},t)}
.
Similarly, take any
y_{2}=\argmax_{y\in[-H,0]}\mathcal{L}(y,0;x_{2},t)+\mathcal{B}(y)
, and any geodesic
\pi_{(y_{2},0;x_{2},t)}
.
Then we can find a rational
0<t_{*}<t
such that
x_{1,*}<x_{2,*}
, where
x_{1,*}=\pi_{(y_{1},0;x_{1},t)}(t_{*})
and
x_{2,*}=\pi_{(y_{2},0;x_{2},t)}(t_{*})
.
We then have that

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}^{L}_{H}(x_{1,*},t_{*})

\displaystyle=\sup_{-H\leq y\leq 0}\mathcal{L}(y,0;x_{1,*},t_{*})+\mathcal{B}(y)

\displaystyle\leq\sup_{-H\leq y\leq 0}\mathcal{L}(y,0;x_{1},t_{*})-\mathcal{L}(x_{1,*},t_{*};x_{1},t)+\mathcal{B}(y)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{L}^{L}_{H}(x_{1},t)-\mathcal{L}(x_{1,*},t_{*};x_{1},t)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{L}^{R}_{H}(x_{1},t)-\mathcal{L}(x_{1,*},t_{*};x_{1},t)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{L}^{R}_{H}(x_{1,*},t_{*}),

where the inequality is by the reverse triangle inequality of the directed landscape, or the composition law (
1.1
).
Thus we have
\mathcal{L}^{L}_{H}(x_{1,*},t_{*})-\mathcal{L}^{R}_{H}(x_{1,*},t_{*})\leq 0
.
Similarly we have
\mathcal{L}^{L}_{H}(x_{2,*},t_{*})-\mathcal{L}^{R}_{H}(x_{2,*},t_{*})\geq 0
.

As above mentioned, by arguments similar to Lemma
3.8
, or those at the beginning of this section, we have that the function
x\mapsto\mathcal{L}^{L}_{H}(x,t_{*})-\mathcal{L}^{R}_{H}(x,t_{*})
is non-increasing.
Now we take any rational
x_{*}
such that
x_{1,*}\leq x_{*}\leq x_{2,*}
.
Then we have

0\leq\mathcal{L}^{L}_{H}(x_{2,*},t_{*})-\mathcal{L}^{R}_{H}(x_{2,*},t_{*})\leq\mathcal{L}^{L}_{H}(x_{*},t_{*})-\mathcal{L}^{R}_{H}(x_{*},t_{*})\leq\mathcal{L}^{L}_{H}(x_{1,*},t_{*})-\mathcal{L}^{R}_{H}(x_{1,*},t_{*})\leq 0.

This implies that
\mathcal{L}^{L}_{H}(x_{*},t_{*})=\mathcal{L}^{R}_{H}(x_{*},t_{*})
.
As we’ve shown that this almost surely cannot happen, we get a contradiction.
We thus conclude that
|\{x\in\mathbb{R}:\mathcal{L}^{L}_{H}(x,t)-\mathcal{L}^{R}_{H}(x,t)=0\}|\leq 1
for any
t>0
.

Finally, by Lemma
3.9
and growth estimates of
\mathcal{B}
, we have that for any
t>0
,
\mathcal{L}^{L}_{H}(x,t)
has parabolic decay as
x\to\infty
.
Also, for
x\leq 0
we have

\mathcal{L}^{L}_{H}(x,t)\geq\mathcal{B}(x)+\mathcal{L}(x,0;x,t),

so by Lemma
3.9
and growth estimates of
\mathcal{B}
we have that
\mathcal{L}^{L}_{H}(x,t)\geq-|x|^{1/2}\log^{3}(|x|)
for all
x
small enough.
Similalry,
\mathcal{L}^{R}_{H}(x,t)
has parabolic decay as
x\to-\infty
, and
\mathcal{L}^{R}_{H}(x,t)\geq-|x|^{1/2}\log^{3}(|x|)
for all
x
large enough.
Thus we conclude that
\lim_{x\to\infty}\mathcal{L}^{L}_{H}(x,t)-\mathcal{L}^{R}_{H}(x,t)=-\infty
and
\lim_{x\to-\infty}\mathcal{L}^{L}_{H}(x,t)-\mathcal{L}^{R}_{H}(x,t)=\infty
. With continuity of
\mathcal{L}^{L}_{H}-\mathcal{L}^{R}_{H}
we get that
|\{x\in\mathbb{R}:\mathcal{L}^{L}_{H}(x,t)-\mathcal{L}^{R}_{H}(x,t)=0\}|\geq 1
, so the conclusion follows.
∎

We also define the
exponential LPP
(n,H)
-competition interface

\Delta_{H,n}
as follows.
Recall that
G^{\vee}
is the
(-1,-1)
direction Busemann function in exponential LPP, defined in Section
3.1.3
. Analogous to the un-truncated definitions, we split
\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}^{2}\setminus\{\mathbf{0}\}
into two parts
P_{H,n,1}
and
P_{H,n,2}
, as follows.
Let
\mathbb{N}\times\{0\}\subset P_{H,n,1}
; and for any
p\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}\times\mathbb{N}
, if we let

m_{*}=\argmax_{m\in\mathbb{Z},|m|\leq 2^{5/3}Hn^{2/3}}G^{\vee}(m,0)+T_{(m,1),p}

(which is almost surely unique), then
p\in P_{H,n,1}
if and only if
m_{*}>0
.
We again note that the constant
2^{5/3}
is a result of the scaling in Section
3.1.1
.
Let
\Delta_{H,n}
be the boundary of
P_{H,n,1}
and
P_{H,n,2}
: for any
p\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}^{2}
, we let
p\in\Delta_{H,n}
if and only if
p+(1,0)\in P_{H,n,1}
, and
p+(0,1)\in P_{H,n,2}
.
See the right panel in Figure
9
for an illustration.

\mathbf{0}
\Delta
p
\Gamma_{p}^{\vee}

\mathbf{0}
\Delta_{H,n}
2^{5/3}Hn^{2/3}
-2^{5/3}Hn^{2/3}

Figure 9
:
A comparison between the competition interfaces
\Delta
and
\Delta_{H,n}
: for
\Delta
we consider semi-infinite geodesics in the
(-1,-1)
-direction; while for
\Delta_{H,n}
, we consider the optimal paths in the
(-1,-1)
-direction passing through
[-2^{5/3}Hn^{2/3},2^{5/3}Hn^{2/3}]\times\{0\}
.

We next prove convergence of these
(n,H)
-competition interfaces to the truncated competition interface in the directed landscape.

Lemma 5.7
.

For any
H>0
and
0<g<1
, we have
\mathsf{R}_{n}(\Delta_{H,n})\cap([-H,H]\times[g,1])\to\Graph(\vartheta^{\mathcal{B}}_{H})\cap([-H,H]\times[g,1])
in distribution in the Hausdorff topology.

We note that this where the requirement of
g>0
in Proposition
5.2
comes from.

In the proof we work under the coupling from Theorem
3.2
, and show almost sure convergence.
Note that (compared to Lemma
5.4
), here we only consider the sets
\mathsf{R}_{n}(\Delta_{H,n})
and
\Graph(\vartheta^{\mathcal{B}}_{H})
in
[-H,H]\times[0,1]
rather than
\mathbb{R}\times[0,1]
. This can be viewed as a further truncation, and is also due to the compactness limitation of Theorem
3.2
.

Proof.

We take the coupling from Theorem
3.2
of all
\mathcal{K}_{n}
and
\mathcal{L}
on the set
\{(x,s;y,t)\in\mathbb{R}^{4}:0<s<t\}\subset\mathbb{R}^{4}_{\uparrow}
.
We also consider the function
\mathcal{B}_{n}:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
, where

\mathcal{B}_{n}(x)=2^{-4/3}n^{-1/3}(G^{\vee}((\lceil 2^{5/3}n^{2/3}x\rceil,0))-2\lceil 2^{5/3}n^{2/3}x\rceil).

This is a discrete version of the Brownian motion
\mathcal{B}
.
Using the properties of
G^{\vee}
stated in Section
3.1.3
,
\mathcal{B}_{n}
is a rescaled random walk with i.i.d. steps, and is independent of
\mathcal{K}_{n}
on
\{(x,s;y,t)\in\mathbb{R}^{4}:0<s<t\}
.
We can couple
\mathcal{B}_{n}
with
\mathcal{B}
such that
\mathcal{B}_{n}\to\mathcal{B}
almost surely, uniformly on each compact set, by Skorokhod’s representation theorem.

We show that under this coupling, almost surely, we have
\mathsf{R}_{n}(\Delta_{H,n})\cap([-H,H]\times[g,1])\to\Graph(\vartheta^{\mathcal{B}}_{H})\cap([-H,H]\times[g,1])
.

For each
x\in\mathbb{R}
and
t>0
, we let

\mathcal{L}^{L}_{H,n}(x,t)=\sup_{-H\leq y\leq 0}\lim_{s\searrow 0}\mathcal{K}_{n}(y,s;x,t)+\mathcal{B}_{n}(y),

and

\mathcal{L}^{R}_{H,n}(x,t)=\sup_{0\leq y\leq H}\lim_{s\searrow 0}\mathcal{K}_{n}(y,s;x,t)+\mathcal{B}_{n}(y).

We use the limit
\lim_{s\searrow 0}\mathcal{K}_{n}(y,s;x,t)
(instead of
\mathcal{K}_{n}(y,0;x,t)
) due to the rounding setup in Section
3.1.1
:
under the rouding setup, the limit depends on the values of
\mathcal{K}_{n}
on
\{(x,s;y,t)\in\mathbb{R}^{4}:0<s<t\}
, thus is independent of
\mathcal{B}_{n}
.

From the convergence of
\mathcal{K}_{n}
to
\mathcal{L}
(Theorem
3.2
) and the convergence of
\mathcal{B}_{n}
to
\mathcal{B}
, we have
\mathcal{L}^{L}_{H,n}\to\mathcal{L}^{L}_{H}
and
\mathcal{L}^{R}_{H,n}\to\mathcal{L}^{R}_{H}
, both uniformly in the set
[-2H,2H]\times[g/2,2]
.
From the above constructions and the definitons of
P_{H,n,1}
and
P_{H,n,1}
, we have that

P_{H,n,1}=\{p\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}^{2}\setminus\{\mathbf{0}\}:\mathcal{L}^{L}_{H,n}(\mathsf{R}_{n}(p))-\mathcal{L}^{R}_{H,n}(\mathsf{R}_{n}(p))<0\},

and

P_{H,n,2}=\{p\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}^{2}\setminus\{\mathbf{0}\}:\mathcal{L}^{L}_{H,n}(\mathsf{R}_{n}(p))-\mathcal{L}^{R}_{H,n}(\mathsf{R}_{n}(p))>0\}.

Further, from the definition of
\Graph
, for any
(x,t)\in(-H,H)\times(g,1)
, we have that
\vartheta^{\mathcal{B}}_{H}(t)=x
if
(x,t)\in\Graph(\vartheta^{\mathcal{B}}_{H})
. Take any
\delta>0
small enough (depending on
g
,
H
, and
(x,t)
). By Lemma
5.6
we have
\mathcal{L}^{L}_{H}(x-\delta/2,t)>\mathcal{L}^{R}_{H}(x-\delta/2,t)
and
\mathcal{L}^{L}_{H}(x+\delta/2,t)<\mathcal{L}^{R}_{H}(x+\delta/2,t)
.
By continuity of
\mathcal{L}
, thus of
\mathcal{L}^{L}_{H}
and
\mathcal{L}^{R}_{H}
, and uniform convergence, we have that for any
n
large enough, we can find
(x_{L,n},t_{n})\in\mathsf{R}_{n}(P_{H,n,2})
and
(x_{R,n},t_{n})\in\mathsf{R}_{n}(P_{H,n,1})
, in the
\delta/3
neighborhood of
(x-\delta/2,t)
and
(x+\delta/2,t)
respectively.
Now consider the
\delta
neighborhood of
(x,t)
: its pre-image under
\mathsf{R}_{n}
must contain a lattice path from
\mathsf{R}_{n}^{-1}((x_{L,n},t_{n}))
and
\mathsf{R}_{n}^{-1}((x_{L,n},t_{n}))
.
Since
\Delta_{H,n}
is the boundary of
P_{H,n,1}
and
P_{H,n,2}
, the lattice path must intersect
\Delta_{H,n}
, so there must be a vertex of
\mathsf{R}_{n}(\Delta_{H,n})
in the
\delta
neighborhood of
(x,t)
.
Thus we conclude that
(x,t)
is in any subsequential limit of
\mathsf{R}_{n}(\Delta_{H,n})\cap([-H,H]\times[g,1])
.

If
(x,t)\not\in\Graph(\vartheta^{\mathcal{B}}_{H})
, by Lemma
5.6
, we have
\mathcal{L}^{L}_{H}(x,t)\neq\mathcal{L}^{R}_{H}(x,t)
and without loss of generality we assume that
\mathcal{L}^{L}_{H}(x,t)<\mathcal{L}^{R}_{H}(x,t)
.
Then by continuity of
\mathcal{L}^{L}_{H}
and
\mathcal{L}^{R}_{H}
, and uniform convergence, there is a neighborhood of
(x,t)
, that is disjoint from
\mathsf{R}_{n}(P_{H,n,1})
for any
n
large enough.
Thus
(x,t)
cannot be in any subsequential limit of
\mathsf{R}_{n}(\Delta_{H,n})\cap([-H,H]\times[g,1])
.
This completes the proof. ∎

We next prove that the competition interfaces are with high probability the same as the truncated versions.
We start with the pre-limiting ones, and the arguements are via estimates on transversal fluctuations of semi-infinite geodesics in the
(-1,-1)
direction.

Lemma 5.8
.

There are constants
c,C>0
, such that for any
H>0
and any
n
large enough (depending on
H
), we have
\mathbb{P}[\mathsf{R}_{n}(\Delta_{H,n})\cap([-H,H]\times[0,1])\neq\mathsf{R}_{n}(\Delta)\cap(\mathbb{R}\times[0,1])]<Ce^{-cH}
.

Proof.

Let
p_{1}=(n-\lfloor n^{2/3}H\rfloor,n)
and
p_{2}=(n+\lfloor n^{2/3}H\rfloor,n)
,
and
U_{1}
be the parallelogram whose vertices are
(-2^{5/3}n^{2/3}H,0)
,
\mathbf{0}
, and
(n-2^{5/3}n^{2/3}H,n)
,
(n,n)
,
and
U_{2}=U_{1}+(2^{5/3}n^{2/3}H,0)
. See Figure
10
for an illustration of these objects.

We now consider the events where
\Gamma^{\vee}_{p_{1}}\cap(\mathbb{Z}\times\llbracket 0,n\rrbracket)\subset U_{1}
and
\Gamma^{\vee}_{p_{2}}\cap(\mathbb{Z}\times\llbracket 0,n\rrbracket)\subset U_{2}
.
By Lemma
3.4
these happen with probability at least
1-Ce^{-cH}
, for some constants
c,C>0
.

It now suffices to show that these events imply that
\mathsf{R}_{n}(\Delta_{H,n})\cap([-H,H]\times[0,1])=\mathsf{R}_{n}(\Delta)\cap(\mathbb{R}\times[0,1])
.
Assuming that the events occur,
let
m_{1,*},m_{2,*}\in\mathbb{Z}
such that
(m_{1,*},0),(m_{1,*},1)\in\Gamma^{\vee}_{p_{1}}
, and
(m_{2,*},0),(m_{2,*},1)\in\Gamma^{\vee}_{p_{2}}
.
Then we have

m_{1,*}=\argmax_{m\in\mathbb{Z}}G^{\vee}((m,0))+T_{(m,1),p_{1}},\quad m_{2,*}=\argmax_{m\in\mathbb{Z}}G^{\vee}((m,0))+T_{(m,1),p_{2}}.

Then we would have that
-2^{5/3}n^{2/3}H\leq m_{1,*}\leq 0
and
0<m_{2,*}\leq 2^{5/3}n^{2/3}H
.
We now take
p=(x,y)\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}\times\llbracket 0,n\rrbracket\setminus\{\mathbf{0}\}
that is between
\Gamma^{\vee}_{p_{1}}
and
\Gamma^{\vee}_{p_{2}}
;
i.e. there are
x_{1}\leq x\leq x_{2}
such that
(x_{1},y)\in\Gamma^{\vee}_{p_{1}}
and
(x_{2},y)\in\Gamma^{\vee}_{p_{2}}
.

Let
m_{*}\in\mathbb{Z}
such that
(m_{*},0),(m_{*},1)\in\Gamma^{\vee}_{p}
. Due to the tree structure of the
(-1,-1)
-direction semi-infinite geodesics,
we must have that
m_{1,*}\leq m_{*}\leq m_{2,*}
, and thus
m_{*}\in[-2^{5/3}Hn^{2/3},2^{5/3}Hn^{2/3}]
.
This implies that
p\in P_{H,n,1}
if and only if
p\in P_{1}
, and that
p\in P_{H,n,2}
if and only if
p\in P_{2}
.
Since
\Gamma^{\vee}_{p_{1}}\cap\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}^{2}\setminus\{\mathbf{0}\}\subset P_{1}\cap U_{1}
and
\Gamma^{\vee}_{p_{2}}\cap\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}^{2}\setminus\{\mathbf{0}\}\subset P_{2}\cap U_{2}
, we conclude that
\Delta_{H,n}=\Delta\cap(\mathbb{Z}\times\llbracket 0,n\rrbracket)\subset U_{1}\cup U_{2}
, which implies that
\mathsf{R}_{n}(\Delta_{H,n})\cap([-H,H]\times[0,1])=\mathsf{R}_{n}(\Delta)\cap(\mathbb{R}\times[0,1])
.
∎

\mathbf{0}
p_{1}
p_{2}
U_{1}
U_{2}
\Gamma^{\vee}_{p_{1}}
\Gamma^{\vee}_{p_{2}}
\Delta

Figure 10
:
An illustration of the proof of Lemma
5.8
: if
\Gamma^{\vee}_{p_{1}}
and
\Gamma^{\vee}_{p_{2}}
in
\mathbb{Z}\times\llbracket 0,n\rrbracket
are contained in the parallelograms
U_{1}
and
U_{2}
, respectively, the competition interface
\Delta
(in
\mathbb{Z}\times\llbracket 0,n\rrbracket
) should be between them and equal
\Delta_{H,n}
.

As we have pointed out, unlike for semi-infinite geodesics, it is not immediate that
\vartheta^{\mathcal{B}}_{H}
becomes
\vartheta^{\mathcal{B}}
when
H
is large enough. We prove this result now.

Lemma 5.9
.

Almost surely there is a (random)
H_{0}>0
, such that
\Graph(\vartheta^{\mathcal{B}}_{H})\cap([-H,H]\times[0,1])=\Graph(\vartheta^{\mathcal{B}})\cap(\mathbb{R}\times[0,1])
for any
H>H_{0}
.

Proof.

We let
C
denote a large constant in this proof, whose value may change from line to line.
Let
R_{1}
be given by Lemma
3.9
, and let
R_{2}=\sup_{x}|\mathcal{B}(x)|-|x|
. Let
R=R_{1}\vee R_{2}\vee C
.
For any
x,y\in\mathbb{R}
and
0<t\leq 1
, suppose that
|y|>10|x|\vee 10
, then we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}(0,0;x,t)-\mathcal{L}(y,0;x,t)-\mathcal{B}(y)>

\displaystyle-\frac{x^{2}}{t}+\frac{(x-y)^{2}}{t}-|y|-R

\displaystyle-2Rt^{1/3}\log^{4/3}(2(|x|+|y|+3)^{3/2}/t)\log^{2/3}(|x|+|y|+3)

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\frac{y^{2}-2xy}{t}-|y|-R-2R\log^{2}(2(|x|+|y|+3)^{3/2})

\displaystyle>

\displaystyle\frac{y^{2}}{2}-5R\log^{2}(10(|y|+1)).

Since the last line is positive if
y
is large enough,
this implies that we can find some (random)
H_{1}>0
, such that for any
H>10H_{1}
,
|x|<H_{1}
and
0<t\leq 1
, we have
\mathcal{L}^{L}_{H}(x,t)=\mathcal{L}^{L}(x,t)
and
\mathcal{L}^{R}_{H}(x,t)=\mathcal{L}^{R}(x,t)
.
We also have that for any
x,y\in\mathbb{R}
and
0<t\leq 1
,

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}(x,0;x,t)+\mathcal{B}(x)-\mathcal{L}(y,0;x,t)-\mathcal{B}(y)>

\displaystyle\frac{(x-y)^{2}}{t}-|x|-|y|-2R

\displaystyle-2Rt^{1/3}\log^{4/3}(2(2|x|+|y|+3)^{3/2}/t)\log^{2/3}(2|x|+|y|+3)

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\frac{(x-y)^{2}}{t}-|x|-|y|-2R-2R\log^{2}(2(2|x|+|y|+3)^{3/2})

\displaystyle>

\displaystyle\frac{(x-y)^{2}}{t}-|x|-|y|-5R\log^{2}(10(|x|+|y|+1)).

This implies that we can find some (random)
H_{2}>0
, such that
\mathcal{L}^{L}(x,t)<\mathcal{L}^{R}(x,t)
for any
x>H_{2}
,
0<t\leq 1
; and
\mathcal{L}^{L}(x,t)>\mathcal{L}^{R}(x,t)
for any
x<-H_{2}
,
0<t\leq 1
.
By taking
H_{0}=10H_{1}\vee H_{2}
the conclusion follows.
∎

Now we can deduce Proposition
5.2
, and its proof is similar to the proof of Proposition
5.1
.

Proof of Proposition
5.2
.

Again we just prove for the case where
h=1
, and the case of general
h>0
follows similar arguments.
For any continuous function
f
on the space of all compact subsets of
\mathbb{R}^{2}
with
0\leq f\leq 1
, by Lemma
5.7
we have

\mathbb{E}[f(\mathsf{R}_{n}(\Delta_{H,n})\cap([-H,H]\times[g,1]))]\to\mathbb{E}[f(\Graph(\vartheta^{\mathcal{B}}_{H})\cap([-H,H]\times[g,1]))]

as
n\to\infty
for any
H>0
.
Then by Lemma
5.8
we have

\limsup_{n\to\infty}|\mathbb{E}[f(\mathsf{R}_{n}(\Delta)\cap(\mathbb{R}\times[g,1]))]-\mathbb{E}[f(\Graph(\vartheta^{\mathcal{B}})\cap(\mathbb{R}\times[g,1]))]|\\
\leq Ce^{-cH}+\mathbb{P}[\Graph(\vartheta^{\mathcal{B}}_{H})\cap([-H,H]\times[g,1])\neq\Graph(\vartheta^{\mathcal{B}})\cap(\mathbb{R}\times[g,1])].

As
H\to\infty
the right hand side decays to zero, by Lemma
5.9
. Thus we have
\mathbb{E}[f(\mathsf{R}_{n}(\Delta)\cap(\mathbb{R}\times[g,1]))]\to\mathbb{E}[f(\Graph(\vartheta^{\mathcal{B}})\cap(\mathbb{R}\times[g,1]))]
, and the conclusion follows.
∎

With the above, somewhat long, sequence of preparatory results, we can finally delve in to the proof of Theorem
2.1
.

6
Hausdorff dimension of
\mathsf{NC}
: Proof of Theorem
2.1

Our first observation is that for the difference profile
\mathcal{D}
, being constant in a 2D neighborhood is equivalent to being constant in the space direction. Recall the sets
\overline{\mathsf{NC}}_{t}
and
\mathsf{NC}
from the statement of Theorem
2.1
.

Lemma 6.1
.

Almost surely, we have
\mathsf{NC}\cap(\mathbb{R}\times\{t\})=\overline{\mathsf{NC}}_{t}
for any
t>0
.

(-1,0)
(1,0)
(x_{-},t)
(x_{+},t)
(x,t)
\pi_{-}
\pi_{+}
(x-\delta,x+\delta)\times(t,t+\delta)

Figure 11
:
An illustration of the proof of Lemma
6.1
: we prove that
\mathcal{D}
is flat in the blue region.

The idea is to show that, if
\mathcal{D}
is constant on a segment
(x_{-},x_{+})\times\{t\}
, it would also be constant in a 2D open neighborhood of it, using monotonicity of
\mathcal{D}
and transversal fluctuation estimates of geodesics.

Proof.

The inclusion of
\overline{\mathsf{NC}}_{t}\subset\mathsf{NC}\cap(\mathbb{R}\times\{t\})
is obvious, and we prove the other inclusion now.
Take any
(x,t)\in\mathbb{H}
such that
(x,t)\not\in\overline{\mathsf{NC}}_{t}
, i.e.,
\mathcal{D}(\cdot,t)
is constant in a neighborhood of
x
.
Denote
X=\mathcal{D}(x,t)
.
We can find
x_{-}<x<x_{+}
such that

\mathcal{D}(x_{-},t)=\mathcal{D}(x_{+},t)=X.

We will show that the difference profile is constant in a 2D neighborhood of
(x,t)
.
Although the proof below mainly consists of equations, there is a geometric heuristic behind this, as illustrated by Figure
11
. From the equality, we would expect that the geodesics
\pi_{(-1,0;x_{-},t)}
and
\pi_{(1,0;x_{+},t)}
coalesce, assuming their uniqueness (by Lemma
3.15
). This then implies that for all
p
and
p^{\prime}
in a neighborhood of
(x,t)
,
\pi_{(-1,0;p)}
and
\pi_{(1,0;p^{\prime})}
coalesce, due to transversal fluctuation estimates and the ordering of the geodesics, and this would imply that the difference profile is constant in the neighborhood of
(x,t)
, whose generic point is, say,
(z,s).

We argue separately for
(z,s)
with
s<t
(case (1)) or
s>t
(case (2)).

Case 1.
We let
\pi_{-}
and
\pi_{+}
be any geodesics from
(1,0)
to
(x_{-},t)
, and
(-1,0)
to
(x_{+},t)
respectively.
Take any
0<s<t
with
\pi_{-}(s)<\pi_{+}(s)
.
We then have

\mathcal{D}(\pi_{-}(s),s)=\mathcal{L}(-1,0;\pi_{-}(s),s)-\mathcal{L}(1,0;x_{-},t)+\mathcal{L}(\pi_{-}(s),s;x_{-},t)\leq\mathcal{D}(x_{-},t)=:X

and

\mathcal{D}(\pi_{+}(s),s)=\mathcal{L}(-1,0;x_{+},t)-\mathcal{L}(1,0;\pi_{+}(s),s)-\mathcal{L}(\pi_{+}(s),s;x_{+},t)\geq\mathcal{D}(x_{+},t)=X.

By monotonicity of
\mathcal{D}(\cdot,s)
, for any
z\in(\pi_{-}(s),\pi_{+}(s))
we have

\mathcal{D}(x_{-}(s),s)\geq\mathcal{D}(z,s)\geq\mathcal{D}(x_{+}(s),s),

so
\mathcal{D}(z,s)=X
.
Since the geodesics
\pi_{-}
and
\pi_{+}
are continuous, we conclude that there exists some
\delta>0
, such that for any
t-\delta<s\leq t
and
|z-x|<\delta
, we have
\mathcal{D}(z,s)=X
.

Case 2:

Take any
s\geq t
and
z\in\mathbb{R}
.
Consider any geodesics
\pi_{-}^{\prime}
from
(-1,0)
to
(z,s)
, and
\pi_{+}^{\prime}
from
(1,0)
to
(z,s)
.
We claim that if
\pi_{-}^{\prime}(t),\pi_{+}^{\prime}(t)\in[x_{-},x_{+}]
, we must have
\mathcal{D}(z,s)=X
.
Indeed, assuming that
\pi_{-}^{\prime}(t),\pi_{+}^{\prime}(t)\in[x_{-},x_{+}]
, we have

\mathcal{D}(z,s)\leq\mathcal{D}(\pi_{-}^{\prime}(t),t)\leq\mathcal{D}(x_{-},t)=X,

where the first inequality is by
\mathcal{L}(-1,0;z,s)=\mathcal{L}(-1,0;\pi_{-}^{\prime}(t),t)+\mathcal{L}(\pi_{-}^{\prime}(t),t;z,s)
and
\mathcal{L}(1,0;z,s)\geq\mathcal{L}(1,0;\pi_{-}^{\prime}(t),t)+\mathcal{L}(\pi_{-}^{\prime}(t),t;z,s)
, and the second inequality is by Lemma
3.8
. Similarly we have

\mathcal{D}(z,s)\geq\mathcal{D}(\pi_{+}^{\prime}(t),t)\geq\mathcal{D}(x_{+},t)=X,

so the claim follows.

By Lemma
3.11
, (almost surely) we can find some
\delta>0
, such that for any
t\leq s\leq t+\delta
and
|z-x|<\delta
, and any geodesic
\pi^{\prime}
from
(-1,0)
to
(z,s)
or from
(1,0)
to
(z,s)
, there is
\pi^{\prime}(t)\in[x_{-},x_{+}]
.
Thus by the above claim we have
\mathcal{D}(z,s)=X
(for each
(z,s)\in[t,t+\delta]\times(x-\delta,x+\delta)
) and hence the proof is complete.
∎

We now are in a position to embark on proving Theorem
2.1
.
The upper bound is straightforward, and the arguments are similar to those in
[
BGH21
]
.
The lower bound is significantly more delicate, and we now briefly reiterate our strategy from Section
2.3
.

We define a measure
\zeta
on
\mathbb{H}
, such that for any
a<b
and
0\leq g<h
,

\zeta([a,b]\times(g,h))=\int_{g}^{h}[\mathcal{D}(a,t)-\mathcal{D}(b,t)]dt.

(6.1)

We first observe that the support of
\zeta
is contained in
\mathsf{NC}
, since for any
(x,t)\in\mathbb{H}\setminus\mathsf{NC}
, there is an open neighborhood of
(x,t)
with zero measure under
\zeta
.
We further prove that
\zeta
is not degenerate, i.e.,
\zeta(\mathbb{H})>0
.
In fact, by Lemma
3.9
, as
a\to\infty
we have

\mathcal{D}(-a,t)-\mathcal{D}(a,t)\to\infty

uniformly for
t\in[g,h]
, for any
0<g<h
.
Thus we conclude that

\zeta(\mathbb{H})\geq\lim_{a\to\infty}\zeta([-a,a]\times[g,h])=\infty.

(6.2)

Given the above no-degeneracy of the measure,
to show that
\mathsf{NC}
has Hausdorff dimension at least
5/3
, it now suffices to prove the following “2D Hölder” bound.

Proposition 6.2
.

For any
0<g<h
and
N,\epsilon>0
, almost surely

\sup_{A\subset[-N,N]\times[g,h]}\frac{\zeta(A)}{\diam(A)^{5/3-\epsilon}}<\infty.

As indicated in Section
2.3
, to prove such bound on the measure
\zeta
, we will obtain a “level set decomposition” of it.

6.1
Level set decomposition

We consider “level functions” of the difference profile, as given in (
2.3
); i.e., for each
\ell\in\mathbb{R}
and
t\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
, we take

\vartheta_{\ell}(t)=\inf\{x\in\mathbb{R}:\mathcal{D}(x,t)\leq\ell\}.

Then by continuity of
\mathcal{L}
we have
\mathcal{D}(\vartheta_{\ell}(t),t)=\ell
. Actually, for all but countably many
\ell
, the infimum in the above definition is not needed.

Lemma 6.3
.

For each
\ell\in\mathbb{R},
except for countably many of them, there is a unique
x\in\mathbb{R}
with
\mathcal{D}(x,t)=\ell
for every
t\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
.

Proof.

Suppose that for some
\ell\in\mathbb{R}
there are
t\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
and
x_{1}<x_{2}
, such that
\mathcal{D}(x_{1},t)=\mathcal{D}(x_{2},t)=\ell
.
By monotonicity of
\mathcal{D}(\cdot,t)
, we have
\mathcal{D}(x,t)=\ell
for any
x_{1}\leq x\leq x_{2}
.
Then by Lemma
6.1
, one can find some rational
x^{\prime}
and
t^{\prime}>0
with
\mathcal{D}(x^{\prime},t^{\prime})=\ell
. Thus the conclusion follows.
∎

Another basic property of
\vartheta_{\ell}
is its continuity.

Lemma 6.4
.

For each
\ell\in\mathbb{R}
,
\vartheta_{\ell}
is a continuous function on
\mathbb{R}_{+}
.

As a standard exercise in measure theory, using Lemma
6.4
and the fact that
\ell\mapsto\vartheta_{\ell}(t)
is right continuous for each
t
, one can show that
(\ell,t)\mapsto\vartheta_{\ell}(t)
is a measurable function on
\mathbb{H}
.
Now, for each
\ell\in\mathbb{R}
, we define the occupation measure of
\vartheta_{\ell}(\cdot)
to be denoted
\zeta_{\ell}
on
\mathbb{H}
, such that for any
0\leq g<h
and
a<b
, we have

\zeta_{\ell}([a,b]\times(g,h))=\int_{g}^{h}\mathds{1}[\vartheta_{\ell}(t)\in[a,b]]dt.

(6.3)

Recalling (
6.1
), observing that
\mathcal{D}(a,t)-\mathcal{D}(b,t)=\int\mathds{1}[\vartheta_{\ell}(t)\in[a,b]]d\ell,
using Fubini’s theorem, we have that

\zeta([a,b]\times(g,h))=\int_{g}^{h}\int\mathds{1}[\vartheta_{\ell}(t)\in[a,b]]d\ell dt=\int\int_{g}^{h}\mathds{1}[\vartheta_{\ell}(t)\in[a,b]]dtd\ell=\int\zeta_{\ell}([a,b]\times(g,h))d\ell.

Thus (by standard measurable theory arguments)

\zeta(A)=\int\zeta_{\ell}(A)d\ell

(6.4)

for any measurable
A\subset\mathbb{H}
.
Then to bound
\zeta(A)
, we shall bound an average of
\zeta_{\ell}(A)
over
\ell
.

Before proceeding further we prove the continuity of each
\vartheta_{\ell}
.

Proof of Lemma
6.4
.

We argue by contradiction.
Suppose that
\vartheta_{\ell}
is not continuous at some
t>0
. Denote
x=\vartheta_{\ell}(t)
.
Then there is a sequence
\{t_{k}\}_{k=1}^{\infty}
, such that
t_{k}\to t
while
\vartheta_{\ell}(t_{k})
does not converge to
x
, as
k\to\infty
.
By Lemma
3.9
,
|\vartheta_{\ell}(t_{k})|
are bounded as
k\to\infty
, so by taking a subsequence we can assume that
\lim_{k\to\infty}\vartheta_{\ell}(t_{k})=y
for some
y\neq x
.
Since
\mathcal{D}(\vartheta_{\ell}(t_{k}),t_{k})=\ell
for each
k
, by continuity of
\mathcal{L}
we have
\mathcal{D}(y,t)=\ell
. Thus we have
y>x
.
We take some
x<z<y
.
By Lemma
6.1
, there is some
\delta>0
, such that for any
s\in(t-\delta,t+\delta)
,
\mathcal{D}(z,s)=\ell
.
In particular, we can find some large enough
k
, such that
t_{k}\in(t-\delta,t+\delta)
, and
z<\vartheta_{\ell}(t_{k})
, whereas
\mathcal{D}(z,t_{k})=\ell
.
This contradicts the definition of
\vartheta_{\ell}(t_{k})
.
∎

6.2
Comparison to competition interface

To analyze the measures
\zeta_{\ell}
, we study the level functions
\vartheta_{\ell}
, by connecting them with the competition interface starting from the stationary initial condition.

We start by recalling certain objects. Namely, recall that we let
\mathcal{B}
be a standard two sided Brownian motion,
\mathcal{L}^{L}(x,t)
and
\mathcal{L}^{R}(x,t)
be the left and right weight profiles from
\mathcal{B}
, and that
\vartheta^{\mathcal{B}}
denotes the competition interface (from (
2.4
)).
Recall also the equality in law statement in Proposition
2.8
.
We define
\zeta^{\mathcal{B}}
as the occupation measure for
\vartheta^{\mathcal{B}}
on
\mathbb{H},
i.e., for any
a<b
and
0\leq g<h
,

\zeta^{\mathcal{B}}([a,b]\times(g,h))=\int_{g}^{h}\mathds{1}[\vartheta^{\mathcal{B}}(t)\in[a,b]]dt.

(6.5)

As indicated in Section
2.3
, the main idea is to show that, on an event with positive probability,
\vartheta^{\mathcal{B}}
equals
\vartheta_{\ell}
for some random
\ell
; thus bounds on
\zeta^{\mathcal{B}}
would imply bounds on
\zeta_{\ell}
averaged over
\ell
.
The former would be obtained by comparison with the semi-infinite geodesic
\pi_{\mathbf{0}}
.

The following is a crucial estimate on the occupation measure
\zeta^{\mathcal{B}}
(and is equivalent to Lemma
2.7
).

Lemma 6.5
.

There are universal constants
C,c>0
such that the following is true.
For any closed interval
I\subset\mathbb{R}
and
J\subset\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
, we have

\mathbb{P}[\zeta^{\mathcal{B}}(I\times J)>M\mathscr{L}(I)\mathscr{L}(J)^{1/3}]<Ce^{-cM}

for any
M>0
.

However, it turns out that for later applications, we will need a stronger version (see Lemma
7.5
) of the above. As already mentioned, the proofs are adaptations of arguments appearing previously in
[
SSZ21
]
. For completeness we include the proofs in the Appendix
B
.

In the remainder of this section we finish the proof of Theorem
2.1
.
In Section
6.3
we set up the events where
\vartheta^{\mathcal{B}}
equals
\vartheta_{\ell}
for some random
\ell
.
We prove Proposition
6.2
in Section
6.4
, using Lemma
6.5
, and the relations between
\vartheta^{\mathcal{B}}
and the level functions.
In Section
6.5
we prove the upper bound on the Hausdorff dimension.

Before proceeding to establish the comparison estimates between
\vartheta_{\ell}
and the competition interface
\vartheta^{\mathcal{B}}
in the next section, it might be instructive to point out that such results only hold in a compact set (i.e. away from zero and infinity).
In fact, it is not too difficult to pin down the behaviors near zero (short times) and infinity (large times) respectively. It turns out that for
t
near zero,
\vartheta_{\ell}(t)
is roughly
-\ell t/4
plus an order
t^{4/3}
fluctuation, while as
t\to\infty
,
\vartheta_{\ell}(t)
is linear in
t
with a random slope, as evident from Figure
1
.
See Remark
8.4
below for more details regarding this.

6.3
Spiky boundary behavior

-1-\delta
-1+\delta
1-\delta
1+\delta
U_{g,h,N}
(x_{-},t_{-})
(x_{+},t_{+})
\mathcal{B}

Figure 12
:
The top half of this figure illustrates the event
\Good_{g,h,N,\delta}
, and the bottom half of this figure illustrates the event where
\mathcal{B}
has spikes around
-1
and
1
. These two events imply that for any
p
in the box
U_{g,h,N}
, the optimal paths would land near
-1
or
1
.

We will first establish how one can relate
\vartheta^{\mathcal{B}}
, and
\vartheta_{\ell}
for different
\ell
.
For each
0<g<h
,
N>0
, and
\delta>0
, let
U_{g,h,N}
denote the box
[-N,N]\times[g/2,2h]
. We consider the event
\Good_{g,h,N,\delta}
, where the following natural conditions (with somewhat technical looking quantifiers which the reader could without hesitation pay no attention to on first read) hold.

1.

Coalescence of geodesics to
U_{g,h,N}
: there exist some
t_{-},t_{+}>0
,
x_{-},x_{+}\in\mathbb{R}
, such that for any
x\in(-1-\delta,-1+\delta)
and
q\in U_{g,h,N}
, there is
\pi_{(x,0;q)}(t_{-})=x_{-}
; and for any
x\in(1-\delta,1+\delta)
and
q\in U_{g,h,N}
, there is
\pi_{(x,0;q)}(t_{+})=x_{+}
. See the top half of Figure
12
for an illustration of the event.

2.

Regularity of the directed landscape:
R<\delta^{-0.01}
, where
R
is the maximum of the random variables given by Lemma
3.9
, Lemma
3.10
for
K=([-4N,4N]\times[-1,g/6])\times([-4N,4N]\times[g/3,2h])
, and Lemma
3.11
.

The next result says that the above event holds with high probability as
\delta
tends to
0.

Lemma 6.6
.

For any
0<g<h
,
N>0
, we have
\lim_{\delta\searrow 0}\mathbb{P}[\Good_{g,h,N,\delta}]=1
.

Proof.

We denote the two events as
\mathcal{E}_{1},\mathcal{E}_{2}
respectively.

By Lemmas
3.9
,
3.10
, and
3.11
, we have
\mathbb{P}[\mathcal{E}_{2}]\to 1
as
\delta\searrow 0
.
Thus it remains to analyze
\mathcal{E}_{1}
.
Assuming that
\mathcal{E}_{2}
holds, by Lemma
3.11
, there exists some
Q\in\mathbb{N}
, determined by
g
,
h
,
\delta
,
N
(which are the parameters appearing in the definition of the good event), such that

\pi_{(-1-\delta,0;Q,2h)}(t)>N,\;\pi_{(1+\delta,0;-Q,2h)}(t)<-N

for any
g/2\leq t\leq 2h
.
By Theorem
3.16
, almost surely there is some random
\delta^{\prime}>0
, such that
\pi_{(-1-\delta^{\prime},0;-Q,2h)}
intersects
\pi_{(-1+\delta^{\prime},0;Q,2h)}
, and
\pi_{(1-\delta^{\prime},0;-Q,2h)}
intersects
\pi_{(1+\delta^{\prime},0;Q,2h)}
, and all of these geodesics are unique.
Then we just take
t_{-},x_{-}
such that
\pi_{(-1-\delta^{\prime},0;-Q,2h)}(t_{-})=\pi_{(-1+\delta^{\prime},0;Q,2h)}(t_{-})=x_{-}
, and
t_{+},x_{+}
such that
\pi_{(1-\delta^{\prime},0;-Q,2h)}(t_{+})=\pi_{(1+\delta^{\prime},0;Q,2h)}(t_{+})=x_{+}
.
Thus by the ordering of geodesics (Lemma
3.14
), we have that for any
x\in(-1-\delta^{\prime},-1+\delta^{\prime})
and
q\in U_{g,h,N}
, there is
\pi_{(x,0;q)}(t_{-})=x_{-}
; and for any
x\in(1-\delta^{\prime},1+\delta^{\prime})
and
q\in U_{g,h,N}
, there is
\pi_{(x,0;q)}(t_{+})=x_{+}
.
This implies that
\mathbb{P}[\mathcal{E}_{2}\setminus\mathcal{E}_{1}]\to 0
as
\delta\searrow 0
. Thus the conclusion follows.
∎

We next consider the key event
\ConsDiff_{g,h,N}
which allows us to relate the difference profile rooted at
(\pm 1,0)
to the one corresponding to Brownian initial data. This is defined as follows: there exists a (random) number
\alpha
, such that for any
p\in U_{g,h,N}
, there is

\alpha=\mathcal{D}(p)-(\mathcal{L}^{L}(p)-\mathcal{L}^{R}(p)),

i.e., the two difference profiles are a shift of each other when restricted to
U_{g,h,N}.

As an useful consequence, we have that under
\ConsDiff_{g,h,N}
,

\{(\vartheta_{\alpha}(t),t):t\geq 0\}\cap U_{g,h,N}=\{(\vartheta^{\mathcal{B}}(t),t):t\geq 0\}\cap U_{g,h,N}.

We next come to the crucial result which allows us to go between the level sets of
\mathcal{D}
and
\vartheta^{\mathcal{B}}
.

Lemma 6.7
.

For any
0<g<h
,
N>0
, and any
\delta>0
,
H>0
, there is some
\delta^{\prime}>0
, such that for any interval
I\subset[-H,H]
, and any sampling of
\mathcal{L}
such that
\Good_{g,h,N,\delta}
holds, we have

\mathbb{P}[\ConsDiff_{g,h,N},\alpha\in I\mid\mathcal{L}]>\delta^{\prime}\mathscr{L}(I).

where
\mathscr{L}(\cdot)
denotes the Lebesgue measure.

Re-iterating from Section
2.3
, the proof of this lemma is to force
\mathcal{B}
to have spikes around
-1
and
1
. Thus for any
p
in a box
U_{g,h,N}
, the optimal path to the left half boundary (i.e. the path corresponding to
\mathcal{L}^{L}(p)
) would land near
-1
; and the optimal path to the right half boundary (i.e. the path corresponding to
\mathcal{L}^{R}(p)
) would land near
1
, respectively. See Figure
12
for an illustration. Further, we will show that on the coalescence event
\Good_{g,h,N,\delta}
, the event
\ConsDiff_{g,h,N}
would hold.
Finally, by a simple argument involving resampling
\mathcal{B},
we will also establish that the random difference
\alpha
is independent of the events
\Good_{g,h,N,\delta}
and
\mathcal{B}
being spiky and has a smooth distribution.

-1-\delta
-1+\delta
1-\delta
1+\delta
A/2
-A/2
\mathcal{B}
\mathcal{B}(-1+\delta)+A
\mathcal{B}(-1+\delta)+A+1
\mathcal{B}(1-\delta)+A
\mathcal{B}(1-\delta)+A+1
\mathcal{B}(-1+\delta)+1-x/A
\mathcal{B}(1-\delta)+1+x/A
0

Figure 13
:
An illustration of the events in the proof of Lemma
6.7
: the blue curves are the processes
\mathcal{B}(1-\delta+x)-\mathcal{B}(1-\delta)
and
\mathcal{B}(-1+\delta-x)-\mathcal{B}(-1+\delta)
for
x>0
, which determine the event
\mathcal{E}
. Conditional on these two processes,
\mathcal{B}
within
[-1-\delta,-1+\delta]
is still a two-sided Brownian motion, and determines
\mathcal{E}^{\prime}
.

Proof.

Since
\mathcal{L}
is conditioned on, the only randomness is from the Brownian motion
\mathcal{B}
. The general idea is to make
\mathcal{B}
“spiky” in the intervals
[-1-\delta,-1+\delta]
and
[1-\delta,1+\delta]
, such that for any
p\in U_{g,h,N}
, we have

\mathcal{L}^{L}(p)=\sup_{|y+1|\leq\delta}\mathcal{L}(y,0;p)+\mathcal{B}(y),\quad\mathcal{L}^{R}(p)=\sup_{|y-1|\leq\delta}\mathcal{L}(y,0;p)+\mathcal{B}(y).

(6.6)

Assuming
\Good_{g,h,N,\delta}
, we have that (
6.6
) implies
\ConsDiff_{g,h,N}
.
Indeed, by the coalecence condition of the event
\Good_{g,h,N,\delta}
(as illustrated by Figure
12
, and recall the points
(x_{-},t_{-})
and
(x_{+},t_{+})
there), we have for any
p_{1},p_{2}\in U_{g,h,N}
,

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}^{L}(p_{1})-\mathcal{L}^{L}(p_{2})

\displaystyle=\mathcal{L}(x_{-},t_{-};p_{1})-\mathcal{L}(x_{-},t_{-};p_{2})=\mathcal{L}(-1,0;p_{1})-\mathcal{L}(-1,0;p_{2}),

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}^{R}(p_{1})-\mathcal{L}^{R}(p_{2})

\displaystyle=\mathcal{L}(x_{+},t_{+};p_{1})-\mathcal{L}(x_{+},t_{+};p_{2})=\mathcal{L}(1,0;p_{1})-\mathcal{L}(1,0;p_{2}).

These imply that
\mathcal{D}(p_{1})-(\mathcal{L}^{L}(p_{1})-\mathcal{L}^{R}(p_{1}))=\mathcal{D}(p_{2})-(\mathcal{L}^{L}(p_{2})-\mathcal{L}^{R}(p_{2}))
, so
\ConsDiff_{g,h,N}
holds.
The value of
\alpha
(from the definition of the event
\ConsDiff_{g,h,N}
), can be altered by “adjusting the difference in the heights of the spikes”, i.e., by adding different constants to
\mathcal{B}
in
[-1-\delta,-1+\delta]
and
[1-\delta,1+\delta]
. This is done by decomposing the process
\mathcal{B}
into three independent parts: the processes
\mathcal{B}(1-\delta+x)-\mathcal{B}(1-\delta)
and
\mathcal{B}(-1+\delta-x)-\mathcal{B}(-1+\delta)
for
x>0
, and
\mathcal{B}
in
[-1+\delta,1-\delta]
, and modifying the last part while fixing the first two.
See Figure
13
for an illustration of some of the events we define next.

Take
A
to be a large enough number depending on
g
,
h
,
\delta
,
N
(which are parameters in the definition of the good event) and
H
.
We consider the event
\mathcal{E}
, given by the following conditions:

•

\sup_{x<-1-\delta}\mathcal{B}(x)-\mathcal{B}(-1+\delta)+x/A<1
,

•

\sup_{x>1+\delta}\mathcal{B}(x)-\mathcal{B}(1-\delta)-x/A<1
,

•

\sup_{x\in(-1-\delta,-1+\delta)}\mathcal{B}(x)-\mathcal{B}(-1+\delta)\in[A,A+1]
,

•

\sup_{x\in(1-\delta,1+\delta)}\mathcal{B}(x)-\mathcal{B}(1-\delta)\in[A,A+1]
.

In words, the first two conditions stipulate a bound for the processes
\mathcal{B}(1-\delta+x)-\mathcal{B}(1-\delta)
and
\mathcal{B}(-1+\delta-x)-\mathcal{B}(-1+\delta)
for
x>2\delta
that force them to be essentially bounded by
1
for an interval of size
A
(a large number) while beyond that, not growing faster than the linear function
|x|/A.
Note that the last part can be ensured in fact by any function that grows faster than
\sqrt{x}
.

The third and fourth conditions say that the processes
\mathcal{B}(1-\delta+x)-\mathcal{B}(1-\delta)
and
\mathcal{B}(-1+\delta-x)-\mathcal{B}(-1+\delta)
attain the value
A
in
[0,2\delta]
.
Thus together, the above ensures the spiky behavior we desire.

As indicated above, we also want to adjust the difference in the spike heights and to be able to do that we define
for any
x\in\mathbb{R},t>0
, the following proxies for
\mathcal{L}^{L}(x,t)
and
\mathcal{L}^{R}(x,t)
, by restricting the range of
y
and shifting by the value
\mathcal{B}
at the endpoint. Namely,

\hat{\mathcal{L}}^{L}(x,t)=\sup_{y\leq-1+\delta}\mathcal{L}(y,0;x,t)+\mathcal{B}(y)-\mathcal{B}(-1+\delta),

and

\hat{\mathcal{L}}^{R}(x,t)=\sup_{y\geq 1-\delta}\mathcal{L}(y,0;x,t)+\mathcal{B}(y)-\mathcal{B}(1-\delta).

Observe that
\hat{\mathcal{L}}^{L}
and
\hat{\mathcal{L}}^{R}
are independent of
\mathcal{B}
in
[-1+\delta,1-\delta]
and we will use the randomness in the latter to alter the heights of the spikes.
Recalling the parameter
g
appearing in the definition of
\Good_{g,h,N,\delta},
let
p_{0}=(0,g)
, and define
\alpha^{\prime}
via

\alpha^{\prime}=\mathcal{D}(p_{0})-(\hat{\mathcal{L}}^{L}(p_{0})-\hat{\mathcal{L}}^{R}(p_{0})).

Recalling that
I
is the interval in the statement of Lemma
6.7
, we next consider the event
\mathcal{E}^{\prime}
, where

•

\mathcal{B}(-1+\delta)-\mathcal{B}(1-\delta)\in\alpha^{\prime}-I
;

•

\sup_{|x|\leq 1-\delta}|\mathcal{B}(x)|<A/2
.

Note that for a given
\alpha^{\prime}
, the event
\mathcal{E}^{\prime}
is independent of
\mathcal{E}
.
Using basic properties of Brownian motions, we can find some
\delta^{\prime}>0
, depending on all parameters, such that
\mathbb{P}[\mathcal{E}]>\sqrt{\delta^{\prime}}
; and conditional on
\mathcal{E}
, and any
\mathcal{L}
such that
\Good_{g,h,N,\delta}
holds, we have
\mathbb{P}[\mathcal{E}^{\prime}\mid\mathcal{E},\mathcal{L}]>\sqrt{\delta^{\prime}}\mathscr{L}(I)
.
Here for the second part, we use that
\mathbb{P}[\mathcal{E}^{\prime}\mid\mathcal{E},\mathcal{L},\alpha^{\prime}]=\mathbb{P}[\mathcal{E}^{\prime}\mid\mathcal{L},\alpha^{\prime}]=\mathbb{P}[\mathcal{E}^{\prime}\mid\Good_{g,h,N,\delta},\alpha^{\prime}]
, and that under the event
\Good_{g,h,N,\delta}
,
\alpha^{\prime}-I\subset[-A/2,A/2]
by taking
A
large enough.

Thus we have

\mathbb{P}[\mathcal{E}\cap\mathcal{E}^{\prime}\mid\mathcal{L}]=\mathbb{P}[\mathcal{E}^{\prime}\mid\mathcal{E},\mathcal{L}]\mathbb{P}[\mathcal{E}]>\delta^{\prime}\mathscr{L}(I),

for any
\mathcal{L}
such that
\Good_{g,h,N,\delta}
holds.

It remains to show that
\mathcal{E}\cap\mathcal{E}^{\prime}\cap\Good_{g,h,N,\delta}\subset\ConsDiff_{g,h,N}\cap\{\alpha\in I\}
.
Under the event
\mathcal{E}\cap\mathcal{E}^{\prime}\cap\Good_{g,h,N,\delta}
, since
A
is taken large enough, we have that (
6.6
) holds for any
p\in U_{g,h,N}
. Indeed, for this, we just use the event
\mathcal{E}\cap\mathcal{E}^{\prime}
to lower bound

\sup_{|y+1|\leq\delta}\mathcal{B}(y)
and
\sup_{|y-1|\leq\delta}\mathcal{B}(y)
, and upper bound
\mathcal{B}(y)
for
y\not\in[-1-\delta,-1+\delta]\cup[1-\delta,1+\delta]
; and we use
R<\delta^{-0.01}
(where
R
is the random variable given by Lemma
3.9
) from the event
\Good_{g,h,N,\delta}
to bound
\mathcal{L}(y,0;p)
for any
y\in\mathbb{R}
.
Then (
6.6
) implies
\ConsDiff_{g,h,N}
, as argued at the beginning of this proof. Under
\ConsDiff_{g,h,N}
, the event
\mathcal{E}^{\prime}
implies that
\alpha^{\prime}-\alpha=\mathcal{B}(-1+\delta)-\mathcal{B}(1-\delta)
, so
\alpha\in I
. Thus we conclude that
\mathcal{E}\cap\mathcal{E}^{\prime}\cap\Good_{g,h,N,\delta}\subset\ConsDiff_{g,h,N}\cap\{\alpha\in I\}
.
∎

6.4
Proof of the lower bound

Proposition
6.2
is an immediate consequence of the following result.

Lemma 6.8
.

For any
0<g<h
and
N,\epsilon>0
,
there is a random number
Q
such that the following is true.
For any
k\in\mathbb{N}
and
i\in\llbracket-2^{k},2^{k}-1\rrbracket
,
j\in\llbracket 0,2^{k}-1\rrbracket
we have

\zeta([i2^{-k}N,(i+1)2^{-k}N]\times[j2^{-k}(h-g)+g,(j+1)2^{-k}(h-g)+g])<Q2^{-(5/3-\epsilon)k}.

Let us iterate from Section
2.3
the source of the exponent
5/3
in the right hand side.
By the regularity condition of the directed landscape under the event
\Good_{g,h,N,\delta}
, the measure
\zeta_{\ell}
on a set of size
2^{-k}\times 2^{-k}
is nonzero only for
\ell
in an interval of length (in the order of)
2^{-k/3}
.
Thus to bound
\zeta
on a set of size
2^{-k}\times 2^{-k}
, we just bound an integral of
\zeta_{\ell}
(on the set) for
\ell
in an interval of length
2^{-k/3}
, and take the maximum over all such intervals.
By Lemma
6.7
, (under
\Good_{g,h,N,\delta}
) such a quantity is bounded by
2^{-k/3}
times
