\zeta^{\mathcal{B}}
(on the set of size
2^{-k}\times 2^{-k}
).
Using Lemma
6.5
, we can bound
\zeta^{\mathcal{B}}
(on a set of size
2^{-k}\times 2^{-k}
) by the order of
2^{-4k/3}
, with exponential tails.
These combined would give the power of
5/3
.

Proof.

We let
C,c>0
denote large and small constants depending on
g,h,N,\epsilon
, and their values may change from line to line.
All other constants and parameters in this proof would also depend on
g,h,N,\epsilon
.

For any
\delta>0
, it suffices to prove the result under the event
\Good_{g,h,N,\delta}
.
To control
\zeta
on a dyadic box, for any
\ell,
we let

Q_{\ell}=\sup_{k\in\mathbb{N},i\in\llbracket-2^{k},2^{k}-1\rrbracket,j\in\llbracket 0,2^{k}-1\rrbracket}2^{(4/3-\epsilon/2)k}\zeta_{\ell}([i2^{-k}N,(i+1)2^{-k}N]\times[j2^{-k}(h-g)+g,(j+1)2^{-k}(h-g)+g]).

We note that, as we expect that
\zeta_{\ell}
would assign mass of the order of
2^{-k/3}\times 2^{-k}=2^{-4k/3}
to any dyadic box of size
2^{-k}\times 2^{-k}
, we multiply by the factor of
2^{(4/3-\epsilon/2)k}
to make
Q_{\ell}
to be of an order slightly smaller than
1
.
Now for any
H>0
large enough (depending on
\delta
),
for any
\varepsilon>0
, we denote

J_{\varepsilon}=\sup_{A\subset[-H,H],\mathscr{L}(A)<\delta^{-1}\varepsilon^{1/3}\log^{2/3}(1+\varepsilon^{-1})}\int_{A}Q_{\ell}d\ell,

and

J=\sup_{\varepsilon\in(0,1)}\frac{J_{\varepsilon}}{\varepsilon^{1/3}\log(1+\varepsilon^{-1})}.

(6.7)

The definition of
J_{\varepsilon}
is motivated by the following (above mentioned) heuristic: by continuity estimates of
\mathcal{L}
(ensured by the second condition of
\Good_{g,h,N,\delta}
), for any
k\in\mathbb{N},i\in\llbracket-2^{k},2^{k}-1\rrbracket,j\in\llbracket 0,2^{k}-1\rrbracket
, the measure
\zeta_{\ell}([i2^{-k}N,(i+1)2^{-k}N]\times[j2^{-k}(h-g)+g,(j+1)2^{-k}(h-g)+g])
is non-zero only for
\ell
in an interval of length roughly in the order of
2^{-k/3}
.

This number
J
is essentially (up to a constant factor) the desired
Q
(in the statement of this lemma).
This will be shown in two steps: (1) we bound the upper tail of
\mathds{1}[\Good_{g,h,N,\delta}]J
; (2) we bound
\zeta
on small rectangles, using
J
under the event
\Good_{g,h,N,\delta}
.

Step 1.

We carry out the first step next relying on the following input about the competition interface with stationary initial condition. Let the counterpart of
Q_{\ell}
for the stationary initial condition be defined as

Q^{\mathcal{B}}=\sup_{k\in\mathbb{N},i\in\llbracket-2^{k},2^{k}-1\rrbracket,j\in\llbracket 0,2^{k}-1\rrbracket}2^{(4/3-\epsilon/2)k}\zeta^{\mathcal{B}}([i2^{-k}N,(i+1)2^{-k}N]\times[j2^{-k}(h-g)+g,(j+1)2^{-k}(h-g)+g]).

By applying Lemma
6.5
to the boxes
[i2^{-k}N,(i+1)2^{-k}N]\times[j2^{-k}(h-g)+g,(j+1)2^{-k}(h-g)+g]
, and a union bound over all
k,i,j
, we have

\mathbb{P}[Q^{\mathcal{B}}>M]<C_{1}e^{-c_{1}M},

for any
M>0
, for some constants
c_{1},C_{1}>0
.
Thus by Lemma
6.7
, we have

\mathbb{E}\left[\mathds{1}[\Good_{g,h,N,\delta}]\int_{-H}^{H}\mathds{1}[Q_{\ell}>M]d\ell\right]\leq{\delta^{\prime}}^{-1}\mathbb{P}[Q^{\mathcal{B}}>M]<{\delta^{\prime}}^{-1}C_{1}e^{-c_{1}M}.

(6.8)

where
\delta^{\prime}
appears in the statement of Lemma
6.7
.
We also assume that
\delta^{\prime}\leq\delta
, since otherwise we can repalce
\delta^{\prime}
by
\delta^{\prime}\wedge\delta
.

The next few lines are technical and can be a bit hard to parse, but are used to spell out the algebra needed to translate this expectation bound (
6.8
) into bounds on the tail of
\mathds{1}[\Good_{g,h,N,\delta}]J
.
For any
\varepsilon,M>0,
let

J_{\varepsilon,M}=\sup_{A\subset[-H,H],\mathscr{L}(A)<\delta^{-1}\varepsilon^{1/3}\log^{2/3}(1+\varepsilon^{-1})}\int_{A}\mathds{1}[Q_{\ell}>M]d\ell.

By simply bounding the integrand by
1
, we have

\mathds{1}[\Good_{g,h,N,\delta}]J_{\varepsilon,M}\leq\delta^{-1}\varepsilon^{1/3}\log^{2/3}(1+\varepsilon^{-1}).

(6.9)

On the other hand, since
J_{\varepsilon,M}\leq\int_{-H}^{H}\mathds{1}[Q_{\ell}>M]d\ell
, by (
6.8
) we have

\mathbb{E}[\mathds{1}[\Good_{g,h,N,\delta}]J_{\varepsilon,M}]\leq{\delta^{\prime}}^{-1}C_{1}e^{-c_{1}M}.

(6.10)

We take

E_{\varepsilon}=\begin{cases}\sup_{M}c_{1}^{-1}\log(C_{1}^{-1}\delta^{\prime}J_{\varepsilon,M})+M/2&\text{if }\Good_{g,h,N,\delta}\text{ holds,}\\
0&\text{otherwise.}\end{cases}

which then rewritten yields

\mathds{1}[\Good_{g,h,N,\delta}]J_{\varepsilon,M}\leq{\delta^{\prime}}^{-1}C_{1}e^{-c_{1}M/2+c_{1}E_{\varepsilon}},\;\;\forall M>0.

(6.11)

We next bound the upper tail of
E_{\varepsilon}
(which in particular implies that almost surely it is finite).
Assume that
\Good_{g,h,N,\delta}
holds,
then from (
6.9
) and the fact that
\delta^{\prime}<\delta
, we have
c_{1}^{-1}\log(C_{1}^{-1}\delta^{\prime}J_{\varepsilon,M})+M/2<C
for any
M<1
.
Also, by (
6.10
), and Markov’s inequality, for any
i\in\mathbb{N}
and
M>0
, we have

\mathbb{P}[\{c_{1}^{-1}\log(C_{1}^{-1}\delta^{\prime}J_{\varepsilon,i})+(i+1)/2>M\}\cap\Good_{g,h,N,\delta}]<e^{-c_{1}((i-1)/2+M)}.

Thus by a union bound over
i\in\mathbb{N}
, and noting that
J_{\varepsilon,M}
is non-increasing in
M
, we have
\mathbb{P}[E_{\varepsilon}>M]<Ce^{-c_{1}M}
for any
M>0
.

Denote by
M_{\varepsilon}
the smallest non-negative number such that

{\delta^{\prime}}^{-1}C_{1}e^{-c_{1}M_{\varepsilon}/2+c_{1}E_{\varepsilon}}\leq\delta^{-1}\varepsilon^{1/3}\log^{2/3}(1+\varepsilon^{-1}).

Then we have

\displaystyle\mathds{1}[\Good_{g,h,N,\delta}]J_{\varepsilon}

\displaystyle\leq\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathds{1}[\Good_{g,h,N,\delta}]J_{\varepsilon,M}dM

\displaystyle\leq\delta^{-1}\varepsilon^{1/3}\log^{2/3}(1+\varepsilon^{-1})M_{\varepsilon}+\int_{M_{\varepsilon}}^{\infty}\mathds{1}[\Good_{g,h,N,\delta}]J_{\varepsilon,M}dM

\displaystyle\leq\delta^{-1}\varepsilon^{1/3}\log^{2/3}(1+\varepsilon^{-1})M_{\varepsilon}+\int_{M_{\varepsilon}}^{\infty}{\delta^{\prime}}^{-1}C_{1}e^{-c_{1}M/2+c_{1}E_{\varepsilon}}dM

\displaystyle=\delta^{-1}\varepsilon^{1/3}\log^{2/3}(1+\varepsilon^{-1})M_{\varepsilon}+2c_{1}^{-1}{\delta^{\prime}}^{-1}C_{1}e^{-c_{1}M_{\varepsilon}/2+c_{1}E_{\varepsilon}}

\displaystyle\leq(\delta^{-1}M_{\varepsilon}+2c_{1}^{-1}\delta^{-1})\varepsilon^{1/3}\log^{2/3}(1+\varepsilon^{-1}).

Here the first inequality is by the definition of
J_{\varepsilon}
and
J_{\varepsilon,M}
, the second inequality is by splitting the integral at
M_{\varepsilon}
and (
6.9
), the third inequality is by (
6.11
), and the last inequality is by the definition of
M_{\varepsilon}
.
From the tail of
E_{\varepsilon}
and the relation between
E_{\varepsilon}
and
M_{\varepsilon}
, we conclude that for any
M>0
,

\mathbb{P}[\mathds{1}[\Good_{g,h,N,\delta}]J_{\varepsilon}>M\varepsilon^{1/3}\log^{2/3}(1+\varepsilon^{-1})]<C_{\delta}^{\prime}e^{-c_{\delta}^{\prime}M},

where
C_{\delta}^{\prime},c_{\delta}^{\prime}
are constants depending on
\delta
.
Recall
J
from (
6.7
). By taking the union bound over an exponentially decreasing sequence of
\varepsilon
, and noting that
J_{\varepsilon}
is monotone in
\varepsilon
(for
\varepsilon
small enough),
we conclude that
\mathbb{P}[\mathds{1}[\Good_{g,h,N,\delta}]J>M]<C_{\delta}e^{-c_{\delta}M}
, for any
M>0
, where
C_{\delta},c_{\delta}
are constants depending on
\delta
.

Step 2.

We now implement the second step.
Under the event
\Good_{g,h,N,\delta}
, using its second condition and Lemma
3.9
, we can bound
|\mathcal{D}|
in
[-N,N]\times[g,h]
.
Then as
H
is taken to be large enough depending on
\delta
, we have
\zeta_{\ell}([-N,N]\times[g,h])=0
for any
|\ell|>H
.
Thus for any measurable
A\subset[-N,N]\times[g,h]
, we have
\zeta(A)=\int_{-H}^{H}\zeta_{\ell}(A)d\ell
.
Hence, for any
k\in\mathbb{N}
large enough (depending on
\delta
) and
i\in\llbracket-2^{k},2^{k}-1\rrbracket
,
j\in\llbracket 0,2^{k}-1\rrbracket
we have

\displaystyle\zeta([i2^{-k}N,(i+1)2^{-k}N]\times[j2^{-k}(h-g)+g,(j+1)2^{-k}(h-g)+g])

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{-H}^{H}\zeta_{\ell}([i2^{-k}N,(i+1)2^{-k}N]\times[j2^{-k}(h-g)+g,(j+1)2^{-k}(h-g)+g])d\ell

\displaystyle<

\displaystyle 2^{-(4/3-\epsilon/2)k}2^{-k/3}\log(1+2^{k})J

\displaystyle<

\displaystyle CJ2^{-(5/3-\epsilon)k}.

Here the first inequality is by the definition of
J
, and the following reasoning: under the second condition of
\Good_{g,h,N,\delta}
and using Lemma
3.10
, for any
\ell_{1},\ell_{2}
, if
\zeta_{\ell_{1}}([i2^{-k}N,(i+1)2^{-k}N]\times[j2^{-k}(h-g)+g,(j+1)2^{-k}(h-g)+g])
and
\zeta_{\ell_{2}}([i2^{-k}N,(i+1)2^{-k}N]\times[j2^{-k}(h-g)+g,(j+1)2^{-k}(h-g)+g])
are both nonzero, we must have

|\ell_{1}-\ell_{2}|<C\delta^{-0.01}2^{-k/3}\log^{2/3}(1+2^{k})<\delta^{-1}2^{-k/3}\log^{2/3}(1+2^{k}),

where the second inequality is by taking
\delta
small enough.
Finally, by taking
Q=CJ
the conclusion follows.
∎

This lemma immediately implies Proposition
6.2
. Recall from Section
2.3
the general strategy to prove lower bound for Hausdorff dimension, Proposition
6.2
and (
6.2
) imply that almost surely the Hausdorff dimension of
\mathsf{NC}
is at least
5/3
.

6.5
Proof of the upper bound

We next upper bound the Hausdorff dimension of the set
\mathsf{NC}
. This is a more straightforward argument involving splitting
\mathbb{H}
into boxes, and computing the expected number of boxes intersecting
\mathsf{NC}
.

Proposition 6.9
.

For any
0<g<h
,
N>0
, and any
d>5/3
, almost surely the
d
-dimensional Hausdorff measure of the set
\mathsf{NC}\cap([-N,N]\times[g,h])
is zero.

The following is the key estimate relying on non-coalescence of geodesics similar to the argument in
[
BGH21
]
.

Proposition 6.10
.

For any
0<g<h
and
N>0
, there is a constant
C
, such that for any
g\leq t<t+\epsilon\leq h
and
-N\leq x<x+\epsilon\leq N
, we have

\mathbb{P}[\mathsf{NC}\cap((x,x+\epsilon)\times(t,t+\epsilon))\neq\emptyset]<C\epsilon^{1/3}e^{C|\log(\epsilon)|^{5/6}}.

Before jumping into the proof we take a moment to explain the form of the above bound.
For any geodesic from
(-1,0)
or
(1,0)
to
(x,x+\epsilon)\times(t,t+\epsilon)
, the fluctuation of its intersection with the line
\mathbb{R}\times\{t\}
would be of the order of
\epsilon^{2/3}
.
Thus the event
\mathsf{NC}\cap((x,x+\epsilon)\times(t,t+\epsilon))\neq\emptyset
would roughly imply disjointness of geodesics from
(-1,0)
and
(1,0)
to
(x-\epsilon^{2/3},t)
and
(x+\epsilon^{2/3},t)
, respectively.
Theorem
3.16
implies that the probability is of the order of
\epsilon^{1/3}
, with the extra sub-polynomial factor.

Assuming Proposition
6.10
, the proof of Proposition
6.9
is quick.

Proof of Proposition
6.9
.

Take any large enough
n\in\mathbb{N}
and let

\mathscr{S}_{n}=\{i,j\in\mathbb{Z}:([i/n,(i+1)/n]\times[j/n,(j+1)/n])\cap\mathsf{NC}\cap([-N,N]\times[g,h])\neq\emptyset\}.

By Proposition
6.10
, we have that
\mathbb{E}[|\mathscr{S}_{n}|]<Cn^{5/3}e^{C(\log(n))^{5/6}}
, where
C
is a constant depending only on
g,h,N
.
Taking
0<\alpha<d-5/3
, we then have that

\mathbb{P}[n^{-d}|\mathscr{S}_{n}|>n^{-\alpha}]<Cn^{5/3-d+\alpha}e^{C(\log(n))^{5/6}}.

This implies by the Borel-Cantelli lemma, that almost surely, we can find a random
k_{0}
, such that for any integer
k>k_{0}
,

2^{-dk}|\mathscr{S}_{2^{k}}|\leq 2^{-\alpha k}.

Since the right hand side converges to
0
as
k\to\infty
, and that
\mathsf{NC}\cap([-N,N]\times[g,h])\subset\cup_{i\in\mathscr{S}_{n}}[i/n,(i+1)/n]\times[j/n,(j+1)/n]
, the conclusion follows.
∎

We now finish the proof of Proposition
6.10
.
Recall that for any
x_{1}<x_{2}
,
y_{1}<y_{2}
, and
s<t
,
\Dist_{(x_{1},x_{2})\to(y_{1},y_{2})}^{[s,t]}
denotes the event where
\pi_{(x_{1},s),(y_{1},t)}
and
\pi_{(x_{2},s),(y_{2},t)}
are unique and disjoint.

(-1,0)
(1,0)
(x-\epsilon^{2/3}|\log(\epsilon)|^{5},t)
(x+\epsilon^{2/3}|\log(\epsilon)|^{5},t)
[x,x+\epsilon]\times[t,t+\epsilon]

Figure 14
:
An illustration of the proof of Proposition
6.10
: the events
\mathcal{E}_{1}^{c}
and
\mathcal{E}_{2}^{c}
.

Proof of Proposition
6.10
.

We can assume that
\epsilon
is small enough since otherwise the inequality is obvious by taking the constant large enough.

We consider the following two events.

1.

Let
\mathcal{E}_{1}
be the event
\Dist_{(-1,1)\to(x-\epsilon^{2/3}|\log(\epsilon)|^{5},x+\epsilon^{2/3}|\log(\epsilon)|^{5})}^{[0,t]}
.

2.

Let
\mathcal{E}_{2}
be the event that there is some
y\in[x,x+\epsilon]
and
s\in[t,t+\epsilon]
, such that

|\pi_{(-1,0;y,s)}(t)-x|\vee|\pi_{(1,0;y,s)}(t)-x|>\epsilon^{2/3}|\log(\epsilon)|^{5}.

Under
\mathcal{E}_{1}^{c}\cap\mathcal{E}_{2}^{c}
, we have that (almost surely) the function
(y,s)\mapsto\mathcal{L}(-1,0;y,s)-\mathcal{L}(1,0;y,s)
is constant on
[x,x+\epsilon]\times[t,t+\epsilon]
(see Figure
14
for an illustration).
Indeed, by
\mathcal{E}_{1}^{c}
we can find some
t_{*}\in(0,t)
and
x_{*}
, such that

\pi_{(-1,0;x-\epsilon^{2/3}|\log(\epsilon)|^{5},t)}(t_{*})=\pi_{(1,0;x+\epsilon^{2/3}|\log(\epsilon)|^{5},t)}(t_{*})=x_{*}.

We also assume (the probability
1
event) that the geodesics
\pi_{(-1,0;x-\epsilon^{2/3}|\log(\epsilon)|^{5},t)}
and
\pi_{(1,0;x+\epsilon^{2/3}|\log(\epsilon)|^{5},t)}
are unique.
Then by
\mathcal{E}_{2}^{c}
and the ordering of geodesics (Lemma
3.14
), we must have that

\pi_{(1,0;y,s)}(t_{*})=\pi_{(-1,0;y,s)}(t_{*})=x_{*},

for any
(y,s)\in[x,x+\epsilon]\times[t,t+\epsilon]
.
We then have that

\mathcal{L}(-1,0;y,s)-\mathcal{L}(1,0;y,s)=\mathcal{L}(-1,0;x_{*},t_{*})-\mathcal{L}(1,0;x_{*},t_{*}),

which implies that the left hand side is a constant (in
[x,x+\epsilon]\times[t,t+\epsilon]
).
Thus we have that the event
\mathsf{NC}\cap((x,x+\epsilon)\times(t,t+\epsilon))\neq\emptyset
(almost surely) implies
\mathcal{E}_{1}\cup\mathcal{E}_{2}
.

We next bound the probabilities of these events.
Below we let
C,c>0
denote large and small constants depending on
g,h,N
whose values may change from line to line.
By Theorem
3.16
we have that
\mathbb{P}[\mathcal{E}_{1}]<C\epsilon^{1/3}e^{C|\log(\epsilon)|^{5/6}}
.
For
\mathbb{P}[\mathcal{E}_{2}]
, we take
R
to be the random varialbe given by Lemma
3.11
.
Then
\mathbb{P}[R>|\log(\epsilon)|]<Ce^{-c|\log(\epsilon)|^{9/4}/\log(|\log(\epsilon)|)^{4}}
, and
R\leq|\log(\epsilon)|
implies
\mathcal{E}_{2}^{c}
.
So we have
\mathbb{P}[\mathcal{E}_{2}]<Ce^{-c|\log(\epsilon)|^{9/4}/\log(|\log(\epsilon)|)^{4}}
.
Thus
\mathbb{P}[\mathcal{E}_{1}\cup\mathcal{E}_{2}]<C\epsilon^{1/3}e^{C|\log(\epsilon)|^{5/6}}
, and the conclusion follows.
∎

Having completed our analysis of the two dimensional process
\mathcal{D},
we now shift our focus on one dimensional objects for which, as outlined in Section
2.3
, the arguments are more delicate. We start with GLT and the geodesic zero set.

7
Constructing GLT: Proof of Theorem
2.3

Recall from the discussion following the statement of the theorem that we construct a non-decreasing function
L:\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}\to\mathbb{R}
, which we call GLT, in analogue with the well known BLT.
This
L
induces a (random) measure
\mu
on
\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
, supported on the set
Z
defined in (
2.1
).
We show that almost surely,
L
is
1/3-\epsilon
Hölder for any
\epsilon>0
, and that it is almost surely non-degenerate, i.e.,
L>0
on
\mathbb{R}_{+}
. This implies that the set
Z
has Hausdorff dimension at least
1/3
.
The upper bound of the Hausdorff dimension is easier, by splitting the line
\{(0,t):t\geq 0\}
into segments, and bounding the expected number of segments that intersect with
\pi_{\mathbf{0}}
.

In Section
7.1
we construct
L
.
The Hölder property of
L
is proved in Section
7.2
.
In Section
7.3
we show that
L
is almost surely non-zero.
Finally we prove the Hausdorff dimension upper bound in Section
7.4
.

7.1
Construction: occupation measure and the limit

We recall from (
2.2
) the occupation measure
\nu_{h}
for the geodesic
\pi_{\mathbf{0}}
at height
h
, where for any measurable
A\subset\mathbb{R}

\nu_{h}(A)=\int_{0}^{h}\mathds{1}[\pi_{\mathbf{0}}(t)\in A]dt.

(7.1)

The function
L
will be constructed as in the classical case of Brownian motion by showing the existence of the density of
\nu_{h}
at
0
.
For this, we prove Proposition
2.4
, which asserts that almost surely the limit
\lim_{w\searrow 0}(2w)^{-1}\nu_{h}([-w,w])
exists and is finite. In fact, in the forthcoming work
[
GZ22
]
, we will show that properly normalized counting measure on the zero set of the geodesic in the discrete prelimiting model of Exponential LPP converges to
L,
similar to how it is classically known that the random walk local time converges to BLT.

Reiterating the outline from Section
2.3
,
note that by Lebesgue’s theorem for the differentiability of monotone functions, for any (locally finite) measure on
\mathbb{R},
its density exists almost everywhere. However, for our purposes we would need it to exist at
0.
To ensure this we will construct the occupation measure
\nu_{h}^{\prime}
of semi-infinite geodesics starting from random points. The latter has the advantage of being translation invariant which is enough to imply that almost surely its density exists at a fixed point.
We will then use coalescence of geodesics to compare
\nu_{h}
and
\nu_{h}^{\prime}
, and deduce the same for
\nu_{h}
.

We start by recording the following bound on the number of intersections of semi-infinite geodesics with a line segment in the space direction.

Lemma 7.1
.

For any
t>0
, and interval
A\subset\mathbb{R}
, we have

\mathbb{E}[|\{\pi_{(x,0)}(t):x\in\mathbb{R},\pi_{(x,0)}\text{ is unique}\}\cap A|]<C\mathscr{L}(A)t^{-2/3},

where
C
is a universal constant.

We start by briefly explaining the occurrence of the factor of
t^{-2/3}
: by the KPZ scaling (of the directed landscape
\mathcal{L}
), we expect
\pi_{(x,0)}
for
x
varying in an interval of length (of the order of)
t^{2/3}
to coalesce before height
t
.
Thus there would be constant many points in
\{\pi_{(x,0)}(t):x\in\mathbb{R},\pi_{(x,0)}\text{ is unique}\}
, in an interval of length
t^{2/3}
.

Next we prove Proposition
2.4
assuming Lemma
7.1
.

\xi

(a)

Semi-infinite geodesics starting from
\xi\times\{0\}
.

\xi
-a
\mathbf{0}
a
-a^{-1}
a^{-1}

(b)

\pi_{\mathbf{0}}
and semi-infinite geodesics starting from
\xi\times\{0\}
, conditional on
\mathcal{E}_{a}
.

Figure 15
:
An illustration of the proof of Proposition
2.4
.

Proof of Proposition
2.4
.

The proof goes in two steps: in the first step we prove that the density of
\nu_{h}-\nu_{g}
exists at
0
for any
0<g<h
, using a translation invariant argument; in the second step we bound
\limsup_{w\searrow 0}(2w)^{-1}\nu_{g}([-w,w])
, and show that it decays to zero as
g\searrow 0
.

Step 1.

We start with the first step, which is illustrated by Figure
15
.
Let
\xi\subset\mathbb{R}
be a Poisson point process with rate
1
. We consider the measure
\nu_{h}^{\prime}
such that for any measurable
A\subset\mathbb{R}
,

\nu_{h}^{\prime}(A)=\int_{0}^{h}\sum_{x\in\xi}\mathds{1}[\pi_{(x,0)}(t)\in A]dt.

Recall that for any (locally finite) measure, the density exists and is finite Lebesgue almost everywhere, by Lebesgue’s theorem for the differentiability of monotone functions.
We next show that
\nu_{h}^{\prime}
is locally finite.
Note that under the probability one event where
\pi_{(x,0)}
is unique for any
x\in\xi
, we have
\nu_{h}^{\prime}(A)\leq\int_{0}^{h}|\{\pi_{(x,0)}(t):x\in\mathbb{R},\pi_{(x,0)}\text{ is unique}\}\cap A|dt
. So by Lemma
7.1
we have that almost surely
\nu_{h}^{\prime}(A)<\infty
for any compact
A
.
Then if we let
E
be the set where such limit
\lim_{w\searrow 0}(2w)^{-1}\nu_{h}^{\prime}([x-w,x+w])
does not exist or is infinite, we would have that
E
is Lebesgue measurable
1
1

1

The fact that
E
is measurable can be argued as follows: for each
w>0
, the function
x\mapsto(2w)^{-1}\nu_{h}^{\prime}([x-w,x+w])
is continuous except for countably many points, thus it is measurable. Then
E
is measurable, since it is the set where a sequence of measurable functions does not converge or converges to infinity.
with
\mathscr{L}(E)=0
.
By translation invariance of the law of
\nu_{h}^{\prime}
and Fubini’s theorem, we have
\mathbb{P}[0\in E]=\mathbb{E}[\mathscr{L}(E\cap[0,1])]=0
.
Thus we have that almost surely the limit
\lim_{w\searrow 0}(2w)^{-1}\nu_{h}^{\prime}([-w,w])
exists and is finite.

We next compare
\nu^{\prime}
and
\nu.
Take
0<g<h
.
For
0<a<1
, consider the following event

\mathcal{E}_{a}:=\{|\xi\cap[-a,a]|=|\xi\cap[-a^{-1},a^{-1}]|=1\}.

The idea being that on
\mathcal{E}_{a},
the presence of only a single point near the origin would allow us to carry out the desired comparison.
Since
\mathbb{P}[\mathcal{E}_{a}]>0
,
conditional on
\mathcal{E}_{a}
we still have that almost surely the limit
\lim_{w\searrow 0}(2w)^{-1}(\nu_{h}^{\prime}([-w,w])-\nu_{g}^{\prime}([-w,w]))
exists and is finite.

We note that, almost surely, as
x\to 0
, the geodesic
\pi_{(x,0)}\to\pi_{\mathbf{0}}
in the overlap topology (by Lemma
4.12
).
Also, by Lemma
4.8
and the Borel-Cantelli lemma, we have that almost surely
|\pi_{(x,0)}(h)|\to\infty
as
|x|\to\infty
.
Then by Lemma
3.11
, and ordering of finite geodesics, we further have that almost surely
\min_{t\in[0,h]}|\pi_{(x,0)}(t)|\to\infty
as
|x|\to\infty
.
Hence, given
0<g<h
, almost surely for all small enough
a
, on
\mathcal{E}_{a}
,

\nu_{h}([-w,w])-\nu_{g}([-w,w]=\nu^{\prime}_{h}([-w,w])-\nu^{\prime}_{g}([-w,w].

Thus we have that (almost surely for the directed landscape) the limit
\lim_{w\searrow 0}(2w)^{-1}(\nu_{h}([-w,w])-\nu_{g}([-w,w]))
exists and is finite.

Step 2.

We next carry out the second step, i.e., to bound
\limsup_{w\searrow 0}(2w)^{-1}\nu_{g}([-w,w])
.
The idea is to bound
\nu_{g}
by the occupation measure of the union of
\pi_{(x,0)}

for all

x\in\mathbb{R}
, and to apply Lemma
7.1
.
We begin by denoting by
\nu_{g}^{\prime\prime}
the measure such that for any Borel measurable set
A,

\nu_{g}^{\prime\prime}(A)=\int_{0}^{g}|\{\pi_{(x,0)}(t):x\in\mathbb{R},\pi_{(x,0)}\text{ is unique}\}\cap A|dt.

From this definition, almost surely
\nu_{g}(A)\leq\nu^{\prime\prime}_{g}(A)
for any compact
A
. Also, for compact
A
, by Lemma
7.1
we have
\mathbb{E}[\nu_{g}^{\prime\prime}(A)]<\infty
, so almost surely we have
\nu_{g}^{\prime\prime}(A)<\infty
.
Since the density of
\nu_{g}^{\prime\prime}
exists Lebesgue almost everywhere, by translation invariance of the law of
\nu_{g}^{\prime\prime}
, we have that almost surely
\lim_{w\searrow 0}(2w)^{-1}\nu_{g}^{\prime\prime}([-w,w])
exists.
Assuming this limit exists, we have

\limsup_{w\searrow 0}(2w)^{-1}\nu_{g}([-w,w])\leq\lim_{w\searrow 0}(2w)^{-1}\nu_{g}^{\prime\prime}([-w,w]).

We next bound the upper tail of this limit.
Let
E_{z}\subset\mathbb{R}
consist of all
x
, where
\lim_{w\searrow 0}(2w)^{-1}\nu_{g}^{\prime\prime}([x-w,x+w])
exists and is at least
z
.
Then
E_{z}
is measurable, by the same arguments as proving that
E
is measurable (see the footnote above).
Since the law of
\nu_{g}^{\prime\prime}
is translation invariant, we have
\mathbb{P}[0\in E_{z}]=(2l)^{-1}\mathbb{E}[\mathscr{L}(E_{z}\cap[-l,l])]
for any
l>0
.
We also have
2
2

2

The first inequality is by the following argument: for any
w>0
, we have

\nu_{g}^{\prime\prime}([-l-w,l+w])\geq\int_{-l}^{l}(2w)^{-1}\nu_{g}^{\prime\prime}([x-w,x+w])dx
.
Then the inequality follows by sending
w\to 0
and using Fatou’s lemma for the functions
x\mapsto(2w)^{-1}\nu_{g}^{\prime\prime}([x-w,x+w])
.

\nu_{g}^{\prime\prime}([-l,l])\geq\int_{E_{z}\cap[-l,l]}\lim_{w\searrow 0}(2w)^{-1}\nu_{g}^{\prime\prime}([x-w,x+w])dx\geq z\mathscr{L}(E_{z}\cap[-l,l]),

so

\mathbb{P}[0\in E_{z}]\leq(2l)^{-1}z^{-1}\mathbb{E}[\nu_{g}^{\prime\prime}([-l,l])]<Cg^{1/3}z^{-1}

for some constant
C>0
.
Thus we conclude that

\mathbb{P}[\limsup_{w\searrow 0}(2w)^{-1}\nu_{g}([-w,w])>z]<Cg^{1/3}z^{-1}.

By the Borel-Cantelli lemma, this implies that as
g\searrow 0
,
\limsup_{w\searrow 0}(2w)^{-1}\nu_{g}([-w,w])\to 0
almost surely. Thus we get Proposition
2.4
.
∎

It remains to prove Lemma
7.1
.

Proof of Lemma
7.1
.

Recall that to justify the factor of
t^{-2/3}
, we would need to prove coalescence of semi-infinite geodesics, and for that we rely on Lemma
3.12
.

By scaling invariance it suffices to prove this result for
t=1
; and by translation invariance it suffices to consider a fixed interval
A=[-1,1]
;
i.e. we just need to show that
\mathbb{E}[|\{\pi_{(x,0)}(1):x\in\mathbb{R},\pi_{(x,0)}\text{ is unique}\}\cap[-1,1]|]
is finite.
As throughout the article, below we use
C,c>0
to denote large and small constants whose values may change from line to line.

Take
m\in\mathbb{N}
and we consider
\pi_{(-m,0)}
and
\pi_{(m,0)}
.
By passing Lemma
3.4
to the limit using Proposition
5.1
, we have that

\mathbb{P}[\sup_{s\in[0,2]}|\pi_{(-m,0)}(s)+m|>m/2]=\mathbb{P}[\sup_{s\in[0,2]}|\pi_{(m,0)}(s)-m|>m/2]<Ce^{-cm^{3}}.

(7.2)

By the ordering of semi-infinite geodesics (Lemma
4.9
), when
\sup_{s\in[0,2]}|\pi_{(-m,0)}(s)+m|\leq m/2
and
\sup_{s\in[0,2]}|\pi_{(m,0)}(s)-m|\leq m/2
, we must have that
\sup_{s\in[0,2],x\in[-m,m]}|\pi_{(x,0)}(s)|\leq 3m/2
, and hence

|\{\pi_{(x,0)}(1):x\in\mathbb{R},\pi_{(x,0)}\text{ is unique}\}\cap[-1,1]|\\
\leq|\{\pi_{(x,0),(y,2)}(1):|x|,|y|\leq 3m/2,\pi_{(x,0),(y,2)}\text{ is unique}\}\cap[-1,1]|.

Then by (
7.2
) and Lemma
3.12
, we have that
|\{\pi_{(x,0)}(1):x\in\mathbb{R},\pi_{(x,0)}\text{ is unique}\}\cap[-1,1]|
has a stretched exponential tail, so the conclusion holds.
∎

We record the following useful immediate corollary.

Corollary 7.2
.

For any
t>0
and interval
A\subset\mathbb{R}
, we have

\mathbb{P}[\pi_{\mathbf{0}}(t)\in A]<C\mathscr{L}(A)t^{-2/3}
, where
C
is a universal constant.

Such an estimate in the pre-limit has appeared as
[
BB21
, Theorem 2]
where the proof also uses translation invariance of the models.

We finish this subsection by recording the following absolute continuity property of the occupation measure
\nu_{h}
, which will be used later.

Proposition 7.3
.

Almost surely the measure
\nu_{h}
is absolutely continuous to the Lebesgue measure for any
h>0
.

The proof is similar to the one showing absolute continuity for the Brownian occupation measure, in studying BLT (see e.g.,
[
MP10
, Theorem 3.25]
).

Proof.

Since
\nu_{h}
is monotone in
h
, it suffices to prove this for fixed
h>0
.
By an equivalent definition of absolute continuity, we need to show that almost surely, for
\nu_{h}
-almost every
x\in\mathbb{R}
, we have

\liminf_{w\searrow 0}\frac{\nu_{h}([x-w,x+w])}{w}<\infty.

We take arbitrary
H>0
, and by Fatou’s lemma we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\int_{-H}^{H}\liminf_{w\searrow 0}\frac{\nu_{h}([x-w,x+w])}{w}d\nu_{h}(x)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\liminf_{w\searrow 0}w^{-1}\mathbb{E}\int_{-H}^{H}\nu_{h}([x-w,x+w])d\nu_{h}(x)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\liminf_{w\searrow 0}w^{-1}\int_{0}^{h}\int_{0}^{h}\mathbb{P}[|\pi_{\mathbf{0}}(t)|<H,|\pi_{\mathbf{0}}(t)-\pi_{\mathbf{0}}(s)|<w]dtds.

For each
i\in\mathbb{Z}
, we have

\int_{0}^{h}\int_{0}^{h}\mathbb{P}[\pi_{\mathbf{0}}(t),\pi_{\mathbf{0}}(s)\in[(i-1)w,(i+1)w]]dtds=\mathbb{E}[\nu_{h}([(i-1)w,(i+1)w])^{2}]\leq Cw^{2},

where
C>0
is a constant depending on
h,H
, and the inequality is by Lemma
6.5
.
Then we have

\int_{0}^{h}\int_{0}^{h}\mathbb{P}[|\pi_{\mathbf{0}}(t)|<H,|\pi_{\mathbf{0}}(t)-\pi_{\mathbf{0}}(s)|<w]dtds\\
\leq\sum_{i\in\mathbb{Z}:w(|i|-1)\leq H}\int_{0}^{h}\int_{0}^{h}\mathbb{P}[\pi_{\mathbf{0}}(t),\pi_{\mathbf{0}}(s)\in[(i-1)w,(i+1)w]]dtds\leq(2\lfloor H/w\rfloor+3)Cw^{2}<3CHw

when
w
is small enough.
Thus we conclude that
\nu_{h}
is absolutely continuous with respect to Lebesgue measure.
∎

7.2
1/3
Hölder regularity of GLT

By Proposition
2.4
, almost surely we can define
L(h)=\lim_{w\searrow 0}(2w)^{-1}\nu_{h}([-w,w])
for any non-negative rational
h
.
The following Hölder estimate of
L
in particular implies continuity and will allow us to extend
L
to
\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
, and conclude that
L
is almost surely
\frac{1}{3}-\epsilon
Hölder for any
\epsilon>0
.

Proposition 7.4
.

There exist constants
c,C>0
such that the following is true.
For any
g,h\in\mathbb{Q}
,
0\leq g<h
, and
M>0
, we have

\mathbb{P}[L(h)-L(g)>M(h-g)^{1/3}]<Ce^{-cM}.

In fact, we will prove a more general estimate.
Namely, for some intervals
I,J
, we will obtain a uniform bound on the occupation measure in the set
I\times J
, for any geodesic whose endpoints are at the boundary of
I\times J
.
It would imply both Proposition
7.4
and Lemma
6.5
, and have many other applications in the remainder of this article.

For any
u=(p;q)=(x,s;y,t)\in\mathbb{R}^{4}_{\uparrow}
, recall that
\pi_{u}
denotes any geodesic from
p=(x,s)
to
q=(y,t)
. For any interval
I
we define

W[u,I]=\int_{s}^{t}\mathds{1}[\pi_{u}(r)\in I,\pi_{u}\text{ is unique}]dr.

In words,
W[u,I]
is the total amount of time
\pi_{u}
spends in
I
, if
\pi_{u}
is the unique geodesic; and
W[u,I]=0
otherwise.

For any intervals
I,J
, we denote

\Phi_{I,J}=\{(p;q)\in\mathbb{R}^{4}_{\uparrow}:p,q\in\partial(I\times J)\},

(7.3)

where
\partial(I\times J)
denotes the boundary of
I\times J
, i.e.,
\partial(I\times J)=(\{\inf I,\sup I\}\times J)\cup(I\times\{\inf J,\sup J\})
,
and

W_{I,J}=\sup_{u\in\Phi_{I,J}}W[u,I].

We note that
W_{I,J}
is also an upper bound for the occupation measure in
I\times J
, for any unique geodesic
\pi_{u}
, where
u=(p;q)=(x,s;y,t)\in\mathbb{R}^{4}_{\uparrow}
with
p,q\not\in I\times J
. Here this occupation measure is defined as

\int_{[s,t]\cap J}\mathds{1}[\pi_{u}(r)\in I]dr.

Indeed, if the graph of
\pi_{u}
intersects
I\times J
, the above expression equals
W[(p^{\prime};q^{\prime}),I]
, for
p^{\prime},q^{\prime}
being the first and last intersection of the graph of
\pi_{u}
with
I\times J
. Since
\pi_{u}
is unique,
\pi_{(p^{\prime};q^{\prime})}
must also be unique, so
W[(p^{\prime};q^{\prime}),I]\leq W_{I,J}
.
Similarly, for any
p\not\in I\times J
, if
\pi_{p}
is unique, its occupation measure in
I\times J
is also upper bounded by
W_{I,J}
. We now state the following uniform estimate.

Lemma 7.5
.

There are universal constants
C,c>0
, such that for any
w,M>0
we have

\mathbb{P}[W_{[-w,w],[0,1]}>Mw]<Ce^{-cM}.

Its proof follows induction arguments that are essentially the same as those in
[
SSZ21
, Section 3]
, where a version for exponential LPP is proved.
We provide the proof adapted to the directed landscape setting in Appendix
B
.

7.3
Non-degeneracy of GLT

The goal of this section is to show that
L
is strictly positive on
\mathbb{R}_{+}
.

Proposition 7.6
.

Almost surely,
L(h)>0
for any
h>0
.

Before proving the above, we quickly prove the lower bound of the Hausdorff dimension.

Proof of Theorem
2.3
: lower bound.

By Proposition
7.4

L
is continuous, and we can define a measure
\mu
on
\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
, via
\mu([g,h])=L(h)-L(g)
for any
0\leq g<h
.
We next claim that
\mu
is supported on
Z
(although we believe that the support is exactly equal to
Z,
the proof of this unfortunately still eludes us).
Indeed, as
\pi_{\mathbf{0}}
is continuous, for any
t\in\mathbb{R}_{+}\setminus Z
we can find some
\delta>0
with
|\pi_{\mathbf{0}}(s)|>\delta
for any
|s-t|\leq\delta
.
Thus
\nu_{t+\delta}([-\delta,\delta])-\nu_{t-\delta}([-\delta,\delta])=0
, and hence
L(t+\delta)=L(t-\delta)
, leading to
\mu([t-\delta,t+\delta])=0
.

Take any
h,\epsilon>0
.
By Proposition
7.6
we have (almost surely)
\mu([0,h])>0
.
By Proposition
7.4
, (almost surely) there is a constant
C>0
such that
L([a,b])<C(b-a)^{1/3-\epsilon}
for any
0\leq a<b\leq h
.
Hence by the general strategy from Section
2.3
, the Hausdorff dimension of
Z\cap[0,h]
is at least
1/3-\epsilon
.
Since
h,\epsilon
are arbitrary, the Hausdorff dimension of
Z
is at least
1/3
.
∎

The proof of Proposition
7.6
is via the monotonicity and scaling invariance of the function
L
, and the following 0-1 law of the tail sigma algebra of the directed landscape.

For any
r\in\mathbb{N},
we let
\mathcal{F}_{\geq r}
be the sigma algebra generated by
\mathcal{L}(x,s;y,t)
for
r\leq s<t
, and let
\mathcal{F}_{\infty}=\cap_{r>0}\mathcal{F}_{\geq r}
.

Lemma 7.7
.

Any
\mathcal{F}_{\infty}
-measurable event has probability
0
or
1
.

Proof.

This follows from the definition of
\mathcal{F}_{\infty}
. For any
(x,s;y,t)\in\mathbb{R}^{4}_{\uparrow}
, and
r>t
,
\mathcal{L}(x,s;y,t)
is independent of
\mathcal{F}_{\geq r}
.
This implies that
\mathcal{L}
is independent of
\mathcal{F}_{\infty}
. So any
\mathcal{F}_{\infty}
-measurable event is independent of itself, thus has probability
0
or
1
.
∎

Proof of Proposition
7.6
.

By scaling invariance and monotonicity of the function
L
, it suffices to show that
\mathbb{P}[L(1)>0]=1
.
We first show that
\mathbb{P}[L(1)>0]>0
.
For this it suffices to show that
\mathbb{E}[L(1)]>0
.
We note that by Lemma
7.5
, the random variables
(2w)^{-1}\nu_{1}([-w,w])
for all small enough
w
are uniformly integrable.
This implies that
\mathbb{E}[(2w)^{-1}\nu_{1}([-w,w])]\to\mathbb{E}[L(1)]
as
w\searrow 0
; so it suffices to lower bound
\mathbb{E}[(2w)^{-1}\nu_{1}([-w,w])]
.

By Lemma
7.8
below and scaling invariance, we have that
\mathbb{P}[\pi_{\mathbf{0}}(t)\in[-w,w]]>cw
for any
0\leq t\leq 1
; and by integrating over
t
we get
\mathbb{E}[(2w)^{-1}\nu_{1}([-w,w])]>c/2
for any
0<w<1
. We thus conclude that
\mathbb{E}[L(1)]>0
and hence
\mathbb{P}[L(1)>0]>0
.

Now suppose that
\mathbb{P}[L(1)>0]=\alpha
for some
0<\alpha<1
.
We then claim that conditional on
L(1)>0
, we have
\lim_{h\to\infty}L(h)-L(g)>0
for any
g>0
.
Indeed, we take

g_{*}=\inf\{g>0:L(h)=L(g),\forall h>g\}\cup\{\infty\}.

By scaling invariance of
L
, the probability
\mathbb{P}[g_{*}>g]
is same for any
g>0
; thus we have
\mathbb{P}[g<g_{*}\leq h]=0
for any
0<g<h
, so
\mathbb{P}[0<g_{*}<\infty]=0
. Then conditional on
L(1)>0
we have that
g_{*}=\infty
almost surely.
Also by scaling invariance we have that
\mathbb{P}[L(h)>0]=\alpha
for any
h>0
, so by monotonicity of
L
we have that conditional on
L(1)>0
, almost surely
L(h)>0
for any
h>0
, and
g_{*}=\infty
.
Then we conclude that the events
L(1)>0
and
g_{*}=\infty
are almost surely equivalent; i.e., the probability of exactly one of them happening equals zero.

We will now show
g_{*}=\infty
happens almost surely.
Towards this for any
r\in\mathbb{N}
and
h>0
, we denote
\nu_{h}^{r}
as the occupation measure for
\pi_{(0,r)}
at height
h+r
; i.e., for any measurable
A\subset\mathbb{R}
, we let

\nu_{h}^{r}(A)=\int_{0}^{h}\mathds{1}[\pi_{(0,r)}(r+t)\in A]dt,

and we let
L^{r}(h)=\lim_{w\searrow 0}(2w)^{-1}\nu_{h}^{r}([-w,w])
. Almost surely this limit exists and is finite, by Proposition
2.4
and translation invariance.
Then we can define
g_{*}^{r}=\inf\{g>0:L^{r}(h)=L^{r}(g),\forall h>g\}\cup\{\infty\}
.
By the coalescence of semi-infinite geodesics (Lemma
4.14
), we have that
g_{*}=\infty
is almost surely equivalent to
g_{*}^{r}=\infty
, thus to
L^{r}(1)>0
, for any
r\in\mathbb{N}
.
Thus we can consider the
\mathcal{F}_{\infty}
measurable event
|\{r\in\mathbb{N}:L^{r}(1)>0\}|=\infty
.
By the arguments above, this is almost surely equivalent to
L(1)>0
.
These imply that
\mathbb{P}[|\{r\in\mathbb{N}:L^{r}(1)>0\}|=\infty]=\alpha
.
By Lemma
7.7
, this contradicts with the fact that
0<\alpha<1
, so we must have that
\mathbb{P}[L(1)>0]=1
.
∎

It remains to prove the following technical result.

Lemma 7.8
.

For any
0<w<1
we have
\mathbb{P}[\pi_{\mathbf{0}}(1)\in[-w,w]]>cw
, where
c>0
is a universal constant.

The matching upper bound for this probability is already given by Corollary
7.2
. A similar lower bound in the pre-limit is given by
[
BB21
, Theorem 2]
and our arguments are similar.

Unlike the upper bound, such a lower bound does not directly follow from translation invariance. In addition, we need to use the ordering of geodesics, and some additional geometric arguments.

\mathbf{0}
(-a,0)
(a,0)
(\pi_{(-a,0)}(1),1)
(\pi_{(a,0)}(1),1)

Figure 16
:
An illustration for the proof of Lemma
7.8
: consider
\pi_{(ai/n,0)}(1)
for each
i\in\llbracket-n,n\rrbracket
; assuming
\pi_{(-a,0)}(1)>-a
and
\pi_{(a,0)}(1)<a
, there must exist an
i
with
|\pi_{(ai/n,0)}(1)-ai/n|\leq a/n
(such semi-infinite geodesic
\pi_{(ai/n,0)}
is colored blue). Then
\mathbb{P}[\pi_{(-a,0)}(1)>-a,\pi_{(a,0)}(1)<a]>0
implies that
\mathbb{P}[\pi_{\mathbf{0}}(1)\in[-a/n,a/n]]
is at least in the order of
1/n
.

Proof.

We claim that there is some
a\in[1,2]
such that

\mathbb{P}[\pi_{(-a,0)}(1)>-a,\pi_{(a,0)}(1)<a]>0.

(7.4)

We note that here
[1,2]
is chosen arbitrarily and can be replaced by any closed interval in
\mathbb{R}_{+}
.
We show this by contradiction.
Using Corollary
7.2
, and translation and scaling invariance of
\mathcal{L}
, we have that almost surely
\pi_{(x,0)}(t)\neq x
for any
x,t\in\mathbb{Q}
.
If the claim is not true, then by translation and scaling invariance, almost surely the following is true: for any
x,y,t\in\mathbb{Q}
, with
t>0
and
y-x\in[2,4]
, we have
(\pi_{(x,0)}(t)-x)(\pi_{(y,0)}(t)-y)>0
.
By using this repeatedly, we have that almost surely,
\pi_{\mathbf{0}}(t)(\pi_{(x,0)}(t)-x)>0
for any
x,t\in\mathbb{Q}
,
t>0
.
Consider
\pi_{\mathbf{0}}
and
\pi_{(1,0)}
: there is some (random)
t_{0}>0
such that
\pi_{\mathbf{0}}(t)=\pi_{(1,0)}(t)
for any
t>t_{0}
.
Then either
\liminf_{t\to\infty}\pi_{\mathbf{0}}(t)>1
or
\limsup_{t\to\infty}\pi_{(1,0)}(t)<0
.
By flip invariance, these two events have equal probability, so we have

0<\mathbb{P}[\liminf_{t\to\infty}\pi_{\mathbf{0}}(t)>1]<1.

(7.5)

However, by coalescing of semi-infinite geodesics, the event
\liminf_{t\to\infty}\pi_{\mathbf{0}}(t)>1
is (up to a probability zero event) equivalent to the event
\liminf_{t\to\infty}\pi_{(0,r)}(t)>1
, for any
r\in\mathbb{R}
; thus it is (up to a probability zero event) equivalent to an event that is
\mathcal{F}_{\infty}
measurable. So (
7.5
) contradicts Lemma
7.7
, and we can find an
a\in[1,2]
such that (
7.4
) holds.

Now we fix such an
a\in[1,2]
.
It suffices to prove this lemma for
w=\frac{a}{n}
,
n\in\mathbb{N}
.
We claim that under
\pi_{(-a,0)}(1)>-a,\pi_{(a,0)}(1)<a
, there exists some
i\in\llbracket-n,n\rrbracket
, such that
|\pi_{(ai/n,0)}(1)-ai/n|\leq a/n
(see Figure
16
).
Indeed, otherwise we denote

I_{-}=\{i\in\llbracket-n,n\rrbracket:\pi_{(ai/n,0)}(1)<a(i-1)/n\}

and

I_{+}=\{i\in\llbracket-n,n\rrbracket:\pi_{(ai/n,0)}(1)>a(i+1)/n\}.

We have
-n\in I_{+}
and
n\in I_{-}
, and
\llbracket-n,n\rrbracket\subset I_{-}\cup I_{+}
.
Then we can find some
i\in\llbracket-n,n-1\rrbracket
such that
i\in I_{+}
and
i+1\in I_{-}
.
These would lead to a contradiction with the ordering of semi-infinite geodesics (Lemma
4.9
).
Then we conclude that
|\pi_{(ai/n,0)}(1)-ai/n|\leq a/n
for some
i\in\llbracket-n,n\rrbracket
; thus by translation invariance we have

\mathbb{P}[\pi_{\mathbf{0}}(1)\in[-a/n,a/n]]>(2n+1)^{-1}\mathbb{P}[\pi_{(-a,0)}(1)>-a,\pi_{(a,0)}(1)<a],

so our conclusion follows.
∎

7.4
Hausdorff dimension of the zero set: upper bound

To finish the proof of Theorem
2.3
, it remains to prove the upper bound for the Hausdorff dimension of the set
Z=\{t\geq 0:\pi_{\mathbf{0}}(t)=0\}
. This is immediately implied by the following result.

Proposition 7.9
.

For any
0<g<h
, and any
d>1/3
, almost surely the
d
-dimensional Hausdorff measure of the set
Z\cap[g,h]
is zero.

Recall the formal definition of Hausdorff dimension in Definition
2.5
.
As indicated in Section
2.3
, we would split
[g,h]
into segments, and bound the expected number of segments that intersect with
Z
.
The key estimate is as follows.

Proposition 7.10
.

There is a constant
C
depending on
g,h
, such that for any
g\leq t<t+\epsilon\leq h
, we have

\mathbb{P}[Z\cap(t,t+\epsilon)\neq\emptyset]<C\epsilon^{2/3}|\log(\epsilon)|^{5}.

There are two mechanisms for
Z\cap(t,t+\epsilon)\neq\emptyset
: either
\pi_{\mathbf{0}}(t)
is in a neighborhood of
0
of size in the order of
\epsilon^{2/3}
, or
\pi_{\mathbf{0}}
has a transversal fluctuation in
(t,t+\epsilon)
larger than its typical order (of
\epsilon^{2/3}
). We shall bound the probabilities of both events.
The proof is similar in spirit to the proof of Proposition
6.10
.

Proof.

We can assume that
\epsilon
is small enough since otherwise the inequality is obvious by taking the constant large enough.

We consider the following two events, corresponding to the above two mechanisms.

•

Let
\mathcal{E}_{1}
be the event where
|\pi_{\mathbf{0}}(t)|\leq\epsilon^{2/3}|\log(\epsilon)|^{5}
.

•

Let
\mathcal{E}_{2}
be the event where there is some
s\in[t,t+\epsilon]
, such that

|\pi_{(\mathbf{0};0,s)}(t)|>\epsilon^{2/3}|\log(\epsilon)|^{5}.

Then we have that the event
Z\cap(t,t+\epsilon)\neq\emptyset
implies
\mathcal{E}_{1}\cup\mathcal{E}_{2}
.
Indeed, suppose that there is some
s\in Z\cap(t,t+\epsilon)
, then
\pi_{(\mathbf{0};0,s)}
is a part of
\pi_{\mathbf{0}}
; i.e., for any
s^{\prime}\in[0,s]
we have
\pi_{(\mathbf{0};0,s)}(s^{\prime})=\pi_{\mathbf{0}}(s^{\prime})
.
In particular, we would have either
|\pi_{\mathbf{0}}(t)|\leq\epsilon^{2/3}|\log(\epsilon)|^{5}
, or
\pi_{(\mathbf{0};0,s)}(t)|>\epsilon^{2/3}|\log(\epsilon)|^{5}
, implying that either
\mathcal{E}_{1}
or
\mathcal{E}_{2}
occurs.

It now suffices to bound the probabilities of these events by the order of
\epsilon^{2/3}|\log(\epsilon)|^{5}
.
The bound on
\mathbb{P}[\mathcal{E}_{1}]
is by Corollary
7.2
.
For
\mathbb{P}[\mathcal{E}_{2}]
, we us Lemma
3.11
, as bounding
\mathbb{P}[\mathcal{E}_{2}]
in the proof of Proposition
6.10
. We omit the details here.
∎

Proof of Proposition
7.9
.

For any large enough
n\in\mathbb{N}
, let

\mathscr{Z}_{n}=\{i\in\mathbb{Z}:[i/n,(i+1)/n]\cap Z\cap[g,h]\neq\emptyset\}.

By Proposition
7.10
,
\mathbb{E}[|\mathscr{Z}_{n}|]<Cn^{2/3}(\log(n))^{5}
, where
C
is a constant depending only on
g,h
.
For
0<\alpha<d-1/3
, we then have

\mathbb{P}[n^{-d}|\mathscr{Z}_{n}|>n^{-\alpha}]<Cn^{1/3-d+\alpha}(\log(n))^{5}.

This implies that almost surely, we can find a random
k_{0}
, such that for any
k>k_{0}
,

2^{-dk}|\mathscr{Z}_{2^{k}}|\leq 2^{-\alpha k}.

Since the right hand side
\to 0
as
k\to\infty
, and
Z\cap[g,h]\subset\cup_{i\in\mathscr{Z}_{n}}[i/n,(i+1)/n]
, the conclusion follows.
∎

We have now spelled out all the key ingredients and ideas for us to be able to plunge in to the proof of Theorem
2.2
.

8
Hausdorff dimension of
\widehat{\mathsf{NC}}
: Proof of Theorem
2.2

Our general strategy would be similar to that implemented in Section
6
. However while the upper bound straight forwardly follows from Proposition
6.10
, there are several key differences in the lower bounds, which we explain below.

8.1
Proof of the upper bound

The proof is similar to that of the proof of Proposition
7.9
with the application of Proposition
7.10
replaced by an application of Proposition
6.10
. We provide the details below for completeness at the risk of being slightly repetitive.
For any
0<g<h
, and any
d>2/3
, we show that almost surely, the
d
-dimensional Hausdorff measure of the set
\widehat{\mathsf{NC}}\cap[g,h]
is zero.
Take any large enough
n\in\mathbb{N}
, and let

\mathscr{S}_{n}=\{i\in\mathbb{Z}:[i/n,(i+1)/n]\cap S\cap[g,h]\neq\emptyset\}.

By Proposition
6.10
, we have that
\mathbb{E}[|\mathscr{S}_{n}|]<Cn^{2/3}e^{C(\log(n))^{5/6}}
, where
C
is a constant depending only on
g,h
.
Taking
0<\alpha<d-2/3
, we then have

\mathbb{P}[n^{-d}|\mathscr{S}_{n}|>n^{-\alpha}]<Cn^{2/3-d+\alpha}e^{C(\log(n))^{5/6}}.

This implies that almost surely, we can find a random
k_{0}
, such that for any
k>k_{0}
,

2^{-dk}|\mathscr{S}_{2^{k}}|\leq 2^{-\alpha k}.

The proof can then be finished as for Proposition
7.9
.
∎

We next move on to the proof of the lower bound.
For this, we need to construct a measure
\kappa
on
\mathbb{R}_{+}
, which is the density of the measure
\zeta
from Section
6
, as described in Section
2.3
(see (
2.7
) and the ensuing discussion). We shall call it
the measure induced by the difference profile density
, and show that it is supported on
\widehat{\mathsf{NC}}
.
We then prove a Hölder property of
\kappa
, and that
\kappa
is strictly positive.
Recall the level functions
\vartheta_{\ell}
defined as

\vartheta_{\ell}(t)=\inf\{x\in\mathbb{R}:\mathcal{D}(x,t)\leq\ell\}.

The Hölder property of
\kappa
is proved by writing
\kappa
as local times of
\vartheta_{\ell}
, averaged over
\ell\in\mathbb{R}
. Via results in Section
6.3
, this is reduced to analyzing the local time of the competition interface
\vartheta^{\mathcal{B}}
, which has the same law as the semi-infinite geodesic
\pi_{\mathbf{0}}
(Proposition
2.8
).

There are two main additional difficulties in the setting of
\widehat{\mathsf{NC}}
(compared to that of
\mathsf{NC}
in Section
6
).
First, the construction of
\kappa
is not straightforward, and we need arguments similar to those in Section
7.1
.
In addition, there is some work needed to show that
\kappa
is an average of occupation measures of
\vartheta_{\ell}
.
We resolve these difficulties in Section
8.2
.
Second, the fact that
\kappa
is non-degenerate is not obvious, and requires some work using estimates about the Airy line ensemble. We conduct these analyses in Section
8.4
.
The desired Hölder regularity of
\kappa
is proved in Section
8.3
.

Recall the notations
U_{g,h,N}
and
\Good_{g,h,N,\delta}
from Section
6.3
.
For this section, we shall usually take
N=1/2
, and use the abbreviations
U_{g,h}=U_{g,h,1/2}
and
\Good_{g,h,\delta}=\Good_{g,h,1/2,\delta}
, for simplicity of notations.

8.2
Construction and average of occupation measures

Recall
\zeta^{\mathcal{B}}
,
\zeta_{\ell}
for each
\ell\in\mathbb{R}
, and
\zeta
on
\mathbb{H}
, which are defined in (
6.5
), (
6.3
), and (
6.1
) respectively, and are given by

\zeta^{\mathcal{B}}([a,b]\times(g,h))=\int_{g}^{h}\mathds{1}[\vartheta^{\mathcal{B}}(t)\in[a,b]]dt,

and

\zeta_{\ell}([a,b]\times(g,h))=\int_{g}^{h}\mathds{1}[\vartheta_{\ell}(t)\in[a,b]]dt,

\zeta([a,b]\times(g,h))=\int_{g}^{h}[\mathcal{D}(a,t)-\mathcal{D}(b,t)]dt,

for any
a<b
and
0\leq g<h
.
In this section, we will mostly consider these measures on rectangles. To ease notations, for each
h\geq 0
, we denote by
\lambda^{\mathcal{B}}_{h}
and
\lambda_{\ell,h}
the measures on
\mathbb{R}
, such that

\lambda^{\mathcal{B}}_{h}(A)=\zeta^{\mathcal{B}}(A\times(0,h])=\int_{0}^{h}\mathds{1}[\vartheta^{\mathcal{B}}(t)\in A]dt,

and

\lambda_{\ell,h}(A)=\zeta_{\ell}(A\times(0,h])=\int_{0}^{h}\mathds{1}[\vartheta_{\ell}(t)\in A]dt,

for any measurable
A\subset\mathbb{R}
. Thus
\lambda^{\mathcal{B}}_{h}
and
\lambda_{\ell,h}
can be viewed as the occupation measure for
\vartheta^{\mathcal{B}}
and
\vartheta_{\ell}
, respectively, for the time interval
[0,h]
. Also for each
0\leq g<h
, we denote by
\lambda_{(g,h]}
the measures on
\mathbb{R}
, such that

\lambda_{(g,h]}(A)=\zeta(A\times(g,h])

(8.1)

for any measurable
A\subset\mathbb{R}
.

Remark 8.1
.

For
\lambda_{(g,h]}
, we shall usually take
g>0
, because
\lambda_{(0,h]}
is infinite. Indeed, by symmetry of the directed landscape, for any
a<b
and
t>0
we have
\mathbb{E}[\mathcal{D}(a,t)-\mathcal{D}(b,t)]=4(b-a)/t
, and the fluctuation of
\mathcal{D}(a,t)-\mathcal{D}(b,t)
is
O(t^{1/3})
(by Lemma
3.9
).
Thus when
g=0
, we have
\lambda_{(0,h]}([a,b])=\int_{0}^{h}[\mathcal{D}(a,t)-\mathcal{D}(b,t)]dt=\infty
. On the other hand, when
g>0
, we have
\mathbb{E}[\lambda_{(g,h]}([a,b])]<\infty
, so almost surely we have
\lambda_{(g,h]}([a,b])<\infty
.

The relation (
6.4
) for the set
A\times(g,h]
can be written as

\lambda_{(g,h]}(A)=\int[\lambda_{\ell,h}(A)-\lambda_{\ell,g}(A)]d\ell.

(8.2)

For the remainder of this section, we will use
\lambda_{(g,h]}
,
\lambda^{\mathcal{B}}_{h}
,
\lambda_{\ell,h}
, instead of
\zeta
,
\zeta^{\mathcal{B}}
,
\zeta_{\ell}
, at the risk of introducing new notation. This is because we will mostly consider sets of the form of
A\times(g,h]
, and
\lambda^{\mathcal{B}}_{h}
has the same law as
\nu_{h}
(defined in Section
7.1
) by Proposition
2.8
.

We next consider the densities of these measures at zero, by defining, for any
0\leq g<h
,

K_{g,h}:=\lim_{w\searrow 0}(2w)^{-1}\lambda_{(g,h]}([-w,w]),

(8.3)

K^{\mathcal{B}}_{g,h}:=\lim_{w\searrow 0}(2w)^{-1}(\lambda^{\mathcal{B}}_{h}([-w,w])-\lambda^{\mathcal{B}}_{g}([-w,w])),

and

K_{\ell,g,h}:=\lim_{w\searrow 0}(2w)^{-1}(\lambda_{\ell,h}([-w,w])-\lambda_{\ell,g}([-w,w])).

We now show that these limits are (in some sense) well-defined and finite.

For the stationary initial setting, as
\lambda^{\mathcal{B}}_{h}
has the same law as
\nu_{h}
, almost surely
K^{\mathcal{B}}_{g,h}
is well defined and finite by Proposition
2.4
, and
\lambda^{\mathcal{B}}_{h}
is absolutely continuous with respect to Lebesgue measure by Proposition
7.3
.

We next study the limits in defining
K_{g,h}
and
K_{\ell,g,h}
.

Lemma 8.2
.

For any
0<g<h
, almost surely
K_{g,h}
is well defined, i.e., the limit exists and is finite.

Proof.

This proof is similar to the proof of Proposition
2.4
. We construct a translation invariant version of the difference profile.
Let
\xi\subset\mathbb{R}
be a Poisson point process with rate
1
. For each
0\leq g<h,
we consider a measure
\lambda_{(g,h]}^{\prime}
such that for any
a<b
, we have

\lambda_{(g,h]}^{\prime}([a,b])=\int_{g}^{h}\sum_{x\in\xi}(\mathcal{L}(x-1,0;a\vee(x-1),t)-\mathcal{L}(x+1,0;a\vee(x-1),t))\\
-(\mathcal{L}(x-1,0;b\wedge(x+1),t)-\mathcal{L}(x+1,0;b\wedge(x+1),t))dt.

Basically this is just the sum of
\lambda_{(g,h]}
centered at each
x\in\xi
and truncated by
[x-1,x+1]
.
The truncation is taken so that at any point
y
, the measure only depends on passage times of finitely many starting points, and each is on the segment
[y-2,y+2]\times\{0\}
. Crucially however, even with this truncation the law of the measure
\lambda_{(g,h]}^{\prime}
is translation invariant.

Via the translation invariance, we have that when
g>0
,
\lim_{w\searrow 0}(2w)^{-1}\lambda_{(g,h]}^{\prime}([-w,w])
exists and is finite almost surely, using the same arguments as in the proof of Proposition
2.4
and the fact that
\lambda_{(g,h]}
is finite in any compact interval (as mentioned in Remark
8.1
).

Next, for small enough
\delta
, we consider the event
\mathcal{E}_{\delta}
, where
|\xi\cap[-\delta,\delta]|=|\xi\cap[-3,3]|=1
.
Then, given
0<g<h
and recalling the event
\Good_{g,h,\delta}
from Section
6.3
, we notice that under the latter, we have

(\mathcal{L}(x_{1}-1,0;p_{1})-\mathcal{L}(x_{1}+1,0;p_{1}))-(\mathcal{L}(x_{1}-1,0;p_{2})-\mathcal{L}(x_{1}+1,0;p_{2}))\\
=(\mathcal{L}(x_{2}-1,0;p_{1})-\mathcal{L}(x_{2}+1,0;p_{1}))-(\mathcal{L}(x_{2}-1,0;p_{2})-\mathcal{L}(x_{2}+1,0;p_{2}))

for any
|x_{1}|,|x_{2}|<\delta
and
p_{1},p_{2}\in U_{g,h}=[-1/2,1/2]\times[g/2,2h]
.
In words, for any
|x_{1}|,|x_{2}|<\delta
, the difference profiles centered at
x_{1}
and
x_{2}
are the same inside the box
U_{g,h}
, up to adding a (random) constant.

From the above arguments, we have that

(2w)^{-1}(\lambda_{(g,h]}([-w,w])=(2w)^{-1}(\lambda_{(g,h]}^{\prime}([-w,w])

for any
0<w<1/2
, assuming the event
\mathcal{E}_{\delta}\cap\Good_{g,h,\delta}
.
By Lemma
6.6
we have that
\mathbb{P}[\Good_{g,h,\delta}]\to 1
as
\delta\searrow 0
.
So by the independence of
\mathcal{E}_{\delta}
and
\Good_{g,h,\delta}
, we have
\mathbb{P}[\mathcal{E}_{\delta}\cap\Good_{g,h,\delta}]=\mathbb{P}[\mathcal{E}_{\delta}]\mathbb{P}[\Good_{g,h,\delta}]>0
when
\delta
is small enough.
These imply that conditional on the event
\mathcal{E}_{\delta}\cap\Good_{g,h,\delta}
, we still have the (almost sure) existence and finiteness of the limit
\lim_{w\searrow 0}(2w)^{-1}(\lambda_{(g,h]}^{\prime}([-w,w]))
, thus we have the (almost sure) existence and finiteness of the limit
\lim_{w\searrow 0}(2w)^{-1}(\lambda_{(g,h]}([-w,w]))
.
Since
\mathbb{P}[\Good_{g,h,\delta}]\to 1
as
\delta\searrow 0
, and
\mathcal{E}_{\delta}
is independent of
\lambda_{(g,h]}
, the existence and finiteness of the limit hold almost surely.
∎

Lemma 8.3
.

For any
0<g<h
, almost surely, for almost every
\ell\in\mathbb{R}
,
K_{\ell,g,h}
is well defined (i.e., the limit exists and is finite), and
\lambda_{\ell,h}-\lambda_{\ell,g}
is absolutely continuous with respect to Lebesgue measure.
By (
8.2
) we then conclude that (almost surely)
\lambda_{(g,h]}
is also absolutely continuous with respect to Lebesgue measure.

Proof.

Take any
\delta,H>0
.
By Proposition
2.8
and Proposition
2.4
, there is a probability one event, under which
K_{g,h}^{\mathcal{B}}
is well-defined and finite; and by Proposition
7.3
, there is a probability one event, under which
\lambda^{\mathcal{B}}_{h}-\lambda^{\mathcal{B}}_{g}
is absolutely continuous with respect to Lebesgue measure.
On the other hand, by Lemma
6.7
, under the event
\Good_{g,h,\delta}
(which has positive probability by Lemma
6.6
and taking
\delta
small) almost surely we have
\mathbb{P}[K_{g,h}^{\mathcal{B}}=K_{\alpha,g,h},\lambda^{\mathcal{B}}_{h}-\lambda^{\mathcal{B}}_{g}=\lambda_{\alpha,h}-\lambda_{\alpha,g}\mid\mathcal{L}]>0
, with
\alpha
being random and has (conditional on
\mathcal{L}
) probability density
\geq\delta^{\prime}
on
[-H,H]
.
These imply that under the event
\Good_{g,h,\delta}
, almost surely there is a full measure subset of
[-H,H]
, which is random and determined by
\mathcal{L}
, such that for each
\ell
in this set,
K_{\ell,g,h}
is well-defined and finite, and
\lambda_{\ell,h}-\lambda_{\ell,g}
is absolutely continuous with respect to Lebesgue measure.
Then the conclusion follows by taking
\delta\searrow 0
and
H\to\infty
, and using Lemma
6.6
.
∎

Remark 8.4
.

We explain the reason why the above two lemmas require that
g>0
(unlike in the case of semi-infinite geodesics).
For Lemma
8.2
, it is simply because
\lambda_{(g,h]}
is finite only when
g>0
, as discussed in Remark
8.1
.
For Lemma
8.3
, one can actually show convergence to
K_{\ell,g,h}
when
g=0
; but since that is not used in the remainder of this paper we omit the details.
In fact, the occupation measures for short times can be (roughly) described as follows.
For any
(x,t)\in\mathbb{H}
and
\ell\in\mathbb{R}
with
|x+\ell t/4|\gg t^{4/3}
, it is unlikely to have
\mathcal{D}(x,t)=\ell
, by Lemma
3.9
.
This implies that
|\vartheta_{\ell}(t)+\ell t/4|
is at most in the order of
t^{4/3}
.
From these we expect that
\lambda_{\ell,1}([-w,w])
is in the order of
|\ell|^{-1}w
, if
\ell\neq 0
; and it is in the order of
w^{3/4}
if
\ell=0
. On the other hand, for large times,
\vartheta_{\ell}
grows linearly, as depicted in Figure
1
. More precisely, it is proved in
[
RV21
, Proposition 4.5]
that
\vartheta_{\ell}(t)/t
converges almost surely to a Gaussian random variable (while
[
RV21
, Proposition 4.5]
proves this for the
\ell=0
case, the arguments for general
\ell
are essentially verbatim).
A discrete (or pre-limiting) analogue of this large
t
behavior is the motion of a second class particle in a step initial TASEP or equivalently a single particle in the TASEP speed process (see
[
MG05
,
AAV11
]
).

By Lemma
8.3
, we now have that almost surely,
K_{g,h}
is well-defined and is positive and finite for each rational
0<g<h
.
We also have that almost surely for
\mathcal{L}
, we can find a random full measure set
\Theta\subset\mathbb{R}
, which can be viewed as a function of
\mathcal{L}
, such that for any
\ell\in\Theta
, and each rational
0<g<h
,
K_{\ell,g,h}
is well-defined and finite.

Recall the measures
\kappa
and
\kappa_{\ell}
described in Section
2.3
. We are now ready to define them.
For any rational
0<h_{1}<h_{2}<h_{3}
, we have
K_{h_{1},h_{2}}+K_{h_{2},h_{3}}=K_{h_{1},h_{3}}
.
Hence we can define the measure
\kappa
on
\mathbb{R}_{+}
, by letting

\kappa((g,h))=\sup_{g<g^{\prime}<h^{\prime}<h,\;g^{\prime},h^{\prime}\in\mathbb{Q}}K_{g^{\prime},h^{\prime}},

(8.4)

for any
0<g<h
. We take the supremum from interior since we need
\kappa((g,h))
be right continuous in
g
and left continuous in
h
.
Using standard measure theory arguments, this suffices to define
\kappa
as a measure on
\mathbb{R}_{+}
.

We now move on to the construction of the decomposition of
\kappa
indicated in (
2.7
), which is the one dimensional analogue of (
8.2
).
For each
\ell\in\Theta
and
0<g<h
, we let

\kappa_{\ell}((g,h))=\sup_{g<g^{\prime}<h^{\prime}<h,\;g^{\prime},h^{\prime}\in\mathbb{Q}}K_{\ell,g^{\prime},h^{\prime}},

(8.5)

and we extend this to define a measure
\kappa_{\ell}
on
\mathbb{R}_{+}
.

Remark 8.5
.

Actually, almost surely
K_{\ell,g,h}
is continuous in
g
and
h
for
\ell
in a random set with full measure.
This is by Proposition
7.4
and the equality in distribution between
\pi_{\mathbf{0}}
and
\vartheta^{\mathcal{B}}
(Lemma
2.8
), and taking
\kappa^{\mathcal{B}}
as
\kappa_{\ell}
for a random
\ell
using Lemma
6.7
(as done in the proof of Lemma
8.3
).
Thus
\kappa_{\ell}((g,h))=K_{\ell,g,h}
for almost every
\ell
and any rational
0<g<h
.

We next show that
\kappa
is the integral of
\kappa_{\ell}
over
\ell
. For the convenience of notations, we let
\kappa_{\ell}
be an arbitrary measure on
\mathbb{R}_{+}
for each
\ell\in\mathbb{R}\setminus\Theta
. Since
\mathbb{R}\setminus\Theta
has zero Lebesgue measure, the choice of such
\kappa_{\ell}
does not affect the integral.

Lemma 8.6
.

Almost surely,
\kappa(A)=\int\kappa_{\ell}(A)d\ell
, for any measurable set
A
that is contained in a compact subset of
\mathbb{R}_{+}
.

To prove this, we first prove the same relation for
K_{g,h}
and
K_{\ell,g,h}
, for fixed
g,h
.

Lemma 8.7
.

For any
0<g<h
, almost surely we have that
K_{g,h}=\int K_{\ell,g,h}d\ell
.

We note that this is a “density” and hence a limiting version of (
8.2
).
The proof of this lemma is via a uniform integrability result, when passing from
\lambda_{(g,h]}
and
\lambda_{\ell,h}-\lambda_{\ell,g}
to
K_{g,h}
and
K_{\ell,g,h}
.
We would again rely on the connections with
\lambda^{\mathcal{B}}_{h}-\lambda^{\mathcal{B}}_{g}
, and with semi-infinite geodesics, and the estimate in Lemma
7.5
.

Proof.

Fixing
0<g<h,
for brevity, let

f_{w}(\ell)=(2w)^{-1}(\lambda_{\ell,h}([-w,w])-\lambda_{\ell,g}([-w,w])),

for
w>0
, and

f(\ell)=K_{\ell,g,h}.

From Lemma
8.2
and Lemma
8.3
, we have that almost surely,
f_{w}\to f
almost everywhere, and also
\|f_{w}\|_{L_{1}}
converges as
w\searrow 0
. Now we want to show (almost surely) the limit of
\lim_{w\searrow 0}\|f_{w}\|_{L_{1}}=\|f\|_{L_{1}}
.
For this we show for any
H>0
, there is some (random) positive sequence
w_{1},w_{2},\cdots\to 0
, such that
f_{w_{n}}\to f
in
L_{1}([-H,H])
.

Note that almost surely, all
f_{w}
with
0<w<1
and
f
are supported on some compact interval. This is because, by Lemma
3.9
, for the level function
\vartheta_{\ell}
, we have
|\vartheta_{\ell}(t)|>1
for
t\in[g,h]
when
|\ell|
is large enough.
Thus it suffices to show that
\{f_{w_{n}}\}_{n=1}^{\infty}
are uniformly integrable in
[-H,H]
, for any
H>0
.

Analogous to
f_{w}(\cdot)
, let

f^{\mathcal{B}}_{w}=(2w)^{-1}(\lambda^{\mathcal{B}}_{h}([-w,w])-\lambda^{\mathcal{B}}_{g}([-w,w])).

We then have (by Lemma
7.5
),

\mathbb{P}[f^{\mathcal{B}}_{w}>M]<Ce^{-cM}

for some
c,C>0
that are independent of
w
(but depend on
g,h
).
To show uniform integrablity, we let
\gamma_{w,m}=\int_{-H}^{H}\mathds{1}[f_{w}(\ell)>m]f_{w}(\ell)d\ell
, for any
m\in\mathbb{N}
.
Now, for any
\delta>0
, let
\delta^{\prime}
be given by Lemma
6.7
. Then,

\mathbb{E}[\mathds{1}[\Good_{g,h,\delta}]\liminf_{w\searrow 0}\gamma_{w,m}]\leq\liminf_{w\searrow 0}\mathbb{E}[\mathds{1}[\Good_{g,h,\delta}]\gamma_{w,m}]\leq 1/\delta^{\prime}\liminf_{w\searrow 0}\mathbb{E}[\mathds{1}[f^{\mathcal{B}}_{w}>m]f^{\mathcal{B}}_{w}]<Ce^{-cm}/\delta^{\prime},

where the first inequality is by Fatou’s lemma, and
C,c>0
are constants independent of
w,m
.
Recall (from Lemma
6.6
) that
\lim_{\delta\searrow 0}\mathbb{P}[\Good_{g,h,\delta}]=1
, and note that
\Good_{g,h,\delta}
does not depend on
w
or
m
. These imply that
\lim_{m\to\infty}\liminf_{w\searrow 0}\gamma_{w,m}=0
almost surely.

Via a diagonal argument, almost surely we can take a (random) positive subsequence
w_{1},w_{2},\ldots\to 0
such that
\lim_{n\to\infty}\gamma_{w_{n},m}=\liminf_{w\searrow 0}\gamma_{w,m}
for each
m\in\mathbb{N}
.
Hence

\lim_{m\to\infty}\lim_{n\to\infty}\gamma_{w_{n},m}=\lim_{m\to\infty}\liminf_{w\searrow 0}\gamma_{w,m}=0.

That is,
\{f_{w_{n}}\}_{n=1}^{\infty}
are uniformly integrable in
[-H,H]
.
For
H
large enough, we have all
f_{w}
and
f
are supported on
[-H,H]
(and the largeness is random).
Thus for the subsequence
w_{1},\ldots,
we have
f_{w_{n}}\to f
in
L_{1}
; this implies that
\|f_{w_{n}}\|_{L_{1}}\to\|f\|_{L_{1}}
, and hence
\|f_{w}\|_{L_{1}}\to\|f\|_{L_{1}}
.
∎

Using the above, the proof of Lemma
8.6
is straightforward.

Proof of Lemma
8.6
.

For the arguments below, we assume the probability one event that Lemma
8.7
holds for any rational
0<g<h
.

We first consider the case where
A
is an interval
(g,h)
for some rational
0<g<h
.
Take a rational sequence
g_{n}\searrow g
and a rational sequence
h_{n}\nearrow h
.
Then
\kappa(A)=\lim_{n\to\infty}K_{g_{n},h_{n}}
and
\kappa_{\ell}(A)=\lim_{n\to\infty}K_{\ell,g_{n},h_{n}}
for any
\ell\in\Theta
.
Since
K_{\ell,g_{n},h_{n}}\leq K_{\ell,g,h}
and
K_{\ell,g,h}
is integrable as a function of
\ell
(by Lemma
8.7
), we can apply dominated convergence theorem to conclude that

\int\kappa_{\ell}(A)d\ell=\lim_{n\to\infty}\int K_{\ell,g_{n},h_{n}}d\ell.

By Lemma
8.7
, the right hand side equals
\lim_{n\to\infty}K_{g_{n},h_{n}}=\kappa(A)
, so the conclusion holds in the case where
A
is a rational interval.

Then we can extend this to any measurable set
A
contained in a compact set in
\mathbb{R}_{+}
, using
\pi-\lambda
theorem. For this, we need to show that for any rational
0<g<h
, the collection
\{A\subset(g,h):\kappa(A)=\int\kappa_{\ell}(A)d\ell\}
is a Dynkin system: closed under complements is obvious, and closed under countable disjoint union is by dominated convergence theorem (since
\kappa_{\ell}(A)\leq\kappa_{\ell}((g,h))
for any
A\subset(g,h)
and any
\ell\in\Theta
).
∎

As was indicated in Section
2.3
, the following statement highlights the usefulness of
\kappa.

Lemma 8.8
.

Almost surely, the measure
\kappa
is supported inside
\widehat{\mathsf{NC}}
.

Proof.

In this proof we assume the event where we can find a measurable set
\Theta^{\prime}\subset\Theta\subset\mathbb{R}
with
\mathscr{L}(\mathbb{R}\setminus\Theta^{\prime})=0
, such that each
\ell\in\Theta^{\prime}
satisfies the following: (1) for each
t\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
, there is a unique
x\in\mathbb{R}
with
\mathcal{D}(x,t)=\ell
; (2)
K_{\ell,g,h}
is well-defined and finite for each rational
0<g<h
.
By Lemma
6.3
and Lemma
8.3
, this event happens almost surely.

We now fix some
0<g<h
, and show that
\kappa
restricted on
(g,h)
is supported inside
\widehat{\mathsf{NC}}\cap(g,h)
.

Denote
S
to be the closure of
\cup_{\ell\in\Theta^{\prime}}\{t:g<t<h,\vartheta_{\ell}(t)=0\}
. Here the closure is taken to ensure the measurability of
S
.
We claim that
S\subset\widehat{\mathsf{NC}}
.
Indeed, for any
t\not\in\widehat{\mathsf{NC}}
, we have
\mathcal{D}(0,s)=\ell_{*}
for each
s\in A
, where
A
is a neighborhood of
t
and
\ell_{*}\in\mathbb{R}
is a constant.
If
t\in S
, by the definition of
S
we can find some
s\in A
and some
\ell^{\prime}\in\Theta^{\prime}
, such that
\vartheta_{\ell^{\prime}}(s)=0
. Thus we must have that
\ell^{\prime}=\ell_{*}
, so
\ell_{*}\in\Theta^{\prime}
.
This implies that
\vartheta_{\ell_{*}}(s)=0
for each
s\in A
and
K_{\ell_{*},g,h}
is well-defined and finite.
However, from its definition we have
K_{\ell_{*},g,h}=\infty
when
\vartheta_{\ell_{*}}(s)=0
for each
s\in A
.
Thus we must have
t\not\in S
.

We shall next show that
\kappa
restricted to
(g,h)
is supported inside
S
.
Take any
\ell\in\Theta^{\prime}
. By Lemma
6.4
, the set
\{t:g\leq t\leq h,\vartheta_{\ell}(t)=0\}
is closed, and for any
g<t<h
with
\vartheta_{\ell}(t)\neq 0
, we can find an open neighborhood around
(0,t)
that is disjoint from the trajectory of
\vartheta_{\ell}
.
The later implies that for any
g<t<h
with
\vartheta_{\ell}(t)\neq 0
, one can find some
\delta>0
such that
\kappa_{\ell}((t-\delta,t+\delta))=0
.
Thus
\kappa_{\ell}(\{t:g<t<h,\vartheta_{\ell}(t)\neq 0\})=0
. Since
(g,h)\setminus S\subset\{t:g<t<h,\vartheta_{\ell}(t)\neq 0\}
we have
\kappa_{\ell}((g,h)\setminus S)=0
.
By Lemma
8.6
, we have that
\kappa((g,h)\setminus S)=\int\kappa_{\ell}((g,h)\setminus S)d\ell=0
.
This implies that
\kappa
restricted to
(g,h)
is supported inside
S
, thus supported inside
\widehat{\mathsf{NC}}
.
∎

To show that the Hausdorff dimension of
\widehat{\mathsf{NC}}
is at least
2/3
,
it remains to show that
\kappa
is
2/3-\epsilon
Hölder in any interval
[g,h]
for any
\epsilon>0
, and
\kappa([g,h])>0
for small enough
g>0
.
These two steps are carried out in the next two subsections.

8.3
2/3
Hölder of the difference profile density

Lemma 8.9
.

For any
0<g<h
and
\epsilon>0
, almost surely there is some random
Q
, such that
\kappa([a,b])<Q(b-a)^{2/3-\epsilon}
for any
g\leq a<b\leq h
.

The proof follows a similar strategy as the proof of Lemma
6.8
.
Specifically, it suffices to prove
K_{a,b}<Q(b-a)^{2/3-\epsilon}
for any
a,b\in\mathbb{Q}
satisfying
g\leq a<b\leq h
and
b-a<1
.
By the regularity condition of the directed landscape under the event
\Good_{g,h,\delta}
,
K_{a,b}^{\ell}
is nonzero only for
\ell
in an interval of length (in the order of)
(b-a)^{1/3}
.
To bound
K_{a,b}
, we just bound an integral of
K_{a,b}^{\ell}
over
\ell
in an interval of length (in the order of)
(b-a)^{1/3}
, and take the supremum over all such intervals.
Using Lemma
6.7
, (under
\Good_{g,h,\delta}
) such a supremum of integrals is bound by
(b-a)^{1/3}
times
K_{a,b}^{\mathcal{B}}
; and by Proposition
7.4
,
K_{a,b}^{\mathcal{B}}
is in the order of
(b-a)^{1/3}
with exponential tails.

Proof.

Recall the almost sure event where we have a set
\Theta\subset\mathbb{R}
with full measure, such that for every
\ell\in\Theta
, and any
0<a<b
,
a,b\in\mathbb{Q}
, the numbers
K_{a,b}
,
K_{\ell,a,b}
, and
K_{a,b}^{\mathcal{B}}
are well defined and finite. We assume this event for the rest of this proof.

Fix any small enough
\delta>0
. It suffices to bound
K_{a,b}
for any rational
a,b
satisfying
g\leq a<b\leq h
and
b-a<1
, under the event
\Good_{g,h,\delta}
.
For each
\ell
, let

Q_{\ell}=\sup_{g\leq a<b\leq h,a,b\in\mathbb{Q}}\frac{K_{\ell,a,b}}{(b-a)^{1/3}(1+|\log(b-a)|^{2})}.

Here the extra factor of
1+|\log(b-a)|^{2}
is due to the fact that we take the supremum over
a,b
, and the power of
2
can be replaced by any number
>1
.
To bound
K_{a,b}
, we would bound the integral of
Q_{\ell}
over
\ell
.
We take
H>0
, large enough and depending on
g,h,\delta
.
For any
\varepsilon>0
we denote

J_{\varepsilon}=\sup_{A\subset[-H,H],\mathscr{L}(A)<\delta^{-1}\varepsilon^{1/3}\log^{2/3}(1+\varepsilon^{-1})}\int_{A}Q_{\ell}d\ell,

and let

J=\sup_{\varepsilon\in(0,1)}\frac{J_{\varepsilon}}{\varepsilon^{1/3}\log^{3}(1+\varepsilon^{-1})}.

The motivation behind these definitions is that, by regularity estimates of
\mathcal{L}
,
K_{\ell,a,b}
is non-zero only for
\ell
in an interval of length of the order of
(b-a)^{1/3}
.

As in the proof of Lemma
6.8
, this random variable
J
is essentially the desired
Q
in the statement of the lemma, and the remainder of this proof mainly consists of two steps: (1) we bound the upper tail of
\mathds{1}[\Good_{g,h,\delta}]J
; (2) we bound
K_{a,b}
for any
g\leq a<b\leq h
,
a,b\in\mathbb{Q}
using
J
under the event
\Good_{g,h,\delta}
. Unavoidably, the following sequence of arguments, like in the proof of Lemma
6.8
, will be somewhat technical.

Step 1.

We first bound
\mathds{1}[\Good_{g,h,\delta}]J
.
As in the proof of Lemma
6.8
, we take inputs from estimates on the competition interface with stationary initial condition.
Define

Q^{\mathcal{B}}=\sup_{g\leq a<b\leq h,a,b\in\mathbb{Q}}\frac{K^{\mathcal{B}}_{a,b}}{(b-a)^{1/3}(1+|\log(b-a)|^{2})}.

By Lemma
6.7
and taking
\delta^{\prime}
there, for any
M>0
we have

\mathbb{E}\left[\mathds{1}[\Good_{g,h,\delta}]\int_{-H}^{H}\mathds{1}[Q_{\ell}>M]d\ell\right]<{\delta^{\prime}}^{-1}\mathbb{P}[Q^{\mathcal{B}}>M]<{\delta^{\prime}}^{-1}C_{1}e^{-c_{1}M},

(8.6)

where
c_{1},C_{1}>0
are some constants (depending on
g,h
) and the last inequality is by Proposition
7.4
.

Recall the arguments in Step 1 in the proof of Lemma
6.8
, where (
6.8
) is translated into an upper tail bound of
\mathds{1}[\Good_{g,h,\delta}]J
there.
Using the same algebra, (
8.6
) can be translated into the following bound on the upper tail of
\mathds{1}[\Good_{g,h,\delta}]J
:
for any
M>0
, we have that

\mathbb{P}[\mathds{1}[\Good_{g,h,\delta}]J>M]<C_{\delta}e^{-c_{\delta}M},

for
C_{\delta},c_{\delta}
being constants depending on
g,h,\delta
.

Step 2.

Under
\Good_{g,h,\delta}
, using its second condition and Lemma
3.9
, we can bound
|\mathcal{D}|
in
[-1/2,1/2]\times[g,h]
.
Consequently, we have that
K_{\ell,g,h}=0
for any
|\ell|>H
, as
H
is large enough depending on
g,h,\delta
.
Then by Lemma
8.7
, for any rational
a,b
with
g\leq a<b\leq h
, we have
K_{a,b}=\int_{-H}^{H}K_{\ell,a,b}d\ell
.
We note that by the second condition of
\Good_{g,h,\delta}
and using Lemma
3.10
, for any
\ell_{1},\ell_{2}
, if both
K_{\ell_{1},a,b}
and
K_{\ell_{2},a,b}
are nonzero, we must have

|\ell_{1}-\ell_{2}|\leq C\delta^{-0.01}(b-a)^{1/3}\log^{2/3}(1+(b-a)^{-1})<\delta^{-1}(b-a)^{1/3}\log^{2/3}(1+(b-a)^{-1}),

where
C>0
is a constant depending on
g,h
, and the last inequality is by taking
\delta
small enough depending on
g,h
.
Thus by the definitions of
J_{b-a}
and
J
, we have (for
b-a<1
)

K_{a,b}=\int_{-H}^{H}K_{\ell,a,b}d\ell\leq(b-a)^{1/3}(1+|\log(b-a)|^{2})J_{b-a}<(b-a)^{2/3}(1+|\log(b-a)|^{2})\log^{3}(1+(b-a)^{-1})J.

By taking
Q
to be
J
multiplied by a constant depending on
g,h,\epsilon
, we get the desired bound
K_{a,b}<Q(b-a)^{2/3-\epsilon}
under
\Good_{g,h,\delta}
.
Finally, by taking
\delta\searrow 0
, we get that the desired bound holds almost surely.
∎

8.4
Non-degeneracy of the difference profile density

In this subsection we implement the last step in our strategy to lower bound the Hausdorff dimension of
\widehat{\mathsf{NC}}
, namely showing that
\kappa
is strictly positive This along with Lemma
8.8
and Lemma
8.9
concludes the proof of the lower bound of Theorem
2.2
, as indicated by the general strategy in Section
2.3
.

Proposition 8.10
.

Almost surely, there exist
0<g<h
such that
\kappa([g,h])>0
.

Note that for fixed
0<g<h,
with positive probability,
owing to coalescence of geodesics, the difference profile
\mathcal{D}
can be flat in the box
[-1,1]\times[g,h]
, thus
\kappa([g,h])=0
, with positive probability.

The proof of Proposition
8.10
follows a similar strategy as the proof of Proposition
7.6
: we start by showing that for a fixed interval,
\kappa
is positive with positive probability; then we use ergodicity to show that
\kappa
is almost surely strictly positive.
Recall the notation of
K_{g,h}
for any
0<g<h
, from (
8.3
).

Lemma 8.11
.

We have
\mathbb{P}[K_{1,2}>0]>0
.

To prove this we need estimates using the Airy line ensemble. We defer the Airy line ensemble arguments to Appendix
C
, and state the rest of the arguments here.

-1-\delta
-1+\delta
1-\delta
1+\delta
(x_{-},t_{-})
(x_{+},t_{+})
[-1,1]\times[1,1+\theta]

(a)

\mathcal{E}_{coal}

-1-\delta
-1+\delta
1-\delta
1+\delta
-2
2

(b)

\mathcal{E}_{coal}^{\prime}

-1
1
-\delta
\delta

(c)

\mathcal{E}_{disj}

Figure 17
:
Illustrations of the events
\mathcal{E}_{coal}
,
\mathcal{E}_{coal}^{\prime}
, and
\mathcal{E}_{disj}
in the proof of Lemma
8.11
:
assuming
\mathcal{E}_{disj}
, we have
\int_{-\delta}^{\delta}\overline{K}^{s}_{1,1+\theta}ds>0
for some random
\theta>0
, where
\overline{K}^{s}_{1,1+\theta}
is
K_{1,1+\theta}
translated by
(s,0)
in its definition.
Then by translation invariance (and assuming
\mathbb{P}[\mathcal{E}_{coal}\cap\mathcal{E}_{disj}]>0
), we have
\mathbb{E}[K_{1,1+\theta}]>0
for small enough
\theta
. To show
\mathbb{P}[\mathcal{E}_{coal}\cap\mathcal{E}_{disj}]>0
we reduce
\mathcal{E}_{coal}
to
\mathcal{E}_{coal}^{\prime}
, and use tools from the Airy line ensemble to show
\mathbb{P}[\mathcal{E}_{coal}^{\prime}\cap\mathcal{E}_{disj}]>0
(in Appendix
C
).

Proof.

Take some small
\theta>0
to be determined.
It suffices to prove that
\mathbb{E}[K_{1,1+\theta}]>0
.
The general idea is to use translation invariance and coalescence of geodesics, to reduce this to proving that, for some small
\delta>0
,
\lambda_{(1,1+\theta]}([-\delta,\delta])>0
with positive probability (recall (
8.1
) for the definition of
\lambda_{(1,1+\theta]}
); and this event is further implied by some coalescence and disjointness of geodesics. Such coalescence and disjointness of geodesics are studied using the Airy line ensemble, and we leave the arguments to Appendix
C
.

We first define translated versions of
K_{1,1+\theta}
. For any
s\in\mathbb{R}
we let

\overline{K}^{s}_{1,1+\theta}=\lim_{w\searrow 0}(2w)^{-1}\int_{1}^{1+\theta}[(\mathcal{L}(-1+s,0;-w+s,t)-\mathcal{L}(1+s,0;-w+s,t))\\
-(\mathcal{L}(-1+s,0;w+s,t)-\mathcal{L}(1+s,0;w+s,t))]dt.

By Lemma
8.2
and translation invariance of the directed landscape, for fixed
s,\theta
, almost surely
\overline{K}^{s}_{1,1+\theta}
is well-defined and finite, and has the same distribution as
K_{1,1+\theta}
.
We also define

\displaystyle\hat{K}_{1,1+\theta}^{s}=

\displaystyle\lim_{w\searrow 0}(2w)^{-1}(\lambda_{(1,1+\theta]}([s-w,s+w]))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\lim_{w\searrow 0}(2w)^{-1}\int_{1}^{1+\theta}\big[(\mathcal{L}(-1,0;-w+s,t)-\mathcal{L}(1,0;-w+s,t))

\displaystyle\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad-(\mathcal{L}(-1,0;w+s,t)-\mathcal{L}(1,0;w+s,t))\big]dt.

We note that (by Lebesgue’s theorem for the differentiability of monotone functions as before), almost surely this is well-defined for almost every
s
.
The difference between these two definitions is that, for
\hat{K}_{1,1+\theta}^{s}
only the upper endpoints of the considered passage times are shifted by
(s,0)
, while everything is shifted by
(s,0)
for
\overline{K}_{1,1+\theta}^{s}
.

We now explain the reason behind defining
\overline{K}_{1,1+\theta}^{s}
and
\hat{K}_{1,1+\theta}^{s}
. We will write
\mathbb{E}[K_{1,1+\theta}]
as an integral of
\mathbb{E}[\overline{K}^{s}_{1,1+\theta}]
over
s
.
We will show that
\overline{K}^{s}_{1,1+\theta}=\hat{K}^{s}_{1,1+\theta}
under certain coalescence events, so it suffices to consider the expectation of some integral of
\hat{K}^{s}_{1,1+\theta}
over
s
, which can be written as
\lambda_{(1,1+\theta]}([-\delta,\delta])
.

We first define the coalescence event under which
\overline{K}^{s}_{1,1+\theta}=\hat{K}^{s}_{1,1+\theta}
holds.
Let
\delta>0
be a small number. Let
\mathcal{E}_{coal}
be the following event (see Figure
17
for an illustration): there exist some
t_{-},t_{+}>0
,
x_{-},x_{+}\in\mathbb{R}
, such that for any
x\in(-1-\delta,-1+\delta)
and
p\in[-1,1]\times[1,1+\theta]
, there is
\pi_{(x,0;p)}(t_{-})=x_{-}
; and for any
x\in(1-\delta,1+\delta)
and
p\in[-1,1]\times[1,1+\theta]
, there is
\pi_{(x,0;p)}(t_{+})=x_{+}
.
Under
\mathcal{E}_{coal}
, for any
p_{1},p_{2}\in[-1,1]\times[1,1+\theta]
and
|s|<\delta
, we have

\mathcal{L}(-1,0;p_{1})-\mathcal{L}(-1,0;p_{2})=\mathcal{L}(-1+s,0;p_{1})-\mathcal{L}(-1+s,0;p_{2}).

This is because, from the coalescence of geodesics as illustrated by Figure
17
, we have that both sides would equal
\mathcal{L}(x_{-},t_{-};p_{1})-\mathcal{L}(x_{-},t_{-};p_{2})
.
Similarly,

\mathcal{L}(1,0;p_{1})-\mathcal{L}(1,0;p_{2})=\mathcal{L}(1+s,0;p_{1})-\mathcal{L}(1+s,0;p_{2}).

By taking the difference of the above two equations, letting
p_{1}=(-w+s,t)
,
p_{2}=(w+s,t)
, integrating over
t
from
1
to
1+\theta
, dividing both hand sides by
2w
, and sending
w\searrow 0
, we conclude that
\overline{K}^{s}_{1,1+\theta}=\hat{K}^{s}_{1,1+\theta}
under
\mathcal{E}_{coal}
.
We then have

2\delta\mathbb{E}[K_{1,1+\theta}]=\int_{-\delta}^{\delta}\mathbb{E}[\overline{K}^{s}_{1,1+\theta}]ds\geq\int_{-\delta}^{\delta}\mathbb{E}[\mathds{1}[\mathcal{E}_{coal}]\hat{K}^{s}_{1,1+\theta}]ds=\mathbb{E}[\mathds{1}[\mathcal{E}_{coal}]\int_{-\delta}^{\delta}\hat{K}^{s}_{1,1+\theta}ds].

(8.7)

It remains to show that (for appropriate choice of
\delta,\theta
) the last term is positive.
Note that as
\lambda_{(1,1+\theta]}
is absolutely continuous with respect to Lebesgue measure by Lemma
8.3
(almost surely for fixed
\theta
), we can write

\displaystyle\int_{-\delta}^{\delta}\hat{K}^{s}_{1,1+\theta}ds

\displaystyle=\lambda_{(1,1+\theta]}([-\delta,\delta])

\displaystyle=\int_{1}^{1+\theta}[(\mathcal{L}(-1,0;-\delta,t)-\mathcal{L}(1,0;-\delta,t))-(\mathcal{L}(-1,0;\delta,t)-\mathcal{L}(1,0;\delta,t))]dt.

We now find an event where the right hand side is positive.
Recall that for any
x_{1}<x_{2}
,
y_{1}<y_{2}
, and
s<t
,
\Dist_{(x_{1},x_{2})\to(y_{1},y_{2})}^{[s,t]}
is the event where
\pi_{(x_{1},s;y_{1},t)}
and
\pi_{(x_{2},s;y_{2},t)}
are unique and disjoint.
We then let
\mathcal{E}_{disj}=\Dist_{(-1,1)\to(-\delta,\delta)}^{[0,1]}
.
Then by Lemma
3.15
and spatial monotonicity of
\mathcal{D}
, and the fact that almost surely
\mathcal{L}
is continuous, we have that
\mathcal{E}_{disj}
implies
\int_{-\delta}^{\delta}\hat{K}^{s}_{1,1+\theta}ds>0
.

It remains to show that
\mathbb{P}[\mathcal{E}_{coal}\cap\mathcal{E}_{disj}]>0
.
The event
\mathcal{E}_{coal}
involves geodesics whose upper endpoints vary in the box
[-1,1]\times[1,1+\theta]
, and is not easy to analyze using the Airy line ensemble.
For this, we consider

\mathcal{E}_{coal}^{\prime}=(\Dist_{(-1-\delta,-1+\delta)\to(-2,2)}^{[0,1]}\cup\Dist_{(1-\delta,1+\delta)\to(-2,2)}^{[0,1]})^{c}

(see Figure
17
for an illustration).
The event
\mathcal{E}_{coal}^{\prime}
only involves geodesics from time
0
to time
1
, and can be written as an event of the Airy line ensemble. From this we can show that
\mathbb{P}[\mathcal{E}_{coal}^{\prime}\cap\mathcal{E}_{disj}]>0
. We leave the details to Lemma
C.1
.

By Lemma
3.11
, given
\mathcal{E}_{coal}^{\prime}
almost surely we have that
\mathcal{E}_{coal}
holds for some random
\theta>0
.
Indeed, by Lemma
3.11
we can find some
\theta>0
, such that for any
x\in(-1-\delta,-1+\delta)\cup(1-\delta,1+\delta)
and
p\in[-1,1]\times[1,1+\theta]
, we have
|\pi_{(x,0;p)}(1)|\leq 2
.
Then by ordering of geodesics, for any
t_{-},t_{+}
and
x_{-},x_{+}\in\mathbb{R}
such that
\pi_{(-1-\delta,0;-2,1)}(t_{-})=\pi_{(-1+\delta,0;2,1)}(t_{-})=x_{-}
and
\pi_{(1-\delta,0;-2,1)}(t_{+})=\pi_{(1+\delta,0;2,1)}(t_{+})=x_{+}
, they satisfy the properties in the definition of
\mathcal{E}_{coal}
above, so
\mathcal{E}_{coal}
holds.
Thus
\mathbb{P}[\mathcal{E}_{coal}^{\prime}\cap\mathcal{E}_{disj}]>0
implies that for some
\theta
small enough (depending on
\delta
), we have
\mathbb{P}[\mathcal{E}_{coal}\cap\mathcal{E}_{disj}]>0
.
We then have that
\mathbb{E}[\mathds{1}[\mathcal{E}_{coal}]\int_{-\delta}^{\delta}\hat{K}^{s}_{1,1+\theta}ds]>0
, which with (
8.7
) implies the conclusion.
∎

We need one more ingredient to finish the proof of Proposition
8.10
, namely a 0-1 law.
For any
r>0,
we let
\mathcal{F}_{r}
be the sigma algebra generated by
\mathcal{L}(x,s;y,t)
, for
0\leq s<t\leq r
.
Let
\mathcal{F}_{0+}=\cap_{r>0}\mathcal{F}_{r}
be the corresponding germ sigma-algebra.

Lemma 8.12
.

Any
\mathcal{F}_{0+}
-measurable event has probability
0
or
1
.

Proof.

We show that
\mathcal{L}(x,s;y,t)
is independent of
\mathcal{F}_{0+}
for any
(x,s;y,t)\in\mathbb{R}^{4}_{\uparrow}
.
If
s>0
or
t\leq 0
, this is obvious.
If
s=0
, we have
\mathcal{L}(x,s;y,t)=\lim_{\delta\searrow 0}\mathcal{L}(x,s+\delta;y,t)
, which also implies the statement, since each
\mathcal{L}(x,s+\delta;y,t)
is independent of
\mathcal{F}_{0+}
.
Now for
s<0<t
, we have the composition law

\mathcal{L}(x,s;y,t)=\sup_{z}\mathcal{L}(x,s;z,0)+\mathcal{L}(z,0;y,t).

Since
\mathcal{L}(x,s;z,0)
is independent of
\mathcal{F}_{0+}
, and that we have just shown that
\mathcal{L}(z,0;y,t)
is independent of
\mathcal{F}_{0+}
, we must have that the left hand side is also independent of
\mathcal{F}_{0+}
.
Thus the conclusion follows.
∎

(-1,0)
(-2^{-2(m+1)/3},0)
(2^{-2(m+1)/3},0)
(1,0)
(0,2^{-m-1})
(0,2^{-m})
(0,1)
(0,2)

Figure 18
:
An illustration of the proof of Proposition
8.10
: the black dashed lines indicate the passage times involved in
K_{1,2}
, and the blue dashed lines indicate the passage times involved in
K_{2^{-m-1},2^{-m}}
. The red dashed lines are rescaled versions of the black ones, which gives a rescaled version of
K_{1,2}
that lower bounds
K_{2^{-m-1},2^{-m}}
, implying that
\mathbb{P}[K_{2^{-m-1},2^{-m}}>0]\geq\mathbb{P}[K_{1,2}>0]
. By the 0-1 law for the germ sigma algebra
\mathcal{F}_{0+}
