, almost surely one can find some
m\in\mathbb{N}
with
K_{2^{-m-1},2^{-m}}>0
.

We can finally put things together to finish the proof of Proposition
8.10
.

Proof of Proposition
8.10
.

For each
m\in\mathbb{N},
we consider
K_{2^{-m-1},2^{-m}}
(recall its definition from (
8.3
)), which we assume to be well-defined and finite.
By the quadrangle inequality (Lemma
3.8
), we have

K_{2^{-m-1},2^{-m}}=\lim_{w\searrow 0}(2w)^{-1}\int_{2^{-m-1}}^{2^{-m}}\mathcal{D}(-w,t)-\mathcal{D}(w,t)dt\\
\geq\limsup_{w\searrow 0}(2w)^{-1}\int_{2^{-m-1}}^{2^{-m}}(\mathcal{L}(-2^{-2(m+1)/3},0;-w,t)-\mathcal{L}(2^{-2(m+1)/3},0;-w,t))\\
-(\mathcal{L}(-2^{-2(m+1)/3},0;w,t)-\mathcal{L}(2^{-2(m+1)/3},0;w,t))dt,

where (by scaling invariance) the right hand side has the same distribution as
2^{-2(m+1)/3}K_{1,2}
(see Figure
18
).
So we must have
\mathbb{P}[K_{2^{-m-1},2^{-m}}>0]\geq\mathbb{P}[K_{1,2}>0]
, which implies that

\mathbb{P}[\limsup_{m\to\infty}\mathds{1}[K_{2^{-m-1},2^{-m}}>0]=1]>0

by Lemma
8.11
.
Since
K_{2^{-m-1},2^{-m}}
is
\mathcal{F}_{2^{-m}}
measurable,
\limsup_{m\to\infty}\mathds{1}[K_{2^{-m-1},2^{-m}}>0]
is
\mathcal{F}_{0+}
measurable. Then by Lemma
8.12
we conclude that

\mathbb{P}[\limsup_{m\to\infty}\mathds{1}[K_{2^{-m-1},2^{-m}}>0]=1]=1,

which implies the conclusion.
∎

Appendix A
Transversal fluctuation in the directed landscape

In this appendix we prove Lemma
3.11
.
We first prove the following weaker version, which can be directly deduced from Lemma
3.9
.

Lemma A.1
.

For any
u=(x,s;y,t)\in\mathbb{R}_{\uparrow}^{4}
, any geodesic
\pi_{u}
, and
(s+t)/2\leq r<t
, we have

\left|\pi_{u}(r)-\frac{x(t-r)+y(r-s)}{t-s}\right|\\
<C(R+1)^{1/2}\log(R+2)^{2}(t-s)^{1/6}(t-r)^{1/2}\log^{2/3}(2(2\|u\|+2)^{3/2}/(t-r))\log^{1/3}(2\|u\|+2).

(A.1)

where
R
is the random number from Lemma
3.9
, and
C>0
is a universal constant.
Similar result holds when
s<r<(s+t)/2
by symmetry.

Proof.

Denote the right hand side of (
A.1
) by
D
.
Let
z=\pi_{u}(r)
, and
z^{\prime}=z-\frac{x(t-r)+y(r-s)}{t-s}
.
We argue by contradiction: we show that if
|z^{\prime}|\geq D
, we must have

\mathcal{L}(x,s;y,t)-(\mathcal{L}(x,s;z,r)+\mathcal{L}(z,r;y,t))>0,

(A.2)

which contradicts with the fact that
\pi_{u}
is a geodesic.
Indeed, by Lemma
3.9
, we have

\mathcal{L}(x,s;y,t)\geq-\frac{(x-y)^{2}}{t-s}-R(t-s)^{1/3}\log^{4/3}(2(\|u\|+2)^{3/2}/(t-s))\log^{2/3}(\|u\|+2),

-\mathcal{L}(x,s;z,r)\geq\frac{(x-z)^{2}}{r-s}-R(r-s)^{1/3}\log^{4/3}(2(2\|u\|+|z^{\prime}|+2)^{3/2}/(r-s))\log^{2/3}(2\|u\|+|z^{\prime}|+2),

-\mathcal{L}(z,r;y,t)\geq\frac{(z-y)^{2}}{t-r}-R(t-r)^{1/3}\log^{4/3}(2(2\|u\|+|z^{\prime}|+2)^{3/2}/(t-r))\log^{2/3}(2\|u\|+|z^{\prime}|+2).

(A.3)

Here we use that
\|(x,s;z,r)\|,\|(z,r;y,t)\|\leq 2\|u\|+|z^{\prime}|
.
By adding up these three inequalities, we have that the left hand side of (
A.2
) is lower bounded by

\frac{|z^{\prime}|^{2}(t-s)}{(r-s)(t-r)}-3R(t-s)^{1/3}\log^{4/3}(2(2\|u\|+|z^{\prime}|+2)^{3/2}/(t-r))\log^{2/3}(2\|u\|+|z^{\prime}|+2).

(A.4)

One can check that when
C
is large enough, for any
i\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
we have

\frac{(2^{i}D)^{2}(t-s)}{(r-s)(t-r)}>3R(t-s)^{1/3}\log^{4/3}(2(2\|u\|+2^{i+1}D+2)^{3/2}/(t-r))\log^{2/3}(2\|u\|+2^{i+1}D+2),

so (
A.4
)
>0
when
|z^{\prime}|\in[2^{i}D,2^{i+1}D]
. Thus (
A.4
)
>0
whenever
|z^{\prime}|\geq D
, and the conclusion follows.
∎

To upgrade this to Lemma
3.11
, we need more refined estimates in a compact subset of
\mathbb{R}_{\uparrow}^{4}
. These can be obtained using Lemma
3.10
.

Lemma A.2
.

Let
K
be a compact subset of
\mathbb{R}_{\uparrow}^{4}
, and
R
be the maximum of the random numbers from Lemma
3.9
and Lemma
3.10
for
K
.
For any
u=(x,s;y,t)\in\mathbb{R}_{\uparrow}^{4}
, any geodesic
\pi_{u}
, and
(s+t)/2\leq r<t
, such that
(x,s;\pi_{u}(r),r),u\in K
,
we must have

\left|\pi_{u}(r)-\frac{x(t-r)+y(r-s)}{t-s}\right|<C(R+1)^{2/3}\log(R+2)^{2}(t-r)^{2/3}\log^{2}(C(1+(t-r)^{-1})),

(A.5)

where
C>0
is a constant depending only on
K
.
Similar result holds when
s<r<(s+t)/2
by symmetry.

Proof.

This proof is similar to the proof of the previous corollary.
We now denote the right hand side of (
A.5
) by
D
, and define
z,z^{\prime}
as before.
We still show that if
|z^{\prime}|\geq D
, the inequality (
A.2
) holds.

Note that (
A.3
) still holds.
By Lemma
3.10
, we have

\left(\mathcal{L}(x,s;y,t)+\frac{(x-y)^{2}}{t-s}\right)-\left(\mathcal{L}(x,s;z,r)+\frac{(x-z)^{2}}{r-s}\right)\\
\geq-R\left((t-r)^{1/3}\log^{2/3}(1+(t-r)^{-1})+|y-z|^{1/2}\log^{1/2}(1+|y-z|^{-1})\right)

Adding this to (
A.3
), we get that the left hand side of (
A.2
) is lower bounded by

\frac{|z^{\prime}|^{2}(t-s)}{(r-s)(t-r)}-R(t-r)^{1/3}\log^{4/3}(2(2\|u\|+|z^{\prime}|+2)^{3/2}/(t-r))\log^{2/3}(2\|u\|+|z^{\prime}|+2)\\
-R\left((t-r)^{1/3}\log^{2/3}(1+(t-r)^{-1})+|y-z|^{1/2}\log^{1/2}(1+|y-z|^{-1})\right).

Below we let
C>0
denote a constant depending on
K
, and its value can change from line to line.
Then we have
\|u\|<C
, and
|y-z|<C|z^{\prime}|+C|t-r|
, so the above can be lower bounded by

\frac{|z^{\prime}|^{2}}{t-r}-CR(t-r)^{1/3}\log^{2}(C(1+|z^{\prime}|)/(t-r))-CR(|z^{\prime}|^{1/2}+(t-r)^{1/3})\log^{2/3}(C(1+(t-r)^{-1})).

(A.6)

When
|z^{\prime}|>D
, the derivative of (
A.6
) with respect to
|z^{\prime}|
is
>0
; and when
|z^{\prime}|=D
, (
A.6
) is positive. Thus we have that (
A.6
) is positive whenever
|z^{\prime}|>D
, and the conclusion follows.
∎

Proof of Lemma
3.11
.

We let
C,c>0
denote large and small universal constants, and the values may change from line to line.
Let
K=([-1,1]\times[0,1])\times([-1,1]\times[2,3])
, and

K_{+}=\{\left(x,s;\frac{x(t-r)+y(r-s)}{t-s},r\right):|x|,|y|\leq 2,s\in[0,1],t\in[2,3],r\in[(s+t)/2,t]\}.

Let
R_{1}
be the random number from Lemma
3.9
, and
R_{2}
be the random number from Lemma
3.10
with
K_{+}
.
For any
M>2
, if
R_{1},R_{2}<M
, by Lemma
A.1
and Lemma
A.2
for
K_{+}
, for any
u=(x,s;y,t)\in K
and
r\in[(s+t)/2,t]
, we have

\left|\pi_{u}(r)-\frac{x(t-r)+y(r-s)}{t-s}\right|<CM^{1/2}\log(M)^{2}(t-r)^{1/2}\log^{2/3}(1+(t-r)^{-1}),

and unless the left hand below side is
>1
, we also have

\left|\pi_{u}(r)-\frac{x(t-r)+y(r-s)}{t-s}\right|<CM^{2/3}\log(M)^{2}(t-r)^{2/3}\log^{2}((1+(t-r)^{-1})).

Denote the right hand sides of the above two estimates by
D_{1}
and
D_{2}
, respectively. Then if
D_{1}\leq 1
, we can bound
\left|\pi_{u}(r)-\frac{x(t-r)+y(r-s)}{t-s}\right|
by
D_{2}
; otherwise we can bound it by
D_{1}^{4/3}
.
Thus we always have

\left|\pi_{u}(r)-\frac{x(t-r)+y(r-s)}{t-s}\right|<CM^{2/3}\log(M)^{8/3}(t-r)^{2/3}\log^{2}((1+(t-r)^{-1})).

These imply that, if we denote

R_{*}=\sup_{u=(x,s;y,t)\in K,r\in[(s+t)/2,t]}\frac{\left|\pi_{u}(r)-\frac{x(t-r)+y(r-s)}{t-s}\right|}{(t-r)^{2/3}\log^{2}((1+(t-r)^{-1}))},

then
\mathbb{P}[R_{*}>M]<Ce^{-cM^{9/4}\log(M)^{-4}}
for any
M>0
.

Now for any
k,a,b,d\in\mathbb{Z}
, we consider

K[k,a,b,d]=\{(2^{2k/3}(x+b/10),2^{k}(s+a/10);2^{2k/3}(y+d/10),2^{k}(t+a/10)):(x,s;y,t)\in K\},

and

R[k,a,b,d]=\sup_{u=(x,s;y,t)\in K[k,a,b,d],r\in[(s+t)/2,t]}\frac{\left|\pi_{u}(r)-\frac{x(t-r)+y(r-s)}{t-s}\right|}{(t-r)^{2/3}\log^{2}((1+2^{k}(t-r)^{-1}))}.

By skew-shift invariance and scaling invariance of the directed landscape, each
R[k,a,b,d]
has the same distribution as
R_{*}
. Since
\cup_{k,a,b,d\in\mathbb{Z}}K[k,a,b,d]=(x,s;y,t)=\mathbb{R}_{\uparrow}^{4}
, we can take

R=C\sup_{k,a,b,d\in\mathbb{Z}}\frac{R[k,a,b,d]}{\log(1+|k|+|a|+|b|+|d|)}.

Then (
3.1
) holds, and by a union bound over all
k
,
a
,
b
,
d\in\mathbb{Z}
we have the desired bound on the upper tail of
R
.
∎

Appendix B
Occupation time in a tube

q

q
I
I
J
\overline{J}_{-}
J_{+}

J_{-}
\overline{J}_{+}

Figure 19
:

An illustration of the set
\Phi_{I,J}^{\prime}
: for
(p;q)=(x,s;y,t)\in\Phi_{I,J}^{\prime}
, either
s\in\overline{J}_{-}
and
t\in J_{+}
, as illustrated in the left diagram; or
s\in J_{-}
and
t\in\overline{J}_{+}
, as illustrated in the right diagram. The spatial coordinates
x,y
are always in
I
.

As promised before, in this appendix we provide the proof of Lemma
7.5
. Now, recalling the set
\Phi_{I,J}
and the random variable
W_{I,J}
appearing in the discussion preceding the statement of the lemma, it would be simpler to consider the following, similar but different, objects.
Namely, for each interval
J=[s,t]
, let
J_{-}=(-\infty,s]
,
J_{+}=[t,\infty)
, and
\overline{J}_{-}=[2s-t,s]
,
\overline{J}_{+}=[t,2t-s]
.
We define

\Phi_{I,J}^{\prime}=\{(p;q)\in\mathbb{R}^{4}_{\uparrow}:p\in I\times J_{-},q\in I\times\overline{J}_{+}\}\cup\{(p;q)\in\mathbb{R}^{4}_{\uparrow}:p\in I\times\overline{J}_{-},q\in I\times J_{+}\}.

See Figure
19
for an illustration.
Then we let

W_{I,J}^{\prime}=\int_{J}\sup_{u\in\Phi_{I,J}^{\prime}}\mathds{1}[\pi_{u}(r)\in I,\pi_{u}\text{is unique}]dr.

Note that in the definition of
W_{I,J}^{\prime}
, the supremum over
u\in\Phi_{I,J}^{\prime}
is inside the integral over
J
. This upper bounds the occupation measure in
I\times J
for any unique geodesic
\pi_{u}
(with
u\in\Phi_{I,J}^{\prime}
).

We then have the following crucial estimate.

Lemma B.1
.

We have
\mathbb{E}[W_{I,J}^{\prime}]<C_{1}\mathscr{L}(I)\mathscr{L}(J)^{1/3}
for some universal constant
C_{1}
.

An upper bound of the order of
\mathscr{L}(I)\mathscr{L}(J)^{1/3}
can be explained by a simple scaling argument as follows.
First, we expect
\mathbb{E}[W_{I,J}^{\prime}]
to be linear in
\mathscr{L}(I)
, at least when
\mathscr{L}(I)
is small (compared to
\mathscr{L}(J)^{2/3}
).
Second, by scaling invariance of
\mathcal{L}
, when
\mathscr{L}(J)
is multiplied by
h>0
and
\mathscr{L}(I)
is multiplied by
h^{2/3}
,
\mathbb{E}[W_{I,J}^{\prime}]
should be multiplied by
h
.

Compared to
\mathbb{E}[W_{I,J}]
, the expectation
\mathbb{E}[W_{I,J}^{\prime}]
is easier to bound: since the time coordinates of the endpoints are outside or at the boundary of
J
(whereas in the definition of
\Phi_{I,J}
in (
7.3
), the endpoints are on the boundary of
I\times J
), we can directly bound
\mathbb{E}[W_{I,J}^{\prime}]
using translation invariance of
\mathcal{L}
, and bounds on the number of intersections that geodesics can have with a line (from Lemma
3.12
).

This is then used in an inductive proof, where we work on different dyadic scales. The definition of
\Phi_{I,J}^{\prime}
enables one to bound the occupation time for a geodesic in different time intervals.

We also remark that, actually, the same upper bound holds for the expectation of the integral
\int_{J}\sup_{u\in\Phi_{I,J}^{\prime},\pi_{u}\text{ is any geodesic}}\mathds{1}[\pi_{u}(r)\in I]dr
; i.e., the uniqueness requirement of the geodesic
\pi_{u}
can be dropped and all possible ones can be considered instead. The uniqueness requirement is inherited from Lemma
3.12
, and can actually be removed (see the discussions after Lemma
3.12
).

We now prove Lemma
7.5
assuming Lemma
B.1
, and using an induction argument. A key ingredient is a dyadic decomposition of the time interval
[0,1]
.
Further, the exponential tail (in addition to bounding the expectations) in Lemma
7.5
is proved via a concentration estimate, using independence of the directed landscape across different time increments.

W^{\prime}_{[-w,w],[2\cdot 2^{-2},3\cdot 2^{-2}]}

\pi_{u}

:

An illustration of (
B.3
) in the proof of Lemma
7.5
: for
u\in\Phi_{[-w,w],[0,1]}
, we can cover the domain of
\pi_{u}
by intervals
J
(that are disjoint except for the endpoints), such that for
J
other than the first and last one,
J\in\Lambda
and the time of
\pi_{u}\in[-w,w]
in
J
is at most
W^{\prime}_{[-w,w],J}
; and for
J
being the first or last one,
J\in\partial\Lambda
and the time of
\pi_{u}\in[-w,w]
in
J
is at most
W_{[-w,w],J}
.

Proof of Lemma
7.5
.

We use induction in
w
, and prove the following estimates: there exist constants
A,C,c>0
, such that for any
w>0
, there is

\mathbb{E}[W_{[-w,w],[0,1]}]<A^{2/3+0.01}w,

(B.1)

and for any
M>0
,

\mathbb{P}[W_{[-w,w],[0,1]}>AMw]<Ce^{-cM}.

(B.2)

We assume that
A,C
are large enough and
c
is small enough, and the precise relation between them is evident from the proof.

The case when
w>A^{-2/3}
is obvious (for
B.2
just take
C
large enough), so we assume that
w\leq A^{-2/3}
.
Now to use an induction argument, we assume that the inequalities hold when
w
is replaced by any number
\geq 2^{2/3}w
.

We first prove (
B.1
).
Take
g\in\mathbb{N}
such that
2^{g}\leq A<2^{g+1}
. Let
\Lambda
consist of all intervals
[a2^{-b},(a+1)2^{-b}]
such that
a,b\in\mathbb{Z}
,
0\leq b\leq g
, and
0\leq a2^{-b}<(a+1)2^{-b}\leq 1
.
Let
\partial\Lambda
consist of all intervals
[a2^{-g},(a+1)2^{-g}]
such that
a\in\mathbb{Z}
,
0\leq a2^{-g}<(a+1)2^{-g}\leq 1
.
We have that

W_{[-w,w],[0,1]}\leq\sum_{J\in\Lambda}W_{[-w,w],J}^{\prime}+2\max_{J\in\partial\Lambda}W_{[-w,w],J}.

(B.3)

This is via a dyadic decomposition of
\pi_{u}
for
u\in\Phi_{[-w,w],[0,1]}
, as illustrated by Figure
20
.The precise details of the proof of (
B.3
) are left for the moment for later.

By Lemma
B.1
, for each
J=[a2^{-b},(a+1)2^{-b}]\in\Lambda
,
we have
\mathbb{E}[W_{[-w,w],J}^{\prime}]<2C_{1}w2^{-b/3}
, thus

\sum_{J\in\Lambda}\mathbb{E}[W_{[-w,w],J}^{\prime}]<\sum_{b=0}^{g}\sum_{a=0}^{2^{b}-1}2C_{1}w2^{-b/3}=\sum_{b=0}^{g}2C_{1}w2^{2b/3}<10C_{1}w2^{2g/3}.

So we have

\mathbb{E}[W_{[-w,w],[0,1]}]<10C_{1}w2^{2g/3}+2\mathbb{E}[\max_{J\in\partial\Lambda}W_{[-w,w],J}].

By induction hypothesis and scale invariance, for each
J\in\partial\Lambda
we have

\mathbb{P}[W_{[-w,w],J}>AMw2^{-g/3}]<Ce^{-cM}.

Thus we have

\mathbb{E}[\max_{J\in\partial\Lambda}W_{[-w,w],J}]<c^{-1}(\log(2^{g})+C)Aw2^{-g/3},

and this implies that

\mathbb{E}[W_{[-w,w],[0,1]}]<(10C_{1}+4c^{-1}(\log(A)+C))A^{2/3}w<A^{2/3+0.01}w

by requiring
A
being large enough so that
A^{0.01}>(10C_{1}+4c^{-1}(\log(A)+C))
.

We next prove (
B.2
).
Denote
m=\lfloor A^{1/10}\rfloor
.
For each
1\leq i\leq m
let
W_{i}=W_{[-w,w],[(i-1)/m,i/m]}
, and we have
W_{[-w,w],[0,1]}\leq\sum_{i=1}^{m}W_{i}
.
We shall get the exponential tail of
W_{[-w,w],[0,1]}/(Aw)
by considering the exponential moments of each
W_{i}
, using the mutual independence of the latter across
i,
and applying the induction hypothesis for them.

We write

\sum_{i=1}^{m}W_{i}\leq\sum_{i=1}^{m}W_{i}\mathds{1}[W_{i}\leq Aw/m]+\sum_{i=1}^{m}W_{i}\mathds{1}[W_{i}>Aw/m]\leq Aw+\sum_{i=1}^{m}W_{i}\mathds{1}[W_{i}>Aw/m].

When
A
is large enough, we have
m\geq 2
, so we can apply the induction hypothesis and use scaling invariance to conclude that

\mathbb{E}[W_{i}]\leq A^{2/3+0.01}wm^{-1/3}\quad\quad\text{and}\,\,\quad\quad\mathbb{P}[W_{i}>MAwm^{-1/3}]<Ce^{-cM},

for any
M>0
.
From these bounds we expect that the sum of
W_{i}
should have exponential tail at the order of
Aw
, and we analyze it as follows.
Let
X_{i}=\frac{m^{1/3}}{Aw}W_{i}\mathds{1}[W_{i}>Aw/m]
, then for any
M>0
, by the exponential tail we have

\mathbb{P}[X_{i}>M]<Ce^{-cM}.

Using Markov’s inequality, for any
M>0
we also have

\mathbb{P}[X_{i}>M]\leq\mathbb{P}\left[W_{i}>Aw/m\right]<\mathbb{E}[W_{i}]/(Aw/m)\leq A^{-1/3+0.01}m^{2/3}.

Take
M_{*}>0
such that
Ce^{-cM_{*}}=A^{-1/3+0.01}m^{2/3}
.
Take
a=c/10
. We then have

\mathbb{E}[e^{aX_{i}}]=1+\int_{0}^{\infty}ae^{aM}\mathbb{P}[X_{i}>M]dM\\
<1+\int_{0}^{M_{*}}ae^{aM}A^{-1/3+0.01}m^{2/3}dM+\int_{M_{*}}^{\infty}ae^{aM}Ce^{-cM}dM<1+2e^{aM_{*}}A^{-1/3+0.01}m^{2/3},

and hence

\mathbb{E}[e^{a\sum_{i=1}^{m}X_{i}}]<(1+2e^{aM_{*}}A^{-1/3+0.01}m^{2/3})^{m}<e^{C(A^{-1/3+0.01}m^{2/3})^{1/2}m},

where the last inequality follows by using

2e^{aM_{*}}A^{-1/3+0.01}m^{2/3}<C(A^{-1/3+0.01}m^{2/3})^{1/2}m,

which is satisfied by taking
C>10
.
Then we have

\mathbb{P}[\sum_{i=1}^{m}W_{i}>AMw]\leq\mathbb{P}[\sum_{i=1}^{m}X_{i}>m^{1/3}(M-1)]<e^{C(A^{-1/3+0.01}m^{2/3})^{1/2}m-am^{1/3}(M-1)}<Ce^{-cM},

where the last inequality is true by taking
A
large enough (so that
am^{1/3}>2c
and
e^{Cm^{-1/3}}<C
), for
M>2
.
For
0<M\leq 2
we also have (
B.2
), by taking
c
small and
C
large.
∎

Proof of (
B.3
).

Consider any
u=(x,s;y,t)\in\Phi_{[-w,w],[0,1]}
. The general strategy is to show that
[s,t]
can be covered the union of (1) two intervals in
\partial\Lambda
(around the endpoints) and (2) several intervals in
\Lambda
that are contained in
[s,t]
.
Then we can bound
W[u,[-w,w]]
by the sum of
W_{[-w,w],J}
or
W_{[-w,w],J}^{\prime}
for
J
among these intervals.

For each
i\in\mathbb{N}
, we let
a_{i}^{-},a_{i}^{+}\in\mathbb{Z}
be the numbers with
a_{i}^{-}2^{-i}\leq s<(a_{i}^{-}+1)2^{-i}
, and
(a_{i}^{+}-1)2^{-i}<t\leq a_{i}^{+}2^{-i}
.
Consider collections of intervals:

\partial\Lambda_{u}=\{[a_{g}^{-}2^{-g},(a_{g}^{-}+1)2^{-g}],[(a_{g}^{+}-1)2^{-g},a_{g}^{+}2^{-g}]\},

and

\Lambda_{u}=\{[(a_{i}^{-}+1)2^{-i},(a_{i}^{-}+2)2^{-i}],[(a_{i}^{+}-2)2^{-i},(a_{i}^{+}-1)2^{-i}]:i\in\llbracket 1,g\rrbracket,a_{i}^{+}-a_{i}^{-}>2\}.

We note that
\partial\Lambda_{u}\subset\partial\Lambda
and
\Lambda_{u}\subset\Lambda
. We claim that the union of the intervals in
\partial\Lambda_{u}\cup\Lambda_{u}
covers
[s,t]
.
Indeed, if
a_{g}^{+}-a_{g}^{-}\leq 2
, the union of the intervals in
\partial\Lambda_{u}
covers
[s,t]
.
Otherwise, let
i_{*}
be the smallest integer with
a_{i_{*}}^{+}-a_{i_{*}}^{-}>2
, and we must have
1<i_{*}\leq g
.
We next show that the intervals in
\partial\Lambda_{u}\cup\Lambda_{u}
can be written in a sequence:

[a_{g}^{-}2^{-g},(a_{g}^{-}+1)2^{-g}],[(a_{g}^{-}+1)2^{-g},(a_{g}^{-}+2)2^{-g}],\cdots,[(a_{i_{*}}^{-}+1)^{-}2^{-i_{*}},(a_{i_{*}}^{-}+2)2^{-i_{*}}],\\
[(a_{i_{*}}^{+}-2)2^{-i_{*}},(a_{i_{*}}^{+}-1)2^{-i_{*}}],\cdots,[(a_{g}^{+}-2)2^{-g},(a_{g}^{+}-1)2^{-g}],[(a_{g}^{+}-1)2^{-g},a_{g}^{+}2^{-g}],

such that any two neighboring ones are not disjoint. Then the union of all these intervals is a closed interval.

1.

The facts that
[a_{g}^{-}2^{-g},(a_{g}^{-}+1)2^{-g}]\cap[(a_{g}^{-}+1)2^{-g},(a_{g}^{-}+2)2^{-g}]\neq\emptyset
and
[(a_{g}^{+}-2)2^{-g},(a_{g}^{+}-1)2^{-g}]\cap[(a_{g}^{+}-1)2^{-g},a_{g}^{+}2^{-g}]\neq\emptyset
are obvious.

2.

We must have that
a_{i_{*}}^{+}-a_{i_{*}}^{-}\leq 4
. This is because, otherwise we have
a_{i_{*}}^{+}2^{-i_{*}}-a_{i_{*}}^{-}2^{-i_{*}}>2^{-i_{*}+2}
. From the definitions (of
a_{i}^{-},a_{i}^{+}
) above we have

a_{i_{*}-1}^{-}2^{-i_{*}+1}\leq a_{i_{*}}^{-}2^{-i_{*}},\quad a_{i_{*}}^{+}2^{-i_{*}}\leq a_{i_{*}-1}^{+}2^{-i_{*}+1},

so
a_{i_{*}-1}^{+}2^{-i_{*}+1}-a_{i_{*}-1}^{-}2^{-i_{*}+1}>2^{-i_{*}+2}
, implying that
a_{i_{*}-1}^{+}-a_{i_{*}-1}^{-}>2
, which contradicts with the choice of
i_{*}
.
Thus we have
a_{i_{*}}^{+}-a_{i_{*}}^{-}=3
or
4
; and in each case we have that
[(a_{i_{*}}^{-}+1)^{-}2^{-i_{*}},(a_{i_{*}}^{-}+2)2^{-i_{*}}]
and
[(a_{i_{*}}^{+}-2)2^{-i_{*}},(a_{i_{*}}^{+}-1)2^{-i_{*}}]
are not disjoint.

3.

From the definition (of
a_{i}^{-},a_{i}^{+}
) above, for any
i\in\mathbb{N}
we have that

a_{i}^{-}2^{-i}\leq a_{i+1}^{-}2^{-i-1},\;(a_{i+1}^{-}+1)2^{-i-1}\leq(a_{i}^{-}+1)2^{-i},\;

so the intervals
[(a_{i}^{-}+1)^{-}2^{-i},(a_{i}^{-}+2)2^{-i}]
and
[(a_{i+1}^{-}+1)^{-}2^{-i-1},(a_{i+1}^{-}+2)2^{-i-1}]
are not disjoint.
Similarly, the intervals
[(a_{i}^{+}-2)2^{-i},(a_{i}^{+}-1)2^{-i}]
and
[(a_{i+1}^{+}-2)2^{-i-1},(a_{i+1}^{+}-1)2^{-i-1}]
are not disjoint.

From the above statements on non-disjointness of intervals in
\partial\Lambda_{u}\cup\Lambda_{u}
, we conclude that the union of these intervals is a closed interval. The union also contains
s
and
t
, so it must contain
[s,t]
.

Further, because of the way the definitions were set up we crucially now have the following (see Figure
20
).
For each
J\in\Lambda_{u}
, we have

\int_{J}\mathds{1}[\pi_{u}(r)\in[-w,w],\pi_{u}\text{ is unique}]dr\leq W_{[-w,w],J}^{\prime},

and for each
J\in\partial\Lambda_{u}
, we have

\int_{J\cap[s,t]}\mathds{1}[\pi_{u}(r)\in[-w,w],\pi_{u}\text{ is unique}]dr\leq W_{[-w,w],J}.

Thus we have

W[u,[-w,w]]\leq\sum_{J\in\Lambda}W_{[-w,w],J}^{\prime}+2\max_{J\in\partial\Lambda}W_{[-w,w],J},

and that (
B.3
) holds.
∎

It remains to prove the bound for
\mathbb{E}[W_{I,J}^{\prime}]
.
We would again do a dyadic decomposition, but now for the locations of the possible starting and ending points of the geodesics.

Proof of Lemma
B.1
.

As
\mathcal{L}
is translation and scaling invariant we can assume that
J=[0,1]
, and
I=[-w,w]
for some
w>0
.

For each
0\leq t\leq 1
, we consider

A_{t}=\sup_{u=(p;q):p\in[-w,w]\times[-1,0],q\in[-w,w]\times[1,\infty)}\mathds{1}[\pi_{u}(t)\in[-w,w],\pi_{u}\text{is unique}],

and

A_{t}^{\prime}=\sup_{u=(p;q):p\in[-w,w]\times(-\infty,0],q\in[-w,w]\times[1,2]}\mathds{1}[\pi_{u}(t)\in[-w,w],\pi_{u}\text{is unique}].

We then have

\mathbb{E}[W_{[-w,w],[0,1]}^{\prime}]\leq\int_{0}^{1}\mathbb{E}[A_{t}]+\mathbb{E}[A_{t}^{\prime}]dt=2\int_{0}^{1}\mathbb{E}[A_{t}]dt\leq 4w^{3/2}+2\int_{w^{3/2}}^{1-w^{3/2}}\mathbb{E}[A_{t}]dt,

where the equality is by the fact that
\int_{0}^{1}\mathbb{E}[A_{t}]dt=\int_{0}^{1}\mathbb{E}[A_{t}^{\prime}]dt
, due to symmetry.
We next show that, there is a universal constant
C_{2}
, such that for any
w^{3/2}<t<1/2
,

\mathbb{E}[A_{t}]<C_{2}wt^{-2/3}

(B.4)

and for
1/2<t<1-w^{3/2}

\mathbb{E}[A_{t}]<C_{2}w(1-t)^{-2/3}.

(B.5)

Given the above, integrating over
t
using (
B.4
) and (
B.5
) completes the proof.
Thus it remains to prove these two inequalities.

We prove (
B.4
) first.
The general idea is again a dyadic decomposition, but now for the location of the endpoints. Towards this we define the following sets.
For each
a\in\mathbb{N}
with
a\leq\lceil-\log_{2}(t)\rceil+1
, we denote

A_{t,a}=\sup_{u=(p;q):p\in[-w,w]\times[-(2^{a}-1)t,-(2^{a-1}-1)t],q\in[-w,w]\times[1,\infty)}\mathds{1}[\pi_{u}(t)\in[-w,w],\pi_{u}\text{is unique}].

Then we have
\mathbb{E}[A_{t}]\leq\sum_{a=1}^{\lceil-\log_{2}(t)\rceil+1}\mathbb{E}[A_{t,a}]
.
To bound each
\mathbb{E}[A_{t,a}]
, we use translation invariance.
Denote

B_{t,a}=|[-(2^{a}t)^{2/3},(2^{a}t)^{2/3}]\cap\\
\{\pi_{u}(t):u=(p;y,(2^{a-3}+1)t),p\in[-(2^{a}t)^{2/3},(2^{a}t)^{2/3}]\times[-(2^{a}-1)t,-(2^{a-1}-1)t],\pi_{u}\text{is unique}\}|.

In words, for
B_{t,a}
, we allow the lower endpoint to be in a wider box (compared to
[-w,w]\times[-(2^{a}-1)t,-(2^{a-1}-1)t]
in
A_{t,a}
), and the upper endpoint to vary in the line
\mathbb{R}\times\{(2^{a-3}+1)t\}
.
Note that since
(2^{a-3}+1)t\leq 1
, we can bound
A_{t,a}
by

\sup_{u=(p;y,(2^{a-3}+1)t):p\in[-w,w]\times[-(2^{a}-1)t,-(2^{a-1}-1)t]}\mathds{1}[\pi_{u}(t)\in[-w,w],\pi_{u}\text{is unique}].

Also note that
[-(2^{a}t)^{2/3},(2^{a}t)^{2/3}]
contains
\lfloor(2^{a}t)^{2/3}/w\lfloor
non-overlaping translations of
[-w,w]
.
Then

\mathbb{E}[A_{t,a}]\leq\lfloor(2^{a}t)^{2/3}/w\lfloor^{-1}\mathbb{E}[B_{t,a}]\leq 2w(2^{a}t)^{-2/3}\mathbb{E}[B_{t,a}]

by translation invariance.
We then have

\mathbb{E}[A_{t}]\leq\sum_{a=1}^{\lceil-\log_{2}(t)\rceil+1}\mathbb{E}[A_{t,a}]\leq\sum_{a=1}^{\lceil-\log_{2}(t)\rceil+1}2w(2^{a}t)^{-2/3}\mathbb{E}[B_{t,a}]=\sum_{a=1}^{\lceil-\log_{2}(t)\rceil+1}2w(2^{a}t)^{-2/3}\mathbb{E}[B]\\
<10wt^{-2/3}\mathbb{E}[B],

where

B=|[-1,1]\cap\\
\{\pi_{u}(0):u=(p;y,1/8),p\in[-1,1]\times[-1,-1/2],\pi_{u}\text{is unique}\}|,

and by translation and scaling invariance we have each
\mathbb{E}[B_{t,a}]=\mathbb{E}[B]
. Finally, by the following claim we get (
B.4
).

Claim B.2
.

\mathbb{E}[B]
is finite.

Proof of the claim.

Take
M>0
large enough.
Let
\mathcal{E}_{M}
denote the event

|\{\pi_{(x,-1/4;y,1/8)}(0):|x|,|y|\leq M^{0.01},\pi_{(x,-1/4;y,1/8)}\text{is unique}\}|\geq M.

By Lemma
3.12
, and translation and scaling invariance of the directed landscape, we have that
\mathbb{P}[\mathcal{E}_{M}]\leq Ce^{-cM^{1/384}}
for some universal constants
C,c>0
.

We next take
R
from Lemma
3.11
. We show that
\{B>M\}\cap\{R\leq M^{0.001}\}\subset\mathcal{E}_{M}
.
Indeed, by Lemma
3.11
, if
\pi_{(x,s;y,1/8)}(0)\in[-1,1]
for some
y\in\mathbb{R}
,
x\in[-1,1]
,
s\in[-1,-1/2]
, we must have that
|y|<C(R+1)\log^{3}(R+1)
, and
|\pi_{(x,s;y,1/8)}(-1/4)|<C(R+1)\log^{3}(R+1)
, for some universal constant
C>0
.
Thus if
B>M
, we can find
x_{1},\cdots,x_{M}
and
y_{1},\cdots,y_{M}
, all in the interval
[-C(R+1)\log^{3}(R+1),C(R+1)\log^{3}(R+1)]
, such that for each
1\leq i\leq M
,
\pi_{(x_{i},-1/4;y_{i},1/8)}
is unique; and
\pi_{(x_{i},-1/4;y_{i},1/8)}(0)
are mutually different and are all in
[-1,1]
.
If (in addition)
R\leq M^{0.001}
, we must have that
C(R+1)\log^{3}(R+1)<M^{0.01}
, so
\mathcal{E}_{M}
holds.

Thus we have
\{B>M\}\cap\{R\leq M^{0.001}\}\subset\mathcal{E}_{M}
, which implies that
\mathbb{P}[B>M]<\mathbb{P}[R>M^{0.001}]+\mathbb{P}[\mathcal{E}_{M}]
.
Then by
\mathbb{P}[\mathcal{E}_{M}]\leq Ce^{-cM^{1/384}}
, and the bound on
\mathbb{P}[R>M^{0.001}]
from Lemma
3.11
, the conclusion follows.
∎

The proof of (
B.5
) is similar. Denote

\hat{A}_{t}=\sup_{u=(p;q):p\in[-w,w]\times[-1,0],q\in[-w,w]\times[1,2]}\mathds{1}[\pi_{u}(t)\in[-w,w],\pi_{u}\text{is unique}]

and

\overline{A}_{t}=\sup_{u=(p;q):p\in[-w,w]\times[-1,0],q\in[-w,w]\times[2,\infty)}\mathds{1}[\pi_{u}(t)\in[-w,w],\pi_{u}\text{is unique}].

We the have
\mathbb{E}[A_{t}]\leq\mathbb{E}[\hat{A}_{t}]+\mathbb{E}[\overline{A}_{t}]\leq\mathbb{E}[A_{1-t}]+\mathbb{E}[\overline{A}_{t}]
.
Thus by (
B.4
) it suffices to bound
\mathbb{E}[\overline{A}_{t}]
.
Via translation invariance, we have
\mathbb{E}[\overline{A}_{t}]<2w\mathbb{E}[B]
. Thus by Claim
B.2
we get (
B.5
).
∎

Appendix C
Estimates using the Airy line ensemble

In this appendix we include the arguments left from the proof of Lemma
8.11
.
Recall the events
\mathcal{E}_{coal}^{\prime}
and
\mathcal{E}_{disj}
appearing there and that it remained to prove the following result.

Lemma C.1
.

We have
\mathbb{P}[\mathcal{E}_{coal}^{\prime}\cap\mathcal{E}_{disj}]>0
.

To prove this we use the connection between the directed landscape and the Airy line ensemble
\mathcal{P}=\{\mathcal{P}_{n}\}_{n=1}^{\infty}
.
As was the key observation in
[
DOV18
]
and also later exploited in
[
DZ21
]
, marginals of the directed landscape can be obtained from last passage percolation across
\mathcal{P}
, and the disjointness of geodesics in the directed landscape can be reduced to disjointness of geodesics across
\mathcal{P}
.
What makes it useful to work with
\mathcal{P}
is a resampling invariance property introduced and termed as the Brownian Gibbs property in
[
CH14
]
. Informally, this implies that fixing
\{\mathcal{P}_{n}\}_{n=2}^{\infty}
and
\mathcal{P}_{1}
outside some interval; the conditional law of the latter is simply given by a Brownian bridge joining the values at the endpoints of the intervals and further conditioned to avoid
\mathcal{P}_{2}.

Given this, we will reduce computing probabilities of events about coalescence properties of geodesics to certain Brownian bridge computations.

We need a bit of preparation to implement the above strategy. First, as
\mathcal{P}
has infinitely many lines, the last passage times and geodesics across it are not completely straightforward to define; for this we quote the setup developed in
[
DZ21
]
, in Section
C.1
.
To study the disjointness of geodesics, we will need to understand last passage percolation across
\mathcal{P}
whose ending point lives on the second line
\mathcal{P}_{2}
; we call such optimal paths the “second geodesics” and show their existence using the framework of “disjoint optimizers” from
[
DZ21
]
, in Section
C.2
.
The proof of Lemma
C.1
is finally given in Section
C.3
.

5
4
3
2
1
(x,5)
(y,1)

Figure 21
:
An illstration of a non-increasing càdlàg function across
\mathcal{P}
from
(x,5)
to
(y,1)
.

C.1
Parabolic path across the Airy line ensemble: weight, geodesics, and disjointness

For any
x\leq y
, and any non-increasing càdlàg function
\pi:[x,y]\to\mathbb{N}
, we define the
weight of
\pi
across
\mathcal{P}
as

\|\pi\|_{\mathcal{P}}=\sum_{i=\pi(y)}^{\pi(x)}\mathcal{P}_{i}(y_{i})-\mathcal{P}_{i}(x_{i}),

where
x_{i}=\inf\{z:\pi(z)\leq i\}
and
y_{i}=\sup\{z:\pi(z)\geq i\}
.
Now, for any
x\leq y
and
n\geq m\in\mathbb{N}
, we define the
last passage value from
(x,n)
to
(y,m)
as

\mathcal{P}[(x,n)\to(y,m)]=\sup_{\pi}\|\pi\|_{\mathcal{P}},

where the supremum is over all non-increasing càdlàg functions
\pi
on
[x,y]
, with
\pi(x)\leq n
and
\pi(y)\geq m
(see Figure
21
). Continuity of
\mathcal{P}
and compactness of the set of functions ensures that this supremum is always attained.

We let
\mathcal{S}:\mathbb{R}^{2}\to\mathbb{R}
be the Airy sheet constructed in
[
DOV18
]
, which is the fixed time marginal of the directed landscape.
Namely, the function
(x,y)\mapsto\mathcal{L}(x,0;y,1)
has the same distribution as
\mathcal{S}
.
It can be coupled with
\mathcal{P}
such that

•

\mathcal{S}(0,y)=\mathcal{P}_{1}(y)
for
y\in\mathbb{R}
.

•

For
x>0,z_{1}<z_{2}
, we have

\mathcal{S}(x,z_{1})-\mathcal{S}(x,z_{2})=\lim_{y\to-\infty}\mathcal{P}[(y,\pi(y))\to(z_{1},1)]-\mathcal{P}[(y,\pi(y))\to(z_{2},1)],

(C.1)

where
\pi:(-\infty,z_{1}]\to\mathbb{N}
is any non-increasing càdlàg function, such that
\lim_{y\to-\infty}\frac{\pi(y)}{2y^{2}}=x
.

See
[
DOV18
, Definition 1.2]
and
[
DZ21
, Definition 2.20]
; also see
[
DZ21
, Lemma 2.21]
for the second property.
Below we always assume the coupling between
\mathcal{S}
and
\mathcal{P}
.

A
parabolic path
or
path
across
\mathcal{P}
from
x\geq 0
to
z\in\mathbb{R}
is a nonincreasing càdlàg function
\pi:(-\infty,z]\to\mathbb{N}
such that

\lim_{y\to-\infty}\frac{\pi(y)}{2y^{2}}=x.

We then define the weight of
\pi
on
\mathcal{P}
as

\|\pi\|_{\mathcal{P}}=\mathcal{S}(x,z)+\lim_{y\to-\infty}\|\pi|_{[y,z]}\|_{\mathcal{P}}-\mathcal{P}[(y,\pi(y))\to(z,1)].

(C.2)

We note that we use the same notation
\|\cdot\|_{\mathcal{P}}
to denote weight across
\mathcal{P}
for both a non-increasing càdlàg function in a finite interval, and a parabolic path.
The quantity
\|\pi|_{[y,z]}\|_{\mathcal{P}}-\mathcal{P}[(y,\pi(y))\to(z,1)]
can be understood as the “loss of weight” of
\pi|_{[y,z]}
, versus the optimal path from
(y,\pi(y))
to
(z,1)
; whereas the optimal parabolic path from
x
to
z
should have weight
\mathcal{S}(x,z)
.
The limit exists since
\|\pi|_{[y,z]}\|_{\mathcal{P}}-\mathcal{P}[(y,\pi(y))\to(z,1)]
is non-decreasing in
y
, by
[
DZ21
, Lemma 4.1(i)]
.

We quote the following results on computing the weight of paths.

Lemma C.2

(
[
DZ21
, Lemma 4.3]
)
.

The following statement holds almost surely for
\mathcal{P}
.
Let
\pi_{1},\pi_{2}
be any two parabolic paths across
\mathcal{P}
from
x\geq 0
to
z_{1},z_{2}
respectively, such that for some
z_{0}<z_{1}\wedge z_{2}
, we have
\pi_{1}(y)=\pi_{2}(y)
for any
y\leq z_{0}
.
Then

\|\pi_{1}\|_{\mathcal{P}}-\|\pi_{1}|_{[z_{0},z_{1}]}\|_{\mathcal{P}}=\|\pi_{2}\|_{\mathcal{P}}-\|\pi_{2}|_{[z_{0},z_{2}]}\|_{\mathcal{P}}

The next result states that, for a bounded region in
\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{N}
, and any parabolic path without a “jump point” inside that region, the weight of the path is measurable with respect to
\mathcal{P}
outside that region.

Lemma C.3

(
[
DZ21
, Lemma 4.4]
)
.

Take any closed interval
I\subset\mathbb{R}
and
k\in\mathbb{N}
.
Let
\mathcal{F}_{I,k}
be the sigma-algebra generated by all null sets, all
\mathcal{P}_{i}
for
i>k
, and
\{\mathcal{P}_{i}(x):x\not\in I\}
for
1\leq i\leq k
.
Take any
x\geq 0
, and let
\Sigma_{x}
be the set of parabolic paths
\pi
from
x
to some
z\in\mathbb{R}
such that either
\pi(y)>k
for any
y\in(-\infty,z]\cap I
, or else
I\subset(-\infty,z]
and
\pi
is constant on
I
. Let
F:\Sigma_{x}\to\mathbb{R}
be the random function recording path weight in
\mathcal{P}
:
F(\pi)=\|\pi\|_{\mathcal{P}}
. Then
F
is
\mathcal{F}_{I,k}
-measurable.

A parabolic path
\pi
from
x\geq 0
to
y
is a
geodesic
if the weight
\|\pi\|_{\mathcal{P}}
is finite, and is maximal among all parabolic paths in
\mathcal{P}
from
x
to
y
; and this is equivalent to
\|\pi\|_{\mathcal{P}}=\mathcal{S}(x,y)
, by
[
DZ21
, Lemma 4.1(ii)]
.
It is proved in
[
DZ21
, Lemma 4.2]
that, almost surely, there exists a geodesic from any
x\geq 0
to any
y
; and for any fixed
x\geq 0
and
y
, almost surely there is a unique geodesic from
x
to
y
.
We shall use
\pi\{x,y\}
to denote any geodesic from
x
to
y
.

For paths across the Airy line ensemble, we use the following notion for disjointness.
For any paths
\pi_{1}:(-\infty,x_{1}]\to\mathbb{N}
and
\pi_{2}:(-\infty,x_{2}]\to\mathbb{N}
, we say
\pi_{1}<\pi_{2}
, if
x_{1}<x_{2}
, and for any
x\leq x_{1}
, we have
\lim_{y\nearrow x}\pi_{1}(y)<\pi_{2}(x)
(we require this instead of
\pi_{1}(x)<\pi_{2}(x)
because
\pi_{1}
is defined to be càdlàg).
We say that
\pi_{1}
and
\pi_{2}
are
disjoint
, if
\pi_{1}<\pi_{2}
or
\pi_{2}<\pi_{1}
; and we say that
\pi_{1}

intersects

\pi_{2}
, if they are not disjoint.
Just like in the directed landscape (as stated in Lemma
3.15
), the disjointness of geodesics is also equivalent to an inequality for the Airy sheet.

Lemma C.4
.

For any
0\leq x_{1}<x_{2}
,
y_{1}<y_{2}
, assuming that
\pi\{x_{1},y_{1}\}
and
\pi\{x_{2},y_{2}\}
are unique,
\pi\{x_{1},y_{1}\}
and
\pi\{x_{2},y_{2}\}
being disjoint is equivalent to that

\mathcal{S}(x_{1},y_{1})+\mathcal{S}(x_{2},y_{2})>\mathcal{S}(x_{1},y_{2})+\mathcal{S}(x_{2},y_{1}).

(C.3)

Proof.

We follow a similar idea as in the proof of Lemma
3.15
.
Whenever
\pi\{x_{1},y_{1}\}
intersects
\pi\{x_{2},y_{2}\}
, we can find
x_{*}\leq y_{1}
with

\lim_{y\nearrow x_{*}}\pi\{x_{1},y_{1}\}(y)\geq\pi\{x_{2},y_{2}\}(x_{*}).

Again note that we use
\lim_{y\nearrow x_{*}}\pi\{x_{1},y_{1}\}(y)
instead of
\pi\{x_{1},y_{1}\}(x_{*})
here, due to that
\pi\{x_{1},y_{1}\}
is càdlàg.
Using this we construct
\pi_{1}^{\prime}
from
x_{1}
to
y_{2}
and
\pi_{2}^{\prime}
from
x_{2}
to
y_{1}
, by switching the paths
\pi\{x_{1},y_{1}\}
on
[x_{*},y_{1}]
and
\pi\{x_{2},y_{2}\}
on
[x_{*},y_{2}]
.
Then by Lemma
C.2

\|\pi_{1}^{\prime}\|_{\mathcal{P}}+\|\pi_{2}^{\prime}\|_{\mathcal{P}}=\|\pi\{x_{1},y_{1}\}\|_{\mathcal{P}}+\|\pi\{x_{2},y_{2}\}\|_{\mathcal{P}}=\mathcal{S}(x_{1},y_{1})+\mathcal{S}(x_{2},y_{2}),

where the second equality is by the definition of
\pi\{x_{1},y_{1}\}
and
\pi\{x_{2},y_{2}\}
.
Thus (
C.3
) cannot hold.

Now we assume that (
C.3
) does not hold; then by the quadrangle inequality (Lemma
3.8
), we must have that
\mathcal{S}(x_{1},y_{1})+\mathcal{S}(x_{2},y_{2})=\mathcal{S}(x_{1},y_{2})+\mathcal{S}(x_{2},y_{1})
.
We then consider any geodesics
\pi\{x_{1},y_{2}\}
and
\pi\{x_{2},y_{1}\}
.
By planarity they should cross, i.e., we can find some
x_{*}\leq y_{1}
with

\lim_{y\nearrow x_{*}}\pi\{x_{1},y_{2}\}(y)\leq\lim_{y\nearrow x_{*}}\pi\{x_{1},y_{2}\}(y),\quad\pi\{x_{1},y_{2}\}(x_{*})\geq\pi\{x_{1},y_{2}\}(x_{*}).

Then we construct
\pi_{1}^{\prime}
from
x_{1}
to
y_{1}
and
\pi_{2}^{\prime}
from
x_{2}
to
y_{2}
, by switching the paths
\pi\{x_{2},y_{1}\}
on
[x_{*},y_{1}]
and
\pi\{x_{1},y_{2}\}
on
[x_{*},y_{2}]
.
Now by Lemma
C.2
we have

\|\pi_{1}^{\prime}\|_{\mathcal{P}}+\|\pi_{2}^{\prime}\|_{\mathcal{P}}=\|\pi\{x_{1},y_{2}\}\|_{\mathcal{P}}+\|\pi\{x_{1},y_{2}\}\|_{\mathcal{P}}=\mathcal{S}(x_{1},y_{2})+\mathcal{S}(x_{2},y_{1})=\mathcal{S}(x_{1},y_{1})+\mathcal{S}(x_{2},y_{2}),

so
\pi_{1}^{\prime}
and
\pi_{2}^{\prime}
must be geodesics.
By uniqueness, we have
\pi_{1}^{\prime}=\pi\{x_{1},y_{1}\}
and
\pi_{2}^{\prime}=\pi\{x_{2},y_{2}\}
, and they are not disjoint at
x_{*}
.
∎

Using this and Lemma
3.15
, disjointness of geodesics in the directed landscape
\mathcal{L}
can be reduced to disjointness of geodesics in
\mathcal{P}
.

Corollary C.5
.

Couple
\mathcal{L}
with
\mathcal{S}
such that
\mathcal{S}(x,y)=\mathcal{L}(x,0;y,1)
for any
x,y\in\mathbb{R}
.
For any fixed
0\leq x_{1}<x_{2}
,
y_{1}<y_{2}
, the event that
\pi\{x_{1},y_{1}\}
and
\pi\{x_{2},y_{2}\}
are disjoint and
\Dist^{[0,1]}_{(x_{1},x_{2})\to(y_{1},y_{2})}
are equivalent (up to an event with probability zero). (Recall that
\Dist^{[0,1]}_{(x_{1},x_{2})\to(y_{1},y_{2})}
is the event where
\pi_{(x_{1},0;y_{1},1))}
and
\pi_{(x_{2},0;y_{2},1)}
are disjoint.)

C.2
Disjoint optimizers, ordering, and second geodesics

We also need the notion of disjoint optimizers from
[
DZ21
]
.
For any
k\in\mathbb{N}
, we let

\mathbb{R}^{k}_{\leq}=\{(x_{1},\ldots,x_{k}):x_{1}\leq\cdots\leq x_{k}\}.

For any
\mathbf{x}=(x_{1},\ldots,x_{k})
,
\mathbf{y}=(y_{1},\ldots,y_{k})
in
\mathbb{R}^{k}_{\leq}
with
0\leq x_{1}
, a
disjoint
k
-path
from
\mathbf{x}
to
\mathbf{y}
is a collection of parabolic paths
(\pi_{1},\ldots,\pi_{k})
, such that each
\pi_{i}
is from
x_{i}
to
y_{i}
, and
\pi_{i}<\pi_{i+1}
on
(-\infty,x_{i})
,
\pi_{i}(x_{i})\leq\pi_{i+1}(x_{i})
, for each
1\leq i<k
.
Note (due to that the paths are càdlàg) this is a weaker condition than
\pi_{i}
and
\pi_{i+1}
being disjoint.
A
disjoint optimizer
from
\mathbf{x}
to
\mathbf{y}
is a disjoint
k
-path
(\pi_{1},\ldots,\pi_{k})
, such that
\sum_{i=1}^{k}\|\pi_{i}\|_{\mathcal{P}}
achieves its supremum (over all disjoint
k
-paths from
\mathbf{x}
to
\mathbf{y}
).
It has been shown, see
[
DZ21
, Proposition 5.8]
, that almost surely for any
\mathbf{x}=(x_{1},\ldots,x_{k})
and
\mathbf{y}=(y_{1},\ldots,y_{k})
in
\mathbb{R}^{k}_{\leq}
with
0\leq x_{1}
, there is a disjoint optimizer from
\mathbf{x}
to
\mathbf{y}
.

For
x\geq 0
and
y\in\mathbb{R}
, we consider all the parabolic paths
\pi:(-\infty,y]\to\mathbb{N}
across the Airy line ensemble from
x
, with
\lim_{z\nearrow y}\pi(z)\geq 2
.
Note that at the endpoint we allow
\pi(y)=1
.
We denote any one with the maximum weight as a
second geodesic from
x
to
y
.

Lemma C.6
.

Almost surely, for any
x\geq 0
and
y
, there is a second geodesic from
x
to
y
. In addition, if we take
\pi:(-\infty,y]\to\mathbb{N}
such that
\pi\equiv 1
, then any second geodesic from
x
to
y
together with
\pi
would give a disjoint optimizer from
(0,x)
to
(y,y)
.

Proof.

For the first part, we just take any disjoint optimizer
(\pi_{1},\pi_{2})
from
(0,x)
to
(y,y)
, and we claim that
\pi_{2}
is a second geodesic from
x
to
y
.
Otherwise, there is a path
\pi_{2}^{\prime}
from
x
to
y
, with
\lim_{z\nearrow y}\pi_{2}^{\prime}(z)\geq 2
, and
\|\pi_{2}^{\prime}\|_{\mathcal{P}}>\|\pi_{2}\|_{\mathcal{P}}
.
For
\pi:(-\infty,y]\to\mathbb{N}
with
\pi\equiv 1
, we have
\|\pi\|_{\mathcal{P}}=\mathcal{P}_{1}(y)=\mathcal{S}(0,y)\geq\|\pi_{1}\|_{\mathcal{P}}
(where the inequality is by (
C.2
)), so
\|\pi\|_{\mathcal{P}}+\|\pi_{2}^{\prime}\|_{\mathcal{P}}>\|\pi_{1}\|_{\mathcal{P}}+\|\pi_{2}\|_{\mathcal{P}}
, and this contradicts that
(\pi_{1},\pi_{2})
is a disjoint optimizer.

We next prove the second part. Take any second geodesic
\pi_{*}
from
x
to
y
, then
\|\pi_{*}\|_{\mathcal{P}}=\|\pi_{2}\|_{\mathcal{P}}
, implying that
\|\pi\|_{\mathcal{P}}+\|\pi_{*}\|_{\mathcal{P}}\geq\|\pi_{1}\|_{\mathcal{P}}+\|\pi_{2}\|_{\mathcal{P}}
. Thus
(\pi,\pi_{*})
must also be a disjoint optimizer from
(0,x)
to
(y,y)
.
∎

From any
x\geq 0,
to any
y\in\mathbb{R}
, there maybe more than one second geodesic, and below we use
\pi^{2}\{x,y\}:(-\infty,y]\to\mathbb{N}
to denote an arbitrary one.

Next we state the following result, which is about the ordering of disjoint optimizers. It can be viewed as a counterpart of Lemma
3.14
or Lemma
4.9
in the disjoint optimizer setting.
For any
x_{1}<x_{2}
and
\pi_{1}:(-\infty,x_{1}]\to\mathbb{N}
and
\pi_{2}:(-\infty,x_{2}]\to\mathbb{N}
, we define
\pi_{1}\vee\pi_{2}:(-\infty,x_{2}]\to\mathbb{N}
and
\pi_{1}\wedge\pi_{2}:(-\infty,x_{1}]\to\mathbb{N}
via

(\pi_{1}\vee\pi_{2})(x)=\begin{cases}\pi_{1}(x)\vee\pi_{2}(x),\quad&\forall x\in(-\infty,x_{1}],\\
\pi_{2}(x),\quad&\forall x\in(x_{1},x_{2}],\end{cases}

and

(\pi_{1}\wedge\pi_{2})(x)=\pi_{1}(x)\wedge\pi_{2}(x),\quad\forall x\in(-\infty,x_{1}].

Lemma C.7
.

Take any
\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y},\mathbf{x}^{\prime},\mathbf{y}^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}_{\leq}
with
x_{1},x_{1}^{\prime}\geq 0
, and
\mathbf{x}\leq\mathbf{x}^{\prime},\mathbf{y}\leq\mathbf{y}^{\prime}
entry-wise. Take any disjoint optimizers
(\pi_{1},\ldots,\pi_{k})
from
\mathbf{x}
to
\mathbf{y}
, and
(\pi_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,\pi_{k}^{\prime})
from
\mathbf{x}^{\prime}
to
\mathbf{y}^{\prime}
.
Then
(\pi_{1}\vee\pi_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,\pi_{k}\vee\pi_{k}^{\prime})
is a disjoint optimizer from
\mathbf{x}^{\prime}
to
\mathbf{y}^{\prime}
, and
(\pi_{1}\wedge\pi_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,\pi_{k}\wedge\pi_{k}^{\prime})
is a disjoint optimizer from
\mathbf{x}
to
\mathbf{y}
.

For paths in finite intervals (rather than parabolic paths), such result follows obviously from the following facts: the
\wedge
and
\vee
of two disjoint optimizers are still disjoint optimizers (with the respective endpoints), and the sum of their weights is the same as the sum of weights of the disjoint optimizers.
For parabolic paths, there are extra complexities due to infiniteness of the paths.

Proof.

It is straightforward to check that for each
1\leq i\leq k
,
\pi_{i}\vee\pi_{i}^{\prime}
is a path from
x_{i}^{\prime}
to
y_{i}^{\prime}
, and
\pi_{i}\wedge\pi_{i}^{\prime}
is a path from
x_{i}
to
y_{i}
.
For
1\leq i<k
, we have
\pi_{i}\wedge\pi_{i}^{\prime}<\pi_{i+1}\wedge\pi_{i+1}^{\prime}
on
(-\infty,y_{i})
,
\pi_{i}(y_{i})\wedge\pi_{i}^{\prime}(y_{i})\leq\pi_{i+1}(y_{i})\wedge\pi_{i+1}^{\prime}(y_{i})
; and
\pi_{i}\vee\pi_{i}^{\prime}<\pi_{i+1}\vee\pi_{i+1}^{\prime}
on
(-\infty,y_{i}^{\prime})
,
\pi_{i}(y_{i}^{\prime})\vee\pi_{i}^{\prime}(y_{i}^{\prime})\leq\pi_{i+1}(y_{i}^{\prime})\vee\pi_{i+1}^{\prime}(y_{i}^{\prime})
.
Thus
(\pi_{1}\vee\pi_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,\pi_{k}\vee\pi_{k}^{\prime})
is a disjoint
k
-path from
\mathbf{x}^{\prime}
to
\mathbf{y}^{\prime}
, and
(\pi_{1}\wedge\pi_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,\pi_{k}\wedge\pi_{k}^{\prime})
is a disjoint
k
-path from
\mathbf{x}
to
\mathbf{y}
.

We claim that for each
1\leq i\leq k
,

\|\pi_{i}\vee\pi_{i}^{\prime}\|_{\mathcal{P}}+\|\pi_{i}\wedge\pi_{i}^{\prime}\|_{\mathcal{P}}=\|\pi_{i}\|_{\mathcal{P}}+\|\pi_{i}^{\prime}\|_{\mathcal{P}}.

(C.4)

For this, note that for any
y\leq y_{i}
, we have

\|(\pi_{i}\vee\pi_{i}^{\prime})|_{[y,y_{i}^{\prime}]}\|_{\mathcal{P}}+\|(\pi_{i}\wedge\pi_{i}^{\prime})|_{[y,y_{i}]}\|_{\mathcal{P}}-\|\pi_{i}|_{[y,y_{i}]}\|_{\mathcal{P}}-\|\pi_{i}^{\prime}|_{[y,y_{i}^{\prime}]}\|_{\mathcal{P}}=0,

as the involved paths are finite.
By (
C.2
), to prove (
C.4
) it now suffices to show that

\lim_{y\to-\infty}\mathcal{P}[(y,\pi_{i}(y)\wedge\pi_{i}^{\prime}(y))\to(y_{i},1)]+\mathcal{P}[(y,\pi_{i}(y)\vee\pi_{i}^{\prime}(y))\to(y_{i}^{\prime},1)]\\
-\mathcal{P}[(y,\pi_{i}(y))\to(y_{i},1)]-\mathcal{P}[(y,\pi_{i}^{\prime}(y))\to(y_{i}^{\prime},1)]=0.

(C.5)

If there is some
y_{*}
such that
\pi_{i}(y)\leq\pi_{i}^{\prime}(y)
for any
y\leq y_{*}
, (
C.5
) holds obviously.
Otherwise, for infinitely many
y
decreasing to
-\infty
, we have
\pi_{i}(y)>\pi_{i}^{\prime}(y)
.
Then since
\lim_{y\to-\infty}\frac{\pi_{i}(y)}{2y^{2}}=x_{i}
,
\lim_{y\to-\infty}\frac{\pi_{i}^{\prime}(y)}{2y^{2}}=x_{i}^{\prime}
, and (the assumption that)
x_{i}\leq x_{i}^{\prime}
, we must have
x_{i}=x_{i}^{\prime}
.
Thus by (
C.1
) we have

\lim_{y\to-\infty}\mathcal{P}[(y,\pi_{i}(y))\to(y_{i},1)]-\mathcal{P}[(y,\pi_{i}(y))\to(y_{i}^{\prime},1)]\\
=\lim_{y\to-\infty}\mathcal{P}[(y,\pi_{i}^{\prime}(y))\to(y_{i},1)]-\mathcal{P}[(y,\pi_{i}^{\prime}(y))\to(y_{i}^{\prime},1)].

Note that (before sending
y\to-\infty
) the expression in (
C.5
) equals either
0
or

\mathcal{P}[(y,\pi_{i}^{\prime}(y))\to(y_{i},1)]+\mathcal{P}[(y,\pi_{i}(y))\to(y_{i}^{\prime},1)]\\
-\mathcal{P}[(y,\pi_{i}(y))\to(y_{i},1)]-\mathcal{P}[(y,\pi_{i}^{\prime}(y))\to(y_{i}^{\prime},1)].

Thus (
C.5
) holds, and so does (
C.4
). So we have
\sum_{i=1}^{k}\|\pi_{i}\vee\pi_{i}^{\prime}\|_{\mathcal{P}}+\|\pi_{i}\wedge\pi_{i}^{\prime}\|_{\mathcal{P}}=\sum_{i=1}^{k}\|\pi_{i}\|_{\mathcal{P}}+\|\pi_{i}^{\prime}\|_{\mathcal{P}}
, and the conclusion follows.
∎

C.3
Proof of Lemma
C.1

We first translate the event
\mathcal{E}_{coal}^{\prime}\cap\mathcal{E}_{disj}
into an event on disjointness of geodesics in
\mathcal{P}
.
Recall that

\mathcal{E}_{disj}=\Dist_{(-1,1)\to(-\delta,\delta)}^{[0,1]}\cap(\Dist_{(-1-\delta,-1+\delta)\to(-2,2)}^{[0,1]})^{c}\cap(\Dist_{(1-\delta,1+\delta)\to(-2,2)}^{[0,1]})^{c},

i.e., the geodesics
\pi_{(-1,0;-\delta,1)}
and
\pi_{(1,0;\delta,1)}
are disjoint, while, the geodesic pairs given by

\{\pi_{(-1-\delta,0;-2,1)},\pi_{(-1+\delta,0;2,1)}\},\{\pi_{(1-\delta,0;-2,1)},\pi_{(1+\delta,0;2,1)}\},

coalesce.
For easier analysis and to use Corollary
C.5
, we reflect time and then shift the starting points, since the corollary only allows positive starting points, and the skew-shift invariance property of the directed landscape implies that the disjointness of the geodesics stay invariant as well under such a shift. More precisely, by Lemma
3.7
,
\mathcal{E}_{coal}^{\prime}\cap\mathcal{E}_{disj}
has the same probability as

\Dist_{(2-\delta,2+\delta)\to(-1,1)}^{[0,1]}\cap(\Dist_{(0,4)\to(-1-\delta,-1+\delta)}^{[0,1]})^{c}\cap(\Dist_{(0,4)\to(1-\delta,1+\delta)}^{[0,1]})^{c}.

Then by Corollary
C.5
, we have
\mathbb{P}[\mathcal{E}_{coal}^{\prime}\cap\mathcal{E}_{disj}]=\mathbb{P}[\mathcal{E}_{ALE}]
, where
\mathcal{E}_{ALE}
is the following event:

1.

\pi\{0,-1-\delta\}
intersects
\pi\{4,-1+\delta\}
, and
\pi\{0,1-\delta\}
intersects
\pi\{4,1+\delta\}
.

2.

\pi\{2-\delta,-1\}
and
\pi\{2+\delta,1\}
are disjoint.

It remains to show that
\mathcal{E}_{ALE}
happens with positive probability.

Below we assume that the geodesic
\pi\{x,y\}
for any rational
x\geq 0
and
y
is unique. This event has probability one by
[
DZ21
, Lemma 4.2]
.
An implication of this assumption is that, for any rational
y
,
\pi\{0,y\}
would be the function staying at
1
on
(-\infty,y]
. Recall (from the proof of Lemma
8.11
) that
\delta>0
is taken to be an arbitrary small number, and below we assume that it is rational.

Step 1: reduce to events on single geodesics.

We note that
\pi\{0,-1-\delta\}
and
\pi\{0,1-\delta\}
would be constant
1
on the whole domains
(-\infty,-1-\delta]
and
(-\infty,1-\delta]
respectively, since these geodesics are assumed to be unique.
Then the events
\pi\{0,-1-\delta\}
intersects
\pi\{4,-1+\delta\}
, and
\pi\{0,1-\delta\}
intersects
\pi\{4,1+\delta\}
, would be equivalent to
\pi\{4,-1+\delta\}(-1-\delta)=1
and
\pi\{4,1+\delta\}(1-\delta)=1
; and we denote them as
\mathcal{E}_{ALE,1}
and
\mathcal{E}_{ALE,2}
respectively.

For the non-coalescence event, we consider any second geodesics
\pi^{2}\{2-\delta,-1\}
and
\pi^{2}\{2+\delta,0\}
.
Then by the asymptotic directions (of
\pi^{2}\{2-\delta,-1\}
and
\pi^{2}\{2+\delta,0\}(y)
), there exists rational
y_{0}<-1
, such that
\pi^{2}\{2-\delta,-1\}(y)<\pi^{2}\{2+\delta,0\}(y)
for any
y\leq y_{0}
.
Further we claim that we can take
y_{0}
small enough, such that

\|\pi^{2}\{2+\delta,0\}\|_{\mathcal{P}}-\mathcal{P}_{2}(0)>\|\pi^{2}\{2+\delta,y_{0}\}\|_{\mathcal{P}}-\mathcal{P}_{2}(y_{0}),

(C.6)

which will ensure that
\pi^{2}\{2+\delta,0\}
stays below the second line up to
y_{0}
, which will allow us to construct events ensuring disjointness of
\pi\{2-\delta,-1\}
and
\pi\{2+\delta,1\}.
Before describing this, we first show how to establish (
C.6
) using the following lemma which is essentially the same as the quadrangle inequality.

Lemma C.8
.

Almost surely the following is true.
The function
y\mapsto\|\pi^{2}\{2+\delta,y\}\|_{\mathcal{P}}-\mathcal{P}_{2}(y)
is non-decreasing, and
\limsup_{y\to-\infty}\|\pi^{2}\{2+\delta,y\}\|_{\mathcal{P}}-\mathcal{P}_{2}(y)<\|\pi^{2}\{2+\delta,0\}\|_{\mathcal{P}}-\mathcal{P}_{2}(0)
.

Proof.

For any
y_{1}<y_{2}
, and any second geodesic
\pi^{2}\{2+\delta,y_{1}\}
, we can construct a path
\pi
from
2+\delta
to
y_{2}
, which is the same as
\pi^{2}\{2+\delta,y_{1}\}
on
(-\infty,y_{1})
, and equals
1
on
[y_{1},y_{2}]
.
We then have that
\|\pi\|_{\mathcal{P}}=\|\pi^{2}\{2+\delta,y_{1}\}\|_{\mathcal{P}}-\mathcal{P}_{2}(y_{1})+\mathcal{P}_{2}(y_{2})
. We also have that
\|\pi\|_{\mathcal{P}}\leq\|\pi^{2}\{2+\delta,y_{2}\}\|_{\mathcal{P}}
, so we have
\|\pi^{2}\{2+\delta,y_{1}\}\|_{\mathcal{P}}-\mathcal{P}_{2}(y_{1})\leq\|\pi^{2}\{2+\delta,y_{2}\}\|_{\mathcal{P}}-\mathcal{P}_{2}(y_{2})
.

For the second part, we assume the contrary; then we have that
\|\pi^{2}\{2+\delta,y\}\|_{\mathcal{P}}-\mathcal{P}_{2}(y)=\|\pi^{2}\{2+\delta,0\}\|_{\mathcal{P}}-\mathcal{P}_{2}(0)
for any
y\leq 0
.
By Lemma
C.2
we have
\|\pi\{2+\delta,0\}\|_{\mathcal{P}}=\sup_{y\leq 0}\|\pi^{2}\{2+\delta,y\}\|_{\mathcal{P}}+\mathcal{P}[(y,2)\to(0,1)]
, which further means

\begin{split}\|\pi\{2+\delta,0\}\|_{\mathcal{P}}&=\sup_{y\leq y^{\prime}\leq 0}\|\pi^{2}\{2+\delta,y\}\|_{\mathcal{P}}-\mathcal{P}_{2}(y)+\mathcal{P}_{2}(y^{\prime})-\mathcal{P}_{1}(y^{\prime})+\mathcal{P}_{1}(0)\\
&=\sup_{y^{\prime}\leq 0}\|\pi^{2}\{2+\delta,0\}\|_{\mathcal{P}}-\mathcal{P}_{2}(0)+\mathcal{P}_{2}(y^{\prime})-\mathcal{P}_{1}(y^{\prime})+\mathcal{P}_{1}(0)\\
&=\|\pi^{2}\{2+\delta,0\}\|_{\mathcal{P}}-\mathcal{P}_{2}(0)+\mathcal{P}_{1}(0),\end{split}

(C.7)

where the last equality is due to the following reason.
By the Brownian Gibbs property of
\mathcal{P}
, we have
\mathbb{P}[\mathcal{P}_{1}(0)-\mathcal{P}_{2}(0)\leq\delta]>0
for any
\delta>0
.
Then by stationarity and ergodicity of
\mathcal{P}
plus a parabola, we have that
\mathbb{P}[\liminf_{y\to\infty}\mathcal{P}_{1}(y)-\mathcal{P}_{2}(y)\leq\delta]=1
, so almost surely
\liminf_{y\to\infty}\mathcal{P}_{1}(y)-\mathcal{P}_{2}(y)=0
, and
\sup_{y^{\prime}\leq 0}\mathcal{P}_{2}(y^{\prime})-\mathcal{P}_{1}(y^{\prime})=0
.

On the other hand, by the existence of the geodesic
\pi\{2+\delta,0\}
, there exists some
y_{*}\leq 0
with
\|\pi\{2+\delta,0\}\|_{\mathcal{P}}=\|\pi^{2}\{2+\delta,y_{*}\}\|_{\mathcal{P}}+\mathcal{P}_{1}(0)-\mathcal{P}_{1}(y_{*})
, by Lemma
C.2
. Thus with (
C.7
) we have

\|\pi^{2}\{2+\delta,0\}\|_{\mathcal{P}}-\mathcal{P}_{2}(0)=\|\pi^{2}\{2+\delta,y_{*}\}\|_{\mathcal{P}}+-\mathcal{P}_{1}(y_{*}).

Since we have assumed that
\|\pi^{2}\{2+\delta,0\}\|_{\mathcal{P}}-\mathcal{P}_{2}(0)=\|\pi^{2}\{2+\delta,y_{*}\}\|_{\mathcal{P}}-\mathcal{P}_{2}(y_{*})
, we have

\mathcal{P}_{1}(y_{*})=\mathcal{P}_{2}(y_{*})
.
However, by the Brownian Gibbs property of
\mathcal{P}
, we have that
\mathbb{P}[\mathcal{P}_{1}(y)=\mathcal{P}_{2}(y),\;\exists y\leq 0]=0
. Thus we conclude that
\mathbb{P}[\|\pi^{2}\{2+\delta,y\}\|_{\mathcal{P}}-\mathcal{P}_{2}(y)=\|\pi^{2}\{2+\delta,0\}\|_{\mathcal{P}}-\mathcal{P}_{2}(0),\;\forall y\leq 0]=0
.
∎

\mathcal{P}_{1}
\mathcal{P}_{2}
\mathcal{P}_{3}
\mathcal{P}_{4}
\mathcal{P}_{5}
\mathcal{P}_{6}
y_{0}
-1
0
1
\pi\{2-\delta,-1\}
\pi^{2}\{2-\delta,-1\}
\pi^{2}\{2+\delta,0\}
\pi\{2+\delta,1\}

Figure 22
:

An illustration of the proof of Lemma
C.9
: assuming
\pi\{2-\delta,-1\}(y_{0})=1
,
\pi\{2-\delta,-1\}
is to the left of
\pi^{2}\{2-\delta,-1\}
; and assuming
\pi\{2+\delta,1\}(0)>1
,
\pi\{2+\delta,1\}
is to the right of
\pi^{2}\{2+\delta,0\}
.
By the choice of
y_{0}
,
\pi^{2}\{2-\delta,-1\}
and
\pi^{2}\{2+\delta,0\}
are disjoint in
(-\infty,y_{0}]
; thus by the ordering of geodesics,
\pi\{2-\delta,-1\}
and
\pi\{2+\delta,1\}
are disjoint.

We now construct an event which ensures disjointness of geodesics.

Lemma C.9
.

\pi\{2-\delta,-1\}(y_{0})=1
and
\pi\{2+\delta,1\}(0)>1
imply that
\pi\{2-\delta,-1\}
and
\pi\{2+\delta,1\}
are disjoint.

Proof.

The proof is a consequence of ordering properties of geodesics and second geodesics. In particular, we show that (in
(-\infty,-1]
)

\pi\{2-\delta,-1\}\leq\pi^{2}\{2-\delta,-1\}\leq\pi^{2}\{2+\delta,0\}\leq\pi\{2+\delta,1\}

and then establish certain strictness of the inequalities (see Figure
22
). While the inequalities are just a consequence of planarity and uniqueness of the relevant objects, the formal arguments are a bit tedious.

1.

Take some rational
y_{1}<-1
with
\pi\{2-\delta,-1\}(y_{1})>1
. Then
(\pi\{0,y_{1}\},\pi\{2-\delta,-1\})
would be a disjoint optimizer from
(0,2-\delta)
to
(y_{1},-1)\}
.
We also know that
(\pi\{0,-1\},\pi^{2}\{2-\delta,-1\})
is a disjoint optimizer from
(0,2-\delta)
to
(-1,-1)
, by Lemma
C.6
.
Then by Lemma
C.7
,
(\pi\{0,y_{1}\},\pi\{2-\delta,-1\}\wedge\pi^{2}\{2-\delta,-1\})
would be another disjoint optimizer from
(0,2-\delta)
to
(y_{1},-1)
.
This implies that
\|\pi\{2-\delta,-1\}\wedge\pi^{2}\{2-\delta,-1\}\|_{\mathcal{P}}\geq\|\pi\{2-\delta,-1\}\|_{\mathcal{P}}=\mathcal{S}(2-\delta,-1)
, and
\pi\{2-\delta,-1\}\wedge\pi^{2}\{2-\delta,-1\}
is also a geodesic from
2-\delta
to
-1
,
So
\pi\{2-\delta,-1\}=\pi\{2-\delta,-1\}\wedge\pi^{2}\{2-\delta,-1\}
by the uniqueness.
Thus we have that
\pi\{2-\delta,-1\}(y)\leq\pi^{2}\{2-\delta,-1\}(y)
for any
y\in(-\infty,-1]
.

2.

When
\pi\{2+\delta,1\}(0)>1
,
(\pi\{0,0\},\pi\{2+\delta,1\})
is a disjoint optimizer from
(0,2+\delta)
to
(0,1)
.
By Lemma
C.6
,
(\pi\{0,0\},\pi^{2}\{2+\delta,0\})
is a disjoint optimizer from
(0,2+\delta)
to
(0,0)
.
Then by Lemma
C.7
,
(\pi\{0,0\},\pi\{2+\delta,1\}\vee\pi^{2}\{2+\delta,0\})
is another disjoint optimizer from
(0,2+\delta)
to
(0,1)
, so
\pi\{2+\delta,1\}\vee\pi^{2}\{2+\delta,0\}
would also be a geodesic from
2+\delta
to
1
.
So
\pi\{2+\delta,1\}=\pi\{2+\delta,1\}\vee\pi^{2}\{2+\delta,0\}
by the uniqueness,
and
\pi\{2+\delta,1\}(y)\geq\pi^{2}\{2+\delta,0\}(y)
for any
y\in(-\infty,0]
.

Since
\pi^{2}\{2-\delta,-1\}
and
\pi^{2}\{2+\delta,0\}
are disjoint on
(-\infty,y_{0}]
by definition of
y_{0}
, we have that
\pi\{2-\delta,-1\}
and
\pi\{2+\delta,1\}
are disjoint on
(-\infty,y_{0}]
. On
[y_{0},-1]
we have
\pi\{2-\delta,-1\}=1
and
\pi\{2+\delta,1\}>1
by hypothesis, and so
\pi\{2-\delta,-1\}
and
\pi\{2+\delta,1\}
are also disjoint.
∎

To use the above, we let
\mathcal{E}_{ALE,3}
be the event
\pi\{2-\delta,-1\}(y_{0})=1
and
\mathcal{E}_{ALE,4}
be the event
\pi\{2+\delta,1\}(0)>1
.

From the above lemma we have that

\mathcal{E}_{ALE,1}\cap\mathcal{E}_{ALE,2}\cap\mathcal{E}_{ALE,3}\cap\mathcal{E}_{ALE,4}\subset\mathcal{E}_{ALE}=\mathcal{E}_{coal}^{\prime}\cap\mathcal{E}_{disj}.

We will next construct events
\mathcal{E}_{ALE,1}^{\prime}
,
\mathcal{E}_{ALE,2}^{\prime}
,
\mathcal{E}_{ALE,3}^{\prime}
, and
\mathcal{E}_{ALE,4}^{\prime}
, which imply
\mathcal{E}_{ALE,1}
,
\mathcal{E}_{ALE,2}
,
\mathcal{E}_{ALE,3}
,
\mathcal{E}_{ALE,4}
, respectively.
These events are given in terms of inequalities involving
\mathcal{P}_{1}
,
\mathcal{P}_{2}
, and weights of certain second geodesics.
We will show that, conditional on
\{\mathcal{P}_{n}\}_{n=2}^{\infty}
, and
\mathcal{P}_{1}
outside some compact interval, the intersection of these events has positive probability.
This is by considering
\mathcal{P}_{1}
inside that compact interval and using the Brownian Gibbs property of
\mathcal{P}
.

Step 2: reduce to events on
\mathcal{P}_{1}
and weights of second geodesics.

Let
\mathcal{F}
be the sigma-algebra generated by
\{\mathcal{P}_{n}\}_{n=2}^{\infty}
, and
\mathcal{P}_{1}(x)
for
x\leq y_{0}-1
and
x\geq 2
.
By Lemma
C.3
, for any rational
x\geq 0
, the random variable
\|\pi^{2}\{x,y\}\|_{\mathcal{P}}
for any
y
is
\{\mathcal{P}_{n}\}_{n=2}^{\infty}
measurable; and
y_{0}
is
\{\mathcal{P}_{n}\}_{n=2}^{\infty}
measurable.
Then these random variables are all
\mathcal{F}
measurable. By the Brownian Gibbs property of the Airy line ensemble
[
CH14
]
, conditional on
\mathcal{F}
, the process
\mathcal{P}_{1}
on
[y_{0}-1,2]
is a Brownian bridge conditional on avoiding
\mathcal{P}_{2}
.

It would be useful to remember the following simple relation between geodesics and second geodesics.
By Lemma
C.2
, we have that for any rational
x\geq 0
and
y
,

\|\pi\{x,y\}\|_{\mathcal{P}}=\sup_{z\leq y}\|\pi^{2}\{x,z\}\|_{\mathcal{P}}+\mathcal{P}_{1}(y)-\mathcal{P}_{1}(z),

(C.8)

and by uniqueness of
\pi\{x,y\}
this supremum is achieved at a unique
z_{*}\leq y
, such that
\pi\{x,y\}(z)=1
for
z\in[z_{*},y]
, and
\pi\{x,y\}(z)>1
for
z\in(-\infty,z_{*})
.

We will make use of a random variable
H>0
, which is
\mathcal{F}
measurable, and its exact definition will come up only at the end.
Consider the event
\mathcal{E}_{ALE,1}^{\prime}
:

\|\pi^{2}\{4,-1-2\delta\}\|_{\mathcal{P}}-\mathcal{P}_{1}(-1-2\delta)>-H^{2}>\sup_{-1-\delta\leq x\leq-1+\delta}\|\pi^{2}\{4,x\}\|_{\mathcal{P}}-\mathcal{P}_{1}(x)

as illustrated in Figure
23
(at
-1-2\delta
and in the interval
[-1-\delta,-1+\delta]
).
We note that under
\mathcal{E}_{ALE,1}^{\prime}
, for the function
\|\pi^{2}\{4,\cdot\}\|_{\mathcal{P}}-\mathcal{P}_{1}
, its supremum in
[-1-\delta,-1+\delta]
is smaller than its value at
-1-2\delta
.
By the above relation (
C.8
) between geodesics and second geodesics,
\mathcal{E}_{ALE,1}^{\prime}
implies that the
\pi\{4,-1+\delta\}
does not jump to the second line in
[-1-\delta,-1+\delta]
, thus
\pi\{4,-1+\delta\}(-1-\delta)=1
and
\mathcal{E}_{ALE,1}
holds.

Similarly, let
\mathcal{E}_{ALE,2}^{\prime}
be the event where

\|\pi^{2}\{4,1-2\delta\}\|_{\mathcal{P}}-\mathcal{P}_{1}(1-2\delta)>-H>\sup_{1-\delta\leq x\leq 1+\delta}\|\pi^{2}\{4,x\}\|_{\mathcal{P}}-\mathcal{P}_{1}(x),

and
\mathcal{E}_{ALE,3}^{\prime}
be the event where

\|\pi^{2}\{2-\delta,y_{0}-1/2\}\|_{\mathcal{P}}-\mathcal{P}_{1}(y_{0}-1/2)>-H>\sup_{y_{0}\leq x\leq-1}\|\pi^{2}\{2-\delta,x\}\|_{\mathcal{P}}-\mathcal{P}_{1}(x),

which are also illustrated in in Figure
23
.
We can similarly conclude that
\mathcal{E}_{ALE,2}^{\prime}\subset\mathcal{E}_{ALE,2}
and
\mathcal{E}_{ALE,3}^{\prime}\subset\mathcal{E}_{ALE,3}
.

Finally we let
\mathcal{E}_{ALE,4}^{\prime}
:

\mathcal{P}_{1}(1/2)-\mathcal{P}_{2}(1/2)<H^{-1}<\inf_{y_{0}\leq x\leq 0}\mathcal{P}_{1}(x)-\mathcal{P}_{2}(x).

It would be slightly more complicated to get
\mathcal{E}_{ALE,4}
from
\mathcal{E}_{ALE,4}^{\prime}
.

Lemma C.10
.

\mathcal{E}_{ALE,4}^{\prime}\subset\mathcal{E}_{ALE,4}
when
H
is large enough (depending on
\mathcal{F}
).

The idea behind is that, using (
C.6
) (in choosing
y_{0}
), we can show that the event
\mathcal{E}_{ALE,4}^{\prime}
implies
\|\pi^{2}\{2+\delta,1/2\}\|_{\mathcal{P}}-\mathcal{P}_{1}(1/2)>\sup_{x\leq 0}\|\pi^{2}\{2+\delta,x\}\|_{\mathcal{P}}-\mathcal{P}_{1}(x)
. By the relation (
C.8
), this further implies that the geodesic
\pi\{2+\delta,1\}
jumps to the second line at a point
>0
, thus
\mathcal{E}_{ALE,4}
holds.

We leave the detailed proof of Lemma
C.10
to the end, and assume it for now.
Conditional on
\mathcal{F}
, the above inequalities for the event
\mathcal{E}_{ALE,1}^{\prime}\cap\mathcal{E}_{ALE,2}^{\prime}\cap\mathcal{E}_{ALE,3}^{\prime}\cap\mathcal{E}_{ALE,4}^{\prime}
can be summarized as follows (the inequalities are presented essentially in an order such that the points/intervals involved move from left to right):

\begin{aligned} \mathcal{P}_{1}(y_{0}-1/2)<&\|\pi^{2}\{2-\delta,y_{0}-1/2\}\|_{\mathcal{P}}+H,\\
\mathcal{P}_{1}(x)>&\|\pi^{2}\{2-\delta,x\}\|_{\mathcal{P}}+H,\quad\forall y_{0}\leq x\leq-1,\\
\mathcal{P}_{1}(x)>&\mathcal{P}_{2}(x)+H^{-1},\quad\forall y_{0}\leq x\leq 0,\\
{\mathcal{P}_{1}(-1-2\delta)}<&\|\pi^{2}\{4,-1-2\delta\}\|_{\mathcal{P}}+H^{2},\\
\mathcal{P}_{1}(x)>&\|\pi^{2}\{4,x\}\|_{\mathcal{P}}+H^{2},\quad\forall-1-\delta\leq x\leq-1+\delta,\\
\mathcal{P}_{1}(1/2)<&\mathcal{P}_{2}(1/2)+H^{-1},\\
\mathcal{P}_{1}(1-2\delta)<&\|\pi^{2}\{4,1-2\delta\}\|_{\mathcal{P}}+H,\\
\mathcal{P}_{1}(x)>&\|\pi^{2}\{4,x\}\|_{\mathcal{P}}+H,\quad\forall 1-\delta\leq x\leq 1+\delta,\end{aligned}\quad\quad\begin{aligned} &\mathcal{E}_{ALE,3}^{\prime},\\
&\mathcal{E}_{ALE,3}^{\prime},\\
&\mathcal{E}_{ALE,4}^{\prime},\\
&\mathcal{E}_{ALE,1}^{\prime},\\
&\mathcal{E}_{ALE,1}^{\prime},\\
&\mathcal{E}_{ALE,4}^{\prime},\\
&\mathcal{E}_{ALE,2}^{\prime},\\
&\mathcal{E}_{ALE,2}^{\prime}.\\
\end{aligned}

(C.9)

\|\pi^{2}\{4,x\}\|_{\mathcal{P}}+H^{2}
\|\pi^{2}\{4,x\}\|_{\mathcal{P}}+H^{2}

Figure 23
:
An illustration of the conditions (
C.9
) to be satisfied by
\mathcal{P}_{1}
: the green curves are
\mathcal{P}_{2}
and
\mathcal{P}_{1}
outside
(y_{0}-1,2)
, and are
\mathcal{F}
-measurable; the black curve is
\mathcal{P}_{1}
in
(y_{0}-1,2)
, which (give
\mathcal{F}
) is a Brownian bridge conditioned on staying above
\mathcal{P}_{2}
. The red points are upper bounds and the blue curves are lower bounds.

Note that by (
C.8
),
\pi^{2}\{2-\delta,x\}
and
\pi^{2}\{4,x\}
are uniformly bounded for
x
in any compact interval.
Also note that
\mathcal{P}_{1}
on
[y_{0}-1,2]
is a Brownian bridge conditional on
>\mathcal{P}_{2}
(by the Brownian Gibbs property of
\mathcal{P}
mentioned in Section
3.3
).
Then by looking up the inequalities in (
C.9
) (see Figure
23
), we can choose
H
large enough, such that with positive probability, all of them hold simultaneously, and Lemma
C.10
also holds.
Thus we have

\mathbb{P}[\mathcal{E}_{ALE,1}^{\prime}\cap\mathcal{E}_{ALE,2}^{\prime}\cap\mathcal{E}_{ALE,3}^{\prime}\cap\mathcal{E}_{ALE,4}^{\prime}\mid\mathcal{F}]>0.

By averaging over
\mathcal{F}
, we have that

\mathbb{P}[\mathcal{E}_{coal}^{\prime}\cap\mathcal{E}_{disj}]\geq\mathbb{P}[\mathcal{E}_{ALE,1}\cap\mathcal{E}_{ALE,2}\cap\mathcal{E}_{ALE,3}\cap\mathcal{E}_{ALE,4}]\geq\mathbb{P}[\mathcal{E}_{ALE,1}^{\prime}\cap\mathcal{E}_{ALE,2}^{\prime}\cap\mathcal{E}_{ALE,3}^{\prime}\cap\mathcal{E}_{ALE,4}^{\prime}]>0.

It remains to prove Lemma
C.10
.

Proof of Lemma
C.10
.

We show
\mathcal{E}_{ALE,4}^{\prime}
implies that

\|\pi^{2}\{2+\delta,1/2\}\|_{\mathcal{P}}-\mathcal{P}_{1}(1/2)>\sup_{x\leq 0}\|\pi^{2}\{2+\delta,x\}\|_{\mathcal{P}}-\mathcal{P}_{1}(x),

(C.10)

which further implies
\mathcal{E}_{ALE,4}
, by the relation (
C.8
) between geodesics and second geodesics.
Under
\mathcal{E}_{ALE,4}^{\prime}
, we have that

\begin{split}&\|\pi^{2}\{2+\delta,1/2\}\|_{\mathcal{P}}-\mathcal{P}_{1}(1/2)\\
>&\|\pi^{2}\{2+\delta,1/2\}\|_{\mathcal{P}}-\mathcal{P}_{2}(1/2)-H^{-1}\\
\geq&\sup_{y_{0}\leq x\leq 0}\|\pi^{2}\{2+\delta,x\}\|_{\mathcal{P}}-\mathcal{P}_{2}(x)-H^{-1}\\
>&\sup_{y_{0}\leq x\leq 0}\|\pi^{2}\{2+\delta,x\}\|_{\mathcal{P}}-\mathcal{P}_{1}(x).\end{split}

(C.11)

Here the first and third inequality is by
\mathcal{E}_{ALE,4}^{\prime}
. The second inequality is essentially a quadrangle inequality. More precisely, it is due to the following reason: for any
y_{0}\leq x\leq 0
, any any second geodesic
\pi^{2}\{2+\delta,x\}
, we can construct a path from
2+\delta
to
1/2
, which is the same as
\pi^{2}\{2+\delta,x\}
on
(-\infty,x)
, and equals
2
on
[x,1/2]
. By Lemma
C.2
, the weight of this path is
\|\pi^{2}\{2+\delta,x\}\|_{\mathcal{P}}-\mathcal{P}_{2}(x)+\mathcal{P}_{2}(1/2)\leq\|\pi^{2}\{2+\delta,1/2\}\|_{\mathcal{P}}
.

By (
C.6
), we can take
H
large enough and
\mathcal{F}
measurable, such that

\|\pi^{2}\{2+\delta,0\}\|_{\mathcal{P}}-\mathcal{P}_{2}(0)-H^{-1}>\|\pi^{2}\{2+\delta,y_{0}\}\|_{\mathcal{P}}-\mathcal{P}_{2}(y_{0}).

and hence by the second inequality above, we have

\|\pi^{2}\{2+\delta,1/2\}\|_{\mathcal{P}}-\mathcal{P}_{1}(1/2)>\|\pi^{2}\{2+\delta,y_{0}\}\|_{\mathcal{P}}-\mathcal{P}_{2}(y_{0}).

(C.12)

We also have

\|\pi^{2}\{2+\delta,y_{0}\}\|_{\mathcal{P}}-\mathcal{P}_{2}(y_{0})\geq\sup_{x\leq y_{0}}\|\pi^{2}\{2+\delta,x\}\|_{\mathcal{P}}-\mathcal{P}_{1}(x),

(C.13)

which is again essentially a quadrangle inequality, and follows similar arguments.
Note that (
C.10
) is implied by (
C.11
), (
C.12
), and (
C.13
), so the conclusion follows.
∎
